# ¶ Marsilio Ficino of Florence
## On the Threefold Life <note>original Latin: "de triplici vita," Ficino's influential Renaissance treatise on health, longevity, and the astral influences on scholars.</note>

<meta>This page follows a blank flyleaf and marks the formal beginning of Marsilio Ficino’s "De Vita" (On Life). The translation continues from the previous physical description of the book's condition to the actual start of the text.</meta>

¶ The Preface of Marsilio Ficino the Florentine to the book 
"On Life," addressed to the magnanimous Lorenzo de' Medici, 
Savior of the Fatherland.

<margin>b</margin> <note>A small "guide letter" intended to tell the rubricator—the artist who adds red ink—to paint a large letter B.</note> **B**ACCHUS <note>The Roman god of wine and theater, also known as Dionysus</note> is sung of by the poets, the high overseer of priests, as being born twice. Perhaps they signify that it is necessary for a future priest to be reborn immediately upon being initiated. Or perhaps that the mind of a priest, having finally reached perfection and being deeply drunk on God, already appears to have been reborn. Or perhaps, in a more humble sense, that the wine—the offspring of Bacchus—is generated once on the vine just as he was from Semele <note>In mythology, Semele was the mother of Bacchus who died when his father Jove (Jupiter) appeared in his full glory; the unborn Bacchus was then sewn into Jove's thigh until his second "birth."</note>, with the clusters ripening under Phoebus <note>original: *sub phębo*; referring to Apollo, the god of the Sun</note>. Then it is regenerated again after the lightning-bolt of the vintage in its own vessel, just as the pure wine was in the thigh of Jove.

But we must not speak of sacred mysteries at this present time, when we are soon to help the weary with physical aid instead. Nor should we proceed with a style enslaved to gravity, but rather one that is free <term>Latin: *libero*; Ficino is making a pun here, as *Liber* is another name for Bacchus, the god of wine and freedom</term> and playful, since we have somehow begun immediately with Father Liber himself. And I say "somehow" rightly, for perhaps someone more prudent would have begun a medical work with Phoebus <note>Apollo</note>, the first of doctors, rather than with Bacchus. But what of it? If there is any omen—and not a vain one—in my mouth now bringing forth Bacchus by some chance, he perhaps heals more healthfully with a certain nourishing wine and the most joyful security than that Phoebus does with his herbs and songs. In whatever sense you take these things, that leader of priests, Bacchus, [possesses] twin... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>In this preface, Marsilio Ficino introduces his medical and philosophical work "On Life" with a playful mythological allegory about Bacchus, suggesting that joy and wine may be as healing as traditional medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Lorenzo de' Medici, Bacchus, Dionysus, Phoebus Apollo, rebirth, medical philosophy, wine, Semele, Jupiter</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s dedicatory preface to Lorenzo de' Medici. In the previous section, Ficino discussed the "double birth" of Bacchus as a metaphor for the priestly and scholarly life. Here, he applies that metaphor to his own life and the structure of the three books that follow.</meta>

is said to have had, as it were, two mothers. But Melchizedek <note>A mysterious priest-king mentioned in the Book of Genesis and the Letter to the Hebrews; he is often described as having no recorded genealogy, making Ficino's comparison of him having "scarcely" one parent a nod to his unique biblical status.</note>, that highest priest, had scarcely one mother and scarcely one father. I, the least of priests, have had two fathers: Ficino the physician <note>Ficino’s biological father, Diotifeci d'Agnolo, was the personal physician to Cosimo de' Medici.</note> and Cosimo de' Medici. From the former, I was born; from the latter, I was reborn. The former commended me to Galen <note>A 2nd-century Greek physician whose works remained the standard for medical knowledge in the Renaissance.</note>, who was both a physician and a Platonist. The latter, however, consecrated me to the divine Plato. And this one, just like the other, destined Marsilio to be a physician: Galen, indeed, a physician of bodies; but Plato, truly, a physician of souls.

**I** <note>The letter "I" is a large red initial in the original manuscript.</note> For a long time, therefore, under the guidance of Plato, I practiced the health-giving medicine of souls. When, after translating all of his books <note>Ficino completed the first full Latin translation of Plato’s dialogues in 1484.</note>, I soon composed eighteen books on the immortality of souls and eternal happiness, I did so to satisfy the wishes of my [reborn] father to the best of my strength. Then, thinking I must next satisfy my biological father, the physician, I composed a book on caring for the health of scholars <note>This refers to *De sanitate tuenda* (On Caring for Health), the first book of the *De Vita*.</note>. 

Moreover, after this, scholarly men desired not only to be well at certain times, but also to live a long life while remaining in good health. To these men, therefore, I next gave a book on long life <note>This refers to *De vita longa* (On Long Life), the second book of the *De Vita*.</note>. But they distrusted earthly medicines and remedies in a matter of such great importance. I therefore added a book on obtaining life, both healthy and long, from the heavens <note>original: *de uita tũ ualida tũ longa celitus companda*; this is the third and most controversial book of the set, dealing with astral influences and talismans.</note>, so that from the living body of the world itself, a certain more vigorous life might be propagated into our body—as if into a member of the world—just as if it were drawn from a vine. 

I pray, most indulgent Lorenzo, forgive these books of medicine: if, while I wish to be a physician, I somehow, even unintentionally, 

<summary>Ficino explains his dual lineage—biological and spiritual—and how his two "fathers" led him to study both physical medicine (Galen) and the medicine of the soul (Plato). He then outlines the three books of "On Life," explaining their progression from physical health to longevity and finally to celestial influence.</summary>
<keywords>Cosimo de' Medici, Galen, Plato, dual birth, medical philosophy, De Vita structure, scholars' health, astral medicine</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory preface to Lorenzo de' Medici. Ficino has been explaining his dual role as a physician of the body (following his father) and a physician of souls (following Plato and Cosimo de' Medici). He now transitions into why he is binding these three books—on health, long life, and planetary influence—into a single volume.</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

...and even if I am not often a good poet. For Apollo <note>Ficino uses the name "Phoebus," the Greek god of the sun, who was the patron of both healing and the arts.</note> is himself the discoverer of medicine and the master of poetry; he grants both of these gifts to us, not so much through medicinal herbs as through the lyre and song. Even Venus herself, according to the astrologers, produces the musician and the physician in equal measure <note>In Renaissance astrology, Venus governs "harmony." This harmony manifests as music for the ears and as health—the balanced harmony of the humors—for the physician.</note>. 

But enough of this for now. While I have been carefully considering the lives of scholars and similar citizens, I have neglected the health of my own books by allowing them to remain separated from each other. Therefore, acting dutifully toward them now for the first time, I join them into one single body. With its limbs now knit together into one form, let life be present in it immediately. 

However, this physical work <note>original: "opus physicum"</note>—this "body" of mine—cannot receive any life except my own. And life of that kind depends solely upon my soul. This soul, however, has for a long time lived with you, magnanimous Lorenzo, my patron. It dwells especially in that part of your most magnificent palace where, alongside the works of Plato, our own work on the immortality of souls is kept—that work which I dedicated to your name long ago <note>Ficino refers to his *Platonic Theology*, his major philosophical work on the soul, which he had previously dedicated to Lorenzo de' Medici.</note>. 

But this soul of mine, even if it leads a blessed life with you as if in its own homeland, is nevertheless restless (as the theologians say) until it can take up this physical work as its own body. Receive, therefore, most excellent Lorenzo, these books on the body following those books on the soul. Breathe upon these with the same favor with which you recently greeted the others. For in this way, this "body" will live by its own soul under your protection; and in turn, our soul—now united with its own body—will find rest within your household.

->a.iii.<- <note>This is a "signature mark," a guide for early printers and bookbinders to ensure the pages were folded and bound in the correct sequence.</note>

<summary>Ficino concludes his dedication by metaphorically uniting his writings on the soul (his previous philosophy) with these new writings on the body (medicine), presenting the completed volume to Lorenzo de' Medici.</summary>
<keywords>Apollo, Venus, astrology, harmony, Lorenzo de' Medici, soul and body, Platonic Theology, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page provides the formal titles for the three books that comprise Ficino's "Three Books on Life" (De vita libri tria) and begins the first book's specific dedication. Having previously explained his philosophical and medical motivations to Lorenzo de' Medici, he now addresses his close scholarly circle.</meta>

->¶ GENERAL INSCRIPTION<-

The Book of Life. Divided into Three Books. The First on a healthy life. The Second on a long life. ¶ The Third on life from the heavens <note>original: "caelitus." This third book deals with the "spirit" and how to attract favorable planetary influences through music, diet, and talismans.</note>.

¶ ON the care of health for those who apply themselves to the study of letters.

<note>A large initial letter "M" is printed in red ink, decorated with a landscape containing two figures. This likely illustrates the "Peripatetic" style of philosophical discussion—talking while walking—mentioned in the opening lines.</note>

**MARSILIO FICINO** of Florence to Giorgio Antonio Vespucci <note>A Dominican friar and uncle of the explorer Amerigo Vespucci; he was a close scholarly associate of Ficino.</note> and Giovanni Battista Boninsegni, men of distinguished integrity and learning, sends greetings <note>original: "S.D." for "Salutem Dicit," a standard formal greeting.</note>. 

We have talked much with you in these times, walking in the manner of the Peripatetics <note>The Peripatetics were followers of Aristotle, famous for walking while they debated philosophy.</note>, about caring for the health of those who are constantly devoted to the study of letters. These matters, gathered into a brief compendium, I have decided to entrust especially to you. 

Nor will I approve of this little work myself until I have learned that it has been approved by you especially—men and friends of the most proven character—nor will I allow it to undergo the elegant judgment of our Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent," the de facto ruler of Florence and Ficino's primary patron.</note>. For his prosperous health, if there be need, this work will primarily consult. For our present men of letters can hardly expect to be preferred <note>or "to be looked after"</note> unless they have first looked after their patron, their Maecenas <note>Gaius Maecenas was the legendary patron of Virgil and Horace; his name became a title for any great supporter of the arts.</note>. Read therefore diligently; and take care of your health... <unclear>ualitu</unclear> <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "ualitudine" (health).</note>

<summary>This page presents the formal titles for the "Three Books on Life" and opens the dedication of the first book, focusing on the health of scholars, addressed to Ficino's colleagues Vespucci and Boninsegni.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Book of Life, scholars' health, Peripatetics, Lorenzo de' Medici, Maecenas, Giorgio Antonio Vespucci</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s dedication of "On a Healthy Life" (Book I of his "Three Books on Life"). Having addressed his colleagues Vespucci and Boninsegni, he now explains the theological and philosophical necessity of health as a prerequisite for wisdom, transitioning into a discussion of the "Nine Guides" of the scholar's journey.</meta>

<note>A decorative printer’s mark, likely a leaf or fleuron, appears here.</note> 

->ON THE HEALTHY LIFE<-

...health <note>original: "dinẽ," the end of the word "valetudinẽ" or "consuetudinẽ" from the previous page.</note> most diligently. Once health is taken away, the sublime doors of the Muses—unless Almighty God by some wonderful power both leads and opens them—either are never touched by us, or are certainly knocked upon in vain. We wish this physical discussion of ours to look toward this argument above all: namely, if for the sake of attaining wisdom, the health of the body must be sought so greatly, how much more must the health of the mind <note>original: "sanitatẽ mentis." In Ficino's philosophy, the mind cannot function if the "spirit"—the subtle vapor connecting soul and body—is clouded by poor physical health.</note>, by which alone wisdom can be grasped, be sought. Otherwise, those who strive to capture wisdom with an unhealthy mind seek knowledge altogether ignorantly. Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Medicine."</note> promises health of the body, but Socrates promises health of the soul. But only He <note>Referring to Jesus Christ.</note> provides the true health of both, who exclaims thus: "Come to me, all you who labor and are burdened, and I will refresh you <note>original: "Venite ad me omnes qui laboratis, & onerati estis: atq̃ ego vos reficiã." A quotation from Matthew 11:28.</note>. I am the way, the truth, and the life" <note>original: "Ego sum via, veritas, & vita." A quotation from John 14:6.</note>.

<note>A decorative printer’s mark appears here.</note> 

->The Nine Guides of Scholars<-

<note>A large initial letter "Q" is printed here, marking the start of a new section.</note> 
**Q**uicever enters upon that rough, arduous, and long path—which indeed leads only at last through constant labor to the high temple of the nine Muses <note>The Muses were the Greek goddesses of the arts and sciences; here they represent the pinnacle of scholarly achievement.</note>—seems to require nine guides for this journey. Of these, the first three lead us in the heavens; the next three lead us in the soul; the last three lead us on earth. In the beginning, Mercury <note>The Roman god associated with communication, intellect, and the planet Mercury.</note> in the heavens either impels or encourages us to approach the journey of the Muses by investigating. For to Mercury is attributed the investigation...

a.iiii. <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to help binders assemble the book's pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Ficino argues that physical health is a necessary foundation for mental clarity and spiritual wisdom, blending Christian scripture with classical philosophy. He then introduces a cosmological system of nine guides (heavenly, psychological, and earthly) that assist the scholar, beginning with the planet Mercury.</summary>
<keywords>health, wisdom, Muses, Hippocrates, Socrates, Mercury, Neoplatonism, mental health, scholarly life, Nine Guides</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion of the "Nine Guides" necessary for a scholar to reach the Temple of the Muses. On the previous page, he likely introduced Mercury as the guide of investigation; here, he describes the roles of Apollo and Venus before detailing the internal and external guides required for a life of wisdom.</meta>

...of the whole duty of investigation <note>original: "investigationis," the first part of which appeared on the previous page.</note>. Next, Apollo <note>original: "Phębus." Ficino uses the sun god to represent the light of the intellect and the clarity of reason.</note> himself illuminates both the seeking minds and the things they seek with such abundant splendor that what was being searched for is clearly found by us. Then comes most gracious Venus, the mother of the Graces; with her entirely nourishing and joyful rays, she seasons and adorns the whole matter to such an extent that whatever was sought at the urging of Mercury <note>The god of communication and intellectual discovery.</note>, and was already found under the guidance of Apollo, now—surrounded by the marvelous and healthful beauty of Venus—might always delight and be beneficial <note>original: "perfit," likely a contraction of "perficiat" (perfect/complete) or "prosit" (be of benefit).</note>. 

There follow three guides of this journey within the soul <note>original: "in animo."</note>: namely, a burning and stable will, sharpness of intellect, and a tenacious memory. The final three guides are found on earth: a most prudent head of the household, a most esteemed teacher, and a most skilled physician. Without these nine guides, no one has ever been able, nor will ever be able, to reach the temple of the nine Muses themselves. 

Almighty God and nature have granted the first guides to us from the beginning; however, our own diligence must bring the final three into use. But many wise men of old have already treated the instructions and duties that pertain to the head of the household and the teacher regarding the study of letters—especially our Plato <note>Ficino was the leader of the Florentine Platonic Academy and felt a personal connection to the philosopher.</note>, who did so often elsewhere and most diligently in his books on the *Republic* and the *Laws*. Aristotle also addressed this in his *Politics*, as did Plutarch and Quintilian quite excellently. Yet, for those devoted to the study of letters, the only thing missing until now is a physician who might extend a hand to those on the journey,

<summary>Ficino identifies nine "guides" (Mercury, Apollo, Venus, Will, Intellect, Memory, Father, Teacher, and Physician) necessary for the scholar’s journey. He notes that while philosophy and education are well-documented, the role of the physician in supporting the scholar’s health has been neglected.</summary>
<keywords>Nine Guides, Apollo, Venus, Mercury, Muses, Plato, Aristotle, health of scholars, Renaissance education, physician's role</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page follows the introduction of the nine guides required for the intellectual journey. Having established that the final guide is the physician, Ficino now begins his medical and philosophical advice specifically for scholars, whom he views as particularly prone to physical exhaustion due to their devotion to Minerva (wisdom).</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

...and help with healthful counsels and medicines those whom neither heaven, nor the soul, nor the head of the household or teacher has forsaken. I, therefore, having pitied the most laborious lot of those who travel the difficult path of Minerva <note>Athena/Minerva, the goddess of wisdom, here representing the intellectual life. Ficino suggests that deep study literally "diminishes" or wears down the physical nerves.</note> which wears down the nerves, come forward first as a physician for the weak and the sickly <note>original: "valitudinariis," referring to those with a naturally delicate or infirm constitution, a state Ficino frequently associates with the scholarly temperament.</note>; but I wish my ability were as great as my willing spirit. Arise now, eager youth, under God’s guidance. Arise young men and adults, whom an overly ardent study of Minerva enervates too much. Approach the physician willingly, who, with God showing the way and favoring the work, will grant you healthful counsels and remedies for the perfection of your pursuit.

->How diligent a care must be taken for the brain, the heart, the stomach, and the spirit <note>In Ficino’s medical theory, "spirit" (spiritus) is a subtle, airy substance that links the physical body to the immaterial soul.</note>.<-

<margin>P</margin>
**I**n the first place, just as runners are accustomed to take care of their legs, athletes of their arms, and musicians of their voice, so at least should scholars of letters take care of the brain, the heart, the liver, and the stomach. Indeed, they should take so much more care as these organs are more excellent than those, and as scholars use these parts of the body more frequently and for more important purposes than those men use theirs. Furthermore, any skillful craftsman cares for his instruments most diligently. The painter his brushes; the coppersmith his hammers and anvils; the soldier his horses and arms; the hunter his dogs and birds; the lutanist <note>original: "citharedus," one who plays the cithara or lute.</note> his lute; and each likewise with his own tools. Only the priests of the Muses, only those seeking the highest good...

<summary>Ficino introduces himself as a physician for scholars, arguing that those who dedicate their lives to study must care for their vital organs as diligently as an athlete cares for their limbs or a craftsman for his tools.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, health of scholars, Minerva, medical advice, brain and heart, priests of the Muses, spiritus</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the vital organs and introduces the "spirit" as the primary tool of the scholar. Ficino transitions from the basic anatomy of the liver and heart to the refined "animal spirit" in the brain, which he considers the essential instrument for intellectual work.</meta>

...and hunters of truth <note>original: "veritatisque venatores"</note> are so negligent—alas, the shame!—and so unfortunate that they seem to utterly neglect that instrument by which they are able, in a certain way, to measure and grasp the entire universe. An instrument of this kind is the **spirit** <note>In Ficinian physiology, the "spirit" (spiritus) is a very subtle, vaporous substance produced in the body that acts as a bridge between the physical organs and the immaterial soul.</note> itself, which among physicians is defined as a certain vapor of the blood: pure, subtle, hot, and clear. And having been procreated from the subtler blood by the heat of the heart itself, it flies to the brain; and there the soul uses it continually for exercising the senses, both internal and external. For which reason blood serves the spirit, the spirit serves the senses, and finally the senses serve reason. 

Blood, moreover, is produced by the <term>natural power</term> <note>The "natural power" (virtus naturalis) was the Galenic faculty responsible for nutrition and growth, centered in the liver.</note> which flourishes in the liver and the stomach. The thinnest part of the blood flows into the fountain of the heart, where the <term>vital power</term> <note>The "vital power" (virtus vitalis) was the faculty of life and heat, centered in the heart.</note> flourishes. From there, the created spirits ascend to the brain and—if I may say so—the **citadels of Pallas** <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom. Ficino uses this poetic metaphor to designate the brain as the headquarters of the intellect.</note>. In these, the <term>animal power</term> <note>The "animal power" (virtus animalis, from "anima" or soul) was the faculty responsible for sensation, thought, and voluntary movement, centered in the brain.</note>—that is, the power of sensing and moving—rules. 

Therefore, contemplation is almost always of such a quality as the compliance of the sense itself. Moreover, such is the sense as is the spirit. And the spirit is such as is the blood and those three powers which we mentioned: namely, the natural, the vital, and the animal, by which, through which, and in which the spirits themselves are conceived, born, and nourished.

**Men of letters are subject to phlegm and black bile.** <note>original: "Litterati pituitae & atrae bili obnoxii sunt." This refers to the humoral theory where "phlegm" and "black bile" (melancholy) are the primary fluids affecting scholars' health.</note>

**B**ut men desirous of letters [ought] to care most diligently not only for those members, powers, and spirits—

<summary>Ficino explains the physiological process by which the "spirit" is refined from blood through the liver, heart, and brain, arguing that intellectual success depends on the physical quality of this spirit.</summary>
<keywords>spiritus, Galenic physiology, vital power, animal power, brain health, humoral theory, Pallas Athena, melancholy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "animal power" residing in the brain. Having established the physiological process of how blood is refined into "spirit" to serve the intellect, Ficino now warns of the specific health hazards—phlegm and black bile—that uniquely afflict scholars due to their sedentary yet mentally taxing lifestyle.</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE <note>original: "DE VITA SANA"</note>

...they ought to [serve] me; but indeed they are commanded to avoid most cautiously both **phlegm** <note>original: "pituitam"; in the humoral system, phlegm was considered cold and moist. An excess was thought to cause dullness or lethargy.</note> and **black bile** <note>original: "atram bilem"; also known as melancholy, this humor was associated with the earth, coldness, and dryness.</note>, just as sailors are warned to avoid Scylla and Charybdis <note>Two legendary sea monsters from Greek mythology positioned on opposite sides of a narrow strait; to avoid one was to risk the other.</note>. For as much as they are at leisure in the rest of their body, they are so much more occupied in their brain and mind. From this they are compelled to produce phlegm, which the Greeks call *phlegma*, and from that black bile, which the same call *melancholia*. The former [phlegm] indeed often obstructs and suffocates the talent. The latter [black bile], however, if it should overflow too much or burn, harasses the soul with constant worry and frequent delusions, and disturbs the judgment. So it can be said with good reason that men of letters would be exceptionally healthy, were it not that phlegm is troublesome; and they would be the most joyful and wisest of all, unless they were compelled by the defect of black bile either to grieve often or sometimes to lose their senses.

## How many causes there are by which the learned are or become melancholic.

**F**or scholars to be melancholic, three types of causes in particular are responsible. The first is **celestial**, the second **natural**, and the third is **human**. 

The **celestial** cause exists because Mercury—who invites us to investigate doctrines—and Saturn—who brings it about that we persevere in investigating doctrines and preserve those discovered—are said by astronomers to be in a certain way cold and dry. Or if perhaps Mercury is not cold, he is nevertheless often made extremely dry by his proximity to the Sun. Such is the nature of melancholy according to physicians; and Mercury himself and Saturn [bestow] this same nature upon the students of letters, who are their followers...

<summary>Ficino explains that scholars are prone to phlegm and black bile because their minds are overactive while their bodies are idle, and identifies three causes—celestial, natural, and human—for scholarly melancholy.</summary>
<keywords>melancholy, black bile, phlegm, Mercury, Saturn, humoral theory, scholars' health, Scylla and Charybdis</keywords>

[they] impart from the beginning, and preserve and increase it daily.

¶ The **natural cause** <note>original: "Naturalis... causa"; Ficino distinguishes between external astrological influences and the internal physiological processes of the body.</note> seems to be this: that for the pursuit of sciences, especially those that are difficult, it is necessary for the soul to withdraw itself from external things to internal ones, as if moving from a certain **circumference** to the **center** <note>Ficino uses a geometric metaphor: the "circumference" represents the distracting world of the senses, while the "center" represents the focused, inner intellect.</note>; and while it contemplates, it must remain most firmly fixed in that very center (so to speak) of the human being. 

Indeed, to collect oneself from the circumference to the center, and to be fixed in that center, is a quality most proper to the **Earth** <note>In the geocentric model of the universe used in the 15th century, the Earth sat unmoving at the center of all things.</note> itself, to which **black bile** <note>original: "atra bilis"; also known as melancholy. In the theory of the four humors, black bile was associated with the element of Earth because both were considered cold and dry.</note> is very similar. Therefore, black bile provokes the soul to both collect itself into a unity and to stay fixed in that unity, so that it may contemplate diligently. Furthermore, being similar to the center of the world, this humor forces the soul to investigate toward the center of individual things, and it raises it up to comprehend all the highest things, since it agrees most of all with **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturno"; the furthest and slowest-moving planet in the known solar system of the time, Saturn was believed to govern deep thought, solitude, and the "melancholic" temperament.</note>.

Added to this is that nature <note>Here, "nature" refers to the body's innate intelligence or vital force directing resources.</note>, being entirely intent upon the **brain** and the **heart** during contemplation, deserts the **stomach** and the **liver**. Wherefore, since foods—especially those that are either quite abundant or hard to digest—are poorly cooked <note>original: "concoctis"; the Renaissance viewed digestion as a process of "cooking" food through the body's natural heat.</note>, the blood produced from them becomes **cold, thick, and black**. 

Lastly, through the excessive idleness of the limbs, waste products are neither excreted nor are the thick and stifling vapors breathed out. All these factors tend to produce a **melancholic spirit** <note>The "spiritus" was a thin, airy substance that served as the bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note> and a sorrowful and fearful mind. For indeed, **internal darkness** occupies and terrifies the soul with grief much more than external darkness does. 

Of all men of letters, those are most oppressed by **black bile** <note>original: "atra bile"; the humor associated with melancholy, the element of earth, and the planet Saturn.</note> who, having diligently devoted themselves to the study of philosophy, have withdrawn the mind from the body and from bodily things...

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the scholar's "divine madness" and the intense agitation of the soul. # ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.

¶ Democritus, Plato, and Aristotle [asserted] this without doubt. But they seem not to have explained the reason for such a great matter sufficiently. Nevertheless, with God showing the way, one must dare to investigate the causes.

¶ **Melancholy**—that is, **black bile**—is of two kinds. One is called "natural" by physicians; the other happens through **combustion** <note>original: "adustione"; in humoral theory, this refers to the "overheating" or "scorching" of bodily fluids, which changes their properties.</note>. That natural kind is nothing other than a certain thicker and drier part of the blood. The "burned" kind, however, is divided into four types: for it is produced by the burning of either natural melancholy, or of pure blood, or of [yellow] bile, or of salty **phlegm** <term>phlegm: one of the four bodily humors, usually associated with cold and moisture; "salty phlegm" was a specific medical subtype believed to cause irritation</term>. 

Whichever type is born from combustion harms judgment and wisdom. For as long as that humor is ignited and burns, it usually makes men agitated and raving—which the Greeks call *mania*, and we call **fury**. But when it is finally extinguished—the finer and clearer parts having been dissolved and only a foul **soot** remaining—it renders them dull and stupid. This condition they call "melancholy" properly, as well as madness and senselessness.

¶ Therefore, only that black bile which we called **natural** leads us toward judgment and wisdom. Yet not always. To be sure, if it exists alone, by its excessively black and dense mass, it darkens the **spirits** <note>original: "spiritus"; Ficino refers to "medical spirits," the thin, vaporous substances that act as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note>, terrifies the soul, and blunts the **intellect** <note>original: "ingenium"; the innate talent or creative capacity of the mind.</note>.

¶ But if it is mixed with simple phlegm, when the cold blood stands around the heart, it brings on sluggishness and torpor by a certain thick coldness. And just as the most dense [parts] of each...

<summary>Ficino distinguishes between "natural" melancholy, which aids wisdom, and "combusted" melancholy, which causes mania or stupidity. He explores how the physical density and temperature of these humors directly affect a scholar's mental state.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical analysis of "natural" black bile. Having distinguished it from the harmful "burned" variety, Ficino now describes the physical consequences of its temperature and density on the human mind and spirit.</meta>

Its nature is such that when this kind of melancholy cools, it is driven to the extreme of coldness. In this state, nothing is hoped for; all things are feared; and one grows weary of looking upon the curving vaults of heaven <note>original: "tedet cęli cõuexa tueri"; a poetic way of describing the profound loss of interest in life or "acedia" often associated with deep depression.</note>.

¶ If black bile—whether simple or mixed—begins to putrefy, it generates **quartan fever**, tumors of the spleen, and many other ailments of that kind. <term>quartan fever: a type of malaria-like fever that recurs every four days (by inclusive counting), historically believed by physicians to be caused by an excess of cold, dry black bile.</term> Where it overflows too much, whether it is alone or joined with **phlegm**, it makes the **spirits** <note>original: "spiritus"; in Renaissance medicine, these were the subtle, airy vapors that acted as the bridge between the physical body and the soul.</note> thicker and colder. It immediately affects the soul with weariness. It dulls the sharp edge of the mind; nor does the blood leap around the heart with "Arcadian" vigor. <note>The author alludes to a line from the Roman satirist Persius; "Arcadian" here suggests a rustic or heavy dullness—the blood becomes too sluggish even for simple vitality.</note>

It is necessary, however, that black bile not be so scarce that the blood, the [yellow] bile, and the spirits lack a "bridle," as it were. For in its absence, the talent of the mind becomes unstable and the memory slippery. Nor should it be so plentiful that we seem to doze off, weighed down by its excessive weight, and appear to need spurs. Therefore, it is absolutely necessary that it be as refined as its nature allows. For if it is made extremely thin by its own nature, it might perhaps even be abundant without causing harm. It might even be so great as to equal the yellow bile, at least in weight.

¶ Let black bile abound, then, but let it be very refined. Let it not lack the moisture of a more refined **phlegm** flowing around it, so that it does not dry up entirely and become too hard. Yet it should not be mixed completely with phlegm—especially not the cold or excessive kind—lest it become too chilly. Instead, it should be mixed with yellow bile and blood until one substance is formed from the three, with a double portion of blood compared to the other two...

<summary>Ficino explains the ideal balance of black bile, noting that while too much causes fear and sluggishness, a "refined" amount is necessary to act as a stabilizing bridle for the intellect and memory.</summary>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.
...composed in proportion. Where eight parts of blood, two of [yellow] bile <note>original: "bilis"; in this context, Ficino refers to yellow bile (choler), which is hot and dry, to distinguish it from "atra bilis" or black bile.</note>, and again two portions of black bile are present. Let the black bile be ignited somewhat by those two [the blood and yellow bile], and once ignited let it glow; it must not be scorched. Lest, just as harder materials are prone to do when they boil overmuch, it burns too fiercely and agitates the system. But when it cools, let it likewise cool to the utmost. For black bile, like iron, when it is subjected to great cold, is cold in the extreme; when, conversely, it leans strongly toward heat, it is hot in the extreme. 

Nor should it seem a marvel that black bile can be easily set ablaze, and once lit, burns more violently. Indeed, we see that quicklime <note>original: "calcem"; Ficino refers to calcium oxide, which reacts exothermically (producing heat) when touched by water—a common Renaissance metaphor for a substance that contains "hidden fire."</note>—which is similar to it—boils and burns the moment water is poured over it. Melancholy possesses such power toward either extreme because of the unified stability and fixedness of its nature. Indeed, such an extreme range does not occur in the other humors. 

When it is intensely hot, it provides the greatest daring, even ferocity. But when extremely cold, it produces fear and extreme cowardice. In the middle degrees between cold and heat, however, it is affected in various ways and produces various moods, just as pure wine <note>original: "merum"; undiluted wine, which was believed to have a more immediate and potent effect on the "spirits" of the brain.</note>—especially strong wine—is accustomed to induce various states in those drinking to drunkenness or even a little too freely.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the ideal melancholic humor, moving from its physical properties (comparison to wood and fire) to its appearance and its role in producing the "spirits" that fuel intellectual genius.</meta>

It is dry, and to the degree that its nature permits, it is extremely subtle; thus, it is easily ignited by those other humors <note>referring to the blood and yellow bile mentioned on the previous page</note>. Because it is solid and very tenacious, once ignited, it continues to burn for a very long time. Because it is most powerful in the unity of its tenacious dryness, it heats up more vehemently. It is much like the way wood, if ignited along with straw, glows and provides heat more intensely and for a longer duration than the straw. From this long-lasting and vehement heat, a great brilliance and a powerful, enduring movement are produced. To this point tends that saying of Heraclitus: "A dry light is the wisest soul." <note>original: "Lux sicca anima sapientissima." A famous fragment from the Greek philosopher Heraclitus, suggesting that an intellect free from the "dampness" of sluggish humors is the most capable of wisdom.</note>

<margin><note>A red circle containing the letter 'q', likely a shorthand for "quaestio" (question) or a reference mark.</note></margin>

## How black bile contributes to genius

Someone may perhaps ask: what sort of "body" is this humor, fused together from those three humors in the proportion we have described? It is roughly the color of gold, as we see it, but it inclines somewhat toward purple. And when it is ignited—whether by natural heat or by the movement of the body or the soul—it glows and shines almost like gold that has been made red-hot and mixed with purple. And just like **Iris** <term>Iris: the goddess of the rainbow; here used to describe the iridescent or shifting colors produced by the burning humor</term>, it draws forth various colors when the heart is burning.

Someone will ask again: in what way does such a humor benefit one's genius? <note>original: "ingenio"; referring to innate intellectual ability or the creative spark of a scholar.</note> Certainly, the **spirits** <term>spiritus: in Renaissance medicine, these were subtle, vaporous substances that acted as the "drivers" of the body's functions and the messengers between the soul and the physical organs.</term> created from this humor are, first of all, subtle—no different from that "water of life" <note>original: "aqua vitae"</note>, also called "water of the vine" or "burning water" <note>original: "aqua ardens"; an early term for distilled alcohol or brandy</note>, which is expressed from thick, pure wine through distillation over a fire, as is the custom. For the spirits, within the narrow...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the refined "spirits" produced by a specific type of black bile. ->[ON THE HEALTHY LIFE]<-

The spirits of such black bile <note>original: "atrę bilis." In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were the subtle, vaporous substances that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note>, being compressed within the passages of the body, are thinned out to an extreme degree by a heat that is made more intense because of their unity. Furthermore, because they are squeezed out through narrower passages, they break forth even more refined. Second, they are similarly hotter and, by the same logic, more luminous. Third, they are agile in their movement and most powerful in their action. Fourth, because they emanate continually from a humor that is solid and stable, they serve the work of the mind for a very long time. Relying on such service, our soul investigates with great intensity. It perseveres in its investigation for a longer duration. It easily finds whatever it has sought; it perceives clearly, judges with integrity, and retains its judgments for a long time. 

Add to this that, as we have indicated in the preceding sections, the soul possesses an instrument or stimulus of this kind which corresponds in a certain way to the center of the world <note>In the geocentric model, the Earth—associated with the humor of melancholy—is the center of the universe. Ficino suggests this allows the melancholic mind to focus intensely on the "center" or essence of any subject.</note>. By this means, the soul—if I may put it this way—is collected into its own center; it always seeks out the centers of all things and penetrates into their innermost secrets <note>original: "penetralia." This refers to the most hidden, sacred, or deep parts of a subject or physical space.</note>. Moreover, this state corresponds with **Mercury** and **Saturn**. Of these two, the latter <note>Saturn</note> is the highest of all the planets...

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical and medical defense of "refined" melancholy, citing authorities like Aristotle and Avicenna to distinguish between the brilliant "white bile" of the genius and the destructive "black bile" of the sufferer. It then transitions into an allegorical warning for scholars, framing the intellectual life as a perilous journey beset by physical and spiritual obstacles.</meta>

Even Aristotle in his book of *Problems*, and Avicenna in his book on divine things and in his book *On the Soul*, admit this. ¶ Why so much about the humor of black bile? So that we may remember that just as this black bile—or rather "white bile" <note>original: "candida bilis." Ficino uses this term to describe melancholy that has been heated and refined, losing its "black" or depressive qualities and becoming a source of intellectual brilliance.</note> of this kind—is to be sought and nourished as the best sort, so that which is its opposite (as we have said) is to be avoided as the worst. For it is so terrible a thing that Serapio <note>A famous medieval Arabic physician whose works were influential in the Renaissance.</note> said its attacks are incited by an evil demon, and the wise Avicenna did not deny it.

¶ There are five principal enemies of scholars: Phlegm <note>original: "pituita." One of the four humors, associated with sluggishness and coldness.</note>; Black Bile; Sexual Intercourse; Satiety <note>The state of being over-full from eating.</note>; and Morning Sleep <note>The Latin text reads "matutinus sonus" (morning sound), but in the context of Ficino's medical advice, this is almost certainly a printer's error for "matutinus somnus" (morning sleep), which he famously warned against as it clouded the mind.</note>.

**T**o return to the point from which we have already wandered quite far: the path that leads to truth and wisdom is very long, filled with heavy labors on both land and sea. ¶ Therefore, whoever undertakes this journey, as a certain poet might say, is often endangered on land and sea. For if they sail the sea, they are continually tossed between two waves—that is, two humors: phlegm and that harmful melancholy—as if between Scylla and Charybdis <note>Two legendary sea monsters from Greek mythology positioned on either side of a narrow strait, representing a choice between two equally dangerous options.</note>. Or if they travel by land (if I may put it that way), three monsters immediately present themselves. The first is nurtured by the earthly Venus and Priapus <note>Gods associated with sexual desire and fertility, representing the "enemy" of sexual intercourse mentioned above.</note>. The second by Bacchus and Ceres <note>The gods of wine and grain, representing the "enemy" of satiety or overindulgence.</note>. The third is frequently opposed by the nightly Hecate <note>A goddess associated with the moon and the underworld, representing the "enemy" of excessive morning sleep or dark, lethargic dreams.</note>. Therefore, Apollo from the heavens, Neptune from the sea, and from the earth Her- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely referring to Hercules, the hero famous for overcoming monsters through labor.</note>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical warning to scholars begun on the previous page, where the intellectual life was compared to a perilous voyage. Having listed the "five enemies" of scholars (Phlegm, Black Bile, Intercourse, Satiety, and Morning Sleep), the author now invokes the gods to help the scholar defeat these "monsters."</meta>

# ON A HEALTHY LIFE.

...cules <note>The text continues from the previous page: "as a certain [Her]cules."</note> must often be called upon, so that Apollo might pierce these monsters—enemies of Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, or Minerva, the goddess of wisdom.</note>—with his arrows; that Neptune might tame them with his trident; and that Hercules might crush and tear them with his club.

The first monster is sexual intercourse, especially if it exceeds one's strength even by a little. For it suddenly exhausts the spirits <term>spirits: "spiritus," the subtle, airy vapors that Renaissance physicians believed acted as the bridge between the soul and the physical body</term>, particularly the more refined ones; it weakens the brain and undermines the stomach and the heart. Nothing can be more adverse to genius than this evil. Why else did Hippocrates judge intercourse to be similar to the "falling sickness" <note>original: "comitiali morbo." A historical term for epilepsy.</note>, unless because it strikes the mind, which is sacred? It is so harmful that Avicenna, in his book *On Animals*, said: "If any semen—beyond what nature can tolerate—should flow out during intercourse, it is more harmful than if forty times as much blood had flowed out." Thus, it was not without reason that the ancients insisted the Muses and Minerva were virgins.

That Platonic saying looks toward this same point: When Venus threatened the Muses that unless they honored her sacred sexual rites, she would arm her son <note>Cupid, the god of desire.</note> against them, the Muses replied: "O Venus, threaten Mars—yes, Mars—with such things! For your Cupid does not fly among us." Finally, nature has separated no sense further from intelligence than the sense of touch.

The second monster is the satiety of wine and food. Indeed, if wine is either too much, or too hot and potent, it will fill the head with humors and the worst possible vapors. I will skip over the fact that drunkenness makes men insane. Too much food, however, first [affects] the stomach...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "monsters" or physical habits that obstruct a scholar’s intellectual pursuits. The text is mid-argument regarding "Satiety" (overeating) before moving on to the dangers of late-night study and daytime sleep.</meta>

...not <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the Muses' resistance to Cupid.</note> fly to them, because in the very act of digestion <note>original: "coquédo." Renaissance medicine viewed digestion as a process of "concoction" or "cooking" in the stomach.</note>, it calls back all the force of nature to itself. As a result, one cannot attend to the head and to contemplation at the same time. Furthermore, food poorly digested dulls the sharpness of the mind with many thick vapors and humors <term>humors: the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that were believed to determine a person's health and temperament</term>. Moreover, even if it is sufficiently digested, as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD) was the most influential physician of the Roman Empire; his theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note> says, a soul suffocated by fat and blood cannot perceive anything celestial.

¶ Finally, the third monster is to stay awake late into the night—especially after dinner—quite frequently; from which habit you are also forced to sleep after the rising of the sun. Since, indeed, very many scholars err and are deceived in this matter, I will therefore explain more broadly how much it harms genius. I will bring forward seven principal reasons:

1. The first, from heaven itself.
2. The second, from the elements.
3. The third, from the humors.
4. The fourth, from the order of things.
5. The fifth, from the nature of the stomach.
6. The sixth, from the spirits <term>spirits: "spiritus," the subtle vapors that act as the vehicle for the soul's power within the body</term>.
7. The seventh, derived from the imagination <note>original: "phantasia." In this period, the "phantasy" or imagination was considered a specific faculty of the brain that processed sensory data.</note>.

¶ In the beginning, the three planets—just as we were saying in the previous sections—contempl... <note>The text breaks off here mid-word, likely "contemplantur" (contemplate/observe) or "contemplationi" (to contemplation).</note>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument against late-night study, specifically detailing the seven reasons why daytime activity and dawn contemplation are superior for the scholar’s health and intellect. The text picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's list of reasons.</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.

...those who rise to contemplate and write almost at the moment the sun seeks its rising, or when it is already rising; they alone observe most sharply, and write and compose their findings most eloquently. 

¶ The second reason, specifically from the elements, is as follows. When the sun rises, the air is moved, made thin, and becomes clear; when it sets, the opposite occurs. Moreover, the blood and the spirits <term>spirits: "spiritus," the subtle vapors that act as the vehicle for the soul's power within the body</term>, being similar in nature to the surrounding air, are necessarily compelled to follow its motion and quality. 

¶ The third reason, which is drawn from the humors <term>humors: the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that were believed to determine a person's health and temperament</term>, is of this kind. At dawn, the blood is moved and reigns; through this movement, it is thinned, becomes warm, and grows clear. Indeed, the spirits are accustomed to follow and imitate the blood. But when night approaches, that thicker and colder melancholy <note>original: "melancholia," referring here to black bile, which was associated with darkness, cold, and sluggishness</note> and phlegm <note>original: "pituita," the humor associated with moisture and lethargy</note> dominate; these undoubtedly render the spirits most unfit for contemplation. 

¶ The fourth reason, which is drawn from the order of things, will be this. Day is for waking; night is granted for sleep. Since, when the sun either approaches our hemisphere or moves above it, it opens the passages <note>original: "meatus," the pores or internal channels of the body</note> of the body with its rays, and expands the humors and spirits from the center to the periphery. This indeed rouses and leads one toward wakefulness and activity. Conversely, when the sun recedes, everything is constricted; by a certain natural order, this invites sleep—especially after the third or fourth part of the night. Therefore, whoever slumbers in the morning, when the sun and the world are rous-

<meta>This page continues the specific arguments against night-time study, moving from the philosophical order of the universe (the fourth reason) to the physiological mechanics of the stomach and digestion (the fifth reason). It picks up from the previous page's discussion on how the sun influences the body’s internal passages.</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.

...acts <note>Completing the verb "operatur" or similar from the previous page</note>; but he who stays awake late into the night, when nature already commands us to sleep and to rest from our labors, undoubtedly rebels both against the order of the universe and against himself, for he is disturbed and pulled apart by contrary motions at the same time. Indeed, while he is moved by the universe toward the outer parts <note>The scholar explains that during the day, the body's energy and "spirits" naturally flow outward toward the skin and senses</note>, he moves himself toward the inner parts through his focus. And conversely: while he is drawn by the universe toward the inner parts <note>referring to the cooling and inward retreat of energy at night</note>, he meanwhile pulls himself back toward the outer parts. Therefore, by this perverted order and these contrary motions, the whole body, the spirits <term>spirits: the subtle, vaporous substances that connect the soul to the physical body</term>, and the intellect are all utterly weakened.

¶ In the fifth place, we argue from the nature of the stomach in this manner. By the long-lasting action of the daytime air, the stomach is greatly dilated as the pores are opened; and as the spirits fly out, it is finally greatly weakened. Therefore, as night approaches, it requires a new supply of spirits by which it may be nourished. For this reason, whoever begins long and difficult contemplations at that time strives to pull those very spirits back toward the head. These spirits, however, being thus distracted, satisfy neither the requirements of the stomach nor those of the head. 

¶ It is especially harmful if, staying up late <note>original: "lucubrantes," referring to "lucubration," or working by candlelight</note> after dinner, we apply ourselves too intently to studies of this nature for a long time. For the stomach then needs many spirits and much heat to digest <note>original: "concoquendum," literally "to cook together," as digestion was viewed as a heat-based process</note> the food. These two things, however, are diverted to the head by such late-night study and application. The result is that they suffice for neither the brain nor the stomach. Add to this that the head, because of this kind of motion, is filled with the thicker vapors of the food.

<meta>This page continues the specific physiological arguments against studying at night. Having established that nighttime study draws essential "spirits" away from the stomach, the text now describes the resulting failure of digestion and its impact on the brain and the morning routine.</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.

...of the stomach <note>Completing the thought from the previous page: the stomach is left without the "spirits" it needs to digest food because they have been pulled to the head for study.</note>.

And thus food in the stomach, deprived of heat and spirits <term>spirits: in Renaissance medicine, these were subtle, vaporous substances that acted as the "engine" for bodily functions like digestion and thought</term>, becomes raw and rots. From this, it fills the head again with vapors and harms it. Finally, in the morning hours, when one ought to rise so that each limb may be purged of all the excrements <term>excrements: not only feces, but all metabolic waste products including sweat, phlegm, and urine</term> retained during sleep, the worst thing occurs: he who has thoroughly interrupted his digestion by studying late into the night is compelled to hinder the expulsion of these wastes even longer by sleeping late in the morning. All physicians believe this to be extremely harmful to both the intellect and the body.

Rightly, therefore, those who use the night against nature in place of the day, and conversely use the day in place of the night like owls, also imitate owls in this way—even against their will: just as the eyes of owls grow dim under the light of the sun, so too does the sharpness of their minds grow dim under the splendor of truth.

In the sixth place, the same point is proven from the nature of the spirits. By the fatigue of the day, the spirits—especially all the most subtle and refined ones—are eventually dissipated. Therefore, at night, only a few thick spirits remain, which are entirely unfit for literary studies. Thus, the intellect, relying on these crippled wings <note>original: "mancis... alis"</note>, can no more fly than can bats and horned owls.

On the contrary, in the morning after sleep, when the spirits have been restored and the limbs so strengthened that they require very little support from the spirits, many subtle spirits are present to serve the brain. These spirits can obey the mind's commands more swiftly, as they are very little occupied with warming and governing the limbs.

...and thus it is derived from the nature of the phantasy. The phantasy <term>phantasy: in Renaissance psychology, the 'phantasia' was the mental faculty that received and stored images from the five senses, acting as a bridge between the body and the higher intellect</term>—whether it should be called imagination, or thought, or by any other name—is distracted and disturbed by many long and conflicting images, thoughts, and cares while staying awake. This very distraction and disturbance is entirely contrary to the contemplation that follows, which strictly demands a tranquil and serene mind.

In truth, that agitation is finally calmed and pacified only by nightly rest. Therefore, we always approach the night with a disturbed mind; yet, for the most part, we return to our studies only after the mind has become tranquil.

Indeed, whoever attempts to judge matters while the mind is excessively agitated acts no differently than those suffering from vertigo, who think everything is spinning (as Plato says <note>original: "ut Plato inquit"; likely referencing the *Theaetetus* or *Cratylus*, where Plato describes how those with "unsteady souls" believe the world is in flux when they themselves are simply confused</note>), when in fact they are the ones spinning. For this reason, Aristotle wisely commands in his *Economics* <note>original: "Aristoteles in economicis"; a treatise on household management and ethics attributed to Aristotle</note> that one should rise before dawn. He asserts that this is of the greatest benefit both to the health of the body and to the study of philosophy. But this

# ON A HEALTHY LIFE.

<note>A large initial 'E' is decorated with a red circular diagram containing geometric lines, likely representing a cosmic or physiological harmony related to the sun's cycles.</note> From those things which were discussed above, it is now sufficiently clear that we should begin our studies at an opportune time: either exactly at sunrise, or at least one or two hours before the sun rises.

But before you rise from bed, first gently rub your whole body with your palms for a little while. Then, rub your head with your fingernails, but slightly more lightly. In this matter, let Hippocrates <note>original: "Hippocrates"; the ancient Greek physician whose theories on the "humors" and physical regimen dominated medical thought well into the Renaissance.</note> advise you. For he says <note>original: "frictione... inquit"; referring to the medical benefits of massage or "friction" described in the Hippocratic corpus.</note> that by friction, if it is vigorous, the body is hardened; if light, it is softened; if excessive, it is diminished; and if moderate, it is filled out. 

When you have risen from bed, do not immediately throw yourself into reading and meditation. Instead, allow at least half an hour for any necessary purging <note>original: "expurgationi"; in Renaissance medicine, this refers to the evacuation of bodily wastes—such as phlegm or excrement—to ensure the "vapors" do not cloud the brain during study.</note>.

Soon after, prepare yourself diligently for meditation; you shall continue this for about one hour, according to your strength. Then, relax the focus of your mind for a little while; and in the meantime, with an ivory comb, diligently and moderately comb your head from the forehead toward the neck, drawing the comb through forty times. 

Then, rub your neck with a rougher cloth. Finally, returning to meditation, dedicate two more hours, or at least one hour, to study. Studies can indeed sometimes be extended—provided that some intermittent breaks are taken—until the hour of noon. 

Furthermore, although it is rare unless we are compelled to take food in the meantime, study may continue for about two hours after midday. For the Sun <note>original: "Sol"; the sun was viewed not just as a light source, but as a celestial governor of "vital spirits" and intellectual clarity.</note> is powerful around its rising; it is also powerful in the middle of the sky; in the region

...also that part of the heavens which follows immediately after the mid-heaven—which astronomers call the ninth house <note>original: "nonam"; in traditional astrology, the ninth house of a natal chart or the sky is the "House of Wisdom," governing philosophy and higher learning.</note> and the house of wisdom—is where the sun rejoices most. Since all the poets maintain that Phoebus <note>original: "Phebum"; another name for Apollo, the god of the sun, music, and prophecy.</note> is the leader of the muses and the sciences, it is only right that if something deep must be thought out, it should be considered during these hours above all. If the muses are to be sought, they should be sought in these same hours with Phoebus as the guide.

For the remaining hours seem better suited to reading the works of the ancients and others, rather than for thinking out new and original ideas of one’s own. However, we must always remember that in any given hour, the focus of the mind should be relaxed at least once for a little while. For since the spirits <note>original: "spiritus"; in the medical theory of the time, these were subtle substances in the body that acted as the bridge between the soul and the physical organs.</note> are dissolved by such intense focus, it is only natural that if you never cease to stretch them, you will become sluggish. 

While you labor with the mind, let the body rest in the meantime. Fatigue of the body is bad; fatigue of the mind is worse; but fatigue of both at once is the worst of all, for it distracts the person with opposing conditions and destroys their life. Finally, meditation should proceed no further than the point of pleasure—indeed, it is better to stop just short of it.

An excess was thought to cause dullness and respiratory issues.</note> does not increase too much, exercise should be used twice daily on a nearly empty stomach. This exercise should never be laborious, however, lest the "sharp" or vital spirits be dissolved. Furthermore, waste products <note>original: "excrementa"; here referring broadly to any bodily fluids or materials needing expulsion to maintain health.</note> must be most diligently purged from all the passages of the body. ...the skin of the entire body, and especially the head, must be thoroughly cleansed through both washing and rubbing. One must avoid foods that are excessively cold; and, unless black bile <note>original: "atra bilis"; one of the four humors in ancient medicine, believed to cause melancholy and sluggishness if it became too thick or cold.</note> prevents it, moist foods should also be avoided, as well as everything that is fatty, virulent, viscous, oily, or sticky, or those things which are prone to rot easily. If the stomach is cold either by nature or by age, the drinking of water should either be given up entirely or certainly reduced. Food should be moderate, but drink more moderate still. One’s dwelling should be high up, far removed from heavy and cloudy air. Humidity should be driven out by the use of both fire and warm scents. Cold must be kept away from the head, but especially from the neck and the feet; for it greatly hinders the intellect <note>original: "ingenio"; referring to the natural talent or mental capacity of the scholar.</note>. A moderate use of spices in colder dishes is beneficial: especially nutmeg, cinnamon, and saffron; also preserved ginger <note>original: "Siziberis"; an archaic Latin form for ginger.</note> taken in the morning on an empty stomach, which is most helpful for the senses and the memory.

...all black things. Anger, fear, pity, grief, idleness, solitude: and whatever offends the sight, smell, or hearing: but of all things, darkness most of all. Furthermore, excessive drying of the body—whether arising from long nights of wakefulness <note>original: "uigiliis"; the practice of staying awake late to study, common among scholars.</note>, or from much agitation of the mind, or worry, or frequent sexual intercourse—is harmful. So too is the use of things which are very hot and dry: or from some immoderate discharge and purging, or laborious exercise, or lack of food, thirst, heat, a drier wind, or cold.

Since black bile <note>original: "bilis atra"; the heavy, dry, and cold humor that ancient physicians believed caused melancholy when in excess.</note> is always very dry, and also cold (though not to the same degree), this must certainly be resisted by things that are moderately warm, and especially by those that are very moist. Boiled foods should be eaten constantly: those which are easily digested and produce thin and very clear blood. But in the meantime, so that the condition of the stomach and phlegm <note>original: "pituitæ"; the cold, moist humor. The author warns that while treating the dry "black bile," one must not let the stomach become too cold or phlegmatic.</note> is considered just as much as the black bile, the meals should be seasoned with cinnamon, saffron, and sandalwood.

The seeds of the pumpkin <note>original: "peponis"; likely referring to large gourds or melons, which were considered cooling and moistening.</note> and cucumber are beneficial, as are washed pine nuts. All dairy products are suitable: milk, fresh cheese, and sweet almonds. The meat of birds and young chickens is suitable, or that of suckling four-legged animals; soft-boiled eggs <note>original: "oua sorbilia"; literally "sipping eggs," referring to very lightly cooked or poached eggs.</note> especially; and from the parts of animals, the brain. Also suitable are sweet apples, pears, peaches, pumpkins, damson plums, and similar things. Squash properly cooked, and moist herbs that are not viscous, are good. However, I hardly praise cherries, figs, or grapes. I strongly detest nausea and over-satiety. Nothing, however, against this

<meta>This page continues the dietary and environmental regimen for the scholar, specifically focusing on the use of wine, precious metals, and fragrances to balance the dry and cold nature of the melancholic humor.</meta>

### ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

...[is more] powerful [against] the plague than wine that is light, clear, sweet-smelling, and most suitable for generating spirits <note>original: "spiritus"; in Renaissance medicine, these were subtle vapors that connected the body and soul, facilitating thought and movement.</note> that are clearer than all others. For as it pleases Plato and Aristotle, this humor <note>referring to black bile or melancholy.</note> is softened, sweetened, and clarified by this wine in the same way that lupins are softened by being soaked in water, or iron by being heated in flames. However, as much as its use benefits the spirits and the intellect <note>original: "ingenio"; the natural capacity for study and invention.</note>, its abuse causes just as much harm. Furthermore, it is agreed that it is beneficial to infuse gold or silver that has been intensely heated, or to put their leaves <note>referring to gold or silver leaf, which was believed to possess life-extending and heart-strengthening properties.</note> into drinking cups or even into the broth itself; it is also helpful to drink and eat from vessels made of gold or silver.

Likewise, it is very useful if the juice of liquorice is often swallowed on an empty stomach; so too the juice of a sweet pomegranate and of a sweet orange. Sweet smells are also of no small benefit, especially those that are well-balanced; however, if cold prevails, they should lean toward the warm, but if heat dominates, they should tend toward the cold. They should therefore be tempered with roses, violets, myrtle, camphor, sandalwood, and rose water, which are cold. On the other hand, they may be made from cinnamon, citron, orange, cloves <note>original: "garofilis"; a spice prized for its warming and stomach-strengthening properties.</note>, mint, lemon balm, saffron, aloeswood, ambergris, and musk, which are warm. Spring flowers are especially useful, as are the leaves of the citron or orange and fragrant fruits; but most of all, wine. Fragrances of this kind, according to what suits the individual, should be inhaled through the nose and applied to the chest and the stomach. However, we do not approve of odors that are very warm and dry if they are used alone and constantly. One should also hold in the mouth a hyacinth <note>original: "iacitus"; likely referring to the gemstone zircon (called hyacinth), which was believed to have medicinal properties, such as cooling the heart, when held in the mouth.</note> which...

<meta>This page continues the dietary and lifestyle advice for the scholar, transitioning from the consumption of wine and precious metals to the use of specific herbs, environmental factors, and the therapeutic power of music and nature to combat melancholy.</meta>

...strongly gladdens the soul. Also, holy herb <note>original: "Hierobotanum"; likely referring to *Salvia sclarea* or wild clary, a plant believed to "clear" both the eyes and the mind.</note>—that is, wild clary—is beneficial both as food and by its scent. Bugloss <note>a plant with bright blue flowers, traditionally used to "drive away sorrow."</note>, borage, and lemon balm, as well as the waters distilled from these three, are useful. Again, lettuce, endive, raisins, and almond milk <note>almond milk was a staple of Renaissance medicine, valued for its cooling and moistening effects on the "dry" melancholic temperament.</note> should be very familiar at the table. Air that is either too hot, icy, or cloudy must be avoided; instead, temperate and clear air should be admitted most freely. 

Mercury, Pythagoras, and Plato command that a dissonant or grieving soul be composed and uplifted by the song and music of the lyre <note>original: "cithara"; the author, Marsilio Ficino, was famous for playing a "Homeric lyre" to treat his own depression, believing music could retune the human spirit.</note> that is as constant as it is harmonious. David, the holy poet, freed Saul from insanity with his psaltery and psalms. Even I—if I may compare the lowest things to the highest—frequently experience at home how much the sweetness of the lyre and song can prevail against the bitterness of black bile <note>original: "atrae bilis"; the literal Latin term for melancholy, the humor associated with sadness and intellectual depth.</note>. 

We praise the frequent sight of clear water, or of green or red colors; the enjoyment of gardens and groves; a pleasant stroll along rivers and through lovely meadows. We also highly approve of horse riding, being carried <note>this refers to "gestation," a medical term for passive exercise like riding in a litter or carriage.</note>, and gentle sailing. But above all, we recommend variety, easy occupations, and various tasks that are not burdensome—and the constant company of pleasant people.

### ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.

[Every day it] <note>The sentence likely began on the previous page, referring to food or a specific tonic.</note> should be taken, and it should be moderately and lightly seasoned with cinnamon, mace, and nutmeg. Nevertheless, dry food should always exceed soft nourishment and drink in weight: unless by chance we greatly fear the dryness of black bile <note>original: "atrae bilis"; melancholy was characterized as being cold and dry, so the author suggests caution when the body is already too "desiccated."</note>. Let food wait for hunger (if it can be done conveniently), and let drink wait for thirst. Let an appetite for both remain even when leaving the table. Let loathing and over-satiety be far away. 

One must abstain from those things which—because of excessive moisture or a virulent, oily, and viscous matter—weaken the stomach by relaxing it: or even those which are very cold or very hot: or those which are difficult to digest because of their hardness. Also avoid those things which are such that, for a long time after the meal, they return an annoying taste to the palate: whether they cause gas <note>original: "inflent"; literally "inflate" or cause wind.</note> or fill the head with many vapors. Above all, abstain from all things that easily rot either outside the belly or within it. 

We in no way approve of sweet flavors or sharp <note>original: "acres"; meaning pungent, bitter, or spicy.</note> ones if they are served alone. But we want sweet things to be tempered by something sharp, acute, or dry. Mastic <note>a resin from the mastic tree, used to aid digestion and strengthen the stomach.</note> and dried mint: fresh sage: raisins: cooked quinces seasoned with sugar: chicory: rose: coral <note>finely ground precious coral was used as a tonic to "brighten" the spirits and settle the stomach.</note>: lotus, and capers seasoned with vinegar, are most friendly to the stomach. Furthermore, pomegranates with a flavor between sour and sweet: and generally whatever things are moderately dry and somewhat austere—which physicians call "styptics" <note>astringent substances that contract the tissues of the stomach to aid digestion.</note>—or those which are somewhat sharp, salty, or aromatic. But myrobalans <note>a plum-like medicinal fruit from India, highly valued in the Renaissance for balancing the humors, particularly melancholy.</note> surpass all these. Also wine...

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s dietary advice for the scholar, moving from the types of seasonings and foods to avoid, to the specific properties of wine and the vital importance of proper digestion and post-meal behavior.</meta>

[It is better to choose a wine that is] red rather than white, with a flavor that is somewhat slightly bitter; and unless heat or the process of distillation <note>original: "destillatio"; referring here to a medical condition involving the dripping of humors or a specific preparation method.</note> requires otherwise, it will be best when drunk pure and in small sips. In all things, more liquid dishes should be taken before the harder ones. Once the food is eaten, however, it is beneficial to take coriander, and quince preserved in sugar; pomegranates, and tart pears, medlars also, and dried peaches, and similar fruits. It is necessary, moreover, to chew each individual morsel very thoroughly before they are swallowed.

The stomach should be comforted, if it is necessary from the outside, with mastic, rose, mint, and coral. <note>These materials were used in "stomach patches" or topicals to strengthen the "digestive fire" through their aromatic or cooling/binding properties.</note> One must take care not to apply oneself to difficult thought or diligent reading for the next two or three hours after a meal. Perhaps four hours of rest <note>original: "uacationis"; literally a vacation or emptiness from labor.</note> will be necessary if the food or drink was more abundant, or the food was of a harder nature. It is bad to distend the belly with food or drink; it is worst of all to think difficult thoughts while the stomach is so distended. Therefore, either take the lightest possible nourishment, or once it is taken, remain idle until you have, as it were, fully "cooked" it. <note>original: "percoxeris"; in Galenic medicine, digestion was viewed as a process of "concoction" or cooking the food within the heat of the stomach.</note> Nor should one sleep at midday after food, unless the greatest necessity compels it. And even then, not before we have remained awake for at least two hours. At night, however, once dinner is finished, one hour of wakefulness seems to be sufficient. 

Sexual intercourse is ruinous to the stomach, especially if you lie down for it either immediately after being filled or while hungry. The stomach grieves in idleness; it rejoices in exercise—except when it is full of food. Immediately after taking food, one should walk moderately...
