<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical and dietary advice for scholars. It transitions from post-meal physical behavior to the "pharmacopeia" or medicinal workshop, specifically discussing the use of universal tonics like theriac and mithridatum to maintain the "spirits"—the fine vapors that Ficino believed connected the body to the soul and fueled the intellect.</meta>

[red pilcrow] ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.
[for a while,] and then soon one must sit down.

[blue pilcrow] Concerning those things which nourish the principal members, the powers, and the spirits.

[Large red initial S] But now it seems better that we bring forward some items from the doctors' workshop <note>original: "medicorum officina"; refers to the apothecary’s shop or the collective body of medical knowledge.</note> which either preserve the powers of the stomach, heart, brain, and the spirits of the intellect <note>Ficino believed the "spirit" was a subtle vapor in the blood that allowed the mind to function; for scholars, these spirits were often exhausted by intense study.</note> in their entirety, or restore them; and if phlegm <note>original: "pituita"; one of the four humors, often associated with sluggishness.</note> or black bile <note>original: "atra bilis"; the humor associated with melancholy and deep thought.</note> should increase, or if nausea threatens, they drive them far away. All doctors have agreed without controversy: nothing is more beneficial than theriac <note>original: "tiriaca"; a famous ancient medicinal "super-drug" containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh, used as a universal antidote and tonic.</note> for <margin>theriac</margin> nourishing and strengthening both the individual members and powers, as well as the spirits and intellect. Therefore, we shall primarily use half a dram <term>dram: a small unit of weight used by apothecaries, roughly 3.9 grams</term> of this, or at least a third of a dram, twice every week in winter and autumn; but in summer and spring, only once, either alone or—if it pleases you during cold and humid seasons—with a little clear, sweet, unmixed wine. In hot and dry seasons, however, especially if your nature or age is hotter, take it with two or three ounces of rose water; specifically on an empty stomach, six or seven hours before food. If theriac is lacking, we shall give mithridatum <note>original: "mitridatum"; a complex compound similar to theriac, named after King Mithridates VI of Pontus.</note>. But when we take theriac or mithridatum, one must completely abstain from all hot things on that day; and if it is summer or spring, cold things must be used.

[blue pilcrow] Secondly, for the same purpose, aloe is approved by all, provided it is correctly selected and washed. Take two drams of chebulic myrobalans <note>original: "mirobalanorum chebularum"; a bitter fruit used in Ayurvedic and Galenic medicine to purge humors and strengthen the brain.</note>; [two drams] of purple roses, red sandalwood, emblic myrobalans <note>original: "emblicorum"; also known as Indian gooseberry, used to cool the body and aid digestion.</note>, cinnamon, saffron, and citron peel...

c.ii.

<meta>This page continues the specific medicinal recipes for scholars, moving from the general benefits of theriac to a specific recipe for aloe-based pills and a restorative confection (morsels) designed to clear the humors and sharpen the mind.</meta>

ben <note>original: "beem"; likely referring to the Behen root (Centaurea behen), prized in the Renaissance for its heart-strengthening properties</note>, lemon balm <note>original: "melissae, id est citrariae"; an herb known for its citrus scent and ability to lift the spirits</note>, one dram <term>dram: a small unit of weight used by apothecaries, roughly 3.9 grams</term> of each: twelve drams of choice, properly washed aloe. From these, prepare pills with the best undiluted wine. Use these once every week at daybreak, specifically in a weight that suits your constitution <note>original: "naturae tuae"; refers to the individual's unique balance of the four humors</note>. In summer, indeed, take them with rose water; at other times, however, with wine.

On those days when you take neither the theriac <note>original: "tiriacam"; a complex universal antidote and tonic</note> nor the pills, use a confection of this kind in the morning and evening, two or three hours before food. Take four drams of the finest cinnamon; two of chebulic myrobalans <note>original: "chebularum mirobalanorum"; an astringent fruit from India used to purge phlegm and clarify the senses</note>; and the same amount of emblic myrobalans; half a dram each of saffron and purple roses; two drams of red sandalwood; and one dram of red coral. Use as much of the whitest sugar as is sufficient. Dissolve the sugar in rose water and the juice of citron or lemons, in equal portions. Cook gently. Then add a third part of a dram of musk, and just as much ambergris <note>original: "ambrae"; a rare, fragrant substance from the sea used as a potent restorative for the brain and heart</note>. Finally, fashion them into solid boluses, which are commonly called "little morsels" <note>original: "morsulos"</note>, and wrap them in gold leaf.

These three things I myself have experienced in the use I prescribed: the theriac, the aloe prepared as I described, and that confection. They benefit the individual limbs, as well as the powers and the spirits; they sharpen the senses and the intellect, and they strengthen the memory. Furthermore, by those pills, phlegm, bile, and black bile <note>original: "atra bilis"; the humor associated with melancholy and deep intellectual labor</note> are easily either drawn out or corrected. Moreover, for any age...

¶ ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.
...and these three things we have mentioned are judged most beneficial to nature.

¶ Medicines against phlegm.

**I**f <note>The text begins with a large red initial S for "Si."</note> it becomes necessary to fight more vigorously against overflowing phlegm <note>original: "pituitam"; one of the four humors, associated with cold and moisture, which in excess was thought to cause lethargy and respiratory issues</note>, we shall give pills at dawn made from Galen’s *hiera picra* <note>original: "hierapicra Galieni"; literally "holy bitter," a famous ancient laxative formula primarily containing aloe and spices</note>, or those which Mesue <note>original: "Mesues"; referring to Mesue the Younger, an influential but semi-mythical Arabic medical authority whose works were standard in the Renaissance</note> calls "elephantine" pills <note>original: "elefanginas"; specialized pills intended to purge thick, heavy humors</note>. These should be given as many and as often as is required; or, in a person of stronger constitution, pills composed of equal parts *hiera* and agaric troches <term>troches: small, medicated lozenges or cakes; here made from the larch fungus (agaric), used as a purgative</term>. Always administer these, however, with liquid rose honey and oxymel <term>oxymel: a medicinal syrup made of honey and vinegar, prized for its ability to cut through thick mucus</term>, and water of fennel <gloss>that is, fennel</gloss>. This syrup is certainly most useful for digesting phlegm, both before taking the pills and after them. If, along with the phlegm, the other humors are also disturbed, we shall purge them opportunely with Mesue’s rhubarb pills, or those which later physicians call the "pills without which" <note>original: "sine quibus"; referring to "Pilulae sine quibus esse nolo," meaning "pills without which I do not wish to be," a versatile and popular general-purpose laxative</note>. We utterly abhor every violent and sudden evacuation or purging, for it weakens the stomach and the heart, exhausts the vital spirits, confuses the humors, and darkens the spirits with the murky vapors of the humors.

¶ Catarrh.

**W**hen <note>The text begins with a large blue initial U for "Ubi."</note> the head is unstable because of catarrhs <note>original: "destillationibus"; literally "distillations," referring to the downward flow of excess humors from the brain to the nose, throat, or chest</note> caused by phlegm, we shall occasionally give some of the pills described above at bedtime. We shall, moreover, command the patient to chew frankincense <margin>frankincense</margin> often at that hour and at others. For it is wonderfully helpful for catarrhs and for all the senses...

c.iii. <note>This is a signature mark, used by printers to ensure the pages were bound in the correct order.</note>

...which aids the memory. ¶ **Headache.**

**I**f <note>The text begins with a large red decorative initial S for "Si."</note> the head often aches because it is weighed down by a cold humor <note>The humor of phlegm was categorized as cold and moist; an excess in the head was thought to cause heavy, dull pain</note>, besides those things we have already described, we shall command the patient to hold in their mouth that confection which they call *diambra* <note>a fragrant restorative compound containing ambergris and spices</note>, or *diacorus* <note>a preparation made from sweet flag, *Acorus calamus*</note>, or *plisarchoticon* <note>a complex medicinal compound used to strengthen the brain and digestive system</note>. Furthermore, they should often chew mastic <term>mastic: a resin from the *Pistacia lentiscus* tree, used in this period to draw out excess moisture and cleanse the head</term>. Besides this, smear the forehead, temples, and neck with leaves of marjoram, fennel, and rue, pounded together with rose oil. Similarly, [one may use] aloe, vinegar, oil, and rose water, perfectly dissolved together.

¶ **On the Care of Vision.**

**W**hen <note>The text begins with a large blue decorative initial U for "Ubi."</note> the eyes grow dim but do not redden, nor show any other sign of heat <note>Redness and heat were signs of "hot" humors like blood or yellow bile; dimness without redness suggested a "cold" blockage of the visual spirits</note>, then certainly an eye-wash <note>original: "collirium"</note> made from the water of fennel, marjoram, celandine, and rue—with saffron and antimony added—is beneficial. But such water must first be strained through a thick cloth. However, you should apply nothing to the eyes unless you have first frequently purged them with "pills of light" <note>original: "pilulis lucis"; a traditional name for a recipe of pills intended to clear the vision by purging the head of thick humors</note>. But if the dimming eyes should be red, immediately purge them with the prescribed pills of fumitory <note>original: "fumo terre"; literally "smoke of the earth," a plant used to treat skin conditions and "hot" imbalances</note>. Afterward, an eye-wash of rose water and sugar is helpful. Sometimes, indeed, it helps most quickly if egg white, tutty <term>tutty: an impure form of zinc oxide, traditionally used in eye salves for its drying and healing properties</term>, and milk are added. But altogether, the daily use...

¶ **ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.**

<margin>Fennel</margin>
**F**ennel <note>original: "maratrum"</note> preserves and sharpens the sight. Indeed, one ought to hold its seed frequently in the mouth, and eat the leaves. The *Trifere minor* <note>a complex electuary or medicinal paste used for various ailments</note> described by Mesue <note>Masawaih al-Mardini, or Mesue the Younger, a celebrated 10th-century physician whose works were standard in European medical schools</note> is excellent. It is best to take a preserved chebulic myrobalan <term>myrobalan: a dried fruit from the *Terminalia* tree, highly prized in the Middle Ages for its ability to purge "black bile" and "phlegm" and to strengthen the brain</term> daily on an empty stomach; and with it, some bread composed of sugar and fennel reduced to powder. This, moreover, is wonderfully beneficial for the intellect and for prolonging life. The use of eyebright <note>original: "eufragia"</note> is also a singular protection for the eyes. In every case of either headache or dimness of the eyes, the vapors <note>rising fumes from the stomach caused by digestion, which were thought to cloud the senses if they reached the head</note> must be diverted backward by rubbings and cupping glasses <note>cucurbitulae: small glass or metal vessels used to create a vacuum on the skin, drawing blood or humors to the surface</note>. And if heat is the cause, and blood is abundant, we shall apply leeches to the neck and shoulders.

¶ **On Restoring the Sense of Taste.**

<note>A decorative initial letter S contains a figure of a stomach, illustrating the connection between the digestive organ and the faculty of taste discussed in the following text.</note>
**T**he stomach of those devoted to literary studies often loses almost all sense of taste. If this happens through a fault of phlegm <note>original: "pituita"; one of the four bodily humors, characterized as cold and moist</note>, which an acid taste in the mouth or abundant and stickier saliva indicates, then after you have loosened the bowels with the medicines we described above, use aromatic rose preparations: namely, rose sugar mixed with rose honey, and also cinnamon. [One may] also [use] preserved ginger alone, or syrup of mint; but especially theriac <note>original: "tiriaca"; a famous poly-pharmaceutical "universal" remedy</note>. But if perhaps an abundance of bile <note>original: "bilis"; specifically yellow bile, which was hot and dry</note> occurs, which a bitter mouth is usually accustomed to show, similarly after a purgation prepared from aloe (as we have said) or rhubarb, take...

...the stomach, either with "triasandals" <note>original: "triasandalu"; a cooling medicinal powder made from three types of sandalwood</note>, or with oxysaccharum <note>a medicinal syrup made of vinegar and sugar</note> composed of white vinegar and the juice of a sour pomegranate. Nausea, which always arises from either humor <note>referring to an excess of yellow or black bile</note>, is removed by *diacitronite* and the use of capers with vinegar. Likewise, a small drink taken on an empty stomach consisting of white rose vinegar, if mixed with double its weight in sugar. Again, use syrup of mint and wormwood. Also mint, either preserved in vinegar or diluted with the juice of a sour pomegranate.

Whenever this humor overflows and rages, it weakens not only the entire body, but especially the spirit—which serves as the very instrument of the intellect—and even the intellect and judgment themselves. <meta>This page continues the medical instructions for treating black bile (melancholy), focusing on the three primary rules for purging the humor as established by Galen and moving into specific recipes for syrups, pills, and electuaries.</meta>

### ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

First, let the precept be as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician of antiquity whose humoral theories formed the basis of medieval and Renaissance medicine.</note> taught: that we should not strive to draw out that humor <note>Referring to the "black bile" or melancholic humor discussed on the previous page.</note> suddenly. We must take care lest, perhaps, as the liquid part is drawn off through the narrower and more subtle passages, the remainder should reside there in an excessively thick and dry manner. Rather, let it be softened little by little and digested at the same time it is led out. 

Second, in the meantime, let the head and the whole body be moistened to the best of one's ability, using both more humid foods and sweet <note>meaning "fresh" or "non-saline"</note> baths, as well as similar ointments. This must be done with such caution, however, that a catarrh <term>destillatio: a "dripping" or flow of excess fluids (humors) from the head into the body</term> is not provoked, nor the stomach or liver damaged, nor the passages of the body obstructed. 

Third—and this is indeed most necessary—is that the heart be continually cherished and strengthened by suitable things, partly taken internally and partly applied externally to the chest or nostrils. Also, let things that delight be seen and heard; let them be smelled and continuously pondered. Contrary things, however, should be kept far away.

### On Syrups

Many things indeed have been composed by many authors against this humor <note>black bile</note>. I, however, will at present bring forth three most select and safest types of remedies from the many available—first received from the ancients, then confirmed by more recent authors, and occasionally adapted by us for our own use. In the first type is the composition of the best syrup. In the second are the most proven pills. In the third are the most healthful electuaries <term>electuary: a medicinal paste made by mixing powdered herbs with honey or syrup</term>. When these three are applied at the opportune time, the melancholic humor...

### On Syrups

The syrup is of this kind: Take borage <note>original: *borago*, a herb traditionally used to "drive away sorrow" and gladden the heart</note>, bugloss, and the flowers of both, lemon balm <note>original: *melissa*</note>, maidenhair fern <note>original: *capillus veneris*</note>, endive, violets, dodder <note>original: *cuscuta*</note>, polypody <note>a type of fern used as a purgative</note>, senna, and epithyme <note>original: *epithimum*, or dodder of thyme, highly valued for purging black bile</note>: take a handful of each. 

Take twenty damask plums <note>damsons</note>, ten fragrant apples, one ounce of raisins, half an ounce of licorice, and three drachms <term>drachma: a unit of weight, roughly 1/8th of an ounce</term> each of cinnamon, red sandalwood, and citron peels, along with half a drachm of saffron. 

Let all these be boiled in water—except for the epithyme and the aromatic spices—until a third of the liquid has boiled away. After the decoction has been pressed and strained, let it be boiled again moderately with sugar and the epithyme.

Finally, let the spices be infused—namely the cinnamon and the saffron. At dawn, three ounces of this syrup should be drunk warm, along with two or three ounces of bugloss water. Together with these, at least two of the following pills should be taken (or more, as suits the individual), specifically so that the bowels may be moved slightly every day.

Our own invention, these pills are composed under the very influence of Jupiter and Venus <note>In medical astrology, Jupiter and Venus are "benefics" that counteract the cold, dry, and sorrowful influence of Saturn, the planet associated with melancholy.</note>: they draw out phlegm, choler, and black bile without distress. They strengthen each individual limb; they sharpen and illuminate the spirits <note>original: *spiritus*. In Renaissance medicine, these were subtle fluids that carried life and thought through the body.</note>. Thus, they expand the spirits so that they do not produce sadness by being constricted, but rather rejoice in expansion and light. Furthermore, they stabilize the spirits so that they do not vanish through too much extension.

Take, therefore, twelve grains of gold—especially in the form of gold leaf, provided it is pure; of frankincense, myrrh, saffron, aloeswood, cinnamon, citron bark, lemon balm, raw scarlet silk, mint, white been, and red been <note>original: *been albi, been rubei*, roots of the Behen plant used as heart tonics</note>, take a half-drachm <term>drachma: a Greek unit of weight, approximately 3.9 grams</term> of each. Of purple roses, red sandalwood, red coral, and of the three myrobalans <note>astringent, plum-like fruits used as a gentle purgative</note>—namely the emblic, chebulic, and Indian varieties—take one drachm of each. Take an amount of properly washed aloes equal to the total weight of all the other ingredients combined. Fashion these into pills using the most select pure wine.

There follow pills for dissolving melancholy that are somewhat stronger, yet not at all violent. Take peony, myrrh, Arabian stoechas <note>original: *sticados arabici*, a species of lavender used to treat the head and nerves</note>, lemon balm, frankincense, saffron, the three myrobalans (the emblic, chebulic, and Indian), and roses: one drachm of each. Of agaric lozenges <term>trochiscus: a medicinal tablet or disk</term>, polypody, epithyme, senna, and lapis lazuli <note>a deep-blue stone believed to purge black bile from the blood</note>—the latter being properly washed and prepared—and of Armenian stone <note>original: *lapis armenus*, a blue mineral carbonate of copper, also used as a potent purge</note> treated in the same way: take three drachms of each. Combine these with two ounces of washed aloes and excellent wine to form pills. If a manifest heat dominates along with the melancholy, those ingredients which are in this composition...

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for preparing and administering medicinal pills for scholars. It bridges the discussion from stronger recipes to a simpler, personal favorite of the author, emphasizing the need for moisture to balance the drying effects of these treatments.</meta>

cold, they should be increased by an additional third part of their weight. I have composed these pills, as is suitable for those devoted to literary studies, in imitation of the Greeks, the Latins, and the Arabs. However, I did not wish to mix in anything stronger—such as hellebore <note>original: *veratrum*. Hellebore was a potent and dangerous plant used in antiquity to "purge the brain" of madness, but it was often considered too violent for delicate constitutions.</note>, which Carneades the Enthusiast <note>Carneades (c. 214–129 BCE) was a skeptic philosopher who famously took hellebore to sharpen his mind before debating his opponents.</note> used to use. For I offer counsel only to men of letters, or those perhaps slightly stronger than average; for such men, nothing is more destructive than violent remedies. Therefore, I have omitted the "Indian pills," those known for containing lapis lazuli or Armenian stone, and the composition they call *hieralogodion* <note>A complex medieval purgative electuary containing colocynth, or "bitter apple."</note>.

If, finally, it is appropriate to include a simpler recipe—one I use more frequently myself: Take one ounce of washed aloes; two <term>drachma: an ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams</term> each of emblic and chebulic myrobalans <note>The myrobalan is a plum-like fruit valued in medieval medicine for its ability to purge specific humors gently.</note>; two drachms of mastic; and also two drachms of roses, especially the purple variety. Form these into pills using wine.

Furthermore, no one should ever use these pills—neither these nor the others I have approved—entirely on their own, lest the body perhaps be dried out too much. Such dryness is indeed the worst condition for melancholy. Rather, they should be taken together with the syrup—which I described above, following partly Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a pioneering 9th-century Persian Christian physician.</note> and partly Gentile da Foligno <note>A famous 14th-century Italian medical commentator.</note>—or with one, two, or three ounces of light, fragrant wine, as suits the individual. Alternatively, they may be taken with a decoction of honey, raisins, and licorice. Or, if heat dominates the constitution anywhere, take them with violet julep <note>A sweet medicinal syrup made from violet flowers.</note> and violet water. In short, I advise all literary men who are prone to black bile <note>original: *atra bilis*. The literal translation of "melancholy," believed to cause depression and anxiety when it became burnt or too thick.</note>...

...are more prone to this condition, they should use this purgation twice every year, namely in the spring and autumn: for fifteen or twenty continuous days, specifically using these pills taken with syrup and similar liquids. [paraph] On Liquid Medicine.

[Large red initial M]oreover, one must remember that when the greatest danger of drying out <note>original: *exsiccatione*. In Renaissance medicine, excessive "exsiccation" (dryness) was a primary concern for scholars, as it was thought to lead to melancholy, exhaustion, and premature aging.</note> threatens, yet the necessity of the ailment forces one to purge, it is worthwhile to pause the use of pills. Instead, within the syrup itself or a similar decoction made in bugloss <note>A plant used in herbal medicine to "cheer the heart" and temper the heat of the blood.</note> water, when one must purge, one should occasionally infuse one ounce—or at least half an ounce—of *diasenna* <note>original: *diasenæ*. A medicinal confection made primarily of senna, used as a laxative.</note>, *diacatholicon* <note>original: *diachatholiconis*. A "universal" laxative electuary used to purge all four humors.</note>, or *triphera persica* <note>original: *tripheræ persicæ*. A cooling medicinal mixture, often containing peach or related ingredients, used to balance internal heat.</note>. A bitter, complex purgative electuary specifically formulated to expel "black bile."</note>. Prepared cassia <note>A mild laxative derived from the pulp of the *Cassia fistula* pod.</note> is also useful here; manna <note>The sweet, dried sap of the flowering ash tree, prized as a gentle laxative suitable for delicate constitutions.</note> is even more useful. In humoral theory, "burnt" or "adust" melancholy was considered a more toxic, overheated state than "natural" melancholy, requiring more intensive cooling and purging.</note>. If, however, the melancholy is natural, these are indeed appropriate, but especially if a double or triple portion of polypody <note>A type of fern whose root was commonly used in Renaissance pharmacy to purge phlegm and melancholy.</note> is added to the syrup, along with licorice, saffron, and raisins. Likewise, let two ounces of liquid honey of roses be added to the medicine. Whenever...

<meta>This page continues from the discussion on liquid medicines and syrups, moving into specific protocols for taking these medicines based on the patient's "complexion" and the actual presence of melancholic humor. It concludes with a warning against excessive bloodletting.</meta>

...how the syrup itself should be taken, we have spoken in the preceding sections. But the medicine should be taken with it for thrice twenty <note>original: *ter uiginti diebus*. That is, sixty days.</note> days.

[paraph] Truly, if no melancholic humor <note>In the humoral theory of the Renaissance, "humors" were bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile). A humor was a physical substance to be purged, whereas a "complexion" was the body's general temperament.</note> appears to be present, but only a melancholic complexion—namely, a cold and dry quality of the limbs—remember that it is not at all expedient to purge the bowels or to let blood. Instead, do only the remaining things which we have either already said or will say. Focus especially on whatever pertains to moderately heating the body and greatly moistening it, illuminating the spirits <note>original: *spiritus*. These were thought to be subtle vapors that carried life and thought throughout the body; "illuminating" them meant clearing them of the dark "smoke" of melancholy.</note> as much as possible, and soothing the limbs.

[paraph] However, when the black bile <note>original: *atré bilis*. The literal meaning of "melancholy."</note> humor itself overflows, we do not only moisten the body and the humor, but we also loosen the belly with that caution which we mentioned: but never violently. For indeed, Plato in the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s dialogue concerning the nature of the physical world and the human body.</note> warns us that a long-lasting disease, such as the melancholic condition is, should not be provoked by too powerful medicines and troublesome drugs.

[paraph] On the letting of blood.

[large blue initial S]ome people are quite bold in letting blood, whom wise doctors greatly detest. For blood is the tempering of black bile: it is the fuel of the spirit and the treasure of life. Only when an abundance of blood is indicated—either by profuse laughter, great daring and confidence, a reddish color, or the swelling of the veins—is it [appropriate] to let blood for the lettered <note>original: *litteratis*. Referring specifically to scholars and those dedicated to study.</note>...

<summary>The author distinguishes between a melancholic "complexion" (naturally cold and dry) and an actual "humor" (excess black bile), advising against purging or bloodletting for the former. He cites Plato to warn against harsh medicines for chronic conditions and emphasizes that blood is a precious "treasure of life" that should rarely be drawn from scholars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions for managing a melancholic temperament, specifically focusing on the physical procedures of bloodletting and the preparation of medicinal pastes known as electuaries. It follows the previous warning against violent purging.</meta>

[blue and red pilcrow] ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

When the situation demands it, we ought to draw four ounces of blood in the morning and as many in the evening from the spleen vein <note>original: *uena lienis*. In traditional humoral medicine, specific veins in the arms were believed to be connected to specific organs; the vein on the left arm was associated with the spleen, the organ responsible for black bile.</note> of the left arm, through a somewhat wide incision. Then, after a few days—at least seven, or at most fourteen—one should irritate the hemorrhoids <note>original: *mariscas*. Literally "large figs," a term used for hemorrhoids or anal growths. Inducing bleeding from these was a common method to purge "heavy" melancholic blood from the lower body.</note>, first by a somewhat rough rubbing, and then by applying leeches (which they call bloodsuckers), so that three or four ounces of blood may drip out from there. Both of these procedures seem suitable only for the more robust patients. For the weaker ones, however, if necessity demands it, one should only irritate the hemorrhoids as we have taught. But it is permitted neither to loosen the belly with medicines nor to let blood unless you have first softened the body with oily and gentle enemas <note>original: *clysteribus*. Liquids injected into the rectum to cleanse the bowels or deliver medicine.</note>. And let this be a general rule for the melancholic nature: effort must be given so that, if necessary, by the frequent use of such enemas, the lower belly is always lubricated and purged.

[blue and red pilcrow] On Electuaries <note>An electuary is a medicinal paste made by mixing powdered herbs or other ingredients with honey, syrup, or preserves.</note>.

**S**equūtur <note>The text begins with a large red initial 'S'.</note> there follow the electuaries, among which I certainly praise the one that Rhazes <note>original: *Rasis*. A highly influential 9th-century Persian physician known for his medical encyclopedias.</note> called "the exhilarant." Furthermore, I praise those which Avicenna <note>A famous 11th-century Persian polymath whose *Canon of Medicine* was a standard European textbook for centuries.</note> composed in his book *On the Powers of the Heart*. Submerge it in the juice of sweet and pleasantly smelling apples, as well as the juice of bugloss <note>A plant in the borage family, traditionally used to treat "melancholy" and "comfort the heart."</note> and rose water—one pound of each. After twenty-four hours, cook the whole mixture gently, until...

...nor does the water turn red. Then extract the silk <note>original: *sericum*. Raw silk was often boiled in Renaissance medicinal preparations; it was believed to contain "animal spirits" that could fortify the human heart and spirit.</note>, and squeeze it out thoroughly. Soon pour in one hundred and fifty drachms <note>A drachm is a unit of weight roughly equal to 3.9 grams or 1/8th of an ounce.</note> of the brightest white sugar: cook it again until it thickens like honey. Then remove it from the fire, and while it is still warm, pour in six drachms of crude ambergris <note>original: *ambrae crudae*. A waxy, aromatic substance from the digestive system of sperm whales, highly prized in medicine for its stimulant properties.</note> carefully chopped; and permit the ambergris to melt. Finally, add to these a powder of the following kind: 

Take raw aloeswood <note>original: *ligni aloes*. A fragrant, resinous wood used to treat the heart and brain.</note> and cinnamon—six drachms of each in equal parts. Thirteen drachms of properly washed lapis lazuli <note>A deep-blue semi-precious stone. In this period, it was ground and used internally to "purge" melancholy from the blood.</note>. Two drachms of "unions"—that is, white pearls <note>original: *unionum .i. albarum margaritarum*. Large, singular pearls were often called "unions."</note>. One drachm of true gold <note>Often added as gold leaf or fine powder to "brighten" the spirits and honor the heart.</note>. Half a drachm of chosen musk. 

Two drachms of this, or one, are given with wine in the morning and evening, three or four hours before food. This recipe pleases me more than all others. Nevertheless, I also approve of the "Sweet Diamuscum" <note>An aromatic, musk-based cordial described in the works of Mesue, a legendary figure in Arabic-Latin medicine.</note> of Mesue, and the "Composition of Gems," provided they are taken with rose water. 

I would also wonderfully praise the composition crafted by the supreme philosopher Peter of Abano <note>original: *Petro Ebanensi*. A famous 13th-century physician and astrologer known for his "Conciliator" of medical disputes.</note>, were it not that he himself feared that its immoderate use might cause an excessive dilation and evaporation <note>original: *dilatationem exalationemque*. The concern was that the medicine was so "hot" or "airy" that it would cause the vital spirits to expand and leak out of the body, leading to weakness.</note> of the spirits.

[blue and red paragraph mark] Wherefore I have devised two further preparations that are the safest of all, and most suitable for any time, age, or nature because of their balanced temperament. In these, the "useful is mixed with the sweet." They nourish as much as they cherish and strengthen. Furthermore, they provide as much for the spirit and the intellect... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

...as much for sharpening and illuminating [the spirits] as for strengthening them. Take four ounces of rose sugar. Two ounces of sugar cooked together with bugloss flowers <note>original: *buglossae*. Bugloss (Alkanet) was a common medicinal herb believed to "gladden the heart" and purge melancholy.</note>. One ounce of candied citron peel <note>original: *corticis pomi citri*. Citron was prized for its refreshing properties and its ability to protect the heart against "corruption."</note>. Two ounces of candied chebulic myrobalan <note>original: *Chebuli*. A type of fruit from the Haritaki tree, used in humoral medicine as a gentle laxative to purge the body of "black bile" or melancholy.</note>. One drachm of chosen cinnamon. Red sandalwood and red coral: one drachm of each <note>original: *sandali : corallis .s. utriusque rubri*. Red ingredients were often associated with the blood and the vitality of the heart.</note>. Likewise, half a drachm each of raw, cut scarlet silk, saffron, and pearls. One-third of a drachm each of gold and silver. Two grains each of ambergris and musk. Melt all these together in the juice of a citron or lemon cooked with sugar.

There follows another confection, somewhat more healthful, and certainly much sweeter. Take four ounces of sweet almonds; two ounces each of pine nuts (specifically, those washed for the duration of one day) and cucumber seeds <note>original: *seminum cucumeris*. Cucumber seeds were classified as "cold" seeds in the humoral system, used to balance excess heat in the body.</note>. Four ounces of hard sugar, which they call "candy"—that is, white sugar. One and a half pounds of another sugar, but of the whitest kind. Melt all these in rose water and lemon and citron water, in which red-hot gold and silver have been quenched <note>The practice of "quenching" hot metals in liquids was believed to transfer the "virtues" or medicinal spirit of the metal—gold for the heart, silver for the brain—to the liquid without requiring the patient to digest solid metal.</note>. 

Cook them together gently. Finally, add one drachm each of cinnamon, red ben-nut <note>original: *been rubri*. The root of the *Centaurea behen*, highly regarded in the Middle Ages and Renaissance as a cardiac tonic.</note>, red sandalwood, and red coral. Half a drachm each of the brightest pearls, saffron, and raw scarlet silk, ground very finely. Twelve grains each of gold and silver. One-third of a drachm each of hyacinth <note>A zircon or garnet gemstone, believed to protect the wearer and strengthen the heart.</note>, emerald, sapphire, and carbuncle <note>A deep red gemstone, usually a garnet or ruby, associated with warmth and vitality.</note>. If, however, anyone lacks...

### On excessive sleeplessness.

**S**cholars and those of a melancholic temperament are often prone to this: because the brain has become excessively dried out, they find themselves worn down by long bouts of wakefulness. Since nothing increases the evils of <term>black bile</term> <note>original: *atrae bilis*. In humoral medicine, an excess of black bile caused "melancholy," leading to depression, anxiety, and physical wasting.</note> more than prolonged sleeplessness, this great evil must be countered with every effort.

Therefore, let them eat lettuce after their other food, along with a little bread and a tiny bit of saffron. They should sip pure wine after the lettuce, and they must not study by candlelight <note>original: *lucubrent*. From *lucubratio*, meaning to work or study late into the night by lamp-light.</note> for more than an hour. When they retire to bed, let them take a preparation of this kind, which should contain two ounces of white poppy seeds...

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.

Take one ounce of lettuce seed. Of <term>amomum</term> <note>original: *Amomi*. An aromatic spice from the ginger family, similar to cardamom, used in antiquity for its warming properties.</note> and saffron, take half a drachm of each. Take six ounces of sugar. Dissolve and cook all of these together with poppy syrup. Let them <note>the patients or scholars</note> eat two drachms of this; let them also taste a little poppy syrup or wine. You should smear their forehead and temples with oil of violets and water lily <note>original: *nenufare*. Referring to the white water lily, *Nymphaea alba*, prized for its cooling effects.</note>, adding a bit of camphor.

Likewise, use milk and almond oil as well as violet oil. You should bring the scent of saffron, camphor, and the rinds of sweet apples to the nostrils. Use a little vinegar as well, but a great deal of rose water. You should also spread the bed with the leaves of "cold" plants <note>In the humoral system, "cold" plants like willow or lettuce were believed to counteract the "heat" of insomnia and fever.</note>. Soothe the ears with deep, solemn chants and sounds. 

You should frequently moisten the head with washes of this kind: namely, water in which pieces of poppy have been cooked, along with the leaves of lettuce, purslane, mallow, roses, vine, willow, and reeds, adding chamomile. Also, with sweet baths made from such things, the shins and arms—and often the whole body—should be moistened. Moreover, it is very beneficial to drink milk mixed with sugar on an empty stomach, provided the stomach tolerates it well. These moistening things are wonderfully beneficial for <term>melancholics</term> <note>Those with a "melancholic" temperament were thought to be naturally dry and cold; scholars were frequently categorized this way due to their sedentary, pensive lives.</note>, even if they are already sleeping enough. Remember that almond milk should be a most familiar sight at your table.

# On dullness and forgetfulness.

One of the four bodily humors, associated with coldness and moisture; an excess was thought to cause mental dullness.</note>, together with cold...

...lest excessive melancholy, occupying the head, should weigh it down and make people dull and forgetful. Therefore, the head must be unburdened for these individuals with those remedies which we have elsewhere stated are suitable for <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: *pituite*. In humoral theory, phlegm was a cold, moist humor that, like melancholy, could cause sluggishness if it accumulated in the brain.</note>. If those are not sufficient, turn to <term>Indian pills</term> <note>original: *pilulas indas*. These were purgative pills often containing myrobalans, used to "purge" black bile.</note>, <term>cocchias</term> <note>A standard type of purgative pill in medieval medicine, often containing aloes and colocynth.</note>, and <term>hieralogodion</term> <note>A "holy" (hiera) bitter purgative formula intended to clear the senses.</note>. Or, use the "Pills of the Jew" which Mesue <note>Mesue the Younger, an influential but semi-mythical Arabic medical authority whose texts were foundational for Renaissance pharmacology.</note> describes in his chapter on headaches.

If the person's natural temperament or age is somewhat cold, and if the heat of summer does not forbid it, use that <term>anacardine confection</term> after purging, which Mesue calls the "Confection of the Wise" in his book of remedies. Also, use that <term>anacardia</term> <note>A remedy made from the marking nut tree (*Anacardium orientale*), which was famously believed in the Middle Ages to sharpen a failing memory by "warming" the brain.</note> which he reviews in the chapter on forgetfulness, following the opinion of the son of Zacharia <note>Referring to the great Persian physician Rhazes (Abu Bakr Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi).</note>. Give one drachm early in the morning; however, let the one who takes it abstain entirely on that day from anger, sexual intercourse, drunkenness, physical labor, and things of a "hot" nature. These remedies are indeed most powerful against mental dullness and forgetfulness.

But if you prefer more familiar remedies, give ginger preserved in sugar, mixed with a little frankincense; this greatly benefits the senses and the memory, especially if the following are added: namely, honey of anacardia, honey of chebulic myrobalans, <term>acorus</term> <note>Sweet flag, a wetland plant used as a stimulant and for digestive issues.</note>, <term>cyperus</term> <note>Galingale or nut-grass, known for its aromatic and warming properties.</note>, ambergris, and musk. These should be held in the mouth for a long time, and may also be infused into the nostrils and ears. The scent of frankincense, marjoram, fennel, nutmeg, rue, and cloves is also of no small benefit. Remember, however, that <term>theriac</term> <note>original: *tiriacā*. A famous ancient "panacea" or universal antidote containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh, used here as a general tonic for the nervous system.</note> in these and similar cases...

-># ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.<-

...as we said at the beginning: it should never be placed second to any remedy. Furthermore, for those who are dull and forgetful, anoint the temples and the neck with an ointment of this kind. Take one ounce of elderberry oil, two ounces of <term>oil of ben</term> <note>original: *olei deben*. A stable oil pressed from the seeds of the Moringa tree, highly valued by Renaissance apothecaries as a base for ointments.</note>, half an ounce of <term>euphorbium</term> <note>A potent, irritating resin derived from succulent plants of the genus *Euphorbia*, used here as a stimulant to "wake up" the senses.</note>, and the same amount of <term>castoreum</term> <note>original: *caſtorei*. A yellowish secretion from the castor sacs of beavers, used in historical medicine to treat nervous ailments and to sharpen the mind.</note>. You shall rub the arms, legs, and neck vigorously. And if there is a need, you shall apply <term>cupping glasses</term> <note>original: *cucurbitulas*. Heated glass vessels used to create a vacuum on the skin, intended here to draw sluggish humors away from the head.</note> to the neck. Furthermore, you shall cover and warm the crown of the head with crushed marjoram, frankincense, and nutmeg.

[Large red initial C]are for the corporeal spirit indeed; but cherish the incorporeal; and finally, venerate the truth. Medicine provides the first; moral discipline the second; and religion the third.

[Large blue initial S]i <note>The text begins a philosophical argument: "If men..."</note> If men who are desirous of truth must care for the <term>corporeal spirit</term> <note>In the medical philosophy of this era, the "spirit" was a fine, vaporous substance that acted as the bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note> with such great medical diligence—lest, by chance, being entirely neglected, it should become an obstacle or serve those seeking truth ineptly—then without a doubt, it is far more fitting to cherish the incorporeal spirit, that is, the intellect itself, with the teachings of moral discipline. For it is through the intellect alone that truth itself is grasped, since truth is incorporeal. Indeed, it is a sacrilege to cherish only the servant of the soul—that is, the body—while neglecting the soul, which is the master and ruler of the body. This is especially true since it is the opinion of the <term>Magi</term> <note>The "Wise Men" of antiquity; the author likely refers to the "Ancient Theology" tradition which linked Persian, Egyptian, and Greek wisdom.</note> and of Plato that the whole body depends on the soul in such a way that unless the soul is well, the body cannot be well. Therefore, Apollo, the author of medicine, judged not Hippocrates—though he was born of Apollo's own lineage—but Socrates to be the wisest of all. For indeed, just as much as Hippocrates [did] for the body, so [much]...

...then Socrates applied himself to healing the soul <note>original: *animo sanando*. Socrates is famously credited with shifting the focus of philosophy from the physical world to the care of the human soul.</note>: although whatever those ancient philosophers had attempted, Christ alone achieved. Therefore, if we are commanded by Socrates to cultivate our mind with the finest morals for this reason: so that we might more easily attain, with a serene mind, that light and truth which we seek by natural instinct—how much more is it right to venerate that divine truth itself, above all through holy religion? 

The created mind was formed to seek and grasp this truth in no other way than the eye was made to perceive the light of the sun. And as our teacher Plato says, just as sight perceives nothing visible except within the splendor of the highest visible object—that is, the sun itself—so too the human intellect apprehends nothing <term>intelligible</term> <note>original: *intelligibile*. In this philosophical context, this refers to truths or realities that are perceived by the mind or intellect rather than the physical senses.</note> except within the light of the highest intelligible being—which is God—who is always and everywhere present to us. This is the light, as the scripture says, "which illuminates every man coming into this world" <note>A direct reference to the Gospel of John 1:9, used here to bridge the gap between Platonic "Light" and the Christian "Word."</note>. # ¶ ON LONG LIFE.
## ¶ Preface to the Book on Long Life.

<note>Large decorative initial M, with a small 'm' in the left margin</note> MARSILIO Ficino of Florence sends greetings to Filippo Valori, a most excellent and noble citizen. Although our Plato lives by his own <term>genius</term> <note>original: *genio*. In the Neoplatonic sense, this refers to a guardian spirit or an innate divine quality that guides an individual's life and intellect.</note> and—so I believe—will live as long as the world itself shall live, nevertheless my own genius always impels me toward this: that after the worship of the Divine, I should provide for the life of Plato above all things. Toward this very goal, the House of Medici <note>original: *Medica domus*. Ficino is making a clever pun on the family name "Medici" and the Latin word for medical or healing, suggesting they are the "House of Healing."</note> has for a long time aspired before all others. And you, my dear Valori, a most close friend of the Medici and a most devoted student of Platonic glory and teaching, strive toward this same end. Therefore, whatever life I always desire for Plato, I desire that same life for the Medici, and a similar one for you, Valori. For this reason, I exhort and beseech you, my Valori: since you always favor the works of Platonic glory, read these precepts of ours concerning the prolonging of life with great diligence, and observe them. By following these, and living long yourself, you may be able to patronize the recently reborn teaching of Plato for a longer time, together with the magnanimous Lorenzo de' Medici. Farewell.

# ¶ MARSILIO FICINO OF FLORENCE’S
# ¶ BOOK ON LONG LIFE.

# ¶ MARSILIO FICINO OF FLORENCE ON LONG LIFE.

<note>Large decorated initial A in red and blue, with a small guide letter 'a' in the left margin</note> TO PERFECT ART and science, it is not so much a teachable nature or a reliable memory that leads us, as it is the clarity of prudent judgment. Indeed, judgment is so difficult because of the ambiguity arising from a diversity of conjectures, that it must necessarily be confirmed by experiment. Experiment, too, is deceptive, both because of the difficulty of judgment itself and because of the fleeting brevity of the opportune moment <term>opportune moment</term> <note>original: *opportuni... temporis*. This is a reference to the Greek concept of *Kairos*, the critical or "right" moment that must be seized before it passes.</note> for capturing an experiment. For these very reasons, we rightly conclude along with Hippocrates <note>original: *Ippocrate*. Referring to the famous first aphorism of Hippocrates: "Life is short, art is long, opportunity fleeting, experiment perilous, judgment difficult."</note> that art is long, and that we cannot attain it except through length of life. 

Yet the Fates do not only promise a long life once at the beginning; our own diligence also provides it. Astrologers admit this when they deal with <term>elections</term> <note>The practice of choosing the most astrologically favorable time to begin an undertaking.</note> and <term>images</term> <note>original: *imaginibus*. These are astrological talismans or figures engraved at specific times to capture celestial influence, a core part of Ficino's "natural magic."</note>; the diligent care and experience of physicians also confirm it. Through such foresight, not only those who are naturally strong, but even the sickly sometimes attain a long life. Thus, it is no wonder that a certain Herodicus <note>A Greek physician and teacher of gymnastics mentioned by Plato as someone who, being sickly, used medical art to protract his life to old age.</note>, a man of letters who was the weakest of all in his time (as Plato and Aristotle testify), reached his hundredth year through this kind of foresight. Plutarch, moreover, relates that many whose bodies were otherwise not very firm attained a long life through diligence alone. I pass over for now how many weak people I myself have known who, by the gift of prudence, surpassed the years of the most robust...

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Book II of Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life." ...to have persevered. Therefore, it will be neither useless nor vain, after the book we composed on caring for the health of scholars <note>original: *litteratorum*. Ficino refers to Book I of this treatise, *De Vita Sana* (On Healthy Life), which focuses on the specific health risks of students and intellectuals.</note>, to hand down certain additional precepts conducive to a long life for minds greedy for knowledge. 

Truly, we do not wish these instructions to be shared with the sluggish and the lazy. For why should we desire those people to live long? Since they do not even truly live, we would be nourishing drones, not bees. Nor should they be made common to men dissolved in the lost lust for pleasures, who, being fools, daily prefer a brief pleasure far above all else. Nor should they be opened to the wicked and the unjust, whose life is the death of the good. But these instructions are for men who are at least prudent and temperate, who by the powers of a skillful mind are likely to benefit the human race either privately or publicly.

**¶ Vital heat is nourished by moisture: When moisture is lacking, dissolution occurs: When it is excessive, suffocation occurs.**

**L**ife indeed consists in natural heat <term>natural heat</term> <note>original: *naturali calore*. In Galenic medicine, this is the innate warmth of a living being, which must be maintained for survival.</note>, much like a light. The food of this heat is an airy and fatty moisture, like oil in a lamp <term>moisture</term> <note>original: *humor*. Often called "radical moisture," this was thought to be the finite "fuel" one is born with; aging is the process of this oil being consumed by the flame of life.</note>. Therefore, if moisture of this kind should happen to fail, or if it exceeds too much, or becomes fouled, the heat is immediately weakened, and once weakened, it is eventually extinguished. 

If the heat is weakened and lost through a lack of moisture, death occurs by dissolution. If instead it is overwhelmed by an excess or vice of moisture, life perishes by suffocation. Suffocation indeed happens from the overflowing or putrefaction of any moisture—especially from phlegm <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: *pituita*. One of the four humors, associated with coldness and dampness; Ficino warns that its buildup can "drown" the internal fire.</note> either increasing or putrefying in some way...

<summary>Ficino restricts his health advice to the virtuous and intellectual elite, then introduces the "lamp metaphor" of life: natural heat (the flame) fueled by radical moisture (the oil).</summary>
<keywords>vital heat, radical moisture, phlegm, scholars, dissolution, suffocation, ethics of medicine, Galenic physiology</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues Book II of Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life," specifically Chapter 2, discussing the two ways the "lamp of life" can be extinguished: through drying up (dissolution) or being overwhelmed by heavy fluids (suffocation). It then transitions into Chapter 3.</meta>

...has been described: so that not undeservedly it might have been named a "life-seeking phlegm" <note>original: *pituita petens vitam*. Ficino uses this phrase to describe a specific kind of viscous, nourishing moisture that actively sustains the body's heat.</note>. Therefore, the precepts most necessary for prolonging life avoid dissolution <term>dissolution</term> <note>original: *resolutionem*. The process where the body’s heat evaporates its vital fluids, leading to old age and "drying out."</note> on one hand, and suffocation and putrefaction <term>suffocation and putrefaction</term> <note>original: *suffocationem putrefactionemque*. This occurs when moisture is too thick, cold, or stagnant, "drowning" the innate heat like too much green wood on a fire.</note> on the other, in equal measure. 

They avoid these by a common principle. For if physicians happen to be treating a person who is quite warm or dry, and whose passages <note>original: *meatus*. The pores or internal channels of the body.</note> are open, then the humors and the subtle spirits <term>spirits</term> <note>original: *spiritus*. In Renaissance medicine, these are extremely fine vapors that act as the bridge between the physical body and the soul.</note> are more prone to dissolution. But if they treat a body affected by the opposite condition, they provide help against suffocation. They focus especially on one or the other if the location or the season <margin>Location and time.</margin> inclines toward either extreme. 

¶ But when advice is given to the ingenious and the studious <margin>The nature of the studious.</margin>, both precepts are almost equally necessary, and effort must be made similarly on both. For in these men, a sharp and warm intellect and the constant movement of the imagination seem to threaten dissolution; yet the inactivity of the body and indigestion <note>original: *cruditas*. Literally "rawness," referring to undigested food that creates heavy, obstructive humors.</note> seem to threaten suffocation. Therefore, physicians never labor more intensely than in the treatment of men of this kind. 

And although the entire discussion of the previous book <note>Book I, *On Healthy Life*, which focuses on the specific ailments of scholars.</note> contributes greatly to prolonging life, yet such a great matter seems to demand a certain specific care of its own, which I shall hereafter cover in a few words as best I can.

### ¶ How heat must be tempered to moisture and vice versa, by a certain counsel of Minerva. Chapter III. <note>In Roman mythology, Minerva (Athena) is the goddess of wisdom and weaving. Ficino uses her name here to signify the "weaving" or balancing of the body's qualities.</note>

**M**oreover, while we treat this fatty oil—which is necessary for our fiery vigor—with a "thick Minerva" <note>original: *pingui Minerva*. A Latin idiom meaning "with plain common sense" or "without too much academic hair-splitting." Ficino is playing on the word *pingue* (fatty) used to describe the oil.</note>...

# ->ON THE LONG LIFE<-

...[we are able], Minerva, the olive-bearer <note>In classical mythology, Minerva (Athena) gifted the olive tree to humanity; Ficino uses her here as a symbol of the wisdom that provides the "oil" of life.</note> and the origin of the vital oil, born from the head of nourishing Jove, laughs at us. For while we clearly see the quantity of her gift, we do not sufficiently perceive its quality. Laughing, therefore, she says: "I have given you oil in abundance, not only as much as would be enough to nourish the flame, but I have also poured it into the lamp pure and without dregs <term>dregs</term> <note>original: *amurca*. This refers to the watery, bitter sediment found in unfiltered olive oil. In Ficino's medical theory, this represents the impurities in human humors that "clog" the vital heat.</note>." Thus she speaks.

But we have stumbled into error while speaking, for the lamp is a word unto our feet <note>This is a clever play on Psalm 119:105, "Thy word is a lamp unto my feet," redirected here to the physical "lamp" of the body.</note>, though its word has not yet been heard by us. From this lamp, therefore, let us learn that oil must be continually supplied to the flame, and indeed with such diligence that we neither overwhelm the light with a sudden flood, nor again withhold drink from the thirsty wick. These two matters, indeed, we believe have been sufficiently treated in the preceding pages. 

Two things remain for us: one of which we seem to have touched upon lightly, and the other we have scarcely reached at all. In touching upon them, let us entreat Minerva <note>original: *Palladem*. Pallas is another name for Minerva.</note> so that she, who is never accustomed to laugh, might laugh upon us. What then are they? First, we shall consider that the flame, even if very small, is entirely devouring <note>original: *edacem*. Meaning "greedy" or "consuming."</note>. Therefore, that lamp shines longer in which the small flame is so tempered to the wick <term>wick</term> <note>original: *ellychnium* or *lucinium*. The part of the lamp that holds the flame.</note> that it does not gulp down the oil, but rather sips it. 

And so, we also shall take care in our entire diet that the innate fire <term>innate fire</term> <note>original: *insitus ignis*. The "vital heat" centered in the heart which Renaissance doctors believed drove all biological processes.</note>—which is by its own nature voracious—does not grow too strong, especially in youth. It will be enough then to ward off both flooding moisture and penetrating cold. Next, we shall consider that the lamp is frequently extinguished when the oil poured into it is not pure, but (so to speak) full of dregs. From these dregs, a little while later, "mushrooms" <note>original: *fungi*. In the context of oil lamps, these are the carbon crusts or "snuffs" that build up on a wick and dim the flame. Ficino uses them as a metaphor for metabolic waste.</note> grow thick and extinguish the light.

<summary>Ficino uses the allegory of Minerva’s olive oil and a lamp to explain how the body’s innate heat must be carefully managed—neither drowned by fluids nor allowed to "gulp" its fuel too quickly.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the third chapter and begins the fourth chapter of Book II of Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life." Now indeed we have received from Pallas <note>Pallas is another name for Minerva, the goddess of wisdom. In Ficino’s medical allegory, she provides the "oil" of intellectual life.</note> the nourishing oil—namely an "airy" oil, which is most plentiful and pure, and held together by a certain natural viscosity that is both tenacious and firm. Therefore, that which is supplied to replace what is gradually consumed must not only be equal to this oil, but similar to it. For it to be truly similar, it must not only be airy and oily, but also thoroughly separated from the dregs <term>dregs</term> <note>original: *amurca*. This refers to the watery, bitter sediment found in unfiltered olive oil. In the body, it represents the "heavy" humors that cause sluggishness.</note>—that is, from a certain sediment accumulated from thicker earth and water. To avoid this congestion and these dregs, let us shun such heavy foods, as well as idleness, indigestion <note>original: *cruditatem*, meaning undigested food in the stomach which "chills" the vital heat.</note>, and impurities. Meanwhile, let us honor Minerva with such moderation that she may strengthen the head (from which she herself was born) for us, but not diminish our nerves and stomach. <margin>External [things] dry.</margin> <margin>indigestion</margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin>Secondly <unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin>For when</margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin>from these</margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin>

# ->FOR WHAT REASONS THE NATURAL MOISTURE DRIES UP; HOW FOREIGN MOISTURE OVERFLOWS; AND HOW NECESSARY A PERFECT DIGESTION IS FOR LIFE.<-
### ->Chapter 4.<-

**N**atural moisture is quickly dried out by these things: a too abundant flow of blood; a violent purging of the belly; a long-lasting looseness of the bowels; profuse sweating; excessively opened pores <note>original: *meatus*. Ficino believed that if the pores are too open, the vital spirits and moisture "leak" out of the body.</note>; sexual intercourse performed to the point of exhaustion; panting thirst; excruciating hunger; long sleeplessness; the use of things that are both hot and dry; laborious movement of the mind and body; anxiety; anger; and grief. Likewise, air that is both drier and hotter, especially when heated by fire, or a wind that is too arid, violent, and long-lasting. Conversely, the opposites of these things increase the moisture beyond measure.

<meta>This page continues Book II, "On Long Life," of Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life." Having previously established the allegory of "Minerva's oil" as the body’s vital moisture, Ficino now transitions into specific medical warnings regarding digestion and drunkenness, citing the authorities Galen and Avicenna.</meta>

# ON LONG LIFE.

Frequent drunkenness does both: for it dries out the drunkard with too much heat, and then suffocates him with [excessive] moisture. But nothing is more harmful in both respects than indigestion <note>original: *cruditas*, literally "rawness." In the Galenic medical tradition, digestion was viewed as a process of "cooking" food in the stomach; "crudity" is the state of food remaining raw and undigested.</note>. For when nourishment is not thoroughly digested, that which should refresh the natural moisture is lacking; meanwhile, what remains is putrefied, and its overflow overwhelms the natural heat. 

For this reason, Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose *Canon of Medicine* was the primary medical textbook in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> says that blood is corrupted whenever digestion itself is corrupted. And following Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician of antiquity, whose theories on humors formed the basis of Ficino's medical practice.</note>, he calls digestion the "root of life." Therefore, this rule of Galen is the best and almost unique: to prioritize the <term>concoction</term> <note>original: *concoctionem*. The internal physiological process of "ripening" or "cooking" food into blood and vital spirits through the body's natural heat.</note> of food above all else. For even the precept that seems most important—namely, to eat healthy foods—will be of no use unless you digest them. Indeed, a harmful humor flows from these healthy foods almost as much as from their opposites if they reach the limbs in a raw state. Even from foods less praised by medical art, less harm is often received for nutri... <note>The text cuts off mid-word at *nutritio* (nutrition) or *nutrimentum* (nourishment).</note>

<summary>Ficino emphasizes that proper digestion (concoction) is the "root of life," arguing that even healthy food is harmful if the stomach cannot "cook" it properly.</summary>
<keywords>digestion, concoction, Galen, Avicenna, drunkenness, crudity, vital heat, medical authority</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s Book II, "On Long Life," specifically focusing on the Galenic theory of the four stages of digestion and the importance of a healthy lifestyle including physical movement and light.</meta>

Furthermore, we must take the utmost care that we do not impede digestion through sexual intercourse immediately after eating, or through frequent and unnecessary naps at midday, or through staying awake late at night, or through any ill-timed labor of the mind or body, or by any other means. 

When I speak of digestion, I mean not only the first stage, which occurs in the stomach, but also the second, which occurs in the liver; the third, moreover, which takes place in the veins; and finally the fourth, which is completed in the limbs. These stages require a significant interval of time; if any of them is hindered, the <term>humor</term> <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow and black bile) which Renaissance medicine believed must be balanced to maintain health and provide "fodder" for the body's tissues.</note> fails to provide the necessary nourishment. 

Therefore, just as it is vital to aid digestion, it is especially necessary for life to assist in the purging of wastes. It is also necessary to wipe away dirt from the skin. Physical movement of the body is just as necessary—a movement that is as continuous, tempered, and varied as the motions of the heavens, the air, fire, and water—provided that the requirements for <term>concoction</term> <note>original: *percoctionis*. The internal physiological process of "ripening" or "cooking" food into blood and vital spirits through the body's natural heat.</note> and sleep are respected. One should, however, avoid total exhaustion and the weakening of the body. 

Furthermore, we grow moldy and are covered with decay in the shadows; it is under the open sky and in the light that we truly live. This was a saying frequently on the lips of my father, Ficino <note>Diotifeci d'Agnolo (d. 1478), personal physician to Cosimo de' Medici and Marsilio’s primary medical influence.</note>, a distinguished physician. 

But to achieve these things successfully, it would be worthwhile to have accustomed the body from a tender age not so much to city business but to certain rural exercises, and even to varied types of nourishment and a diverse lifestyle. This is what that wise man <margin><unclear>...</unclear></margin> [my father] often advised me. For he who in every stage of life...

<summary>Ficino explains the four stages of digestion and emphasizes that health requires not just good food, but movement, hygiene, and exposure to light. He reflects on the advice of his father, a prominent physician, regarding a rural and active lifestyle.</summary>

<keywords>four stages of digestion, liver, veins, physical exercise, hygiene, sunlight, Diotifeci d'Agnolo, rural life vs. city life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s Book II of "On Long Life." The previous page ended with a discussion on the importance of light and movement, concluding with a personal anecdote about his father’s preference for the open sky.</meta>

# ON THE LONG LIFE

Those who live with a certain extreme curiosity <note>original: *curiositate*. In this context, Ficino likely refers to the "curiosity" of scholars whose intense mental focus can distract them from physical safety and health.</note> often live less safely. But those who did not accustom themselves to these healthy habits in their youth should at least do so as adults, though they must attempt this transition by more cautious steps.

Blood and the <term>humor</term> <note>The vital fluids of the body which must be kept in balance to ensure health and longevity.</note> suited for life ought to be airy in its tempered quality; in its substance, it should be middling and tenacious.

### Chapter V

<margin>All the Greeks, among the precepts most necessary for a long life, command that we be nourished by *euchymia*.</margin>

We use the term <term>euchymia</term> <note>original: *euchimia*. From the Greek *euchymos*, meaning "producing good juices" or healthy humors, particularly regarding digestion.</note> to describe healthy nourishment that brings about good nutrition—that is, good blood. Indeed, we call "good blood" that which is neither cold, nor dry, nor turbulent, but rather warm, moist, and clear. It should be warm, yet with a heat that is not sharp or biting. It should be moist, but with a moisture that is not watery. It should also be clear, yet not so thin as to be weak. 

Indeed, blood that is too boiling both sharpens the natural heat and dries out the body's moisture; any moisture it does introduce, or any heat it provides, is unstable and fleeting. Furthermore, blood that is too moist and prone to wateriness blunts the natural heat; it either dulls the natural humor or compels it to melt away under heat. In doing so, it suffocates the heat with excessive moisture. In every case, if any portion of the natural humor is drawn from such watery blood, it then putrefies easily and quickly flows away and dissolves. 

This is why those who eat softer fruits and herbs—unless they do so rarely or perhaps as medicine to soften the bowels—soon fill their veins entirely with a raw <term>juice</term> <note>original: *succus*. Referring to the chyle or partially digested food matter before it is fully converted into blood.</note> that is prone to decay. And lest this should happen, it is safer to choose, instead of such food...

<summary>Ficino explains the importance of 'euchymia' (good digestive juices) and defines 'good blood' as being warm, moist, and clear, while warning that watery or boiling blood leads to decay.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s "On Long Life," the second book of his "Three Books on Life." Following the definition of *euchymia* (good blood) on the previous page, Ficino now details the ideal elemental composition of the blood and the dangers of extremes in its consistency.</meta>

...are cooked, or at least mixed with bread. Therefore, let the blood be neither fiery nor watery, but airy <note>original: *aerius*. In the system of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water), air was considered warm and moist, making it the ideal state for the blood that sustains life.</note>; not like the thicker air, lest it be too prone to turn into water, nor like the most subtle air, lest it easily flare up into fire. Instead, it should maintain a middle substance in which middle air holds the greatest dominion. Let other nutrients be present only insofar as they accommodate themselves to the rule of air. 

Its substance should not be extremely thin, lest it generate an unstable humor and a volatile <term>spirit</term> <note>The "spirit" (*spiritus*) was understood as a subtle, vaporous substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the soul, carrying out vital functions.</note> subject to dissolution. Nor should it be extremely thick; otherwise, it will not serve the intellect <note>original: *ingenio*. Ficino believed that the quality of one's blood directly influenced their mental clarity and creative capacity.</note> in the least, and it will barely be transformed into the natural humor and spirit. It will obstruct the passages and provide an occasion for suffocation. And the spirit that is eventually created from such thickness is itself, by its own density, unfit for life; it smothers the <term>natural heat</term> <note>The internal "fire" of the body that drives metabolism and digestion.</note> just as dense smoke immediately compresses and extinguishes a flame. I will omit the fact that such blood is so dark and gloomy that it makes life sorrowful—a state worse than death. 

<margin>Rhazes [the Persian physician] and others discuss the blood and its substances, cautioning against extremes in density.</margin>

But above all, as it seems to me, it is beneficial for a long life to have blood that—along with a substance that is indeed very airy and not too thick—possesses within itself some glutinous and tenacious humor. This is similar to the quality found in olive oil (which combines thinness with richness), or the moisture of a fat yet thin eel, or oil drawn from turpentine through a certain distillation <note>original: *sublimatione*. In Renaissance medicine, this refers to the process of purifying or extracting the "essence" of a substance through heat.</note>. Therefore, you [should choose] all other foods and things which...

<summary>Ficino explains that blood must maintain an "airy" consistency—neither too thin to be unstable nor too thick to be gloomy—and suggests that the secret to longevity lies in blood that is both light and "tenacious" like olive oil.</summary>
<keywords>blood consistency, four elements, air, spirit, natural heat, melancholy, olive oil, Rhazes, longevity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s Book II, "On Long Life." Having previously established that ideal blood should be "airy"—balanced between the extremes of watery and fiery—Ficino now transitions into practical advice on how to maintain this balance through diet and lifestyle, citing the great medical authorities Rhazes and Avicenna.</meta>

# ON LONG LIFE

...choose diligently those things which, according to their powers, produce such blood and humor. For such blood and humor is food for the **vital heat**, just as oil is for a flame; it possesses stability alongside its subtlety. 

The precept of **Rhazes** <note>original: *Rasis*. Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925) was a Persian polymath and one of the most influential physicians in the Islamic Golden Age and medieval Europe.</note> for preserving youth is to use things that draw the blood toward the **precordia** <note>The region of the body around the heart and the diaphragm, considered the seat of vital energy.</note>, condensing it there and warming the heart. **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), another monumental Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> approves of this, teaching that watery and unstable blood must be avoided. 

However, in this matter, one must account for the differences between individual bodies. Where a body is naturally denser, one should lean toward remedies that thin the blood; where a body is more "rare" <note>original: *rarius*. This refers to a porous or thin body constitution that allows heat and spirits to escape too easily.</note> or porous, one should lean toward thickening the blood. Where the body's constitution is moderate, we safely travel a **middle path**. Yet, we should never attempt to uproot the natural constitution of the body; otherwise, we would uproot life itself. 

Furthermore, it is useful to remember that if we greatly fear the thinness of the blood, and the stomach is not particularly strong by nature, we must approach the thickening of the blood by very small degrees. While we strive to nourish a thin man with "thicker" (heavier) foods, we must simultaneously warm the stomach internally and induce sleep. **Physical exercise** should be performed according to one's strength, but **mental activity**—which very often harms a great many people—should be decreased. 

If the stomach cannot bear foods that are too viscous, hard, or excessively cold, one should at least make use of: 
*   **corals**
*   **sandalwood**
*   **roses**
*   **coriander**
*   **myrobalans** <note>A plum-like fruit highly valued in medieval pharmacology for its supposed ability to purge "black bile" and strengthen the stomach.</note>
*   **quinces** <note>original: *cidioniorum*</note> 

and so on.

<summary>Ficino provides practical dietary and lifestyle advice for maintaining healthy blood, emphasizing the balance between "thin" and "thick" blood while citing the authorities Rhazes and Avicenna.</summary>
