# ON LONG LIFE

...strong, then with **quince marmalade** <note>original: *diacidoniō*. A thick, medicinal jelly made from quinces, highly valued in the Renaissance for its ability to strengthen the stomach and "bind" the humors.</note>, **rose sugar**, and astringent things, we finally achieve that firmness of the blood and humor which we cannot safely achieve here with overly sticky things. 

Most conveniently, however, those who cannot digest the viscous limbs of larger animals will be nourished by **pine nuts** and **pistachios**, with juice of **licorice** and **starch**, adding **sweet almonds**, their oil, **quince seed**, and **sesame oil**, along with the whitest **sugar** and **rose water**. We will further allow these people the extremities <note>such as the feet or wing-tips, which were considered lighter and less "fleshy."</note> of hens or kids <note>young goats</note>, as well as turtles, snails, and testicles <note>In the medical tradition of the time, these were often prescribed to restore vitality and "firm up" the body's substance.</note>. 

We shall give them wines that are not white, but red and **astringent** <note>original: *stiptica*. These are "binding" wines that cause a puckering sensation in the mouth, thought to prevent the humors from becoming too thin or escaping the body.</note>, and somewhat slightly bitter. Tempering the wine with **iron-water** <note>Water in which hot iron has been quenched, believed to absorb the "strength" of the metal.</note> or **mastic-water**, we shall also lightly soothe the skin with oil of **mastic** and **quinces**. Meanwhile, we shall forbid those things that make the blood thin or boiling, unless perhaps we infuse a bit of **saffron** or **cinnamon** into the more **tenacious** <note>heavy or sticky</note> meals; this ensures they are both more easily digested and, once digested, transferred through narrow passages to the limbs. 

For it is difficult to lead viscous or slightly firmer foods from a stomach that is not very strong all the way to the **third and fourth digestion** <note>According to Galenic medicine, the first digestion happens in the stomach, the second in the liver, the third in the vessels, and the fourth in the individual tissues as they turn food into their own substance.</note>, unless they are carried along by such **"vehicles"** <note>original: *uehiculis*. These are aromatic substances like saffron that act as "drivers," helping the nutrients move through the body's internal channels.</note> and, moreover, encouraged by light rubbings. When you perform these rubbings, do so with softer hands, and remember to moisten the hands with some fragrant wi[ne]...

# ON LONG LIFE

...[infu]se, in which you have cooked **chamomile**, **myrtle**, and **roses**.

-> **Chapter VI. The general rule for eating and drinking, and the quality of meals.** <-

**B**ut for the time being, let us set aside those human bodies that are either too dense or too thin. Let us arrive at a general way of living, suited to the common and moderate constitution of the body. Take care that by no means the passages of the body <note>original: *meatus*. These are the pores and internal channels through which the humors and "vital spirits" circulate.</note> are either opened beyond measure or too much obstructed. For in the former case, there is a risk of dissolution and injury brought in from the outside; in the latter, however, there is danger in putrefaction <note>The stagnation and "rotting" of humors within the body, which was believed to be a primary cause of disease and aging.</note> and suffocation. While I do not restrain you with the tightest bit of a rule—a thing which **Hippocrates** <note>The "Father of Medicine" (c. 460 – c. 370 BCE). Ficino refers to the Hippocratic idea that overly rigid health regimens can be dangerous because they leave no room for the body to adapt to changes.</note> condemns—neither do I slacken the reins to the point of license.

Take moist **herbs** and **fruits** sparingly. Take **milk** and **fish** even more sparingly, and both of these with **honey**. Take **mushrooms** most sparingly of all, and then only with **aromatics** and **pear seeds**. Similarly, drink pure water only with great moderation. 

Season foods that are moister or fatty with sharp, aromatic spices; otherwise, they introduce a great deal of foreign and putrid humor into the limbs. Even if they provide some humor necessary to one's nature, they offer it in a form subject to very rapid corruption. This is no different from how watery wine is quickly disturbed and spoiled. From this comes rapid graying of the hair <note>original: *canie*. In Renaissance medicine, "watery" or "phlegmatic" humors were thought to "drown" the natural heat of the body, leading to premature aging and white hair.</note>...

<summary>Ficino moves from specific medicinal preparations to general dietary rules, advising a "middle path" that avoids both excessive blockage and excessive openness of the body's pores. He warns against moist, fatty, or "watery" foods that lead to the corruption of the body's internal fluids and premature aging.</summary>

<keywords>dietary rules, moderation, humors, putrefaction, Hippocrates, gray hair, digestion, aromatics</keywords>

[appearan]ces, and a pallor and senile wrinkling. Meats also, if they are eaten daily, and especially if they approach the weight of bread [consumed in the same meal], bring about a quick putrefaction <note>In Galenic medicine, putrefaction is the "rotting" of humors caused by excess heat and moisture, leading to disease and decay.</note>. For this reason **Porphyry** <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234 – c. 305 CE), a Neoplatonist philosopher who advocated for vegetarianism in his work "On Abstinence from Animal Food."</note>, relying on the authority of the Pythagoreans and the ancients, detests the eating of animals. Have we not heard that the long-lived men before the Deluge <note>The Great Flood of Noah; in the Bible, humans were said to live for hundreds of years before this event.</note> spared the animals? Although physicians do not forbid the use of meat as much as its abuse. Finally, flee moist things as they are liable to putrefaction, keeping in memory that the moist and the fat grow old more quickly and die. Which **Hippocrates** <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Medicine."</note> also says, and reality itself declares. On the other hand, take very dry things in moderation, or at least temper them with a more liberal amount of drink. Safely choose the middle path. 

Even if **Avicenna** <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian "Prince of Physicians" whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> prefers a form of food that is a little drier to one that is soft in order to avoid graying hair. Be most cautious regarding things that are excessively cold or hot. Follow those that are both warm and moist. If the air is boiling hot, let the moisture of the feast overcome the heat. If it is cold, let the heat overcome the moisture. But in both cases, let the excess be moderate. Everywhere, let the heat and moisture possess something somewhat <term>glutinous</term> <note>Meaning sticky or viscous; intended to help the bodily humors "stick" to the organs rather than evaporating or flowing away.</note> and <term>styptic</term> <note>A substance that contracts or binds tissues. In this context, it refers to foods that "firm up" the body's flesh.</note>, so that the moisture distributed to the limbs may adhere more firmly and endure for a long time under the heat. Choice **wheat** and choice **bread** possess this [quality] above all. Next, a **red and styptic wine** that is slightly sweet. Third, **pine nuts** and things very similar to these in temperament and stickiness. Fourth, meats that are not moist and loose at the same time, such as **suckling pork** and **lamb**. ...matters; **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (c. 129–216 CE) was the most influential physician of the Roman Empire; his theories on the four humors dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years.</note> especially recommends meat and blood to his followers because of a certain similarity they have with our own bodies. Therefore, they are best suited for similar bodies, such as those of peasants and robust men who exercise their bodies a great deal—especially if the meats are preserved for four days with cloves and prepared coriander, and seasoned with salt. And blood is perhaps useful if it is cooked with sugar and has been thoroughly purified and made liquid. 

But to return to the list, we do not approve of very moist meats, such as those we mentioned before; nor those that are both hard and dry, such as the meat of an older hare or ox. Instead, one should choose certain "middle" meats: such as those of roosters, chickens, capons, peacocks, pheasants, partridges, and perhaps also young pigeons, especially domestic ones. Of such a kind also are young roe deer and calves, as well as year-old rams and wild boars. I do not despise suckling kids and fresh cheese. 

I have indeed passed over small birds; for the frequent use of very fine foods results in a stomach that can no longer tolerate coarser ones. He who is stronger, however, carries away from these finer foods a fleeting vapor or moisture. Nevertheless, I do not omit the eggs of hens, provided the yolk is eaten together with the white. For the yolk alone is the nourishment of the delicate; indeed, **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian "Prince of Physicians."</note> proves that no nourishment is more beneficial for the depletion of blood and the "dissolution of the cardiac spirit" <note>original: "spiritus cardiaci." In Renaissance medicine, the "spirit" was a subtle substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the soul; the cardiac spirit was produced in the heart to provide vital heat and life.</note> than the yolk of a hen's egg, or that of a partridge or pheasant. Nor, perhaps, would it be beside the point to feed geese...

...spelt or clear water; and afterward, let the meat be seasoned with salt and coriander prepared with vinegar, and kept for seven days before you eat it. Treat stag <note>original: *cervum*; venison or deer</note> in a similar fashion, provided that the stomach is very strong. For it is probable that certain long-lived animals contribute to a long life—provided, however, that young people eat meat of this kind. Similarly, other meats should be consumed in turn, sometimes roasted and sometimes boiled. 

Food should be double the amount of drink. Bread should be double [the drink], and in a ratio of one and a half <note>original: *sesquialter*; a ratio of 3:2. The OCR "sex galter" is a transcription error for this mathematical term.</note> to eggs; triple the amount of meats; and quadruple the amount of fish, herbs, and more moist fruits. Let the meal not begin with a drink, nor should any single drink be too abundant. Something styptic <note>original: *stypticum*; an astringent substance that "closes" or "binds" the stomach, such as a tart pear or quince, intended to aid digestion by keeping the stomach's heat contained.</note> should always follow the meal without drink, or with very little. 

When the complexion <note>The "complexion" refers to an individual's unique balance of the four humors: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note>, age, place, or season leans toward the hot or the dry, you too should decline for a little while toward their opposites. When it leans toward the cold or the moist, turn similarly toward the opposites. Where there is a temperate balance, preserve that balance. 

To this extent, exercise of the body must be increased and that of the mind decreased, specifically when we are eating the harder feasts that are perhaps sometimes necessary for the longevity of life. Let there be two meals for you within nine hours of the day, and be sparing in both; let the dinner <note>original: *coena*; the second, later meal of the day.</note> be the more sparing of the two. Let there be roughly two physical exercises after the first digestion, continued until a sweat is produced. Indeed, sleep at night is always good because it is always necessary. But sleep during the day is never good, unless it is absolutely necessary.

# ON LONGEVITY.

Do not use foods that quickly spoil <note>original: *putrefcũt*; to rot or putrefy. In the medical theory of the time, "putrefaction" was the process by which the body's vital fluids were corrupted, leading to disease and aging.</note>; nor live in such regions. Choose wine and wheat above all others: avoid decay and dissolution. 

## Chapter VII.

**A**nimals, indeed, whichever come into our care, must be nourished with clean and select foods before we eat them. And these and all other things are to be chosen from higher and fragrant pastures. Among these, always keep in mind a rule approved above all others by the philosopher Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician, alchemist, and author of influential works on health and longevity.</note>. One ought to choose living creatures <note>original: *Aiantes*, likely a contraction of *Animantes*</note>, herbs, fruits, grains, and wines from high and fragrant regions, as we have said: which temperate winds clear, and sweet rays of the sun cherish; where no waters stagnate; where cultivated lands are not made fertile with dung <note>original: *sterq̃linio*; a manure heap. The author suggests that "natural moisture" is superior to animal waste for growing healthy food.</note>, but by natural moisture. Where also whatever things grow remain uncorrupted for a long time. 

In such a place only must one live; these things also must be eaten. Nor should it be trusted that from foods which quickly spoil, we will obtain a lasting moisture <note>original: *humoreʒ*; specifically "radical moisture," the innate fluid believed to be the fuel for the "lamp of life."</note> that is far removed from decay. Nor should it be hoped that we will easily live long where the fruits of the earth are not preserved uncorrupted for a long time, or where men of long life are very rare. 

Indeed, the peach <note>original: *pomum perſicũ*; literally "the Persian apple."</note> demonstrates how great a difference there is in location and diet: in Persia it is indeed a poison, but in Egypt it is a friend to the heart. And hellebore <note>A medicinal plant used in antiquity to treat mental illness, though highly toxic if prepared incorrectly.</note> in Anticyra <note>A Greek city-state famous for the high quality of its hellebore.</note> is taken without harm; elsewhere, however, it is a poison. Aristotle [says] that habitation...

One chooses high land looking toward the south and the east, under a refined air <note>original: *aere subtili*; "subtle air" refers to air that is thin, clear, and free from the heavy, moist vapors thought to cause disease.</note> that is neither humid nor cold. And Plato found long-lived people in the highest and most temperate regions. Truly, the worst thing is to fertilize the fields with dung, or to fail to drain stagnant waters away from the fields. For everything born in such places is subject to quick corruption. Therefore, I cannot help but blame those who criticize the wise Hesiod <note>Hesiod (c. 700 BCE) was an ancient Greek poet; his work *Works and Days* provides early instructions on agriculture.</note> because he omitted the use of the manure-heap in his writings on rustic matters. But he, being prudent, looked more to healthfulness than to fertility. He believed that a field could be sufficiently enriched by the timely plowing-under of the leaves of lupines and beans.

But if we are compelled to inhabit more humid and unclean regions, and to consume food that will last very little time: let us keep to that diet which doctors have commanded for use under pestilential air. Regarding this matter, we have dealt with it sufficiently in our book against the plague <note>Likely referring to Ficino’s *Consilio contro la pestilenza* (1481), written during an outbreak in Florence.</note>. In short, we shall use sweet and somewhat warm scents. With aloe properly prepared, we shall often and lightly be purged. We say it is "properly prepared" if it has been washed in rose water, or in the juice of roses, or if it has been perfectly mixed with crushed fresh roses. Then let myrobalan <note>A dried, plum-like fruit used in Ayurvedic and medieval medicine to "purge" specific humors from the body.</note> be added to it, along with mastic <note>A resin from the mastic tree, used to strengthen the stomach and sweeten the breath.</note>, and perhaps rose.

This medicine is, beyond controversy, wonderful for preserving for a long time a healthy mind in a healthy body <note>original: *mentē ſanā in corpore ſano*; a famous quote from the Roman poet Juvenal, emphasizing that physical health and mental clarity are intertwined.</note>. Furthermore, we shall exercise the body. We shall use a suitable hearth-fire. We shall season our banquets with this powder, of myrobalan—

# ON LONG LIFE.

let there be a fourth part of an ounce of emblic myrobalans <note>original: *lanorũ emblicarũ* (myrobalanorum emblicarum); the Emblic myrobalan or Indian gooseberry was highly prized in Galenic and Ayurvedic medicine for its cooling and restorative properties.</note>; of sandalwood <margin><unclear>marginal note</unclear></margin> indeed a half [ounce]; a whole [ounce] of cinnamon; and an eighth of saffron. But where the opposite is true, we should conversely use aromatic and somewhat harsh seasonings (as we have said), and similar scents at any time to prevent putrefaction. Anointing oneself with oil prevents the injury of cold. Using baths of water and oil prevents the resolution [weakness] threatening from labor or hot seasons. Similarly, wash the mouth often with water; keep licorice juice or crystalline sugar in the mouth. Douse the hands and face with much rose water and a little rose vinegar; and use similar scents. Every seventh hour, be refreshed with moderate food; grow quiet in both body and mind, avoiding heat. Truly, it matters most what sort of wine and wheat we consume regularly. Therefore, let these be of such a quality that they remain whole beyond a year, and preferably up to three years; for only then can we hope for incorruptible nourishment from them. Whether the wine be white or red, let it be clear, sweet, astringent <note>original: *stipticum*; a "styptic" or tannic quality was thought to strengthen the stomach and prevent the humors from flowing too loosely.</note>, fragrant, and of the sort that requires being mixed with water. Unless, by chance, you find a wine that is both light and durable, which is usually very rare.

<summary>This page provides a specific medicinal recipe of spices and myrobalans to prevent bodily decay, alongside hygiene advice such as rose water washes and guidelines for selecting long-lasting wheat and wine.</summary>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on diet and hygiene for longevity, specifically transitioning from external applications like rose water to the internal use of wine as a medicinal tool for balancing the bodily humors.</meta>

But what is even more powerful: the philosopher Isaac <note>original: *Iſaach*; likely referring to Isaac Judaeus, a renowned 10th-century physician and philosopher whose works on diet and fevers were central to medieval and Renaissance medicine.</note> says that wine is a "winy" substance cooked by the sun and purified by the winds. He commands that it be tempered <note>Mixed or diluted.</note> with pure spring water some time before we drink it, so that it may be more perfectly blended. However, he warns that watery, weak, or bitter wine must be avoided, as it quickly becomes acidic within the veins and limbs, or otherwise putrefies. Indeed, while watery wine is prone to putrefaction, if its substance is preserved and it is boiled, it will at least be useful; otherwise, it is not praised, because it will create corruptible humor. But its sharpness <note>original: *acumen*</note> must be tempered with choice water. Truly, the kind of wine we have approved, Isaac says—following the opinion of the ancients—is very similar to the "great theriacs" <note>original: *tiriacis magnis*; Theriac was a complex medical concoction used as a universal antidote and restorative, often containing dozens of ingredients.</note>. As we have said, when properly tempered, it warms a cold bodily temperament, cools a heated one, moistens a dry one, and dries out a moist one; and (as Galen <note>A Greek physician whose theories of the four humors dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> says) it restores the natural humor, fosters internal heat, and balances both. Mixing such wine with water is more necessary for the young, less for the old, and for cold old men, not at all. For cold and hard old age (as Plato says) is thus heated and softened by pure, undiluted wine, just as iron is by fire or a lupin bean is by water. Know also that what we said regarding how wine can achieve opposite effects and temper disagreements in the body can also be achieved through licorice, though more weakly. It can also be achieved through oil...

# ON LONG LIFE

...rose-water: but only externally. Therefore, let these things be familiar to you. Nor should you doubt that whatever is balanced in quality and potent in virtue can balance the rest: just as cold makes other things cold. This should primarily possess the temperament of Jupiter <note>In medical astrology, Jupiter represents a "temperate" influence—warm and moist—which was considered the most life-sustaining and healthful state.</note>, by which means they are also most healthful. But these matters must be discussed elsewhere.

## Diet: Lifestyle: Medicine for the Elderly. Chapter VIII.

Those who have already completed their seventh cycle of seven years <note>original: *septimum septenarium*; a "climacteric" period of 49 years.</note>, approaching their fiftieth year, should consider that while Venus signifies the young, Saturn signifies the old; and among astronomers, these stars are known to be the greatest enemies to one another. Therefore, those of a Saturnine age <note>The elderly, who were thought to be ruled by the planet Saturn, which is cold and dry.</note> should flee from sexual matters <note>original: *Rem Veneream*; literally "the matter of Venus."</note>, for even in youth it withdraws much from one's life. Indeed, Nature does not look out for those already born, but for those yet to be born; for even the herbs themselves are dried out as soon as their seed is produced.

Furthermore, they should consider cold and the night air to be deadly <note>original: *letiferum*; literally "death-bearing."</note> to them. They should maintain a diet from which they may hope for the greatest amount of blood and as much spirit as possible <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirit" (*spiritus*) was a subtle vapor produced from the blood that animated the body and linked it to the soul.</note>: specifically from fresh egg yolks and wine that is somewhat sweet, but especially highly fragrant. 

For the yolk specifically restores the blood of the heart, while wine especially restores the spirit. In summary, they should use only the choicest meats that are easiest to cook, following a diet that increases heat and moisture <note>The "humors" or qualities associated with youth; aging was viewed as a process of growing cold and dry.</note> in equal measure. They should continually restore their spirit with scents, especially those of wine. They should avoid wakefulness <note>Prolonged lack of sleep or insomnia.</note>, lack of food, and thirst...

<summary>This page advises those reaching age fifty to adopt a lifestyle governed by the temperate nature of Jupiter, specifically avoiding sex, cold air, and fasting. It emphasizes a diet of egg yolks and fragrant wine to replenish the "spirit" and blood, combating the cold, dry influence of Saturn.</summary>
<keywords>geriatrics, medical astrology, Saturn, Venus, Jupiter, spiritus, humoral theory, dietetics, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

...[they should avoid] the labor of both body and mind, as well as solitude and sorrow. They should take up music again if they have perhaps stopped it; it should never be interrupted. They should recall certain games and habits from their past childhood, as far as is fitting. For it is very difficult (if I may say so) to rejuvenate in body unless you first become a child again in spirit and disposition <note>original: *repuerascas*; literally "to become a boy again." Ficino suggests that psychological youthfulness is a prerequisite for physical health.</note>. Therefore, at every age, it is greatly conducive to life to retain something of childhood and to always seek out various delights. 

However, long and profuse laughter is advised least of all; for it dilates the spirit too much toward external things <note>In the medical theory of the time, "spirit" (*spiritus*) was a subtle vapor that animated the body. Excessive laughter was thought to cause this vital vapor to "leak" out or dissipate through the pores, weakening the heart.</note>. But let us return to the elderly. Furthermore, if they feel cold, they should seek aromatic poultices that are both warm and moist. Let them remember that it is not a "childish" thing, that famous "childish" remedy of Avicenna <note>Avicenna was a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age; his *Canon of Medicine* was a standard European textbook for centuries.</note>: it was indeed first practiced by David <note>A reference to the biblical King David in 1 Kings 1:1-4, where a young woman, Abishag, was brought to lie with the elderly king to provide him with warmth; here interpreted as a medical "fomentation" or heat-treatment.</note>, though perhaps practiced too late.

A wonderful poultice for an old man: The crumb <note>original: *medulla*; literally "marrow," meaning the soft inner part of the loaf.</note> of bread, still fresh and warm, soaked in Malmsey wine <note>original: *maluatico uino*; Malvasia, a sweet Mediterranean wine prized for its "warming" properties.</note> with powder of mint; applied to the stomach and often held to the nose to be smelled. # ON LONG LIFE.

And at the same time, what is wonderful is that while it increases natural moisture <note>In Galenic medicine, aging was seen as a process of drying out and cooling down. "Natural moisture" (*humor radicalis*) was the vital fluid that sustained life.</note>, it meanwhile dries out the superfluous and purges the putrid moisture. There are those who provide one drachm <note>A unit of weight, roughly 3.88 grams.</note> of such kernels <note>Likely referring to pine nuts, mentioned in the preceding context of the original manuscript.</note> daily after meals to the elderly to be eaten. I would also provide another drachm on an empty stomach to the elderly: either fresh, warm, gilded <note>Covered in gold leaf; Ficino believed gold possessed Solar properties that could preserve the human spirit from decay.</note>, or prepared with pine nuts. I would also compose an electuary <note>A medicinal paste or medicinal conserve made by mixing powders with syrup, honey, or sugar.</note> in this manner:

Take four ounces of blanched sweet almonds; as much of those same kernels; two ounces of pistachios; one of cucumber seeds; one of blanched hazelnuts. Crush them and cook them together with the whitest sugar; to which, however, you shall add one drachm of fresh candied ginger, half a drachm of saffron, a third of a drachm of musk <note>An aromatic secretion from the musk deer, highly valued for its "spirituous" and warming qualities.</note>, and as much ambergris <note>original: *ambrae*; a rare, fragrant substance produced in the digestive system of sperm whales, used as a fixative and medicine.</note>. Melt the sugar in water of melissa—that is, citron-balm—and roses. Apply many leaves of gold to these.

For by the daily use of this, the elderly will attain a stronger and longer life. They can take this at the table, and also several hours before the meal. However, it will be more useful then if they drink some white and fragrant wine along with such a confection. 

Truly, in warmer seasons, rose sugar together with gold leaves and candied myrobalans <note>A bitter, plum-like fruit used in medieval medicine to purge "black bile" or melancholy.</note> will prolong life for the elderly. No one doubts that theriac <note>original: *theriacam*; a famous, ancient "universal" medicine composed of dozens of ingredients, including viper's flesh and opium, used here to balance the body's humors.</note> is suitable for the same purpose for "moist" persons and seasons. Concerning the use of which enough has been said in the previous book. No one will also deny to these...

[red pilcrow] Rhazes <note>Al-Razi (865–925 AD), a Persian physician whose medical texts were foundational in European universities.</note> strongly approves of Tryphera <note>original: *Triferaʒ*; a medicinal confection or electuary, usually containing myrobalans</note> made from Indian, Emblic, and Belleric myrobalans <term>A type of plum-like fruit from India, highly prized in medieval medicine for purging "black bile" and preserving youth</term>. He orders one to chew myrobalans daily—especially Chebulic ones <note>A specific variety of myrobalan used to treat "cold" humors</note> properly preserved—to postpone the discomforts of old age.

[red bracket] Concerning gold and golden foods and the restoration of the elderly. Chapter 10.

Gold is approved by everyone above all things, as it is the most temperate of all things and the most safe from corruption. It is consecrated to the Sun on account of its splendor, and to Jupiter on account of its temperance <note>In medical astrology, the Sun and Jupiter are "benefic" influences that promote life and health</note>: and therefore it is able to wonderfully temper the natural heat with the moisture: and to preserve the humors from corruption. It is able to bring Solar and Jovial virtue into the spirits and limbs. But nevertheless...

They desire to make the very hard substance of gold more subtle <note>In Renaissance alchemy, "subtle" refers to a substance refined into a state where it can easily penetrate and be absorbed by the body's tissues.</note>, and very easy to penetrate. For they know that cordials <note>heart-strengthening medicines</note> most effectively refresh the hidden power of the heart when nature labors the least in drawing them in. To ensure that nature is fatigued as little as possible, these medicines must be offered either already rendered very subtle, or along with other very subtle substances. They think it would be best if potable gold <term>potable gold: *aurum potabile*; a legendary drinkable form of gold believed by alchemists to grant eternal youth and cure all diseases</term> could be made without any foreign mixture. But if that is not possible, they want it to be taken crushed and reduced to leaves.

Temper the golden wine with the same water; and together with a drink of this kind, eat a fresh egg yolk. # On the use of milk and human blood for the life of the elderly. Chapter XI.

**S**often immediately after the tenth, and sometimes even after the ninth septenary <note>A "septenary" is a seven-year cycle. The author is referring to the ages of 63 or 70, which were considered "climacteric" or dangerous turning points in a person's life.</note>, the **human tree** gradually withers as its moisture dries up. Then, for the first time, this human tree must be irrigated with a human and youthful liquid so that it may grow green again. Choose, therefore, a healthy girl—beautiful, cheerful, and temperate—and when you are hungry, suck her milk while the moon is waxing; immediately afterward, eat a small amount of sweet fennel powder properly prepared with sugar. The sugar will not allow the milk to curdle or putrefy in the stomach, while the fennel, since it is both subtle <note>In this context, "subtle" means a substance refined enough to pass easily through the body's smallest channels.</note> and a "friend" to milk, will distribute it to the limbs. 

Those whom senile exhaustion <term>hectica senilis: a chronic wasting condition or "fever" of old age caused by the loss of the body's natural fluids</term> overcomes, diligent physicians strive to refresh with the liquid of human blood that has been distilled by fire through a sublime art <note>This refers to alchemical distillation, intended to extract the "spirit" or essence of the blood while removing its grosser, heavier qualities.</note>. Why, then, should anything prevent us from sometimes refreshing those already nearly worn out by old age with this drink as well? A certain common and true...

[Commu]nus <note>The text begins with a fragment of the word "communis," meaning "common."</note> opinion is that certain old hags known as witches—who are also commonly called *striges* <note>original: "striges" - In Roman folklore, these were blood-drinking screech-owls or bird-like witches who preyed on infants. The author is drawing a parallel between folk magic and his proposed medical treatment.</note>—suck the blood of infants so that they may grow young again according to their strength. Why, then, should our elderly, who are clearly destitute of all other help, not suck the blood of a youth? I mean a youth who is willing, healthy, happy, and temperate <note>In Renaissance medicine, "temperate" meant having a perfect balance of the four humors: blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile.</note>; one whose blood is indeed of the best quality, but perhaps exists in excess. 

They shall suck it, therefore, in the manner of leeches, from a barely opened vein in the left arm, taking only one or two ounces. <margin>... [text cut off]</margin> Immediately afterward, they should take an equal amount of sugar and wine; they should do this while hungry and thirsty during a waxing moon. If the blood is raw and difficult to digest, let it first be cooked together with sugar; or let it be moderately distilled over hot water once mixed with sugar. Then let it be drunk. 

It is also a present help at that time to warm the stomach with pig’s blood. Let a sponge, soaked in warm wine, soak up this blood as it flows from the pig's vein, and let it be applied immediately while warm to the stomach. 

Galen and Serapio <note>Galen was a foundational 2nd-century Greek physician. "Serapio" likely refers to Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century Arabic medical author whose works were popular in the West.</note> say that the bite of a rabid dog is cured by drinking dog's blood. However, it did not please them to assign a reason for this. I, therefore, having searched for a reason for two days, finally conclude that the venomous saliva of the rabid dog, impressed upon a person's injured foot, ascends gradually through the veins toward the heart in the manner of a poison, unless something distracts it in the meantime. 

If, therefore, he drinks the blood of another dog in the meantime, that raw blood floats for many hours in the stomach, and finally, just like a... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

...foreign matter <note>original: "regrinũ" - a fragment of "peregrinum," referring to foreign or toxic substances in the body</note> to be expelled through the bowels. Meanwhile, dog’s blood diverts that canine saliva <note>A reference to rabies, then believed to be a "venom" in the saliva that traveled through the body.</note>—which seizes the upper parts before it reaches the *praecordia* <note>original: "praecordia" - the region of the body around the heart and diaphragm, considered by Renaissance physicians to be the seat of vital heat and spirit.</note>—and directs it instead toward the stomach. For there is a power in dog’s blood to attract the saliva of a dog; and likewise, in that saliva, there is a power to seek out similar blood. Therefore, the poison is moved away from the heart and absorbed into the blood floating in the belly; it is then led down through the lower parts along with the blood, thus leaving the man unharmed. 

To what end do I mention these things? First, indeed, so that I might reveal the hidden cause that provides a remedy while discussing this matter. Secondly, so that I may advise: blood can be drunk, and indeed healthfully. Furthermore, there is a power in human blood by which it attracts other human blood and mutually pursues it. Do not, therefore, doubt that youthful blood, when drunk by an old man, can be drawn to the veins and limbs, and there be of the greatest possible benefit.

...[before the body] has digested, to take more nourishment distracts and fatigues nature; from which weariness, when frequently repeated, untimely old age arrives. In winter, let the elderly seek out sunny spots like sheep; in summer, let them revisit pleasant places and streams like birds. Let them frequently spend time among green and sweet-smelling plants. For these, being living and breathing, breathe forth their essence to increase the spirit <note>In Renaissance medicine, the "spirit" was a subtle substance that connected the soul to the body, often replenished through scents and light.</note> of man. Indeed, let them flee to places generally friendly to bees. 

And let them taste honey in winter. For honey is a food primarily friendly to the elderly, except in cases where they fear the inflammation of bile <note>original: "bilis incendiũ" — Referring to the humoral theory where honey was considered "hot" and could aggravate a choleric or "bilious" temperament.</note>. Very fresh cheese is a friend. Dates are friends, as are figs, raisins, capers, sweet pomegranates, ginger, hyssop, scabious, and betony; but pistachios are much more so. 

However, pine nuts <note>original: "Pinei nuclei"</note> are the greatest [friends] of all, as we have previously stated. From these they will receive the most help, if they soak them for twelve hours in slightly warm water before they eat them. For in this way, they will not harm the stomach. Furthermore, while they use these, if they also dwell among pine groves, olive groves, and vines—or at least receive the vapor and scent of the pine—it will be beneficial. 

Likewise, let them often rub their bodies with the resin and "tears" <note>original: "lachrimoq" — This refers to the sap or resin that "weeps" from the bark of the tree, prized for its aromatic and medicinal properties.</note> of the pine, mixed with oil or wine. For it is probable that trees naturally endowed with long life—especially if they remain green even in winter—will benefit you toward a long life through their shade, their vapor, their fresh fruit, their wood, and any other timely use. We have spoken above about long-lived animals. Now indeed...

## What aids the elderly receive from the planets to cherish all their members. Chapter XIII.

But rather, you anxious elderly, consult Apollo, who judged Socrates to be the wisest of the Greeks. Consult Jupiter as well, and Venus. Phoebus <note>Another name for Apollo, the god of the Sun and medicine.</note> himself, the discoverer of the medical art, will give you nutmeg to cherish the stomach. Jupiter, along with Phoebus, gives mastic <note>A resin obtained from the mastic tree, used to aid digestion.</note> and mint. Venus, however, gives coral.

To cherish the head in turn: Phoebus gives peony, frankincense, marjoram, and myrrh with savory. Jupiter gives spikenard <note>An aromatic essential oil derived from a flowering plant in the valerian family.</note> and mace. Finally, Venus gives sweet fennel and myrtle.

For cherishing the heart, however, you will receive from Phoebus indeed citron-balm <note>original: "citrariam"</note>, saffron, aloeswood, frankincense, ambergris, musk, leopard's bane <note>original: "doronicum"</note>, a small amount of clove, citron peel, and cinnamon. From Venus alone: myrtle, sandalwood, and rose. Along with Saturn: coriander. These

## Chapter XIV: A Conversation of Old Men under Venus through Green Meadows.

**S**ut from these weightier deities <note>The author refers here to Saturn and Jupiter, whose medicinal qualities were discussed in previous chapters.</note>, I recall you for a little while, old men, to Venus through gardens and green meadows. Not indeed playing <note>original: "ludentem"; implying frivolous or childish play.</note> with you, but jesting <note>original: "iocantem"; implying a more refined, witty, or restorative humor.</note>. She says this both to you and to me, who am now an old man. First, she pours out this playful oracle: "My sons, if you do not know it, I gave you life through pleasure and movement. Therefore, I shall preserve your life through a certain pleasure and movement, even if it is not the same sort [of youthful vigor] you once had.

<meta>This page continues the speech of Venus or her oracle regarding the preservation of life, specifically focusing on the medicinal properties of the color green and the "spirit" found in nature.</meta>

...Hesperus <note>The Evening Star, associated with the planet Venus.</note> and the myrtle. After this oracle, she commands us to consider the nature of **green things**: that insofar as they are green, they are not only alive, but even **youthful**, overflowing with a truly healthy moisture and a certain vivid spirit <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirit" (spiritus) was a subtle fluid that connected the physical body to the soul and mediated the senses.</note>. Therefore, through their scent, sight, use, and frequent habitation, a youthful spirit flows into us from them. 

While walking among these green things, we shall inquire into the inner cause: why the color green, more than any other, nourishes the sight and delights it healthily. 

We shall find, finally, that the nature of sight is luminous and a friend of light, yet volatile and easily dissipated. Therefore, while it expands through light as if through a friend, it is sometimes completely carried away by an excessive surfeit of light and dissolved by that violent expansion. But it naturally flees darkness as an enemy, and therefore retracts its rays into a narrow space. 

Sight desires to enjoy light in such a way that it is indeed amplified by this friend of its, but not in the meantime dissipated. Now, in whatever color there is much more darkness or blackness than light, the ray of sight is not expanded, nor is it thus delighted according to its wish. Where, on the other hand, there is much more of a splendid color than of black, it is scattered more widely, distracted by a certain harmful pleasure. 

Therefore, the color **green**, tempering black with white <note>original: "nigrum cum candido temperans"; according to Aristotelian color theory, green was seen as a perfect middle point between the extremes of light and dark.</note> most of all, provides both: **delighting** and **preserving** at the same time. And moreover, with its soft and still tender quality, just as water [refreshes] the rays...

# ON LONG LIFE

...it resists the eyes without offense, lest the rays, in going further out, be dispersed. For those things that are both hard and rough somehow break the rays. Those, however, which are very thin <note>original: "rarissima"; referring to substances of low density or porous nature.</note> open a path for dissolution. But those which possess a certain solidity together with a smooth evenness—like reflective bodies—neither break the rays themselves nor allow them to be dispersed too far. Finally, those things which, besides these benefits, are also tender and soft—such as water and green things—soothe the liquid rays of the eyes with their softness. 

Ultimately, the ray of sight is a certain thing naturally kindled within us in the "water" of the eyes <note>This refers to the aqueous or vitreous humor of the eye, which Renaissance thinkers believed housed the visual spirit.</note>. It requires a tempered light within water that is, in its own way, resistant. Therefore, the sight rejoices in water. It is delighted by mirrors that resemble water. It is charmed by green things. In these green things, indeed, the indwelling light of the sun still retains a youthful <note>original: "vernum"; literally "vernal" or "spring-like."</note> moisture and a subtle water full of a certain hidden light. From this it also happens that when the color green is thinned out, it is resolved into saffron <note>original: "croceum"; a yellowish-gold color associated with the thinning or ripening of green plant matter.</note>. 

To what end is all this? It is so that we may understand that the frequent use of green things—since it recreates the spirit of sight, which is the chief part of the **animal spirit** <note>The "animal spirit" (spiritus animalis) was considered the most refined fluid in the body, responsible for sensation, movement, and thought.</note>—also refreshes the animal spirit itself. And let us also remember: if the color green—because it is the middle and most tempered among the grades of colors—is so beneficial to the animal spirit, then much more so are those things which are most tempered in their qualities [beneficial to] the natural and...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the "temperament of green" and balanced medicinal substances sustain the human spirit, analogizing the spirit's longevity to evergreen plants.</meta>

[the spirit] equally gathers; it so delights the spirit internally and restores it, just as the greenness of nature delights the eyes from without. It preserves this spirit even in old men in a certain natural "greenness" <note>In this context, Ficino uses "greenness" (viriditas) as a metaphor for youthful vigor and the vital power of life.</note> for a very long time: like the laurel, the olive, or the pine, which remain green even in winter. It achieves this all the more effectively because it works internally; and it is most effective if such a medicinal composition burns with an aromatic scent and entices the spirit with its flavor.

Truly, just as the body—composed from the thicker parts of the humors <note>In Galenic medicine, the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) were the bodily fluids that determined health and temperament.</note>—is brought into a certain physical form, so the spirit—constituted from the subtlest portions of those same humors—possesses a form that is naturally most tempered <note>Balanced in its primary qualities of heat, moisture, cold, and dryness.</note>, as well as clear and bright; and therefore it is considered "heavenly." It must be preserved in this very form, so that it remains subtle indeed, and yet firm, as we have said. Let it be entirely clear, but also in a certain way solid. Furthermore, let it be continually nourished by fragrant, firm, and clear things if we desire to preserve that life which thrives in the spirit, and to claim for ourselves these heavenly gifts. Let us consider that these things have been contemplated so far by the command of Venus, and that we have heard the words of Venus herself.

Mercury addresses the elderly, and advises them concerning pleasure, scents, songs, and medicines.

### Chapter XV.

Meanwhile, while Venus herself was conversing, as it were, among the old men—and perhaps so far pleasantly enough—she would thereafter have perhaps spoken more at length.

<summary>Ficino explains how the vital spirit is sustained by aromatic and 'tempered' substances, comparing its lasting vitality to evergreen trees, before transitioning to the counsel of Mercury.</summary>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues Mercury's intervention into the discourse. While the previous page focused on the medicinal "greenness" of the spirit associated with Venus and physical restoration, the text now shifts to a philosophical hierarchy of the senses and reason as one ages.</meta>

Mercury, the author of speeches <note>original: 'sermonum auctor'; Mercury was traditionally the god of eloquence, communication, and the intellect</note>, interrupts this discourse with these words: “What business do you old men have with this Venus, who is always a young girl? What, on the other hand, has Venus to do with speeches? Are not speeches mine as much as yours? My reason <note>original: 'ratio'; referring to both the faculty of logic and the underlying proportions of the universe</note> is likewise yours. Listen to me now with the same attention you gave her—and indeed with much greater attention than you gave to Venus.”

<note>A blue hand-drawn paragraph mark appears here in the original manuscript, signaling a shift from the introductory rhetoric to a formal philosophical argument. Ghosting from the reverse side of the page is also visible at the top of the leaf.</note>

You know that there are five senses: sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch. Learn also that there are, so to speak, five corresponding modes of reason. For while your soul is daily imbued through the five senses and conceives rational understandings of things from them, five notions and habits for judging things result, acting as five types of reason. Furthermore, just as there are five senses here, and in a certain way five modes of reason derived from them, so the course of life is arranged in five stages <note>original: 'gradibus', meaning steps or degrees</note> concerning sense and reason. From this, five ages <note>original: 'aetates'</note> of life are counted. <column-break/> <margin>only</margin> <margin>...certain...</margin> <margin>...bile</margin> <margin>have</margin> <margin>to you</margin> <margin>to you</margin> <margin>fear...</margin> <margin>your...</margin> <margin>indeed</margin> <margin>law</margin> <margin>name</margin> <margin>concerning</margin> <margin>living</margin> <margin>true</margin> <margin>through</margin> <margin>between</margin> <margin>through</margin> <margin>so...</margin> <margin>you</margin> <margin>and</margin> <margin>human</margin> <margin>speak</margin> <margin>which</margin> <margin>other</margin> <margin>...of reason</margin>

The first age is drawn by sense alone. The second is attracted much more by sense than it is led by reason. The third, after these, is driven equally by the persuasions of reason and sense. The fourth is led by reason rather than by sense. The fifth, finally, should be ruled by reason alone. Therefore, let the first and second ages—as if subject to Venus—listen to Venus speaking, if it so pleases them. But the remaining ages should listen to Mercury. I, therefore, address all you who remain; not indeed on my own behalf...

<summary>Mercury asserts his authority over the elderly, arguing that while youth is governed by Venus and the senses, old age must be guided by reason. He outlines a five-stage progression of human life based on the shifting dominance of sensory perception and rational judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Venus, five senses, five ages of man, reason, ratio, philosophy of aging, logic vs. sense, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

# ON LONG LIFE
<note>original: 'DE VITA LONGA'; this is likely the title of the treatise or a specific chapter regarding the extension of human life.</note>

[Pilcrow symbol] ...only: but also on behalf of this Diana <note>Diana, the goddess of the hunt and the moon, often represents the silent forces of nature or the physical body in this philosophical context.</note>, whom you see at my left. Truly, since she is tongueless <note>original: 'elinguis'; she represents a natural force that does not speak for itself.</note>: I indeed am "bilingual" <note>original: 'bilinguis'; Mercury suggests he is the mediator who can speak both for the intellect and for the silent natural world.</note>: by right I speak for her, since I have her tongue. 

[Pilcrow symbol] Indeed, Venus <note>The goddess of love, representing the procreative drive.</note> has implanted in you one harmful pleasure: by which she might indeed harm you, but would benefit those yet to come. She gradually drains you through a certain hidden pipe, as it were <term>fistula</term> <note>a metaphorical tube or channel through which the vital 'liquors' or life essence of the parent flows to the offspring.</note>; filling something else with your fluids and procreating. At last, she leaves you on the ground like some old, discarded skin of cicadas <note>original: 'spolium cicadarum'; referring to the dry, empty husks left behind by insects after they molt or die, a metaphor for an aged body stripped of its vitality.</note>, already exhausted. [Pilcrow symbol] Do you not see? That which Venus generates from your matter <term>materia</term> <note>The physical substance or 'prime matter' of the human body.</note> is something fresh and living, and endowed with sense. Therefore, she steals from you youth, and life, and sense: from the whole body, I say, through the pleasure of the whole, so that she may create a new whole from it. I, meanwhile, of the matter

<summary>Mercury explains how the procreative impulse (Venus) serves the continuation of the species at the expense of the individual, draining the body's vital fluids to create new life and leaving the elderly as empty "cicada skins."</summary>

<meta>Continuing the speech of Mercury from the previous page. Having explained how Venus drains the vital fluids of the parent to create the offspring—leaving the elderly like discarded cicada husks—he now describes her deceptive nature and how she uses the senses of touch and taste to lure humans toward their own depletion.</meta>

You have seen Venus, appearing quite youthful, and as
if polished with the dyes and ornaments of a harlot. This one,
therefore, who is always new, always strives for new things, and
hates the old. She destroys what is made, so that she may build what is
yet to be made. This one again, if it is right to say, is like a harlot
not content with any single man. She loves the multitude. And
to speak logically <note>original: 'dialectice'; Mercury uses the language of formal logic here.</note>, she supports the <term>species</term> <note>the general category or collective group of a living thing.</note> everywhere rather than the <term>individual</term> <note>Venus cares for the survival of the human race as a whole, but is indifferent to the death of the specific person she exhausts.</note>.

[red bracket-like mark] Now indeed, she does not only cast
you down by touch: but also by taste, she deceives daily,
and destroys those she has tricked. For those flavors
you perceive in things that are pleasing with a certain moderate
temperament, these this Diana <note>The goddess of the moon and nature, here representing the balanced, natural state of the body.</note> has delivered by the gift of Apollo <note>God of the sun and health.</note>
and Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus, king of the gods.</note>. But the wondrous allurements of flavors,
by which you daily—as if caught by a hook—secretly and
miserably lose your life, the insidious Venus fabricates.

Mars indeed harms you quite rarely and openly.
Saturn also more often declares himself an enemy by his face:

secretly as an enemy. It is better to accuse her, then, if it is

<meta>Mercury continues his speech. Having previously warned that Venus (sensual pleasure) exhausts the physical body through touch and taste to serve the species, he now introduces the five higher, "healthful" pleasures and warns of a different danger: the intellectual exhaustion of Saturn.</meta>

[¶] ON LONG LIFE.
<note>original: 'DE VITA LONGA'</note>
...you should avoid. She <note>Venus, representing physical lust and gluttony.</note> promises you two pleasures at last, and indeed, these are lethal rather than generous gifts. I, however, by the kindness of my father <note>Jupiter/Jove</note> and brother <note>Apollo</note>, promise you five: five I provide that are pure, perpetual, and healthful. The lowest of these is in smell; the higher in hearing; the more sublime in sight; the more eminent in imagination; and in reason, the most excellent and divine.

[¶] The greater the delight perceived in touching and tasting, the more severe the detriment that frequently occurs to life. Conversely, however, the more pleasure you daily derive from smell, hearing, and sight—and likewise from imagination and often from reason—the longer you extend the <term>threads of life</term> <note>A reference to the Three Fates of mythology who spin, measure, and cut the thread of a human's lifespan.</note>.

[¶] But just as I warned you to beware of treacherous Venus in the allurements of touch and taste, so too must you beware of Saturn in that more secret and excessively persistent delight of the contemplative mind. For there, he frequently <term>devours his own children</term> <note>In mythology, Saturn (Cronus) ate his children to prevent them from overthrowing him. In Renaissance psychology, this refers to how intense intellectual study or "melancholy" can consume the person who practices it.</note>. For those whom he snatches away with the charms of his sublime contemplations—and there he recognizes them as his own—he kills if they remain there too long. He cuts them away from the earth with a certain <term>scythe</term> <note>Saturn's traditional tool, symbolizing both the harvest and death (the Grim Reaper).</note> and often steals the earthly life from the unwary. In this at least he is more indulgent than Venus: for while Venus gives the life she takes from you to another <note>By using up the parent's vitality to create a child.</note>, restoring nothing to you for your loss, Saturn—though he separates you from that earthly life from which he himself is separate—ultimately restores to you a celestial and eternal life.

<meta>Mercury continues his speech, transitioning from the dangers of physical sensory indulgence (Venus) to the dangers of intellectual obsession (Saturn). He establishes a parallel between physical and mental procreation.</meta>

In this very respect, **Venus** and **Saturn** seem to resemble one another: for just as he <note>Saturn</note> rejoices in the sign of **Aquarius**, she <note>Venus</note> reigns in **Libra**. Both the one and the other trouble humanity with a "lust for procreation," and they cause harm to those they afflict so that the species might benefit in the future. 

But while she indeed makes the body fertile and stimulates it for breeding, he <note>Saturn, the planet of deep intellect and melancholy</note> urges the mind—pregnant with its own "seed"—toward the birth of ideas. You must, therefore, be mindful of the proverb <term>nothing in excess</term> <note>original: *ne quid nimis*. A famous ancient Greek maxim (motto of the Oracle at Delphi) advising moderation in all things.</note>, and use the constant reins of prudence to restrain the birthing-lust of both. 

Nevertheless, Saturn harms much more quickly and severely those whom he oppresses with weariness, sluggishness, sorrow, worries, and superstition, than he helps those whom he raises to the heights, far above the strength of the body and the common customs of mortals. 

Above all, I advise you to observe what the just **Jupiter** taught **Pythagoras** and **Plato**: that human life is preserved only through a certain equal proportion of the soul itself with the physical body.

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>Mercury continues his instructions on the preservation of health, moving from the philosophical balance between Venus (pleasure) and Saturn (intellect) to the practical application of music, scent, and diet as medicines for the spirit.</meta>

# ON LONG LIFE.

With all delays set aside: I too have come here as a physician. If the flavors derived from things that are no longer living—and likewise the scents from spices already dried and empty of life—are thought to contribute much to life, why then do you doubt that the scents from plants still clinging to their living roots will accumulate the powers of life in a wonderful way? Finally, if the vapors exhaling from merely vegetable life benefit your own life greatly, how much do you think songs will profit you? These songs are airy indeed, harmonious to an entirely airy spirit <note>In Renaissance medicine, the "spirit" was a subtle vapor that linked the physical body to the immaterial soul. Since both music and this spirit were "airy" in nature, music was thought to be its most direct medicine.</note>; they are warm and living for the living; endowed with sense for the sensitive soul; and conceived by reason for the rational soul.

Therefore, I hand over to you this lyre, crafted by me, and with it a Phoebean song <note>original: *cantumque... phaebeum*. Pertaining to Phoebus Apollo, the god of both music and healing.</note>, a comfort for your labors and a pledge of a long life. For just as things that are most temperate in quality and aromatic at the same time temper the humors among themselves and the <term>natural spirit</term> <note>The "natural spirit" was believed to reside in the liver and govern nutrition and growth.</note> within itself, so do such scents temper the <term>vital spirit</term> <note>The "vital spirit" was centered in the heart and governed heat and life force.</note>, and so again do similar harmonies temper the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>The "animal spirit" (from the Latin *anima*, meaning soul) was located in the brain and governed thought and movement.</note>. While you therefore tune the strings and sounds on the lyre, and while you temper the tones in your voice, consider your own spirit to be tempered within in a similar way.

And lest I myself be more stingy than **Venus**, who "grows cold without **Bacchus**" <note>A reference to the Roman proverb *Sine Cerere et Baccho friget Venus*—without food and wine, love (Venus) grows cold.</note>, receive through me this nectar from **Father Liber** <note>An ancient Italian god of fertility and wine, often identified with Bacchus/Dionysus.</note> himself. Those among you who are especially "cold" <note>In the system of the four humors, "coldness" was associated with old age and melancholy.</note> should, at similar times, every fourteenth day <note>original: *bis septimo quoq[ue] die* (every twice-seventh day)</note> take two ounces of **Vernaccia** or sweet **Malmsey** wine <note>Two prestigious varieties of sweet Mediterranean wine highly prized in the Renaissance for their supposed medicinal qualities.</note> with one ounce of bread.

## Confirmation of the above: and why we must avoid constant contemplation and sexual intercourse. Chapter XVI.

**A**strologers say that Venus and Saturn are enemies to one another. However, since there can be no hatred in heaven—where all things are moved by love and where there is no deficiency—we interpret "enemies" as being different in their effects. Let us set aside other matters for the present. Behold: Saturn has placed contemplation for us in the center, while Venus has placed pleasure at the circumference. Indeed, pleasure is a kind of food for the spirits. Therefore, from opposite sides, Venus and Saturn trap the flight of our spirit <note>The author is describing how the "spirit"—the vital link between body and soul—is pulled in two directions: inward toward the heavy, cold intellect of Saturn, and outward toward the fleeting, warm pleasures of Venus.</note>...

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a medical recipe involving myrobalans, ginger, and gold leaves. This page shifts from physical medicine to the psychological and astrological balance of the "spirit" (the vital breath), specifically contrasting the influences of the planets Venus and Saturn.</meta>

¶ ON LONG LIFE.
<note>original: *DE VITA LONGA*</note>
...are captured. The former <note>Venus, representing physical pleasure and external sensation.</note> lures one toward external things through its own pleasure; the latter <note>Saturn, representing contemplation and internal focus.</note> meanwhile, recalls one to internal things through its own nature. Thus, they pull the spirit <term>spiritus: in Renaissance medicine, a subtle "vapor" that connects the soul to the physical body</term> apart if they are active at almost the same time, and they dissipate it. Therefore, nothing is more pestilential to a contemplative man or a seeker of knowledge than the act of Venus <note>original: *Venereus actus*; sexual intercourse or intense sensual pleasure.</note>; conversely, nothing can be more foreign to one pursuing such pleasure than care and contemplation. We count the natural philosopher and the religious seeker in the same rank of contemplatives. And in a similar rank, we include anyone who is deeply thoughtful in their business or subject to heavy cares.

From this it follows that if we wish to relieve or otherwise comfort someone who is under the influence of Saturn—either too occupied with contemplation or weighed down by care—if we attempt this through the acts of Venus, games, and jokes, we are trying remedies that are too distant. We strive in vain and even with harm. Conversely, if we wish to moderate someone lost in the works of Venus, or dissolved in games and jokes, we cannot easily correct them through Saturnian severity.

The best discipline, however, is this: to use certain studies and similar remedies of Apollo <note>Representing music, light, and medicine.</note> and Jove <note>Jupiter, representing social balance and law.</note>—who are the middle ground between Saturn and Venus—to recall people who are leaning toward either extreme back to the center. But to act as physicians at last: just as a flame is commonly extinguished in two violent ways—either blown apart by winds, or conversely, as if smothered by ashes—so we either dissipate the spirit quickly through the effect of Venus, or we gradually suffocate it through the effect of Saturn.

<summary>This page discusses the psychological and physiological dangers of extremes, arguing that the spirit is destroyed by both excessive sensual pleasure (Venus) and excessive intellectual or mournful labor (Saturn). It suggests that the moderate influences of Apollo and Jupiter are the best remedies to restore balance.</summary>
<keywords>Venus, Saturn, spiritus, contemplation, moderation, Apollo, Jupiter, vital spirit, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

# ON LONG LIFE

## On the medicines of the elderly. And again on habitation and diet. Chapter 17.

The rule of the Chaldeans <note>original: *Chaldeorum*; refers to ancient Mesopotamian astrologers and sages whose medical-magical traditions were highly regarded in the Renaissance</note> is perhaps to be approved for recovering youth: that the foreign humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, black bile, yellow bile) believed to determine health and temperament</term> absorbed into the body should be purged gradually. This includes internal humors, treated with suitable medicines, and external ones, addressed through rubbings, baths, and the provocation of sweat. Meanwhile, one should gradually refill the body with healthy and enduring nourishment.

There are those, however, who promise to entirely draw out all old and putrid humors by means of certain lozenges made from viper, or by prepared hellebore <note>Hellebore is a toxic plant used in antiquity as a powerful purgative for mental and physical ailments</note>. Once these are purged and a very healthy humor is recreated through life-giving foods, they claim to restore youth. Those who are more cautious feed chickens with hellebore, and then feed the person with those chickens <note>This was a common method to "filter" the potency of a poison or strong drug through an animal's metabolism before human consumption</note>. 

Indeed, I believe this kind of treatment is dangerous and should perhaps be attempted in youth rather than in old age, lest we experience that "youth" promised by Medea to the elderly Pelias <note>In Greek mythology, the sorceress Medea tricked the daughters of King Pelias into killing and boiling him by promising a ritual of rejuvenation that never happened</note>. For even young people, as Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine"</note> asserts, grow old quickly from medicines that purge excessively. But where diet does not suffice, you may more safely use a clyster <note>an enema</note>, or manna <term>manna: a sweet, resinous sap from the flowering ash tree, used as a mild laxative</term>, or especially washed aloe. If your bowels are more constricted, use manna with broth...

...of the capon and the power of the myrobalan <note>original: *mirobalani*; a fruit used in ancient and medieval medicine as a digestive and life-extending tonic</note>. If you do not have that, you will preserve yourself as a youth even in old age with this purgation.

Take one ounce of washed aloes, two drams of emblic myrobalans, the same amount of chebulic myrobalans, likewise two drams of purple roses, and the same amount of mastic; prepare the pills with Malmsey wine. This should be done when the Moon is favorably placed and enjoys a propitious aspect <term>aspect: the geometric relationship between planets, believed to influence their medical efficacy</term> of Jupiter, especially if the Moon itself or Jupiter occupies fixed houses <term>fixed houses: signs of the Zodiac that represent stability and endurance, such as Leo or Taurus</term>. Indeed, these things contribute greatly to the long duration of life. 

You can also usefully mix rhubarb here with the aloe—namely, half a part of aloe and half a part of rhubarb. Whenever there is a need, take one to three or even five pills in the morning, and drink a little wine afterward. 

However, when you fear phlegm <term>phlegm: one of the four humors, associated with coldness, moisture, and sluggishness</term> more, you can conveniently take a third part of agaric <note>original: *agarici*; a type of medicinal mushroom used as a purgative</note> lozenges along with two-thirds of aloe in these pills, omitting the rhubarb. But for many years now, I have found that first composition of pills to be the safest for every age.

### ON LONG LIFE <note>original: *DE VITA LONGA*</note>

Melt sugar with citron. Cook it: make boluses <note>large medicinal pills</note> and wrap them in **gold**. We have usefully experienced that taking and giving these four hours before lunch serves to corroborate the virtue <term>virtue: here meaning the "vital faculty" or innate biological power of the body</term>, and to illuminate and strengthen the spirit. Indeed, it will be especially beneficial if you drink a little **golden wine** <note>likely wine in which gold leaf was dissolved or a high-quality white wine believed to have solar properties</note> afterward. It will also be beneficial to frequently soak heated bread in pure golden wine and rose water, and to season it additionally with a little cinnamon and a more generous amount of sugar. Frequently also mix these two with almond milk and a little bread. For mixtures of this kind reflect a **Jovial** nature <term>Jovial: relating to the planet Jupiter, which in medical astrology governs growth, warmth, and the preservation of life</term>.

Beyond all the things we have explained or at least indicated above, all city-dwellers <note>original: *urbani*; those living a refined or sedentary life in the city as opposed to laborers</note> must diligently guard against these: heat and frost. Also against any cold, whether occurring after heat or during the night. They must avoid mists and winds, whether blowing from a marsh or bursting through from a narrow space. Likewise, they should avoid places where the air either moves too violently or does not move at all: any habitation that is somewhat damp, as well as stench, lethargy, and grief.

But the **followers of Mercury** <term>followers of Mercury: scholars, writers, and merchants whose mental labor was thought to dry out the body</term> should be more diligent in this. Most diligent of all should be the elderly. Moreover, after they have lightly rubbed their whole body in the morning, they should soothe it against the inconveniences of the air and labor with warm oil, or some slightly bitter wine into which they have previously infused myrrh, rose, and myrtle. Let them frequently carry **sage** in their mouths, which is most friendly to the nerves and teeth. And when the strength of the teeth...

...they are compelled to return to liquid foods, just like infants. They should be cautious of very soft things. Let them prefer milk mixed with a moderate amount of wine. Otherwise, the fire will dry out the natural moisture as if it were a gluttonous consumer. They should follow the sun as long as it is pleasant, treating it as a form of nourishment, provided that **distillation** <note>original: *destillatione*; here referring to the "dripping" of humors caused by overheating or sunstroke</note> is avoided; and the sweltering heat must be similarly declined. Indeed, they should love gentle movements, which are quite necessary for stirring up heat. However, they should hate labors of the body, and even more so those of the mind. Nor should they less avoid long thirst, fasting, and staying awake through the night.

### On the nourishment of the spirit, and the preservation of life through odors. Chapter XVIII.

<note>The following paragraph begins with a large blue initial letter L.</note>

We read that in certain hot regions, which are fragrant everywhere with a great many scents, there are many people with slender bodies and weak stomachs who are nourished, as it were, by odors alone. This is perhaps because the nature of the place itself reduces almost all the juices of herbs, grains, and fruits into scents, and then dissolves the **humors** <term>humors: the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that determined health and temperament</term> of the human bodies there into **spirit** <term>spirit: a subtle, vaporous substance that connects the soul to the body and carries out vital functions</term>. Since, therefore, both odor and spirit are a certain kind of vapor, and like is nourished by like...

### ON LONG LIFE.

...[nourish]ed, for indeed both the **spirit** <term>spirit: in Renaissance medicine, a subtle, vaporous substance that mediates between the physical body and the soul, responsible for movement and sensation</term> and the **spiritual man** receive a great deal of nourishment from odors. This nourishment—of whatever kind it may be, whether delivered through scents or through a **warming balm** <note>original: *fomentum*; a medicinal application used to provide heat or moisture to the body</note>—is especially necessary for the elderly and for those of slender build. Through this, we can somehow compensate for the lack of more solid and substantial food.

Some people tend to doubt whether the spirit is truly nourished by odors. I, however, am of the opinion that perhaps it is only nourished in such a way that unless the foods, which are dense, are finally thinned out into vapors through digestion, the spirit itself—which we have said is a vapor—receives no nourishment from them. Thus, wine full of fragrance restores the spirit suddenly, whereas other things can only barely refresh it after a long time. Indeed, we call that vapor into which cooked foods are finally transformed "odor," because odor is everywhere a kind of vapor; and unless this vapor, drawn inward from the foods, pleases the spirit with a certain scent, it provides hardly any nourishment to the spirit.

For this reason, we strongly approve of our **Avicenna** <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina, c. 980–1037): A Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age; his *Canon of Medicine* was a standard textbook in Europe.</note> when he says that the body is nourished by **sweetness**, but the spirit by a certain (to use his own words) **aromaticity**. For the density of the body can only grow together with a dense nature, such as is found in sweetness. Yet the thinness of the spirit can be restored by nothing other than a kind of smoke and vapor in which that very aromaticity flourishes. Aro— <note>The text breaks off mid-word; likely "Aromatics" or "Aromaticity" continued on the next page.</note>

...[aro]matic quality we call fragrant, and sharp, and in a way **styptic** <term>styptic: an astringent quality that contracts body tissues</term>. Accordingly, since the **liver** <margin>The spirit is better [nourished] by odors. The aromatic spirit [is] most suitable.</margin> indeed provides nourishment to the body through the blood, it is greatly strengthened by **sweetness**. The **heart**, however, because it both creates the **spirit** <term>spirit: in Renaissance medicine, a subtle, vaporous substance that mediates between the physical body and the soul, responsible for movement and sensation</term> and generates nourishment for that spirit, rightly desires **aromatics**. Yet it is beneficial both for aromatics to be seasoned with sweetness for the heart's sake, and for sweet things to be mixed with aromatics for the liver's sake, while avoiding excessive sweetness in the meantime. 

¶ Why say more? **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD): A prominent Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> himself, following **Hippocrates** <note>Hippocrates (c. 460–c. 370 BC): Often called the "Father of Medicine" and a primary authority for Renaissance physicians.</note> with confidence, believes that the spirit is nourished not only by odor, but by **air**—air, I say, not in its simple form, but rather suitably mixed. If we put our faith in these men, we will judge that no choice—neither of foods nor of anything else—is more necessary for life than that of air suited to us. <margin>Perpetual motion... no [choice] of air... nourishment... choice. For... vital [spirit]... air... and so that it may digest... They say... respiration... health.</margin> For air, being most easily and always affected by the qualities of both lower and celestial things, and having been surrounded by an immense (if I may say so) amplitude, and penetrating us from all sides with perpetual motion, marvelously reduces us to its own quality. This is especially true of the spirit, and specifically the **vital spirit** flourishing in the heart. Into its innermost parts, the air flows both constantly and suddenly. Thus, it immediately affects the spirit according to how the air itself is affected; and through the vital spirit—which is both the substance and the origin of the **animal spirit** <term>animal spirit: the most refined spirit, located in the brain, responsible for thought and nervous function</term>—it affects the animal spirit in like manner. Its quality is indeed of the greatest importance for talented people of this kind...
