# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...both **natural and accidental** <note>original: "naturalem tum accidentalem" — "Natural" dignity refers to a planet's strength based on its position in the zodiac; "accidental" dignity refers to its speed, house placement, or lack of harsh aspects from other planets.</note> dignity, and are together, or gaze upon each other favorably. If ever this cannot be done entirely, mix **Solar** <note>Items associated with the Sun, such as gold, laurel, or frankincense.</note> things: combine them into one, along with **Venereal** things, and thus fashion a **Jovial** <note>Jupiter-like; in this context, a balanced and life-affirming result.</note> result composed of both, namely when the Moon goes from a conjunction with Venus toward a **sextile** <note>An angle of 60 degrees, considered harmonious.</note> aspect with the Sun, or vice versa. 

But remember that in composing things that cherish and strengthen the heart and the **spirit** <note>In Ficino’s medical theory, "spirit" is a subtle vapor in the blood that connects the physical body to the soul.</note>, the Moon contributes most of all—if, along with these gifts, she also has this: that she passes through the **aerial signs** <note>The zodiac signs of Gemini, Libra, and especially Aquarius.</note>, particularly Aquarius, which is considered the most aerial of all; or if she is in her own house, or her **exaltation** <note>A specific sign where a planet’s influence is most powerfully felt; for the Moon, this is Taurus.</note>, or the house of Jupiter or the Sun. And wherever she may be, let her hold one of those **twenty-eight mansions** <note>The "Mansions of the Moon" are a system of 28 divisions of the lunar cycle used in astrological magic.</note> that is suitable both to herself and to the work at hand.

## On the natural, vital, and animal power within us; by which planets they are aided, and how through the aspect of the Moon to the Sun and Venus, but especially to Jupiter. Chapter VI.

**T**he primary discipline is to correctly understand which spirit, which power, and which thing these planets signify most of all. The Moon and Venus, therefore, signify the **natural and generative** power and spirit. Jupiter signifies these same things as well, insofar as he increases them; but more effectively, he rules the liver and the stomach. He also plays no small part in the heart, the spirit, and the **vital power** <note>The "vital power" was believed to reside in the heart and govern the body's heat and life-force.</note>, insofar as by his very nature he is in harmony with the Sun.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the planetary influences on the human spirit, specifically focusing on how Jupiter and the Sun govern the heart and life-force, and how the Moon acts as a celestial intermediary for all planetary effects.</meta>

# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

Indeed, and through itself. Otherwise, the heart would not properly receive the **vital spirit** <note>original: "vitalem spiritum" — in Renaissance medicine, this is the subtle vapor in the blood that sustains life and heat, centered in the heart.</note> in the month of Jupiter. Whence the Greeks call Jupiter "Life" <note>original: "Zēn," related to "zōē" or life</note> and "he through whom there is life" <note>original: "Dia," playing on the Greek "dia" meaning "through."</note>; astrologers also testify that he has power over the **animal spirit** <note>The "spiritus animalis," a refined substance in the brain that governs thought, movement, and the senses.</note>, saying that Jupiter contributes to philosophy, to the discovery of truth, and to religion. 

And Plato also says this where he writes that philosophers originate from Jupiter. Homer indicated the same according to the opinion of the ancients, saying: "Such is the mind of men as the father of men and gods <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note> brings upon them day by day." Truly, no other deity is so named except Jupiter. The **Sun** signifies the vital spirit and especially the heart; and it has not a little—indeed, a great deal of influence—in the head, because of sense and motion, of which the Sun is the lord, nor does it desert the **natural power** <note>The "spiritus naturalis" centered in the liver, governing nutrition and growth.</note>. **Mercury** governs the brain and the instruments of the senses, and therefore the animal spirit. 

Hence, the safest way will be to do nothing without the benefit of the **Moon**. Since she commonly, frequently, and easily transmits celestial things to the lower regions; she whom they call "another Sun," creating the four seasons of the year in every single month. For in her first quarter, the **Peripatetics** <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy who emphasized the physical qualities of the elements.</note> think her to be hot and moist <note>Like Spring.</note>. In the second, hot and dry <note>Like Summer.</note>. In the third, cold and dry <note>Like Autumn.</note>. In the fourth, cold and moist <note>Like Winter.</note>. 

And her light is undoubtedly the light of the Sun. She rules the **humors** <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that determine health and temperament.</note> and generation; and all the changes of the fetus in the womb are measured by her rotations; and as often as she is joined to the Sun, she receives a life-giving power from him...

-># ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS<-

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page regarding the Moon's function.</note> to receive, which she may pour into the humor; and likewise there from **Mercury** <note>In Renaissance astrology, Mercury is the "translator" of light, mixing the influences of other planets.</note> a force mixing the humors. Which force Mercury brings both by his transformation into all his own things, and by his multiple turnings. Likewise, she soon receives from **Venus** a force which leads to forms suitable for procreation. 

Truly, it will be worth the effort to remember that the daily course of the **Moon** is distributed into four quarters. In the first quarter, from the east she ascends to the middle of the sky; and meanwhile she increases the humor and the **natural spirit** <note>The *spiritus naturalis* was believed to be produced in the liver and governed nutrition and growth.</note>. In the second, from the middle of the sky she seeks the west, and produces in us the opposite effect. In the third, from the west she approaches the sky below the middle <note>the nadir, or the point directly below the observer</note>; and again she increases that spirit and humor. The fourth falls from there toward the east: and in turn decreases them. 

This appears most greatly on the shores of the ocean: where, according to this course, the sea more clearly approaches and recedes <note>Ficino is linking the lunar cycle directly to the movement of the tides.</note>; and in the same order, the vigor in the sick. It is also probable that the **Sun**, through the same quarters of its own, increases or diminishes the natural heat and the **vital spirit** <note>The *spiritus vitalis*, centered in the heart, sustained life and heat.</note>. The **animal spirit** <note>The *spiritus animalis*, refined in the brain, governed thought and the senses.</note> as well, insofar as it has Mercury as a companion. 

Once these things are known, the physician will be able to observe the more opportune times for restoring the humor, the natural heat, and any of the spirits. But now, enough about the Moon. 

Nor is it proper to dismiss **Jupiter**, in whose second month [of gestation] we indeed received life: and in the other month <note>Likely referring to the ninth month of pregnancy, associated with a safe birth.</note> we are commonly and more happily born. And he who is between the Sun and Venus; likewise between the Sun and the Moon in quality and effect.

...is the middle: and therefore it embraces all things. Indeed, I consider it wicked and dangerous to pass over the **Sun** himself, the lord of the heavens; unless perhaps someone should say that he who possesses **Jupiter** already possesses the Sun within Jupiter; for in that planet, the Sun is most effectively tempered for the benefit of humans. Certainly, all the virtues of the celestial beings reside in the Sun; not only do **Iamblichus** and **Julian** <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher; Julian refers to the Roman Emperor Julian "the Apostate," who wrote a famous "Hymn to King Helios."</note> affirm this, but all philosophers do. And **Proclus** <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a major Neoplatonist whose work on the "theurgic" or magical use of solar power heavily influenced Ficino.</note> says that at the sight of the Sun, all the virtues of all celestial beings are gathered into one and collected. 

Indeed, no one will deny that Jupiter is a certain "Sun" tempered specifically for us. Nor should you neglect the **Moon**, tempered toward **Venus**; for she helps much toward a healthy and prosperous life. Indeed, if Venus makes a human fertile and makes him joyful, then you shall observe her <note>In this context, "observing" a planet means paying attention to its astrological position and timing.</note> accordingly. Although, if you properly mix the Moon—which is similar to Venus, namely equal in moisture and scarcely less warm—with Jupiter or the Sun, you already possess Venus for all intents and purposes. 

What then? So that you may proceed in the safest and most convenient way of all, observe the Moon when she looks upon the Sun and joins with Jupiter; or at least when she looks upon Jupiter and the Sun at the same time; or certainly when, after looking upon the Sun, she soon proceeds to a conjunction or aspect with Jupiter. At that very time, compose <note>original: "compone"</note> your materials in turn: or apply to yourself **Solar**, **Jovial**, and **Venusian** things <note>The <term>Three Graces</term> of Ficino's system: the Sun (vitality), Jupiter (balance/health), and Venus (joy/fertility).</term> all at once. But if necessity or business urges you to take refuge in only one of the "Greats," flee to Jupiter himself, or rather to the Moon and Jupiter together. For no star governs the natural powers within us—and indeed all of them—more than Jup[iter]...

<summary>Ficino explains that the Sun's overwhelming power is best accessed when "tempered" through Jupiter and the Moon. He advises practitioners to time their health-giving activities for when the Moon is in favorable aspect to the Sun and Jupiter.</summary>

...Jupiter, it nourishes and strengthens. No other planet, in turn, promises more prosperous things, nor such a variety of them at once. And to receive his influence is auspicious everywhere. To receive the Sun, indeed, is perhaps not safe in every instance. For Jupiter always helps; the Sun, however, often seems to cause harm <note>Ficino refers to the Sun's ability to "burn" or overwhelm with excessive heat, whereas Jupiter is considered "temperate" (warm and moist).</note>. Venus, on the other hand, is as if weak. Therefore, Jupiter alone is called the "Helping Father" <note>original: "Iuvans Pater." This is a classic Renaissance etymology, deriving the name *Iuppiter* from *Iuvans* (helping) and *Pater* (father).</note>. 

Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer and astrologer of antiquity.</note> confirms this where he says that medicine hardly moves nature at all unless the Moon is joined with Jupiter. To such an extent does he believe that the entire nature of the whole body is properly strengthened from that source. I have experienced that when the Moon is joined even with Venus, medicine hardly moves the body. Although, when we greatly fear phlegm <note>One of the four humors; associated with cold and moisture.</note>, we should especially observe the Moon in relation to the Sun. But where we fear bile and the drying out associated with it, we observe her in relation to Venus. 

Nevertheless, the direction of the Moon toward Jupiter contributes to all these things in some way, and especially to expelling black bile <note>original: "atram bilem." This is the humor responsible for melancholy, a central concern of Ficino's medical work.</note>, as well as to restoring and strengthening the common complexion of humans. For just as licorice and rose oil warm up colder things and cool down warmer things—and wine does the same—and just as these furthermore moisten what is dry and dry out what is too moist: so Jupiter is congruent to human heat, like wine, rose oil, chamomile, and licorice. 

Therefore, when you hear Abu Ma'shar <note>original: "Abumasar." An influential 9th-century Persian astrologer whose works were translated into Latin and became foundational for European astrology.</note> saying: "There is no life for living things, besides God, except through the Sun and Moon," understand this in terms of the influence that pertains to all things in common. However, the more specific and most suitable influence for humans is from Jupiter. There are, however, in nature—

...the body's powers of attracting, retaining, digesting <note>original: "coquendi," literally 'cooking.' In Galenic medicine, digestion was viewed as a process of internal heat "cooking" food into humors.</note>, and expelling. Therefore, Jupiter himself aids all of these. He especially aids the power of digesting—or concocting—as well as the powers of generating, nourishing, and increasing simultaneously. This is because of his airy and abundant humor and his ample heat, which moderately dominates that same humor <note>In the system of four elements and qualities, Jupiter is "Hot and Moist," which corresponds to the element of Air and the Sanguine temperament, considered the most life-giving combination.</note>.

Indeed, through the rays of Jupiter, which are always diffused everywhere, the Sun's own light is most greatly tempered for the health of mankind; meanwhile, the rays of Venus constantly contribute to the same end, with the Moon similarly transferring these influences. The rays of Venus and the Moon, being as it were more humid, require a certain tempering agent; just as the Sun's rays, being hotter, require the tempering of something more humid. But the rays of Jupiter require no tempering at all. For what else is Jupiter but a certain Sun that has been tempered from the beginning for the health of things, especially human ones? Or again, what else is he but a Moon and Venus made nonetheless hotter and more powerful? 

Therefore, astrologers look to Jupiter to herald <note>original: "auspicanr," to take an omen or beginning.</note> a fertile, serene, and healthy year, and they hope for remedies for imminent diseases from him. And Empedocles <note>A Greek Pre-Socratic philosopher (c. 490–430 BC) who identified the four elements with specific gods.</note>, when he assigns the specific gifts to each of the planets, names Jupiter alone the prince of generation, following the example of Orpheus <note>The legendary poet; Ficino believed the Orphic Hymns contained ancient, "pious" philosophy.</note>.

...and with an aspect <note>original: "aspectu," the geometric angle between planets</note> suitable for the desired work. She <note>The Moon, which is feminine in Latin grammar and astrological tradition.</note> should not be in the ecliptic <note>The path of the Sun; being on the ecliptic refers to being at the lunar nodes, where eclipses occur.</note>, nor "under the rays" of the Sun within twelve degrees on either side, unless by chance she is in the very same minute with the Sun. Many, however, maintain that all planets will be strong when they exist in "unity" <note>This state is known as "Cazimi" or being in the heart of the Sun, traditionally thought to greatly empower a planet rather than burning it up.</note> with the Sun. They measure this unity as thirty-two minutes, so that sixteen are counted before the Sun and sixteen after. 

She should not be hindered by Saturn or Mars; she should not descend in southern latitude when she departs from the aforementioned twelve degrees. She should not be opposite the Sun, nor diminished in light, nor slow in her course—that is, when she does not complete twelve degrees in a single day. She should not be in the "combust way" <term>via combusta: a specific section of the Zodiac, usually from late Libra to early Scorpio, considered unlucky or "burnt"</term> from the twenty-eighth degree of Libra to the third of Scorpio; nor in the eighth house; nor in the ascendant; nor in the terms <term>bounds: specific sub-divisions of a zodiac sign ruled by a particular planet</term> of Mars or Saturn. 

Some indeed do not want the Moon in the sixth, twelfth, ninth, or fourth houses. But they approve of her in the other regions of the sky. Where you cannot encompass all these conditions, at least wait for Jupiter or Venus to be in the ascendant or the tenth house. In this way, they come to the aid of the Moon's detriments. 

Nor would it be irrelevant to remember that as the Moon increases in light, so not only moisture increases in us, but also spirit <note>original: "spiritum," the vital, subtle substance that connects the soul to the body</note> and virtue <note>original: "virtutem," meaning power or biological strength</note>; and these are dilated toward the periphery <note>The outer parts or skin of the body.</note>. This happens especially in her second quarter. However, when she is decreasing, the opposite occurs, particularly in the last quarter. Her first trine <note>An aspect of 120 degrees.</note> to the Sun is considered favorable, as is the second. This is preferred over the first sextile <note>An aspect of 60 degrees.</note> and the second sextile. For as much as the Moon is filled with light, she is likewise filled with heat.

->**ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.**<-

...digestive power through airy things: specifically, when the Moon occupies a sign perfectly suited to that limb <note>This refers to "zodiacal man," the belief that each sign of the zodiac governs a specific part of the body (e.g., Aries for the head, Leo for the heart).</note>. For she moves the humors <note>The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—that determined health and temperament.</note>. But it is better that she be fostered. Let these words suffice regarding the **natural power and spirit** <note>original: "uirtute & spu naturali"; in Renaissance medicine, the "natural spirit" was produced in the liver and governed nutrition and growth.</note> which rule principally in the liver, and which are divided into the four functions we have described.

What, then, should we advise regarding the **vital power and spirit** flourishing in the heart? This has already been sufficiently indicated. For these are aided primarily through fiery things and, in a certain way, through airy things, when the Moon, in similar houses or mansions <note>The "mansions of the moon" are 28 divisions of the lunar orbit used in medieval astrology.</note>, looks upon Jupiter; especially if she embraces <note>original: "complectatur"; a term for a close conjunction or harmonious relationship between planets.</note> Jupiter and the Sun.

You can also strengthen the **animal power** <note>original: "virtutem animalem"; the highest form of spirit, centered in the brain and responsible for sensation, movement, and thought.</note> which rules in the head through sense and motion, primarily through airy things with fiery things added: when the Moon, in similar houses or seats, looks upon Jupiter; especially if she embraces Jupiter and Mercury nearly at the same time.

¶ But here I warn you, do not think that **Mercury** is watery or earthy—a thing which I myself once suspected. ¶ Otherwise, he would not contribute to the motions and speed of the intellect. But you should know that he is, in a certain way, airy. For this very reason, he is so mobile and so easily changeable; he contributes so much to the intellect, especially when positioned in **Aquarius**, which is the most airy sign. 

Indeed, the moisture in him is tempered. His heat is slight. When placed under the Sun, he is said to dry things out. But when he is made more distant from the Sun, he is thought to moisten things. In the former case, he certainly heats much from the nature of the Sun; in the latter, he heats very little and moistens more by his own nature.

-># ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.<-
<note>original: "DE VITA CAELITVS." This is the third book of Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life," titled "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens."</note>

**J**ust as we have said, you will be able to support the entire body; but the head especially, if you observe the planets in Aries, or each planet in its first domicile <note>In astrology, a "domicile" or "house" is the zodiac sign over which a planet has rulership, where its influence is strongest.</note>. The heart <note>original: "praecordia"; refers generally to the diaphragm or the region around the heart and chest.</note> if they are in Leo. The stomach and liver if in Cancer and Sagittarius, or at least in Virgo. And you receive those things which specifically seek out [benefit] the limb. It is also useful to know which limbs each planet holds in any given sign. You can also provide specifically for each person according to the distinction of age through the four ages of the Moon. For the Moon, from the new moon until the first quadrature <note>original: "quadraturam"; the 90-degree angle, known as the first quarter moon.</note>, is young. Then until the full moon, it is young and mature. From there to the second quadrature <note>the last quarter moon</note>, it is simultaneously mature and old. From then until the conjunction <note>the new moon, when the moon and sun are at the same degree</note>, it is old. Therefore, you will successfully apply the age of the moon to the age of the body being treated.

This is especially effective if you then receive its aspect <note>original: "aspectum"; the geometric relationship between planets that allows their influences to combine.</note> toward any of the Three Graces <note>In Ficino’s astrological system, the Three Graces are the planets Jupiter, Venus, and the Sun (Sol), which provide life-giving, beneficial influences.</note>. This aspect of the Moon truly, always, and quickly bestows favorable things. And yet, it does not easily bestow long-lasting or very great things unless—besides the gaze of the Moon—the Graces themselves also behold each other mutually, or are just about to behold each other; either all three of them, or at least two. 

However, fixed signs <note>In astrology, "fixed signs" (Leo, Aquarius, Taurus, Scorpio) are associated with stability, endurance, and the middle of the seasons.</note> provide especially long-lasting effects: namely, Leo, Aquarius, Taurus, and Scorpio. If by chance you cannot at the present time opportunely direct the Moon toward the gracious planets, choose fixed stars having the nature of the Graces—that is, the nature of Jupiter, or Venus, or the Sun—and choose the Moon directed toward them. It is safer, however, for the Moon meanwhile to almost approach Jupiter or Ve... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; "Ve" likely refers to Venus.</note>

## Chapter VIII. On the virtues and use of the fixed stars.

**T**he elder astrologers <note>original: "astrologi maiores"; referring to ancient authorities like Hermes Trismegistus or Ptolemy.</note> hand down that certain stars discovered by Mercury possess the greatest authority. Such as is the navel of Andromeda <note>original: "umblicus Andromade"; the star Mirach</note> at the 22nd degree of Aries, which is of the nature of Mercury and Venus.

¶ Likewise, at the 18th degree of Taurus is the Head of Algol <note>The star Beta Persei, historically associated with the Gorgon’s head and considered one of the most malevolent stars in traditional astrology.</note>, possessing the nature of Saturn and Jupiter. To this they wish the diamond and mugwort <note>original: "artemisiam"</note> to be subject; it is said to grant boldness and victory. At the 22nd degree of the same sign are the Pleiades, a lunar and martial constellation. To this they subject crystal, the herb *diacedon* <note>likely a reference to a specific botanical preparation or a stone like the "diadochos," thought to be similar to beryl</note>, and fennel seed. They believe it contributes to sharpening the eyesight. As for what some say about its usefulness in summoning demons, I judge that to be a fiction.

Within this, you should insert the gemstone with the herb placed underneath it, so that the ring is touching the skin. This should be performed when the Moon joins that star, or beholds it in a trine or sextile aspect <note>beneficial astrological angles of 120 or 60 degrees</note>, and while the star itself is traversing the midheaven or the ascendant. 

[¶] However, regarding what pertains to stars of this kind, I would prefer to compose them in the form of a medicine rather than a ring, to be applied either internally or externally, provided the timing mentioned above is observed. Even if the ancients [used] rings...

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological talismans and rings from the end of Chapter 13. The previous page concluded with a list of stars and their medicinal/magical correspondences. Here, Ficino moves into a defense of these practices by citing historical figures like Apollonius of Tyana and the theological authority of Thomas Aquinas, while shifting the mechanism of action from "magic" to the refinement of the human "spirit."</meta>

-># ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.<-

...valued these rings. For Damis and Philostratus <note>Philostratus was a 3rd-century Greek scholar who wrote the *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*; Damis was Apollonius's purported companion and chronicler.</note> relate that Iarchas, the prince of the Indian sages, fashioned seven rings in a specific manner, named after the seven planets. He gave these to Apollonius of Tyana <note>A famous Neopythagorean philosopher and miracle-worker from the 1st century AD.</note>, who then wore each one individually on its corresponding day of the week, distinguishing them by their names. Indeed, Iarchas told Apollonius that his own grandfather, a philosopher, lived for one hundred and thirty years, perhaps relying on a celestial gift of this kind. Apollonius then made use of this gift as well, so that even in his hundredth year, as they say, he showed the vigor of a young man. 

[¶] Finally, if rings of this kind possess any virtue from on high, I judge that it pertains not so much to the immortal soul or to the gross, heavy body, as it does to the spirit <note>term: *spiritus*; In Ficino's philosophy, the "spirit" is a very subtle vapor or "aerial body" that acts as the physical bridge between the immaterial soul and the material flesh. It is the primary vehicle for astrological influence.</note>. As the ring is gradually warmed by the body, the spirit is affected in one way or another: so that it is made firmer, or clearer, more forceful, or milder, more serious, or more joyful. These affections then pass entirely into the body, and in a certain way into the sensory soul, which most often indulges the needs of the body. 

[¶] Moreover, as for the claim that these rings are useful against demons or enemies, or for gaining the favor of princes: this is either a fiction, or it is derived from the fact that the rings make the wearer's spirit fearless and firm; or perhaps gentle, lovable through compliance, and gracious. 

[¶] Now indeed, if I were to say that celestial things contribute anything beyond bodily health to talent, art, or fortune, I would not disagree with our Thomas Aquinas <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274). Ficino frequently cites Aquinas to prove that his "natural magic" is compatible with Catholic orthodoxy.</note>. In the third book of his work *Against the Gentiles* <note>original: *Summa contra Gentiles*</note>, he proves that by celestial bodies im— <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence; the argument likely continues that celestial bodies influence human dispositions and external circumstances.</note>

<summary>Ficino discusses the use of planetary rings by Apollonius of Tyana for longevity and explains that astrological influences work primarily on the "spirit," the intermediary between body and soul. He begins to justify these views using the theological authority of Thomas Aquinas.</summary>
<keywords>Apollonius of Tyana, Iarchas, planetary rings, spiritus, Thomas Aquinas, astrological medicine, longevity</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the influence of celestial bodies on the human spirit and body. Having previously discussed how astrological rings might affect the "spiritus" (the bridge between soul and body), Ficino now draws upon the authority of Thomas Aquinas and Aristotle to explain how these celestial gifts create natural inclinations or "luck" in specific professions, while maintaining a firm boundary regarding Christian piety.</meta>

...remain initially somewhat in our body; by whose gift we are disposed to often choose what is better, even if we do not know the reason or the goal. In which matter indeed he <note>Ficino is referring to Thomas Aquinas, who discussed these concepts in his *Summa Contra Gentiles* and his commentary on Aristotle's *Eudemian Ethics*.</note> calls such people "the well-fortunated" <note>original: "bene fortunatos"</note>; and—with Aristotle agreeing—"the well-born" <note>original: "bene natos"; in this context, it refers to those naturally endowed with a favorable disposition by the stars at birth.</note>. He adds also that by celestial virtue <note>term: *virtute caeleſti*; the specific power or influence radiating from the stars and planets.</note> some people become effective in certain results of the arts (to use his words), such as a soldier lucky in conquering, a farmer in planting, or a physician in healing. For he says: Just as herbs and stones possess certain wonderful powers from the heavens beyond their elemental nature <note>The "elemental nature" refers to the basic qualities of hot, cold, moist, and dry; "celestial powers" are occult properties that transcend these simple physics.</note>, so too do some men possess such gifts in the arts.

As for me, I shall be satisfied if celestial influences, in whatever way—as if through medicines, whether internal or external—contribute to prosperous health, provided that while we seek the health of the body, we suffer no loss to the health of the soul.

[¶] Let us attempt nothing at all prohibited by our holy religion.

->[¶] CONCERNING LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS<-
[The domicile] of Mars <note>original: "tis domiciliū"; the text completes the name *Martis* from the preceding page.</note> is Scorpio and Aries. His exaltation <note>term: exaltation; a specific sign where a planet's influence is particularly strengthened or "honored," though it is not the planet's primary home.</note> is Capricorn. The seat of the Sun is indeed Leo. His kingdom is Aries.
[¶] The dwelling of Venus is Taurus and Libra. Her exaltation is Pisces. The house of Mercury is Virgo and Gemini. His kingdom is Virgo. The house of the Moon is Cancer. Her exaltation is Taurus.
Saturn and Jupiter have triplicity <note>term: triplicity; a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (fire, earth, air, or water). Planets are said to have "triplicity" when they exert influence over these specific elemental groups.</note> in the fiery and airy signs. The Sun only in the fiery. Mercury only in the airy. Mars, Venus, and the Moon in the watery and earthy.
Any of the planets, except for the Sun and the Moon, possess certain boundaries of their own in any sign, which we also call terms <term>terms: *termini*; specific segments or degrees within a zodiac sign assigned to a planet. A planet located within its own "terms" gains a minor boost in strength, even if it does not rule the entire sign.</term>. Therefore, in Aries, Jupiter holds the first six terms there. Venus the six following. Mercury the later eight. Mars the next five, and Saturn the last five. In Taurus, by a similar order, Venus has eight boundaries. Mercury six; Jupiter eight; Saturn five; and finally Mars three. In Gemini, Mercury has six; Jupiter six; Venus five; Mars seven; Saturn six. In Cancer, Mars seven; Venus six; Mercury just as many; Jupiter seven; Saturn four. In Leo, Jupiter six; Venus five; Saturn seven; Mercury six; Mars just as many. In Virgo, Mercury seven; Venus ten; Jupiter four; Mars seven; Saturn two. In Libra, Saturn six; Mercury eight; Jupiter seven; Venus just as many; Mars two. In Scorpio, Mars seven; Venus four

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific technical breakdown of planetary "terms" (segments of zodiac signs) starting from the end of the sign of Gemini, and moves into the second system of planetary subdivisions known as "Faces" or Decans.</meta>

...four <note>original: "tuor," completing "quattuor" from the previous page</note>. Mercury has eight. Jupiter five. Saturn six. In Sagittarius, Jupiter has twelve. Venus five. Mercury four. Saturn five. Mars four. In Capricorn, Mercury has seven. Jupiter seven. Venus eight. Saturn four. Mars just as many <note>original: "totidem"; referring back to the count of four.</note>.
[¶] In Aquarius, Mercury has seven. Venus six. Jupiter seven. Mars five. Saturn just as many <note>original: "totidem"; five.</note>. In Pisces, Venus has twelve. Jupiter four. Mercury three. Mars nine. Finally, Saturn two. The Sun, however, and the Moon hold their boundaries by another rule. For the Sun indeed has six signs for its boundaries: Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, and Capricorn. The Moon, however, has the remaining ones: Aquarius, Pisces, Aries, Taurus, Gemini, and Cancer. Therefore, the Sun and the Moon in these signs possess that leadership and influence which the other planets are considered to have in their own terms <note>term: terms; specific degrees within a sign where a planet exerts minor dignity. Since the Sun and Moon rule entire halves of the zodiac in this system, they do not have small degree-based "terms" like the other five planets.</note>. 
Besides terms, the planets have their **faces** in the signs—which the Greeks call **Decans** <note>original: "Decanos"; from the Greek *dekanoi*, meaning "ten days each," as they divide each 30-degree sign into three 10-degree parts.</note>—each occupying ten degrees of a sign. In Aries, the first face belongs to Mars. The second to the Sun, who follows Mars in the heavens. According to the **Chaldean order** <note>term: Chaldean order; the traditional sequence of the seven visible planets based on their speed and distance from Earth: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon.</note>, the third is Venus's, who succeeds the Sun in the sky. In Taurus, the first face belongs to Mercury, following Venus. The second to the Moon, who succeeds Mercury. The third belongs to Saturn. For one must return to him, now that the number of the planets has been fulfilled <note>Since there are only seven traditional planets, the sequence restarts at Saturn after the seventh planet, the Moon, is reached.</note>. In Gemini, the first face belongs to Jupiter, clearly following Saturn. The second is of Mars. The third is of the Sun, in the same order and similarly thereafter.

<summary>This page concludes the listing of planetary "terms" for the final zodiac signs and introduces the system of "Faces" (Decans), explaining how planets are assigned to ten-degree segments based on the Chaldean order.</summary>

# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

## ¶ How we should use the planets in medicines. Chapter 10.

**C**ommemorated have we <note>The large red initial 'C' begins the chapter.</note> which dignities the planets possess within the signs, so that whenever we are about to make or compose things that pertain to a certain planet, we may know how to place it within its own dignities <note>term: dignities; specific positions in the zodiac, such as domicile or exaltation, where a planet's influence is considered strongest and most harmonious.</note>. This is especially important when a planet holds the primary influence in our annual revolution <note>term: revolution; the solar return chart, used to predict the themes of a specific year of a person's life.</note> and our birth chart <note>original: "genesi"; the nativity or horoscope at the moment of birth.</note>. In such cases, even planets like Saturn and Mars—which we would otherwise normally seek to diminish <note>Ficino refers to Saturn and Mars as the "malefics," whose heavy or aggressive influences were often mitigated in medical astrology unless specifically needed.</note>—must nonetheless be elevated and strengthened if they happen to be the primary significators <note>term: significator; a planet that represents the individual or their vitality in a horoscope.</note> of our birth.

¶ Indeed, we shall find it especially worthwhile to remember this account if, when we intend to use the beneficial influence of the Moon, Venus, and Jupiter while preparing medicines, we take care that these planets are not located in the **terms** <note>original: "finibus"; refers to the specific degree-segments of a zodiac sign ruled by a particular planet, which was the focus of the previous page.</note> of Saturn or Mars. This rule holds unless, perhaps, we are forced by necessity to use Saturn to check a physical dissolution <note>original: "dissolutionem"; a breaking down of bodily tissue or a discharge.</note> or to suppress feverish heat, or to use Mars to warm those things which are extremely cold and sluggish...

<summary>Ficino explains the medical application of planetary dignities, advising practitioners to ensure "benefic" planets are not hindered by the "terms" of Mars or Saturn when crafting remedies, unless their specific qualities are required.</summary>
<keywords>medical astrology, dignities, terms, birth chart, Saturn, Mars, Jupiter, Venus, Moon, significators</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the strength of planets based on their house placements and introduces rules for medical astrology, specifically how to choose a physician based on the patient's and the doctor's birth charts.</meta>

...makes the **tenth house**. The **eleventh** succeeds this <note>term: succedent; houses that follow the powerful "angular" houses and have moderate strength.</note>. But the **twelfth** is cadent <note>term: cadent; houses that "fall away" from the angles, often considered the weakest or most hidden positions in a chart.</note> from the ascendant. ¶ Therefore, so that the planets may be powerful, they should be held in the **angles**: either of the east <note>the Ascendant</note>, the west <note>the Descendant</note>, or the Midheaven on either side <note>original: "medii utrinq cęli"; referring to the points directly above (MC) and directly below (IC) the observer.</note>. They are most powerful in the angle of the **ascendant**, or the **tenth house**, which holds the midheaven above the head, or at least in the regions which immediately follow the angles. Nevertheless, it is thought that the **Sun rejoices** in the ninth house (which is cadent from the midheaven), and the **Moon** rejoices in the third (also a cadent house).

¶ Amidst these things, astrologers wish for two rules to be kept in mind. One concerns the condition of the sick person, and the other concerns the physician. Specifically, where the **seventh house** of the sick person is made "unfortunate" by **Saturn** or **Mars**, or if the lord of that house <note>the planet ruling the sign on the 7th house cusp</note> is unhappy: separate the doctor from the patient, if you believe Ptolemy <note>Ptolemy was the foundational authority on astrology in the 2nd century; Ficino is citing a tradition where the 7th house represents the physician in a medical inquiry.</note>. 

¶ Furthermore, when choosing a doctor, they command one to avoid a **Saturnian** or **Martial** person <note>someone whose chart is dominated by Saturn (heavy, slow, melancholic) or Mars (aggressive, sharp, choleric)</note>. One should instead seek a doctor in whose birth chart the **sixth house** is made fortunate through an aspect <note>term: aspect; a specific geometric angle between planets that determines how their energies interact.</note> of the Sun, or through Venus or Jupiter, in whatever manner. We say a sign or a planet is **"unfortunate"** because of Saturn or Mars—unless these signs are their own domiciles or kingdoms <note>Saturn and Mars are "malefics," but they are less harmful when they are in their own signs (like Capricorn for Saturn or Aries for Mars), where they behave with more dignity.</note>—namely when they are either located there or look upon that place by **opposition** or **square**. We call the **opposite** aspect that which is between points separated by the longest possible interval <note>180 degrees</note>. The **square** aspect is where one is distant from the other by a fourth part of the sky—that is, by a space of three signs <note>90 degrees</note>. However, Saturn and Mars harass <margin>...tral</margin> <margin>...or king[dom]</margin> <margin>...double</margin> <margin>...[tri]ple</margin> <margin>...proximate</margin> <margin>...truly</margin> <margin>...[sex]tile</margin> <margin>...effect</margin> <margin>...[antis]cis</margin> <margin>...from</margin> <margin>...and</margin> <margin>...[mar]gin</margin> <margin>...and</margin> <margin>...or</margin> <margin>...Cap[ricorn]</margin> <margin>...Pisc[es]</margin> <margin>...twice/to us</margin> <margin>...[fata]l</margin> <margin>...[it] rules</margin> <margin>...con[junction]</margin> <margin>...Ob[ject]</margin> <margin>...[fin]ished</margin> <margin>...[al]ternate</margin> <margin>...[consi]der</margin> <margin>...head/chapter</margin> <margin>...makes</margin> clearly less through a **conjunction** <note>when two planets are in the same degree</note> than through an opposition or square...

### ¶ ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...they are harmful to others, unless they receive them as guests into their own domicile, kingdom, or term <note>term: a specific subdivision of a zodiac sign ruled by a particular planet.</note>. Just as the beneficial planets <note>the "benefics," usually Jupiter and Venus</note> are more helpful when, in addition to a **sextile** or **trine** aspect and a **conjunction**, they also receive other planets as guests, as we have said. Nevertheless, planets fear a **conjunction with the Sun** <note>original: "Solis... coniunctionem planetae verentur"; a planet within a few degrees of the Sun was considered "combust" or burnt up, losing its individual power to the Sun's heat.</note>, though they rejoice in its aspect, specifically the **trine** or **sextile** <note>trine and sextile: harmonious angles of 120 and 60 degrees respectively.</note>.

Furthermore, it is necessary to remember that **Aries** presides over the head and face; **Taurus** over the neck; **Gemini** over the arms and shoulders; **Cancer** over the chest, lungs, stomach, and upper arms; **Leo** over the heart, stomach, liver, back, and the rear ribs; **Virgo** over the intestines and the base of the stomach; **Libra** over the kidneys, the upper thigh, and the buttocks; **Scorpio** over the genitals and the womb; **Sagittarius** over the thighs and the groin; **Capricorn** over the knees; **Aquarius** over the shins and calves; and **Pisces** over the feet.

¶ Being mindful of this order, you must be careful not to touch a body part with **iron** <note>surgical blades</note>, or **fire** <note>cautery</note>, or **cupping jars**, when the **Moon** is passing through its corresponding sign. For at that time, the Moon increases the **humors** <note>humors: the four primary bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to govern health.</note> in that limb. The abundance of these humors prevents the healing of wounds and weighs down the natural vitality of the limb. During such a time, you should instead observe that part of the body and nourish it opportunely and successfully with certain "friendly" remedies, applied either externally or internally.

¶ It is also beneficial to know which sign was **ascending** <note>the Ascendant: the sign rising on the eastern horizon at the exact moment of one's birth.</note> at the time of your birth. For besides Aries, this rising sign also signifies the head for you; and when the Moon is in this sign, it regards your head.

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for medical treatments (bloodletting, purging, and baths) based on the Moon's position in the zodiac, concluding the section with a defense of medical astrology via Galen and Ptolemy.</meta>

...you should not attempt surgeries or baths. When the Moon is in **Cancer**, you may successfully let blood. Take medicine, especially in the form of an **electuary** <term>electuary: a medicinal paste or conserve made by mixing powdered drugs with honey, syrup, or sugar</term>. When it is in **Leo**, do not induce vomiting. When it is in **Libra**, it is suitable for enemas. In **Scorpio**, do not attempt baths. There are some who neither forbid nor command the giving of laxative medicine [at certain times]. In **Capricorn**, taking medicine is harmful. Likewise in **Aquarius**. But in **Pisces**, it is indeed beneficial.

Which body parts each of the planets possesses in any given sign, although it is necessary to know, would be a lengthy narrative. Nor indeed should **Ptolemy’s** <note>Claudius Ptolemy: a 2nd-century Alexandrian polymath whose 'Tetrabiblos' was the foundational text for Western astrology.</note> precept regarding the purging of the bowels remain hidden from us. We approve of taking purgative medicine <note>original: "medicinat̃ purgatoriã"; treatments intended to cleanse the body of excess humors.</note> when the Moon is in **Cancer**, **Pisces**, or **Scorpio**; especially if the **Lord of the Ascendant** <note>the planet that rules the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment the treatment begins.</note> then applies itself to a planet moving beneath the earth.

If, however, the Lord of the Ascendant is joined meanwhile above the head with a planet holding the **Midheaven** <note>the highest point of the sky or the 10th house, representing the peak of planetary influence.</note>, nausea and vomiting will immediately be stirred up.

¶ Finally, let us conclude with **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon: a Greek physician whose medical theories dominated Western medicine for over 1,500 years.</note> that astrology is necessary for the physician. Discussing **critical days** <term>critical days: specific days during an acute illness when a major change or "crisis" in the patient's condition was expected, often calculated by the Moon's movement.</term>, he says the opinion of the Egyptians is certain: namely, that the Moon signifies the condition of the sick and the healthy from day to day. This is true to such an extent that if the rays of **Jupiter** and **Venus** <note>the "benefic" planets, associated with growth and healing.</note> are mixed with the Moon, the person is well-affected; if, however, the rays are of **Saturn** or **Mars** <note>the "malefic" planets, associated with obstruction and inflammation.</note>, the opposite will be the case. But having wandered quite far, let us at last return to the spirit, to life, and to the **Graces**.

### Chapter XI: What things pertain to each planet.

<note>A large initial 'H' is decorated in blue ink, marking the start of the chapter.</note> All these things indeed tend toward this: that our **spirit** <term>spirit: (spiritus) in Ficinian medicine, a subtle, vaporous substance that acts as the bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</term>—properly prepared and purged through natural means—may receive as much as possible from the **spirit of the world’s life** <term>spirit of the world: (spiritus mundi) a cosmic medium that permeates the universe, carrying the powers of the stars to the earth.</term> itself, by means of the rays of the stars received at the opportune time. 

Indeed, the life of the world, implanted in all things, is clearly propagated into herbs and trees, as if they were the hairs and locks of its own body. It swells, moreover, within stones and metals, like teeth and bones. It also buds forth in living shells adhering to the earth and stones. For these live not so much by their own specific life as by that common life of the whole. 

This common life certainly flourishes even more above the earth in subtler bodies, as they are closer to the soul. Through its inner vigor, water, air, and fire possess their own living things and are moved. This life fosters and agitates the air and fire even more than the earth and water with perpetual motion. 

And finally, it especially enlivens the celestial bodies as if they were the world’s head, or heart, or eyes. Whence through the stars, as if through eyes, it pours out rays everywhere—not only visible rays, but also **visual** rays <note>visual rays: Ficino follows the "extramission" theory of vision, where eyes (including the stars) emit active power rather than just receiving light.</note>. By these, in the manner of an **ostrich** <note>original: "more struti"; Ficino references a common medieval belief that the ostrich hatches its eggs simply by gazing at them with intense focus, illustrating how the stars influence the world through sight alone.</note>, as we have said elsewhere, it looks upon the lower things. It fosters them by seeing—indeed, even by "touching" them in this way, it generates, shapes, and moves them in every way. 

Therefore, you will receive the motion of the world's life through the motion of shimmering water, of clear air, of fire at a moderate distance, and of the heavens. If you yourself are also moved gently and in a nearly similar way, performing certain circles according to your strength...

.l.iiii

<summary>Ficino explains the 'Spirit of the World' (Spiritus Mundi) as a cosmic vital force that connects the stars to the earth, comparing stones to bones and trees to hair. He argues that by imitating celestial motions, humans can absorb this life-giving stellar influence.</summary>

...dizziness avoided: surveying the celestial things with your eyes and revolving them in your mind. Likewise, by a certain frequent use of plants and similarly living things, you can drink in a great deal from the **spirit of the world** <term>spirit of the world: (spiritus mundi) the vital, cosmic breath that Ficino believes connects the stars to earthly matter.</term>. This is especially true if you are nourished and fostered by those things while they are still living and fresh, and as if still clinging to Mother Earth. 

You should spend time as frequently as possible among plants that are sweetly fragrant, or at least not foul-smelling. For all herbs, flowers, trees, and fruits give off a scent, although you may often notice it less. By this odor—which acts as if it were the breath and spirit of the world's life—they recreate and enliven you from every side. For your own **spirit** <term>spirit: (spiritus) the thin, vaporous substance in the human body that serves as the bridge between the physical organs and the immaterial soul.</term> is very similar in nature to odors of this kind. Because the spirit is the medium between the body and the soul, these scents easily refresh the body as well, and they benefit the soul wonderfully. First, you should indeed avoid any excessive intemperance of the air. Then, you should move about more frequently at those times when, in addition to the air being temperate and clear, the position of the stars is especially healthy for human beings. Likewise, you should choose locations that are exceptionally fragrant, through which you might change your position daily, keeping yourself in almost continuous and gentle motion. I have always advised that one must change locations with careful selection, because the gifts of the celestial beings and of universal nature are distributed among us across various things and places here and there; all of these, finally, are to be enjoyed. 

I will pass over how variety prevents boredom <note>original: "tedium" — a state of weary dissatisfaction or listlessness</note>, which is an enemy to the **spirits** <term>spirits: (spiritus) the subtle, airy substance that links the body and soul</term> and is characteristic of Saturn <note>In Renaissance astrology, Saturn was associated with melancholy, stagnation, and heavy, cold moods.</note>. Instead, variety brings pleasure, through which (if I may put it this way) Venus herself, the friend of pleasure, enters into the spirit; and once she has entered, she immediately fulfills her duty by propagating and expanding it. 

In short, if anyone considers Paradise and the use of the "fruit of life" in the writings of Moses <note>A reference to the Tree of Life in Genesis.</note>; and likewise the similar way of life proposed by Plato in the *Phaedo* <note>Plato describes a "true earth" above our own where the air is purer and the inhabitants live longer, nourished by scents.</note>; and what Pliny says about certain peoples who live on scent alone <note>Pliny the Elder recorded legends of the "Astomi," a tribe with no mouths who survived solely by smelling flowers and fruits.</note>, he will understand that what we are saying is true.

But let us descend to the actual nature of the **spirit**. The quality of the spirit is undoubtedly Jovian <note>Jovian: relating to the planet Jupiter, associated with health, growth, and "temperate" warmth.</note>, for it is infused into us during his time. It is also Solar. For Jupiter infuses this spirit insofar as he tempers the vast power of the Sun within himself. It is also Jovian for another reason: because the spirit is hot and moist, abounding in heat rather than moisture; it is born from the blood, and is called a certain "bloody vapor" <note>In Ficino's medical theory, the spirit is a very fine, hot vapor distilled from the thinnest part of the blood.</note>. Again, because it glows, is extremely subtle and shining, and arises from the heart, it is undoubtedly judged to be Solar. It has...

Insofar as it serves the soul for sensation and imagination, it is judged to be **Solar** and **Mercurial**; and indeed, it is Mercurial everywhere, since it is so mobile and so easily changeable and adaptable. 

A healthy spirit does not usually contain much of **Saturn**, **Mars**, or the **Moon**. Mercury holds such great authority over this animal spirit that he is said, by means of his **caduceus** <note>the herald's staff carried by Mercury, featuring two entwined snakes; it symbolizes his power to mediate between the heavens and the underworld</note>, to be able to both put minds to sleep and to awaken them. That is to say, by his specific astrological positioning—being situated one way or another—he can wonderfully blunt or sharpen the intellect, weaken or strengthen it, and disturb or soothe it. 

Therefore, whenever you strive to cultivate any spirit, you will not only observe that its patron planet is fortunate and powerful, but you will also choose a **Moon** that is opportunely directed toward it. 

Furthermore, the substance of the spirit is never properly created or restored through the influence of **Saturn** alone; rather, the spirit is always called back by him from external things to the innermost parts, and often from the lowest things to the highest. Hence, Saturn leads one toward the contemplation of more secret and lofty things. 

Nevertheless, the power of **Mars** and **Saturn** can occasionally, though rarely, benefit the spirit as a medicine: either by warming it when necessary and by exciting and expanding it <note>an effect attributed to the heat of Mars</note>, or conversely, by restraining that which is too volatile <note>the "binding" or "contracting" quality of cold Saturn</note>. 

Indeed, the nature of the spirit is primarily created and restored through those things which are in harmony with those four planets <note>the Sun, Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury</note>. But if you turn excessively and without discretion toward things belonging to the **Sun**, you will eventually sharpen the spirit, dry it out, and dissolve it. Similarly, if you turn toward things of **Venus**, you will gradually liquefy or blunt it. If you rely only on things of **Mercury**, you will profit very little from them. 

It will therefore be worthwhile to use things of **Jupiter** first and foremost, and to mix the others with them in moderation, and to use more frequently those things that are equally common to all of them...

Furthermore, this Jovial quality is found in wine that is fragrant, clear, and somewhat sweet and astringent; in the whitest sugar; and in the whitest wheat. To Jupiter also belongs **manna** <note>a sweet, medicinal resin collected from trees, often the flowering ash</note>, provided it is strengthened by the infused virtue of the **myrobalan** <note>an astringent plum-like fruit from India, highly prized in Renaissance pharmacology for balancing the humors</note>. # **ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.**

¶ ...grace and sweetness proper to **Jupiter**, which indeed contributes above all else to the creating and nourishing of the **spirit** <term>spirit: (*spiritus*) the subtle "vapor" that bridges the gap between the physical body and the immaterial soul</term>. Indeed, most of those things are **Jovial** <note>original: *Iouia*; pertaining to the planet Jupiter, associated with growth, health, and kingly balance</note> which we discussed in the book *On Long Life* <note>A reference to *De Vita Longa*, the second part of Marsilio Ficino's *Three Books on Life*</note>, as both preserving youth and being healthy for the elderly.

¶ But if the sweetness is quite minimal, and it contains much that is sharp and **astringent** <note>original: *stiptici*; a dry, puckering sensation in the mouth</note>, or even a certain bitterness, it is judged to be **Solar** <note>pertaining to the Sun, associated with vital heat and clarity</note>.

¶ A similar reasoning and distinction exists regarding odors; for these are the close relatives of flavors. The same applies to colors. Indeed, colors that are watery, white, green, somewhat saffron-colored, or similar to the colors of violets, roses, and lilies—as well as odors of that kind—relate to **Venus**, and the **Moon**, and **Mercury**. However, sapphire-blue colors, which are also called "airy," as well as purple colors, mostly...

<summary>Ficino explains how to identify planetary influences (Jupiter, the Sun, Venus, Moon, and Mercury) through sensory qualities like taste, smell, and color. He notes that Jovial substances are life-giving and restorative, while Solar substances are sharper and more bitter.</summary>
<keywords>Jupiter, Solar influence, medical astrology, planetary qualities, De Vita Longa, colors and odors, Venus, Moon, Mercury</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the classification of planetary qualities, specifically focusing on the sensory characteristics (smell, taste, sound) that distinguish Solar, Jovial, and other planetary influences on the human spirit.</meta>

...and cloves and musk <note>original: *chariophyl* and *muscus*; both prized in the Renaissance for their potent, warming scents</note>, but by smell, not by taste or sight. Ambergris <note>original: *ambra*; a rare substance found in the sea, used here to strengthen the vital spirits</note> is for the most part **Solar** and **Jovial**.

Saffron <note>original: *crocus*</note> is **Solar** in all respects, even though its color and smell are dedicated by astrologers to all the Graces <note>The three Graces represent the harmonious and attractive qualities of the planets</note>. Its flavor, however, belongs properly to the **Sun**.

<note>blue bracket in margin</note> Finally, all fragrant and aromatic things, insofar as they provide a pleasing odor, pertain to **Jupiter**, **Venus**, and **Mercury**, just as they do to the **Sun**. Although among these, the sharper scents belong more to the **Sun**, while the more subtle <note>original: *obtusiora*; literally "blunted," referring to scents that are rounded and sweet rather than piercing</note> scents belong rather to **Venus** and **Mercury**. Those that are properly balanced <term>balanced: (*temperata*) the state of perfect health where the four qualities—hot, cold, moist, and dry—are in harmony</term> in smell, taste, hearing, sight, and touch belong specifically to **Jupiter**.

Pleasant and soothing sounds and songs also look toward all the Graces and **Mercury**. But those that are very threatening and mournful display the influence of **Mars** and **Saturn**. <note>red bracket in margin</note>

Nor should you wonder that we attribute so much to colors, odors, and voices. For flavors indeed pertain chiefly to the natural spirit <term>natural spirit: (*spiritus naturalis*) in Ficino's medical system, the subtle vapor that governs digestion and nutrition</term>; odors, however...

<summary>Ficino organizes sensory experiences like fragrances and music into a planetary hierarchy, associating balanced, pleasant sensations with Jupiter and Mercury, while attributing harsh or mournful ones to Mars and Saturn. He argues that these sensory qualities directly affect the different levels of the human spirit.</summary>
<keywords>astrological medicine, Jupiter, Sun, sensory perception, fragrances, music therapy, spiritus naturalis, Mars, Saturn</keywords>

# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...is moved and formed. And as the spirit itself turns out to be, so in turn it produces a certain disposition of the soul and the overall quality of the body. Finally, when it is appropriately exposed to the Graces <note>The "Graces" in Ficino's work refer to the benefic planetary influences—the Sun, Jupiter, and Venus—which bestow health, charm, and vitality.</note> through those things which are proper to the Graces—since it was also naturally in agreement with them—it immediately brings back the wonderful gifts of the Graces through their rays, which are both active everywhere and akin to the spirit itself.

### Natural and even artificial things have occult virtues from the stars, through which they expose our spirit to those same stars.
### Chapter XII.

**W**hen we say that the spirit is exposed to the Graces through those things belonging to the Graces, we believe it is accommodated not only through qualities that are seen, heard, smelled, and tasted, but also through those that are touched. Remember, therefore, that heat is in the first degree <term>degree: (*gradus*) a measurement of intensity in Galenic medicine and astrology, ranging from 1 (mild) to 4 (extreme)</term> of **Jupiter**. In the second degree, of the **Sun** with **Jupiter**. In the third, of **Mars** with the **Sun**. In the fourth, of **Mars**.

Cold is in the first degree of **Venus**. In the second, of the **Moon**. In the third, of the **Moon** with **Saturn**. In the fourth, of **Saturn**.

Moisture is in the first degree of **Mercury** with **Jupiter**. In the second, of **Venus** with the **Moon**. In the third, of the **Moon** with **Venus**. In the fourth, of the **Moon**, when it is joined to **Venus** and **Mercury**.

Dryness is in the first degree of **Jupiter**. In the second, of **Mercury** with the **Sun**. In the third, of the **Sun** with **Mars**. In the fourth, of **Mars** with **Saturn**.

This is gathered in summary from the qualities of the planets which Ptolemy describes in his *Tetrabiblos* <note>original: *quadripartito*; literally "the four-part book," the foundational 2nd-century astrological work by Claudius Ptolemy</note>.

<summary>Ficino explains how the human spirit attracts planetary 'gifts' through sensory exposure and provides a technical breakdown of the four elemental qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) across four degrees of planetary intensity.</summary>
<keywords>astrological medicine, four degrees, planetary qualities, Ptolemy, Tetrabiblos, Graces, spiritus, Jupiter, Saturn, Mars</keywords>

...the moisture of the Moon and Venus is the second degree.

The total harmony fused from all the stars inclines toward heat and moisture. For the heat of **Mars** is immense, as is that of the **Sun**; and the tempered heat of **Jupiter** overcomes the immense cold of **Saturn** and the slight cold of **Venus** and the **Moon**, so that in that total mixture, heat exceeds cold. Likewise, the moisture of the **Moon** and **Venus**, which is nearest and most related to us, along with the tempered moisture of **Jupiter**, overcomes the great dryness of **Saturn** and **Mars**, as well as the moderate dryness of the **Sun**. 

Heat and moisture, therefore, dominate cold and dryness; and similarly, heat dominates moisture. It is just as it is in a healthy human body, which is tempered to a celestial consonance: from the heat and dryness of the heart, the heat and moisture of the liver, and again from the cold and moisture of the brain, there coalesces a certain complexion <term>complexion: (*complexio*) the specific balance of the four humors and qualities that determines an individual’s health and temperament</term> that leans toward heat and moisture, with heat reigning moderately. For the heat of the heart and liver overcomes the cold of the brain, and the moisture of the liver and brain exceeds the dryness of the heart. 

Nor indeed would I wish it to be overlooked by us that such a harmony exists from the fixed stars common to all, just as we have described regarding the planets. For astrologers consider the planets to be similar in nature to those fixed stars. To what end is all this? So that you may remember that our spirit <note>In Ficinian medicine, the "spirit" is a subtle vapor of blood that acts as the bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note> and our body—through a certain temperament inclining toward heat and moisture, whether established by nature or sought through art <note>By "art," Ficino refers to the intentional use of medicine, diet, music, and talismans to alter one's internal balance.</note>—can be accommodated to the celestial bodies, and can claim the celestial gifts for itself.

¶ However, we do not say that our spirit is prepared for celestial things only through those qualities of things that are known to the senses.

<summary>Ficino explains that the overall influence of the heavens favors heat and moisture, mirroring the healthy balance of the human heart, liver, and brain. He argues that the human spirit can be tuned to these celestial influences either by natural birth or through "artful" medical and magical practices.</summary>
<keywords>celestial harmony, heat and moisture, complexion, spirit, fixed stars, astrology, medical theory, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

->**ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.**<-

But even much more through certain properties implanted in things from the heavens, and hidden from our senses, and finally scarcely known to reason. For since properties of this kind, and their effects, cannot consist of elemental virtue <note>original: "elementali virtute"; referring to the powers of the four elements—earth, water, air, and fire</note> alone, it follows that they proceed uniquely from the life and spirit of the world through the very rays of the stars: and therefore through them, the spirit is affected as much and as quickly as possible, and is vehemently exposed to celestial influences. 

For this reason, certainly, the emerald, hyacinth <note>likely zircon or sapphire in this period's nomenclature</note>, sapphire, topaz, ruby, unicorn horn, and especially the **bezoar stone**—as it is called among the Arabs—are endowed with hidden properties of grace. And therefore, not only when taken internally, but even if they touch the flesh and are warmed there, they release their virtue and convey a celestial force into the spirits, by which they defend themselves against plague and poisons. 

Now, that these and similar things perform such actions by celestial virtue is proven by the fact that, even when taken in a tiny weight, they have no small impact in their action. This is something scarcely ever granted to elemental quality—except perhaps to fire, which is notably celestial. For a material virtue, in order to act greatly, requires much matter; but a **formal virtue** <term>formal virtue: (virtus formalis) a power derived from the "form" or essence of a thing rather than its physical bulk; in Renaissance thought, this was often linked to astrological influence</term> is most powerful even with the smallest amount of matter. 

By a similar virtue, the "Phoebean" peony <note>named for Phoebus Apollo, the god of healing and the Sun; peonies were thought to be solar plants</note>, when touching the flesh, arms the spirits against the "falling sickness" <note>epilepsy</note> by a vapor infused into them within. Likewise, coral and chalcedony act against the illusions of black bile <note>melancholy or depression</note>, especially [those] of **Jupiter** and **Venus**...

                                                                    m.i.

...by that virtue. And other things similarly. By a certain property of this kind, **myrobalans** <note>A dried fruit from various tropical trees, highly prized in medieval and Renaissance medicine for purging humors.</note> preserve youth, sharpen the senses, and benefit the intellect and memory: especially because of **Jupiter**, who tempers **Saturn** <note>In astrology, Jupiter is the "Great Benefic" who softens the cold, melancholic influence of Saturn.</note>, and **Mercury**, the friend of the senses. Someone might perhaps think that this tree existed in Paradise for the prolongation of life.

¶ They consecrate the **agate** stone to Mercury. Hence physicians agree that it is effective for eloquence and sight, and against poisons. **Serapio** <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century Arabic medical author often cited in Europe.</note> writes that he who wears a **hyacinth** <note>In this period, "hyacinth" usually referred to a blue or yellow-orange gemstone, likely sapphire or zircon.</note> or a seal made from it is safe from lightning; and he reports that this virtue of it is very widely known. If it has this power, we believe it holds it from Jupiter. The **aetites** stone, or eagle-stone <note>A hollow stone believed to be found in eagle nests; they were thought to rattle and were used as charms to prevent miscarriage or induce labor.</note>, has from **Lucina**—that is, **Venus** and the **Moon**—the power to quickly and very easily induce birth when applied to the womb. **Rhazes** <note>Abū Bakr al-Rāzī (865–925), a Persian polymath and one of the most influential physicians in the Islamic Golden Age.</note>, with Serapio confirming, says he has frequently experienced this himself. 

Perhaps also from **Phoebus** [Apollo], who transfixed the Python, we say the **Cretan dittany** <note>A medicinal herb (*Origanum dictamnus*) famously used by the goddess Venus in Virgil's Aeneid to heal Aeneas's arrow wound.</note> has the power to resist venomous things and to draw iron from a wound. By the virtue of the **Sun**, **ginger** added to feasts wards off fainting. **Gentian** calms the rabies of a dog and puts serpents to flight. **Verbena** [vervain] is said to provide prophecy, joy, purifications, and sight. **Rue** and **zedoary** <note>A root similar to ginger, used as a spice and medicine.</note> act as a **theriac** <note>original: "theriacam"; a legendary compound "all-cure" antidote against poison.</note> against poisons. **Frankincense** supports the **vital and animal spirits** <note>In traditional medicine, the "animal spirit" was the subtle substance that traveled through the nerves to facilitate thought and movement.</note> against dullness, forgetfulness, and fear. 

But **sage** and **mint** possess the virtue of Jupiter; the former indeed puts paralysis to flight, while the latter strengthens the mind by its odor alone. By that same virtue, **cinquefoil** <note>original: "Pentaphillon," meaning "five-leaved."</note> resists...

# -> <note>A decorative red initial begins the chapter title.</note> ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS. <-

...against poisons: and if one of its leaves is drunk in wine twice daily, it cures ephemeral fever <note>A fever lasting only one day.</note>; three leaves cure tertian fever <note>A fever that returns every third day, often associated with malaria.</note>; while four leaves cure quartan fever <note>A fever that returns every fourth day.</note>. Ancient priests used this herb in their purifications for the sake of their own purity. **Agnus castus** <term>Vitex agnus-castus, or the "Chaste Tree," believed since antiquity to dampen sexual desire and preserve virginity.</term>, with **Saturn** prevailing, halts venereal movement. **Jasper**, however, stops the flow of blood.

Remarkable effects, however, are accustomed to occur when an elemental property serves an occult <note>In this context, "occult" means "hidden" or "celestial," referring to powers that cannot be explained by the simple qualities of hot, cold, wet, or dry.</note> property. This is seen in the **myrobalan** <note>A dried fruit used in medieval medicine to balance the "humors" of the body.</note> for strengthening the spirit and the body: not only does that celestial virtue act, but also a great styptic <note>Astringent or contracting.</note> power, as well as a significant aromatic virtue. These wonderfully prevent putrefaction and dissolution and strengthen the spirit. 

Now, consider how **saffron** <note>original: *crocus*</note> reaches the heart, expands the spirit, and provokes laughter: it is not only the hidden virtue of the **Sun** <note>original: *Sol*</note> that wonderfully effects this, but the very nature of saffron—subtle, expansive, aromatic, and bright—leads to the same result. What I say concerning simple [individual] substances, I wish to be understood as being said similarly about compounded ones. 

Tell me then: how does **Theriac** <term>A famous "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh, used from antiquity through the Renaissance.</term> help us against poison? It does not draw out the poison, for it constricts the bowels; nor does it entirely change the nature of the poison so suddenly. For the poison is not so weak and changeable. Rather, Theriac suddenly strengthens the **vital spirit**—which is itself very thin and changeable—by a certain proportional conformity to it. It does this to such an extent that the spirit itself, now strong as an active agent, uses the Theriac as a tool to immediately prevail over the poison, change it, and keep it away from the heart <note>original: *praecordiis*, the internal organs near the heart and diaphragm, considered the seat of vital emotions.</note>. 

But by what proportion or virtue does Theriac accomplish this? By a virtue both **Jovial** [of Jupiter] and **Phoebean** [of the Sun], which it gains from the mixing of many things according to a certain mutual proportion...

-> m.ii. <-

...to have claimed for itself from the mixed substances. There is, moreover, a **triple virtue** in it. One is that very celestial power I just mentioned. Second is another celestial property found previously in the herbs and aromatics themselves; when these are properly selected and combined, they serve the aforementioned virtue toward the same end. There is, furthermore, another virtue in most of its parts that is elemental rather than celestial—yet of such a kind that it contributes to the protection of the spirit. I am speaking of the **styptic** <note>Astringent or contracting quality used to "bind" the spirit so it does not dissipate.</note> and **aromatic** qualities. The former strengthens the spirit, while the latter nourishes it. 

Therefore, **Theriac** <term>A famous ancient "polypharmacy" or universal antidote consisting of dozens of ingredients, believed to cure all poisons and many diseases.</term> has obtained a wonderful power against premature old age and poison, because these three virtues conspire together toward the same result: 
1. One is celestial, acquired through skillful mixing. 
2. Another is celestial again, but naturally inherent in its individual parts. 
3. The third is entirely elemental. 

But that first power I mentioned—acquired from the heavens—would be much more admirable if it were composed not only with **Jovial** <note>Related to the planet Jupiter, associated in medicine with growth, health, and vital heat.</note> and **Solar** proportions and from things of that kind, but if a favorable time were also chosen through the observation of the celestial bodies. 

For just as a body is related to place and time, so motion and action are related to time. Therefore, just as certain bodies and their forms coalesce and are preserved in specific places and times, so too do certain actions obtain their efficacy from certain proper times. **Socrates** indicates this in the *Alcibiades* <note>Referring to the Platonic dialogue *Alcibiades I*, where Socrates discusses the importance of "the right moment" (kairos) in action.</note>; and
