<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section regarding the importance of celestial timing. Having discussed the composition of medicines like Theriac, the author now moves into the philosophical justification for "Electional Astrology"—the practice of choosing the right moment to act—citing Neoplatonic and Scholastic authorities.</meta>

# -> ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE <-

Proculus <note>Proculus (412–485 AD) was a Greek Neoplatonist philosopher who argued that everything in the lower world is a reflection of the divine, celestial world.</note> explains this. Indeed, Pythagoras, turning his mind toward this, called the very good and the perfection of things "Opportunity" <note>Original: *opportunitatem*. In Greek, this is *Kairos*—the "opportune moment" that distinguishes a successful action from a failed one.</note>. For according to Pythagoras and Plato, the first principle is the measure of all things, in such a way that it has distributed different places and times to different bodies and actions. 

Whence, just as certain things are not born, do not grow, and are not preserved properly or successfully anywhere else than here, nor at any other time than then; so too a material action, motion, or such-and-such an event, does not obtain efficacy and its perfect effect at any other time than when the **harmony of the heavens** sounds in unison from every side toward that same end. 

This harmony is indeed thought to have such great power that it often bestows a wonderful virtue, not only upon the labors of farmers and the arts of doctors—performed through herbs and aromatics—but even upon **images** <note>These "images" refer to astrological talismans or amulets, often carved into stones or cast in metal at specific moments to capture planetary power.</note> which are made from metals and stones by astrologers. But the topic of images now requires its own specific chapter. 

As far as it pertains to choosing the right hours for actions and works, this is strongly confirmed by Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the foundational astronomer and astrologer of antiquity.</note>, where he says in the *Centiloquium* <note>A collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy, highly influential in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>: "He who chooses what is better seems to differ in no way from him who possesses this by nature." In these words, he seems to confirm the power of both the celestial bodies and our own judgment and choice. 

Albertus Magnus <note>Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and philosopher who sought to reconcile Aristotelian science with Christian theology.</note> also says in his *Speculum* <note>Referring to the *Speculum Astronomiae*, a medieval treatise defending the use of certain types of astrology.</note>: "For the freedom of choice is not constrained by the election of a favorable hour; rather, in the beginnings of great things..."

m.iii.

<summary>The author argues that all successful actions depend on "Opportunity" or celestial timing, citing Pythagoras, Plato, and Ptolemy to justify the use of astrology in medicine, agriculture, and the creation of talismans.</summary>
<keywords>Electional Astrology, Celestial Harmony, Kairos, Proculus, Ptolemy, Albertus Magnus, Talismans, Free Will</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion on the necessity of choosing the right astrological moment (electional astrology) before transitioning into a detailed exploration of "astrological images" or talismans. ...to despise the election of the hour <note>original: *electionem horę*. In astrology, an "election" is the process of choosing a specific moment in time to begin an endeavor to ensure success.</note> is a rashness of judgment, not freedom.

### CHAPTER XIII.
### On the power of images according to the ancients, and of medicines acquired from heaven.

<note>A large, decorative blue initial "P" marks the beginning of this chapter.</note> **P**tolemy says in his *Centiloquium* <note>original: *Centiloquium*, a famous collection of 100 astrological aphorisms traditionally attributed to the 2nd-century astronomer Claudius Ptolemy.</note> that the forms <note>original: *effigies*</note> of lower things are subject to the faces of the heavens. He claims that the ancient sages were accustomed to craft certain **images** at those times when the planets entered into celestial "faces" that mirrored them, which served as archetypes for things here below. 

This is indeed confirmed by Haly <note>Haly Abenragel (Albohazen), an 11th-century Arab astrologer whose work was a cornerstone of European astrological theory.</note>, who says: a useful image of a serpent can be made when the Moon enters the celestial Serpent <note>This likely refers to the Moon’s nodes—the "Head" and "Tail" of the Dragon—or the constellation Serpens.</note>, or gazes upon it with a favorable aspect. Likewise, an image of a **scorpion** is effective when the Moon enters the sign of Scorpio, and this sign occupies one of the four cardinal angles <note>In astrology, the "angles" are the four most powerful points in a chart: the Ascendant, Midheaven, Descendant, and Imum Coeli.</note>. 

He says this was done in Egypt in his own day and that he was present for it. From a seal of a scorpion carved into a **bezoar stone** <note>original: *lapide Bezahar*. A bezoar is a stone-like mass found in the stomachs of certain animals, highly prized in the Renaissance as a universal antidote to poison.</note> during the proper time, the image was pressed into frankincense. This was then given as a drink to someone who had been stung by a scorpion, and they were healed instantly. 

The physician Hahamed <note>Likely Ahmad ibn Yusuf, an Egyptian mathematician and physician.</note> affirms that this is done with great utility, and Serapion <note>Johannes Serapion the Elder, a 9th-century Syrian Christian physician widely cited in medical texts.</note> confirms it. Furthermore, Haly relates that a certain wise man known to him made moving images through similar diligence—much like the one we read was created I know not how by Archytas <note>Archytas of Tarentum (c. 400 BC) was a Greek philosopher and mathematician rumored to have built a mechanical flying pigeon.</note>. 

These are like the images that Hermes Trismegistus <note>The legendary "Thrice-Great" founder of Hermeticism, regarded by Renaissance thinkers as a contemporary of Moses and a source of ancient wisdom.</note> says the Egyptians used to make from specific worldly materials; they were accustomed to draw down the souls of spirits <note>original: *Demonum*. In this context, these are "daemons" or celestial intelligences, not "demons" in the modern sense of evil spirits.</note> into them at the opportune moment, such as the soul of his own grandfather, Mer... <note>The text cuts off here; likely referring to Mercurius/Hermes.</note>

<summary>This page explores the ancient practice of "astrological images" (talismans), arguing that physical objects can capture celestial power if crafted at the correct planetary hour. It cites historical examples of healing seals and mechanical wonders as proof of this celestial-material link.</summary>
<keywords>astrological images, talismans, Ptolemy, Haly Abenragel, bezoar stone, Hermes Trismegistus, Egyptian magic, celestial influence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of "astrological images" (talismans) begun on the previous page. It moves from general principles to specific historical and mythological examples of how celestial power was captured in physical objects, citing Greek, Persian, Egyptian, and Hebrew traditions to support the legitimacy of this practice.</meta>

¶ ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.
...of Mercury; likewise those of a certain Phoebus <note>A common epithet for Apollo, the sun god.</note>, and of Isis and Osiris, descended in this way into statues to be beneficial to men, or even harmful. ¶ Similar to this is the story that Prometheus, by means of a certain clay figure, snatched away life and celestial light. Moreover, the Magi, followers of Zoroaster <note>The legendary Persian prophet and sage associated with the origins of magic and astrology.</note>, used a certain golden ball marked with celestial characters to evoke a spirit from Hecate <note>The goddess of magic, crossroads, and the moon. This "golden ball" refers to the *iynx* or "Hecate’s Circle," a ritual tool used in Theurgy.</note>. Into this ball a sapphire was inserted, and they turned it with a whip made of bull’s hide, chanting all the while. However, I willingly omit the incantations themselves, for Psellus the Platonist <note>Michael Psellos (11th century), a Byzantine scholar and philosopher who wrote extensively on Neoplatonism and demonology.</note> disapproves of and even mocks them. 

The Hebrews also, having been raised in Egypt, learned to build a golden calf—as their own astrologers believe—to capture the favor of Venus and the Moon against the influence of Scorpio and Mars, which was hostile to the Jews. Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> also, in his *Letter to Anebo*, testifies that images are effective; he adds that aerial spirits <note>original: *Dęmonas*. In this context, these are intermediary spirits of the air, not necessarily "demons" in the modern evil sense.</note> were accustomed to immediately insinuate themselves by means of certain vapors exhaling from their proper suffumigations <note>The ritual burning of incense or herbs corresponding to specific planets.</note>. 

Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who emphasized the use of ritual (theurgy) to commune with the divine.</note> confirms that in materials which are naturally suited to the higher powers—when they are collected opportunely and according to rite, and fused together from all sides—not only celestial forces and effects can be received, but even demonic and divine ones. Proclus and Synesius <note>Late antique philosophers who integrated Neoplatonism with astrological and magical theory.</note> fully agree. Truly wonderful works for health are achieved by physicians skilled in astrology, through things...

m.iiii <note>This is a printer's signature mark, indicating the position of the page in the physical book.</note>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous discussion regarding "astrological images" (talismans). The author is now comparing the efficacy of solid talismans against medicinal compounds like powders and ointments, arguing that the latter are more effective at absorbing and retaining celestial influences for the benefit of the human body.</meta>

...made from many components—that is, powders, liquids, ointments, and electuaries <term>medicinal pastes or conserves made by mixing drugs with honey or syrup</term>—can be created. These seem to possess a more probable internal logic and are better understood than [astrological] images. This is true, first, because powders, liquids, ointments, and electuaries, when prepared at the right time, receive celestial influences more easily and quickly than the harder materials from which images <note>In this context, "images" refers specifically to talismans, statues, or engraved gems.</note> are usually fashioned. Secondly, it is because they are either taken internally when already affected by the heavens and are thus transformed into our own substance, or at least, when applied externally, they adhere more closely and eventually penetrate the body. Furthermore, images are constructed from only one or very few substances, whereas these medicinal preparations can be blended from a great many ingredients according to one's judgment. 

For example, if a hundred "gifts" <note>Attributes or powers associated with a specific planet.</note> of the Sun or of Jupiter were scattered throughout a hundred different plants, animals, and similar things, you could gather these hundred things together once you found them and combine them into a single form. In such a preparation, you would seem to possess almost the entirety of the Sun or Jupiter. You surely know that a lower nature <note>The physical, earthly world.</note> cannot grasp all the powers of a higher nature <note>The celestial or divine realm.</note> in a single object. For this reason, those powers are dispersed among many different natures here among us. Thus, they can be gathered more conveniently through the works of physicians and similar practices than they can through the making of images. 

Accordingly, images made of wood perhaps have very little power. For wood is perhaps too hard to easily capture a celestial influx, and it is less tenacious in retaining that influence if it has received it. Above all, once wood has been torn from the womb of Mother Earth, it shortly thereafter loses almost all the vigor of the "world-life" <note>original: *mundane vite*. This refers to the *Anima Mundi* or World Soul, the vital force that Neoplatonists believed animated the entire universe.</note> and easily [decays] into [other] qualities...

<meta>This page continues the author's comparison between medicinal compounds and solid talismans. While the previous page argued that medicinal mixtures are more easily absorbed by the human body, this page argues that hard materials like gems and metals are superior at retaining celestial influences for much longer periods due to the "labor" the heavens exert in their formation.</meta>

[Decorative flourish] 
->ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS<-

...transformed into another nature. Gems and metals, indeed, even if they seem harder <note>In the sense of being more resistant or less porous.</note> for receiving a celestial gift, nevertheless retain it for a longer time—which Iamblichus <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy and the divine power residing in material objects.</note> confirms—once they have received it. Indeed, by their very hardness, they preserve for a very long time the traces and gifts of the "world-life" <note>original: "vitae mundanae"—the vital, animating spirit of the universe.</note> even after being pulled from the earth, which they once possessed while clinging to the soil. For this reason at least, they are judged to be suitable materials for capturing and holding celestial influences. 

It is also probable, as I stated in the previous book, that things so beautiful <note>Referring to the luster of gold or the brilliance of gems.</note> cannot be produced under the earth except by some immense effort of the heavens; and that the virtue <note>The specific occult power or property of an object.</note> once impressed by that celestial effort endures within them. For the heavens have labored for a very long time in "cooking" <term>coquendis: the Aristotelian and alchemical theory that minerals are "ripened" or "cooked" in the earth by the heat and influence of the stars over centuries</term> and hardening these things. 

But since you cannot easily compose many things of this kind <note>Unlike medicines, which can be blended from hundreds of herbs, a talisman is usually a single hard object.</note>, you are compelled to search diligently: which metal among the rest stands most prominently in the "order" of a particular star? Which stone is supreme in that hierarchy? You do this so that, by possessing at least one supreme member of a whole genus and order, you may grasp the powers of the rest of that category as much as possible. Through such a receptacle, you may "borrow" the celestial gifts corresponding to it. 

For example, in the Solar order <note>The "Chain of Being" or hierarchy of things on Earth that resonate with the Sun.</note>, beneath the "Phoebeian" <note>relating to Phoebus Apollo, the god of the Sun</note> man, the hawk or the rooster holds the highest rank among animals. Among plants, it is balsam or laurel. Among metals, gold. Among stones, the carbuncle <note>A deep red gemstone, usually a garnet or ruby, thought to glow with its own light.</note> or the *pantaura* <term>pantaura: a legendary stone mentioned in ancient texts, said to attract other stones and embody the essence of the Sun</term>. Among the elements, it is hot air; for fire itself is considered to be "Martial" <note>belonging to the planet Mars, due to its consuming and piercing heat</note>. Furthermore, when we say that the influence of the Sun, Jupiter, or Venus should be increased, we mean by reason of...

<summary>Ficino explains that gems and metals are excellent talismans because their hardness allows them to store celestial "virtue" imprinted by the heavens over long periods. He introduces the concept of planetary hierarchies, where specific animals, plants, and minerals (like the rooster, laurel, and gold for the Sun) act as the primary terrestrial conduits for a planet's power.</summary>

### The orders of things hanging from the stars, such as Solar things and the like; and how the spirit becomes Solar. Chapter 14.

**D**ecidedly, I have said elsewhere that from above, from each and every star—to speak in the Platonic manner—a series of things proper to it hangs down all the way to the lowest depths. Under the very Heart of Scorpio <note>The star Antares, the brightest in the constellation Scorpio.</note> we can place spirits and men of that same nature, as well as the animal known as the scorpion; also the herb *Asterion* <note>Literally "starry"; likely *Aster atticus* or Starwort.</note>, that is, the "star-like" herb, which is similar in shape to a star and shines at night. Physicians report that this herb has the quality of a rose and possesses a marvelous power against diseases of the genitals. 

[pilcrow] Under the celestial Serpent or the Serpent-bearer <note>The constellation Ophiuchus.</note>, they place Saturn and, in a certain way, Jupiter. Next, they place those spirits that often take on the forms of serpents. Furthermore, they include men who are similar to these, as well as living serpents; the "serpentine" herb <note>Possibly *Dracunculus vulgaris* or snakeweed.</note>; the stone called *draconite* <note>A mythical gemstone said to be extracted from the head of a living dragon.</note>, which is born in the head of a dragon; and likewise the stone commonly called "serpentine." And I shall bring forward other such things in what follows. 

[pilcrow] Under the solar star—that is, Sirius—they place the Sun first; then the "Phoebean" spirits <note>Spirits belonging to the order of Apollo (Phoebus), the sun god.</note>, whom Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Neoplatonist who described how divine light descends through different "chains" of being.</note> testifies have sometimes appeared to men in the form of lions or roosters. Next follow men who are very similar to these, as well as solar beasts. Then come Phoebean plants, and metals likewise, and gemstones, and vapor, and burning air. By a similar reasoning, they believe that from any star of the firmament, through some planet, a context of things is woven step-by-step under its... <margin>...property</margin> <margin>...all things</margin> <margin>...in order</margin> <margin>...solar</margin> <margin>...stones</margin> <margin>...each</margin> <margin>...of solar things</margin> <margin>...with the boy</margin> <margin>...he spoke</margin> <margin>...existing</margin> <margin>...similar</margin> <margin>...well</margin> <margin>...another</margin> <margin>...reasoning</margin> <margin>...of these</margin> <margin>...Proclus</margin> <margin>...small</margin> <margin>...small</margin> <margin>...under</margin> <margin>...son</margin> <margin>...to be</margin> <margin>...of the air</margin> <margin>...of the stars</margin> <margin>...these things</margin> <margin>...was</margin>

...and the swan, and the raven. Nor for any other reason does the lion fear the cock, except because in the Solar order <note>original: "ordine phebeo" — belonging to Phoebus Apollo, the sun god.</note> the cock is superior to the lion. For the same reason, says Proclus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on how earthly things relate to divine powers.</note>, an Apollonian spirit—which sometimes appeared in the form of a lion—immediately disappeared when a cock was presented to it. Indeed, in these animals the heart is especially Solar. I also judge that the sea-calf <note>A seal (*Phoca vitulina*), believed in this period to be under the protection of the Sun and Leo.</note> is subject to the heart of the celestial Lion <note>The constellation Leo.</note>; and for this very reason, its hide—when it cinches the bare skin with a buckle made from the same animal's bone—delivers one from pain of the kidneys. For against pain of this kind, astrologers are accustomed to use the influences of that constellation. By the same power, perhaps, this skin is said to protect us from lightning. Among plants, the palm tree is Solar <note>original: "phebea" — of Phoebus.</note>; and especially the laurel, which by its virtue repels poisonous things and lightning. The ash tree also, with a similar faculty against poison...

...drives poisonous things away from afar. That the Lotus is Solar <note>original: "phebeam" — belonging to Phoebus Apollo, the sun god.</note> is proven by both its round leaves and its fruits, as well as the opening of its leaves by day and their folding by night. That the Peony is Solar is indicated not only by its medicinal power <note>original: "uirtus" — its inherent "virtue" or occult efficacy.</note> but also by its name <note>The peony was named after Paeon, the physician to the Greek gods, often identified with Apollo.</note>. To the same category belong flowers and herbs that close when the sun departs and immediately open when it returns, and those that continuously turn toward the sun <note>Heliotropic plants, such as the sunflower or marigold.</note>.

Furthermore, gold is Solar, as is the *elitis* stone <note>Likely the heliotrope or "sunstone," described in antiquity as having golden inclusions.</note> which imitates the sun with golden rays. Also the stone called the "Sun’s Eye" <note>original: "solis oculus"</note>, which has the shape of a pupil from which light flashes forth. Again, there is the carbuncle <note>A deep red gemstone, like a garnet or ruby, believed in the Middle Ages to glow with its own light in the dark.</note> which glows at night, or the *pantaura* <note>A legendary gemstone said to possess the combined powers of all other stones.</note> which contains within itself all the powers of stones, just as gold does for metals and the sun for the stars. Finally, many such things were indicated by us in the preceding sections.

From these things, if only it is permitted, you will be able to prepare an **electuary** <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup.</note> or an **ointment** under the dominion of the Sun, using that blood and the hearts of those animals, and the leaves and fruits of the aforementioned trees. Likewise, you may use the flowers and herbs, gold leaf, and the powders of those gemstones. To these, add: saffron, balsam, aromatic reed <note>Calamus.</note>, frankincense, musk, amber, aloe wood, ginger, mastic, spikenard, cinnamon, leopard’s bane <note>original: "doronico".</note>, citron peel, zedoary <note>A root similar to ginger.</note>, nutmeg, mace, and cloves. Mix these with yellow honey or with oils of balsam, mastic, laurel, or nard. Use this to warm the heart, the stomach, and the head, whether applied internally or externally, so that your own spirit may emerge as "Solar."

I say that from all these things, or at least from many of them, a composition should be made while the Sun is dominant. Whereby also under the same...

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s Book III of "Three Books on Life." ### ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...you may begin to exercise dominion: provided that you also dress in, inhabit, look at, hear, smell, imagine, think about, and desire Solar things. <note>Ficino argues that to capture the "spirit" of a planet, one must engage all five senses and the internal imagination with objects corresponding to that planet.</note> Likewise, you should imitate the life, dignity, and gifts of the Sun. You should spend time among Solar people and plants, and touch the laurel <note>original: "laurum" — the bay laurel, sacred to Apollo (the Sun).</note> frequently.

However, it will be safer for your health to mix Jovial <note>Relating to the planet Jupiter, considered "the Great Benefic" in astrology, known for its temperate and life-sustaining qualities.</note> and Venereal <note>Relating to the planet Venus, which provides moisture and grace.</note> things together with the Solar. Use Venereal moisture especially as a moderator of Solar heat—such as the water and juice of roses and violets.

But medicines of this kind I have partly composed myself, first in the book *On Caring for the Health of Scholars* <note>original: "de curandis litteratis" — the first book of this trilogy.</note>, then in the book *On Long Life* <note>original: "de uita longa" — the second book of this trilogy.</note>; partly I have described those composed by others, and partly I have tempered <note>Adjusted the proportions of ingredients to balance their elemental qualities.</note> them. Likewise, we have spoken in our book *Against the Plague* <note>original: "libro contra pestem" — referring to his 1481 work, "Consilio contro la pestilenza."</note> about which herbs from the Sun and Jupiter possess wonderful powers against epidemics and poison. Among these, *Perforata* <note>St. John's Wort (*Hypericum perforatum*).</note> is called the "Flight of the Demon" <note>original: "fuga Daemonum" — a traditional name for St. John's Wort, believed to repel evil spirits and melancholy.</note>. It is thought to ward off the noxious vapors of evil demons from us by no other faculty than that of celestial graces. And if there is any other among the herbs, or a small stone like coral, it seems to effect the same thing. 

There is also a certain Lunar herb, handed down by Mercury <note>The god Mercury/Hermes was often credited with revealing the hidden virtues of plants in Hermetic tradition.</note>, with blue and round leaves; while the moon is waxing, it produces one leaf daily, and as it wanes, it sheds them. This plant grants "Lunar years" to the user. <note>This may refer to a specific cycle of longevity or a symbolic preservation of the body aligned with the moon's phases.</note> But now let us return to the subject of images by a certain other beginning.

### ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

->Things more than images.<-
[pilcrow] Chapter XV.

**S**hould you obtain those little stones which we recently described as Phoebic <note>original: "phębeos" — belonging to Phoebus Apollo, the personification of the Sun.</note>, there will be no need to carve images into them. You should hang them from your neck, enclosed in gold with threads of saffron-colored silk, at a time when the Sun is passing through Aries or Leo <note>Aries is the sign of the Sun's exaltation and Leo is its domicile; these are the zodiacal signs where the Sun’s influence is most potent.</note> and is either rising or occupying the mid-heaven <note>The "Medium Coeli" or tenth house, the highest point in an astrological chart.</note>, and while it looks upon the Moon.

[pilcrow] Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher whose works on the "hieratic art" or theurgy heavily influenced Ficino’s theories on planetary magic.</note> tells of little stones in the Lunar series <note>term: "series" — a Neoplatonic "chain of being" where earthly objects (stones, plants, animals) are linked to a specific planet by shared qualities.</note> that are much more powerful. First is **Selenite** <note>original: "Selinitim" — named for Selene, the Moon. In the Renaissance, this was often identified as a variety of gypsum or translucent marble believed to wax and wane with the lunar cycle.</note>. It does not only imitate the Moon in its shape, but also in its motion, for it is said to revolve along with the Moon. If you happen to find this, and hang it from your neck enclosed in silver with a silver thread while the Moon is entering Cancer or Taurus <note>Cancer is the Moon's domicile and Taurus is its exaltation.</note> and is occupying the angles <note>term: "angles" — the first, fourth, seventh, and tenth houses of an astrological chart, considered the most active and powerful locations for a planet.</note> suitable to her, you will eventually make your spirit Lunar. This occurs because the lunar stone itself, once warmed by you <note>original: "calefactus ab ste" — likely "a te," referring to the stone being activated by the wearer's body heat.</note>, continually inserts its own power into your spirits.

[pilcrow] He reviews another little stone named **Helioselenon** <note>original: "helioselinon" — from the Greek 'helios' (sun) and 'selene' (moon). A stone believed to embody the union of both luminaries.</note>. It naturally possesses the image of the Sun and Moon joined to the Sun...

<summary>Ficino explains how certain stones like Selenite and Helioselenon naturally capture planetary power without the need for carved images, provided they are worn as talismans at the correct astrological times.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 15 of Book III, "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Ficino has been discussing the "chains" or series of earthly objects that correspond to heavenly bodies. ### ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...be gathered together like Isis <note>original: "uelut Isidez cōgregare" — A reference to the Egyptian myth of Isis searching for and reassembling the scattered limbs of her husband, Osiris. Ficino uses this as a metaphor for the magus gathering disparate earthly materials to capture a single celestial influence.</note>. [pilcrow] But if only we could easily find a **Solar stone** somewhere, or a **Lunar** one—stones so extremely powerful in their own order, just as we have the **magnet** and iron under the series of the northern pole. 

Indeed, they say that **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A 1st-century Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker whose life was chronicled by Philostratus; he was a figure of great interest to Renaissance Neoplatonists.</note> found a Solar stone among the Indians, specifically named the **Pantaura** <note>original: "Pantaurā" — A legendary gemstone described as having the power to attract other stones, much as a magnet attracts iron.</note>, which sparkles like fire. It is said to be born four paces beneath the earth, and so much spirit dwells within it that the ground swells and is often split open where a stone of this kind is conceived. Thus, it draws other little stones to itself, just as a magnet draws iron. 

But this **Herculean stone** <note>term: "Herculean stone" (lapis Herculeus) — A traditional name for the lodestone or magnet, named after the city of Heraclea.</note> seizes us even more vehemently to contemplate it at present. We see in the mariner's compass <note>original: "specula nautarū" — literally "the mariners' watchtower," but here referring to the housing of the nautical compass.</note>, which indicates the pole, a balanced needle affected at its tip by a magnet, moving toward the **Bear** <note>original: "ursā" — referring to the constellation Ursa Major or Ursa Minor, which contains the North Star.</note>, for the magnet clearly draws it there. 

Because the power of the Bear prevails in this stone, it is transferred from the stone into the iron, and the Bear draws both to itself. Now, power of this kind was not only infused into it from the beginning, but it is also continually nourished by the rays of the Bear. Perhaps **amber** <note>original: "succinū"</note> relates in the same way to the other pole <note>Ficino speculates here on a "Southern" equivalent to the North Pole's magnetic attraction.</note> and to chaff. 

But tell me in the meantime: why does a magnet draw iron everywhere? It is not because they are similar, for otherwise a magnet would draw another magnet much more, and iron would draw iron. Nor is it because the magnet is higher in the order of bodies; on the contrary, metal is superior to a small stone. What then? Both are indeed enclosed in the celestial order that follows the Bear. But the magnet holds a higher degree within that specific property of the Bear; the [iron holds] a lower...

<summary>Ficino discusses the legendary Solar stone "Pantaura" and examines the nature of magnetic attraction, arguing that the magnet and iron are both linked to the constellation of the Bear.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 15 of Book III, "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Having previously discussed the magnet’s attraction to iron, Ficino is now explaining the metaphysical principle of "chains" or "series" where higher celestial bodies influence lower earthly beings through shared qualities.</meta>

### ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...indeed iron. Moreover, in that same chain of things, the higher part indeed draws that which is lower and turns it toward itself; or it moves it in whatever other way it pleases, or affects it by a power previously infused into it. The lower, in turn, is converted to the higher by that same infusion, or is otherwise moved, or utterly affected. 

[pilcrow] <note>This red mark in the text indicates a new thematic section.</note> Thus, in the **series of the Sun** <note>The "chain" of all things on Earth that share Solar qualities, such as gold, sunflowers, or certain people.</note>, a lower man admires a higher one. In the **Jovian order** <note>Related to the planet Jupiter, associated with law, governance, and majesty.</note>, he venerates him. In the **Martian order** <note>Related to Mars, the god of war.</note>, he fears him. 

¶ In the **Venereal order** <note>Related to Venus, goddess of love.</note>, the lower is snatched toward the higher by the fire of love and deserts his own self. In the **Mercurial order** <note>Related to Mercury, god of communication and intellect.</note>, the lower man is always learning from or being persuaded by the higher. In the **Lunar order**, the lower more often takes his motion from the higher. In the **Saturnine order**, he takes his rest. 

[pilcrow] But when I had explored these things thus far, I was greatly pleased. While I was still a young man, I thought to engrave—as much as I was able—the figure of the **Celestial Bear** <note>original: "caelestis ursae" — Referring to the constellations Ursa Major or Ursa Minor (the Big and Little Dipper). In talismanic magic, the image of a constellation was believed to "trap" its specific power within a stone.</note> upon a magnet at a time when the Moon might look upon it more favorably. Then, I planned to hang it from my neck by an iron thread. 

I hoped, indeed, that I would finally become a master of the power of that constellation. But when I had explored the matter more deeply, I found at last that the influences of that constellation were mostly **Saturnine** and **Martian** <note>Ficino grew cautious because Saturn and Mars were considered "malefic" or harmful planets in traditional astrology.</note>. I learned from the **Platonists** <note>The Neoplatonic philosophers such as Plotinus or Porphyry whom Ficino translated.</note> that evil spirits <note>term: "demons" (daemones) — In the Renaissance, these were often viewed as intermediary spirits rather than purely "devils," though here Ficino specifically warns of "bad" ones.</note> are mostly Northern. This is also confessed by the astronomers of the **Hebrews**, who place harmful and Martian spirits in the North, but propitious and Jovian spirits in the South. I learned from the theologians and from **Iamblichus** <note>A 4th-century philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy and the use of divine images.</note> that the makers of images <note>talisman-makers</note> are more often occupied by evil spirits and thus deceived. 

[pilcrow] I indeed saw a small stone of Flo... <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to a stone from Florence.</note>

### [Blue initial D] ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...brought by a certain **Lorenzo** <note>original: "Laurentiam" — Likely referring to Lorenzo de' Medici or a merchant associate.</note> from India. It had been extracted there from the head of a dragon; it was round, in the shape of a coin, and naturally marked with many dots arranged like stars. When it was soaked in vinegar, it moved for a short while in a straight line, then diagonally, and soon it was carried in a circle until the vapor of the vinegar evaporated. I certainly thought that a small stone of this kind possessed the nature and, as it were, the form of the **celestial Dragon** <note>The constellation Draco, which winds through the northern sky.</note>. It also received its motion from that source, insofar as it was made more familiar to that Dragon or the firmament through the spirit of the vinegar or a stronger wine. Therefore, he who wore this stone and often soaked it in vinegar might perhaps receive some power from that Dragon, which with its twin coils entwines the **Great Bear** <note>Ursa Major</note> on one side and the **Lesser Bear** <note>Ursa Minor</note> on the other.

¶ There also exists, near the constellation of **Scorpio**, the **Serpent-Bearer** <note>original: "serpentarius" — The constellation Ophiuchus.</note>, which looks like a man girded by a serpent, holding the head of the serpent in his right hand and the tail in his left; his knees are as if bent, and his head is tilted slightly back. I have read that the **Magi of the Persians** advised their king to engrave this image upon a **hematite stone** <note>original: "emathiti" — A reddish-black mineral associated with blood and Mars.</note> and enclose it in a gold ring, but in such a way that he should insert the root of the **serpent-plant** <note>likely *Aristolochia* or *Serpentaria*, plants historically used as antivenoms</note> between the stone and the gold. They claimed that the wearer of this ring would be safe against poison and venomous diseases—provided, of course, that you make it while the **Moon** is looking upon the Serpent-Bearer. **Pietro d'Abano** <note>original: "Petrus Aponensis" (1250–1316) — An influential Italian physician, astrologer, and philosopher whom Ficino frequently cites.</note> confirmed the power of this image. Furthermore, they are referred back to the same stars to which these animals belong. 

This **Alectorius** <note>original: "Alectorius" — A legendary stone, often described as clear like water or crystal, believed to be found in the gizzards of old roosters or capons.</note>, drawn from the stomach of an old rooster, is powerful with **Solar power**. Through this power, **Dioscorides** <note>An ancient Greek physician and pharmacologist whose work was a primary source for Renaissance medicine.</note> says it has often been discovered that a man may fight and remain unconquered if he carries this small stone in his mouth. 

The same author says that the reddish **Chelidonian stone** <note>original: "Celidonium" — From the Greek 'chelidon' (swallow); these "swallow-stones" were used in folk medicine and magic to treat various ailments.</note>, extracted from a swallow, cures the melancholic person and renders them lovable and fit for duties. This stone certainly possesses this quality from **Jupiter**, following what we have said: namely, that all things existing everywhere below the Moon are "stellar" in nature. 

This confirms that truly **Platonic** saying: that this **machine of the world** <note>original: "mundi machinam" — A common Renaissance metaphor for the structured, functional, and divinely designed universe.</note> is so interconnected with itself that celestial things exist on earth in a terrestrial condition, and in the heavens, in turn, terrestrial things exist with celestial dignity. Likewise, in the hidden life of the world, and in the **Mind** that is the queen of the world, celestial things are present in a vital...

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s argument regarding the "stellar" nature of terrestrial objects. # **ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS**

...which we assigned in the earlier parts; both because many such things, when they are gathered by the physician and astrologer, are crushed, mixed, and cooked <note>original: "concoquuntur" — In Renaissance medicine, "concoction" refers to the process of ripening, refining, or "digesting" substances through heat to reach their peak medicinal potency.</note> under a specific star. During this process, by the logic of their own cooking and fermentation, they gradually undergo a new form; they acquire this very form from a certain celestial stimulus <note>original: "fomento" — Literally a 'kindling' or 'fomentation,' implying the warming, life-giving influence of the stars.</note>, with the rays then acting within them; and for that reason, the form is considered celestial. 

But when a metal or a small stone is engraved in a single moment, it does not seem to receive a new quality, but only a figure <note>original: "figuram" — Here meaning the external shape or the geometric image carved into the material.</note>. Furthermore, that motion of engraving does not proceed through the proper stages of digestion <note>original: "digestionis" — The natural, gradual process of development or maturation that substances undergo in nature.</note> that natural alteration and generation are usually seen to follow. Since celestial nature, as the guide of lower nature, usually proceeds in a certain natural rhythm—and breathes its influence upon things that proceed in that same way—many people rightly doubt whether images of this kind possess any celestial power. 

I myself am often in doubt; and unless all of antiquity and all the astrologers believed they possessed a marvelous power, I would deny they had any. Yet, I would not deny it entirely. For I believe—unless someone should persuade me otherwise—that they have at least some power for promoting health, especially by reason of the specific material chosen. Even so, I judge that a much greater power resides in medicines and ointments that have been breathed upon by the favor of the stars. 

What I meant just now by "the reason of the chosen material," I will explain in what follows. 

¶ As for those things which, according to the opinion of the magi <note>original: "magorum" — Learned sages or practitioners of "natural magic," which was the study of the hidden properties of nature.</note> or astrologers, can be cited in favor of images to help interpret **Plotinus** <note>A 3rd-century philosopher and the father of Neoplatonism; Ficino is specifically commenting on Plotinus's views regarding how ancient priests drew down divine power into statues.</note>, I will briefly present them next—if I first warn you here: lest...

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s nuanced defense of "natural magic." Having previously expressed doubt about the efficacy of man-made talismans (images), he now clarifies his personal practice while arguing for the logical consistency of celestial medicine.</meta>

...you might think I am trying to prove the use of images; but I am merely narrating <note>original: "narrare" — Ficino uses this rhetorical device to report on controversial topics (like talismans) without explicitly endorsing them as his own dogma, thus protecting himself from charges of heresy.</note>. For I myself use medicines tempered <term>tempered: the process of "temperamentum," where a physician balances the qualities of substances—hot, cold, moist, dry—to align them with a patient’s constitution or a specific planetary influence.</term> to the heavens, not images. And in this way, I advise others daily. But you, if you concede that God has infused wonderful virtues into things below the Moon <note>original: "infra Lunam" — In the Aristotelian cosmos, the "sublunary" realm is the Earthly world of change and decay, while the "celestial" realm above the moon is eternal and perfect.</note>, you must concede even more wonderful ones to the celestial bodies. Furthermore, if you judge it permissible for a human to use lower things for prosperous health, you should judge it permissible to use higher things as well. And you should judge it right to temper lower things to the pattern of the higher by the skill of doctors, just as they were also tempered by God from the beginning.

->## **On the power of the heavens. On the force of rays, from which images are thought to obtain their power.**<-

### **Chapter XVI**

**I**mmense indeed is the magnitude, power, and motion of the celestial bodies, which causes all the rays of all the stars to penetrate the mass of the Earth—which is like a mere point <note>original: "quasi punctum" — A standard astronomical claim of the time, emphasizing that the Earth is mathematically insignificant in size compared to the vastness of the celestial spheres.</note> compared to the heavens—instantly and very easily, in a straight line all the way to the center. All astronomers admit this. And there, as the Pythagoreans and Platonists believe, these rays are at their strongest: both because they strike the center directly from all sides, and because they are all gathered together into a narrow space. By their intensity, the dry matter of the Earth there, far removed from moisture, is utterly set on fire; and having been set on fire, it is thinned out and dispersed. Through passages on all sides, it breathes out both fires and sulfur <note>Ficino is describing the "central fire" of the Earth, fueled by concentrated stellar rays, which was used to explain volcanic activity and the formation of minerals.</note>. But they think this fire is very dark, like a kind of burning devoid of light—just as in the heavens, there exists light devoid of burning. Fire, however, between the celestial...

<summary>Ficino argues that if Earthly medicines have power, celestial bodies must have even greater influence. He explains how stellar rays penetrate to the center of the Earth, creating a "dark fire" that generates minerals and heat.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, celestial influence, sublunary, tempering, stellar rays, Pythagorean physics, central fire, astrology in medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s exploration of how celestial power reaches the Earth. Having established that the universe is a living organism, he now explains the mechanism by which stellar rays penetrate solid matter, providing a physical justification for the efficacy of talismans and stones.</meta>

->**ON OBTAINING LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.**<-

...and the lower region; it joins light with heat. They believe, moreover, that the fire breathing forth from the center [of the Earth] is the Vestal fire <note>original: "ignem vestalem" — Ficino refers to the sacred fire of the Roman goddess Vesta, whom he associates here with the Earth's internal vital heat, a concept central to his "living Earth" theory.</note>. Indeed, they used to think Vesta was the life and the divinity of the Earth. This is why the ancients built Vesta’s temple in the middle of cities and placed a perpetual fire in the center.

But lest we wander too far, let us now conclude that if the rays of the stars soon penetrate the entire Earth, it cannot easily be denied that they suddenly penetrate metal and small stones when they are being carved with images <note>original: "caelantur imaginibus" — this refers to the practice of engraving astrological symbols or figures onto gems or medals at specific times to "capture" planetary power.</note>; and that they imprint wonderful or at least some kind of gifts <term>gifts: "dotes" — inherent qualities or powers granted by a higher source, such as the stars, which give a substance its specific medical or magical properties.</term> upon them. 

Since they even generate most precious things <note>Ficino refers to the classical belief that gemstones and precious metals "grow" in the Earth's "womb" under the influence of the sun and stars.</note> in the bellies of the Earth. But who would deny that rays penetrate through these? For even air, and its qualities, and sound—which is less powerful—suddenly pass through solid things and affect them with their own quality. Now truly, if hardness resisted the penetrating rays, light would pass through air much faster than water, and through water much faster than glass, and through glass similarly than crystal. 

But since light strikes through all solid things in the same moment it strikes through liquids, it is clearly evident that hardness in no way resists the rays. Therefore, they will say it must not be denied that metals receive celestial rays and influences <term>influences: "influxus" — literally a "flowing in" of celestial energy, the basis for the word "influence."</term>, and even preserve them for the time then destined for them from heaven. Thus, they preserve a certain virtue <term>virtue: "virtutem" — an active, occult power or efficacy inherent in a substance.</term> created from the contact of the converging rays. What then? If harder matter, by the very fact that it seems to resist a powerful cause, exposes itself more to its blows: just as a sword cuts through wood even when it is under wool...

<summary>Ficino argues that celestial rays penetrate even the hardest metals and stones instantaneously, imprinting them with stellar "virtues" just as they create gems deep within the Earth. He uses the physics of light passing through glass and crystal to justify how talismans can capture and store astrological influence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that celestial influences are not merely physical forces like heat, but are "living" energies capable of imbuing objects with specific powers. Ficino is moving from the physical analogy of lightning to the metaphysical concept of the "spiritus" as a bridge between the stars and the human body.</meta>

...even through unshorn wool. In the same way, that ray of lightning sometimes dissolves the metal inside a container without damaging the leather hide itself <note>original: "corio... non laeso" — Ficino uses the phenomenon of lightning striking a sword inside a scabbard or coins inside a purse without burning the container as a natural proof that celestial forces can penetrate solids to reach their target.</note>. Since the celestial nature is incomparably more excellent than this fire of ours, we must not think the function of a celestial ray is merely the same as the work of fire’s rays that we clearly see—namely, to illuminate, heat, dry, penetrate, thin out, and dissolve—which are very well known to our senses. 

But these rays have many more and more wonderful powers and effects. Otherwise, both lower matter and our fleeting senses would be entirely equal to the divinity of heaven. But who would not know that the hidden virtues of things—which physicians call **specific virtues** <term>specific virtues: "vires speciales" — properties of a substance (like a magnet's pull) that cannot be explained by its elemental makeup of hot, cold, wet, or dry, but rather by its unique form or its origin in the stars.</term>—do not come from elemental nature, but from the celestial? 

Therefore, the rays can (as they say) imprint hidden and wonderful powers upon **images** <note>In Ficino’s practice, "images" refers to astrological talismans or seals engraved on stones during specific planetary alignments.</note> beyond those already known, just as they instill them into other things. For they are not inanimate like the rays of a lamp, but living and sentient, as if flashing forth through the eyes of living bodies; and they carry marvelous **gifts** <term>gifts: "dotes" — inherent qualities or talents granted by the stars.</term> with them from the imaginations and minds of the celestial beings. 

They also exert a most vehement force from the strong emotion of those beings and the very rapid motion of their bodies; and they act especially and most powerfully upon the *spirit* <term>spirit: "spiritus" — a subtle, air-like substance in the body that acts as a bridge, allowing the soul to communicate with the physical flesh. Ficino believed this spirit was the primary "receiver" for planetary energy.</term>, which is very similar to the celestial rays. Moreover, they act upon even the hardest bodies. For all these things are very weak compared to heaven. Furthermore, in various stars there are also various powers, and therefore their rays vary among themselves. Besides, from the strikes of rays falling in different ways <note>original: "radiorum ictibus... incidentibus" — This refers to the "aspects" or angles at which starlight hits the Earth, a fundamental concept in astrology.</note>, diverse virtues are born...

<summary>Ficino explains that celestial rays are not just heat and light but are living forces from the "minds" of the stars. He argues that these rays can penetrate solid matter to imbue talismans and the human spirit with "specific virtues."</summary>

¶ ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.
...are produced. Finally, in the mutual crossings of the rays, in one way or another—now here, now there, at this time or another—diverse powers suddenly arise from these effects, much more so and more quickly than in the various mixtures of elements and elemental qualities. This occurs even more quickly than in the tones and musical numbers sounding together <note>original: "percinentibus" — referring to the harmonic resonance of different notes played at once.</note> in various ways according to a musician. If you consider these things diligently, perhaps you will not doubt those who say that, by a certain sudden casting of rays, powers are imprinted upon **images** <note>Ficino uses "images" to refer to astrological talismans, often engraved gemstones, which he believes can "capture" the specific influence of a planet or constellation.</note>, and diverse powers are drawn from a diverse casting. 

Is it then so fast? I pass over fascinations <term>fascinations: "fascinatones" — the "evil eye" or the belief that the soul’s energy can be projected through the eyes to affect another person physically or mentally.</term> made by a certain sudden gaze, and the fiercest loves suddenly kindled by the rays of the eyes—which are themselves a kind of fascination, as we prove in our book *On Love* <note>original: "libro de amore" — Ficino refers to his famous commentary on Plato’s *Symposium*, where he explains romantic love as a physical/spiritual infection transmitted through the eyes.</note>. I pass over how quickly a bloodshot eye infects the one looking at it, and how a menstruating woman infects a mirror by her gaze. 

Is it not also said that certain families among the Illyrians and Triballians, when angry, kill men with a mere look? And that certain women in Scythia are accustomed to doing the same. Even the **catoblepas** <note>A legendary Ethiopian beast mentioned by Pliny the Elder, said to have a head so heavy it always looks down, but whose gaze is fatal.</note> and the **basilisks** <note>original: "reguli" — literally "little kings," the traditional name for the basilisk, a serpent king whose gaze or breath brought death.</note> kill men by the casting of rays from their eyes. The sea **torpedo** <note>The electric ray (Torpedo marmorata), famously cited by ancient and Renaissance writers as a primary example of an "occult" or hidden power because its numbing shock travels through a fisherman's spear or rod without a visible cause.</note> also suddenly numbs the hand by touch, even from a distance through a rod. Furthermore, the little fish called the **remora** <note>original: "echinnus" — here referring to the Echeneis remora or "suckerfish," which was legendary for its supposed ability to stop a massive ship in its tracks by merely clinging to the hull.</note> is said to stop a great ship by touch alone. 

Moreover, the tarantulas in Apulia, by some bite or even an unseen spirit, transform the mind and soul with a sudden stupor <note>original: "Phalangia... in apulia" — referring to the phenomenon of "Tarantism," a form of hysterical dancing mania believed to be caused by a spider's bite and cured by specific musical rhythms.</note>. What does a rabid dog do, even when the bite is not apparent? What of the broom-plant, and then what of the strawberry tree? Do they not stir up poison and madness with the lightest touch? Will you then deny that the celestial [influences] of the eyes...

...with their rays, by which they look upon and touch our things at the same time, immediately accomplish wondrous things? Truly, even now a pregnant woman, by a touch, immediately marks a limb of the unborn child with the sign of the thing she desired <note>Ficino refers to the theory of "maternal impressions," a long-held belief that a mother’s intense cravings or sudden shocks could physically mark the fetus.</note>. When you collect the herb hellebore: whether you pull the leaf downward or upward, by this sudden touch, you cause the hellebore to draw humors downward or upward <note>A common Renaissance botanical belief that the medicinal effect of a plant (as a laxative or emetic) changed depending on the direction it was harvested.</note>. 

¶ Is it not true that from the beginning of each thing to be generated, the celestial influxes—once the cooking <term>percoction: "percoctione" — the natural ripening of matter through internal or celestial heat.</term> and digestion of the matter is complete—bestow wondrous gifts not so much over time as in a single moment? Does it not happen, with the face of heaven favoring it, that countless frogs and similar living creatures often leap forth from the sands in a moment? <note>This refers to "spontaneous generation," the Aristotelian idea that life could spring instantly from inanimate matter like mud or sand under the right celestial conditions.</note> Such is the power and such the speed of the heavens upon prepared materials. 

Finally, if fire has this property—that it does in the briefest time what other things do in a long time, primarily because it is most similar to the heavens—who would doubt that the heaven accomplishes great things as if in a moment, even in material that is less prepared, just as a larger flame is accustomed to do? Why then, they ask, do you doubt that the heaven acts in nearly the same way in the construction of an image? 

¶ You will say, I think, just as I used to say: that the natural steps of alteration are missing here <note>Ficino is addressing a skeptical objection: that a man-made talisman (the "image") lacks the natural "growth" process that living things have, and therefore shouldn't be able to hold celestial power.</note>. Which defect, indeed, certainly diminishes the celestial gift, yet it does not seem to take it away entirely. For the Physicians <note>original: "Phyſici" — referring to natural philosophers or scientists of the era.</note> do not want an image to be fabricated from just any metal or stone, but from a specific one. --- Page 205 ---
<meta>The previous page established that celestial influences can be captured in a single moment by prepared materials, using the "evil eye" and spontaneous generation as examples. This page continues the argument by explaining how "Art" (human craftsmanship or alchemy) awakens the latent potential within natural substances—like sulfur, amber, and gemstones—by aligning them with celestial "figures" or patterns.</meta>

# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

In which indeed celestial nature long ago naturally began [to work] toward this very thing which is desired: and as if it were almost finished: as in the potential for flame in sulfur. Certainly, heaven perfects this power only then: when this matter is vehemently agitated through art, under a certain similar celestial influx: and, having been agitated, it grows warm. Therefore, art rouses the power begun there: and while it reduces the matter to a figure, similar to its own celestial figure <note>Ficino is arguing that carving a specific shape into a stone aligns it with a corresponding constellation or "Idea" in the heavens.</note>: then it exposes the matter entirely to the "Idea" <term>Idea: In Platonic philosophy, a divine archetype or blueprint from which physical things derive their form.</term> of its own celestial figure there: which, thus exposed, heaven perfects with that power with which it had begun. Providing, as it were, a flame to the sulfur: thus a certain power for snatching up chaff was given from heaven to amber <note>original: "succino." This refers to the property of static electricity.</note>, which, being somewhat weak, is often made stronger through friction and heating, and suddenly snatches them up.

**S**erapio <note>Serapio the Younger, an influential 12th-century Arabic physician and author of a book on simple medicines.</note> writes that a similar power was given to the stone *Albuged* <note>original: "Albugedi." Likely a reference to the "abugadi" stone mentioned in medieval lapidaries, often associated with magical attraction.</note>, which is similar to a hyacinth <note>In the Renaissance, "hyacinth" usually referred to a yellow or blue gemstone like zircon or sapphire, rather than the flower.</note>. But it does not draw chaff before hair is rubbed with this stone. Thus also that Jovian stone, the Bezoar <note>original: "Bezaar." A stone-like mass found in the stomachs of animals, highly prized as an antidote to poison. Ficino calls it "Jovian" because it is ruled by the planet Jupiter.</note>—that is, "delivering from death"—which we described in the book *Against the Plague*: it received power against poison from Jupiter from the start. But it was not so strong that it could pass that same power to other materials to be exercised. But truly, as soon as it receives the figure of that celestial creature <note>The constellation Scorpio.</note> under the influx of the celestial Scorpio: it is thought to suddenly carry back perfect power against scorpions. Which it can then communicate to mastic or frankincense. The same reasoning must be held concerning the hyacinth, Topaz, Emerald, and others: that the manufacture of figures does not have efficacy from elsewhere...

¶ CONCERNING THE CELESTIAL LIFE.

# What power figures have in the heavens and under the heavens. 
## Chap. XVII.

**S**ed lest you perhaps distrust figures <note>In this context, "figures" refers to both geometric shapes and the specific images or "characters" used in astrological talismans.</note> too much, you are commanded to remember that in this elemental region beneath the Moon, elemental quality is also extremely powerful—namely, in a transformation tending toward something elemental. That is to say: heat, cold, moisture, and dryness. However, qualities that are less elemental or material—specifically lights (that is, colors), and likewise numbers and figures—perhaps have less power for such material purposes. But for celestial gifts (as they believe), they are of very great value. For in the heavens, lights, numbers, and figures are almost the most powerful of all things, especially if there is no matter there, as most Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle, who taught that the celestial spheres were made of a "fifth element" or aether, distinct from the four heavy elements of Earth.</note> believe. For in this way, figures, numbers, and rays—since they are not supported by any other matter there—seem to be almost substantial <note>Ficino suggests that in the heavens, a shape or a number isn't just a property of a thing, but is the "substance" or essence of the celestial force itself.</note>. And since in the order of things mathematical forms precede physical forms—being more simple and less wanting—they rightly claim the greatest authority for themselves in the preceding levels of the world (that is, the celestial levels), so that no less is accomplished there by number, figure, and light than by some elemental property. Indeed, a sign of this authority is found even beneath the Moon. 

¶ For highly material qualities are common to many species of things; even if these qualities are changed in some way, the species themselves are not changed in every case...

<summary>Ficino argues that while heat and cold rule the Earth, geometric figures, numbers, and light are the primary powers of the heavens. He explains that because mathematical forms are more "pure" than physical matter, they possess the greatest authority in the celestial hierarchy.</summary>

<keywords>astrology, mathematical forms, Peripatetics, elemental qualities, celestial influence, geometry, Marsilio Ficino, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the text from Chapter XVII, which discusses the metaphysical power of shapes and numbers. It concludes a sentence from the previous page regarding how these forms follow or attain their power in the celestial spheres.</meta>

...attain. Moreover, the shapes and numbers of natural parts possess a property that is inseparable and peculiar to their species. These were destined from heaven together with the species themselves. Indeed, they also have the greatest connection with the Ideas <note>In Platonic philosophy, "Ideas" or "Forms" are the perfect, eternal archetypes of all things that exist in the physical world.</note> in the Mind of the World, the queen; and since these numbers and species are themselves certain entities designated there by their own Ideas, they naturally obtain their own powers from that source.

¶ And so, both the species of natural things are bound to certain figures, and their movements, generations, and changes are bound to certain numbers. 

¶ Truly, what shall I say of light? For it is the act or image of the intelligence. Colors, moreover, are certain kinds of lights. For this reason, when Astrologers say that lights—that is, colors—and figures and numbers can do a great deal in preparing our materials for celestial things, you should not (as they say) rashly deny these things. 

¶ You are not unaware that harmonies <note>original: "concentus." Here referring to musical or mathematical concord.</note>, through their own numbers and proportions, have a wonderful power to steady, move, and affect the spirit, the soul, and the body. Moreover, proportions constituted from numbers are like certain figures, as if made from points and lines—but in motion. Similarly, celestial figures relate to action through their own motion. For these, by both their harmonic rays and their motions penetrating all things, daily affect the spirit so secretly, just as powerful music is accustomed to affect it openly. 

¶ Furthermore, you know how easily a figure <note>The text likely refers to the "figure" of a human face or a statue's expression.</note> moves many to mercy...

<margin><unclear>Illegible marginalia</unclear></margin> [the figure] of a mourner. And consider how immediately the figure of a lovely person affects and moves the eyes, the imagination, the spirit, and the humors <note>In ancient and Renaissance medicine, the four "humors" (blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile) were the bodily fluids thought to determine a person's health and temperament.</note>. ¶ A celestial figure is no less alive and effective.

¶ In a city, does not the merciful and cheerful face of a prince gladden everyone? Whereas a fierce or sorrowful one suddenly terrifies? What then do you think the faces of the celestial beings—the masters of all earthly things—can do in response to these things? Indeed, even those coming together to conceive offspring are accustomed to impress upon the children who will be born long afterward not only the faces <note>original: "vultus." While meaning "face," in this context it refers to the outward appearance or character imprinted by a parent's state of mind.</note> they themselves wear at that time, but even the faces they imagine.

¶ The faces of the heaven are celestial figures. You can call "aspects" <note>original: "facies." In astrology, a "face" or decan is a subdivision of a zodiac sign representing ten degrees.</note> those figures there which are more stable than the rest. But you may call "countenances" <note>original: "vultus."</note> those figures which change more frequently. Likewise, you may call the aspects formed between the stars by their daily motion "countenances" and "figures" similarly. For they are named hexagons, pentagons, and tetragons <note>original: "exagoni, pentagoni, tetragoni." These refer to the geometric angles (sextile, quintile, and square) formed between planets, which astrologers believe emit specific influences.</note>.

¶ "So be it," someone might say. ¶ "Let celestial figures be as powerful in their effects as you please. ¶ But what does this have to do with figures of images made by artifice <note>Referring to man-made talismans or statues designed to capture celestial power.</note>?" They will respond that they do not primarily contend that our artificial figures are the most powerful in acting through themselves, but rather that they pre- <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("prae-"), likely leading into "prepare" or "predispose" on the next page.</note>

### What kinds of celestial figures the ancients used to engrave upon images. And on the use of images. Chapter XVIII.

**S**omeone may ask, then, which celestial figures in particular they were accustomed to engrave upon images. For there are forms there in the sky quite visible to the eyes, and described by many as if they were painted, such as Aries, Taurus, and similar figures of the zodiac; and those which are manifest outside the zodiac.

¶ There are, moreover, very many forms there, not so much visible as imaginable, perceived—or at least devised—by the Indians, Egyptians, and Chaldeans <note>The "Chaldeans" were ancient Mesopotamian people renowned for their early mastery of astronomy and astrology.</note> through the "faces" of the signs. <note>original: "facies." These are the "decans," thirty-six 10-degree segments of the zodiac, each associated with a specific symbolic image.</note>

As in the first "face" of Virgo, a beautiful maiden sitting, having two ears of corn in her hand, and nursing a boy. And the rest, such as Albumasar <note>original: "Albumasar." Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a Persian astrologer whose works were foundational to Western medieval and Renaissance astrology.</note> and several others describe.

¶ Finally, there are certain characters <note>original: "characteres." These are abstract symbols or "sigils" believed to represent the internal essence of a planet or sign.</note> of the signs and planets designated by the Egyptians.

¶ They wish, therefore, for all these things to be carved into images. For example, if someone expects a particular benefit from Mercury, he ought to place him in Virgo, or at least the Moon there with an aspect to Mercury. And then to fashion the image out of tin or silver. On which should be the whole sign of Virgo, and its symbol, and the symbol of Mercury. And if the first face of Virgo...

# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.

¶ Indeed, regarding the excellence of the cross among the Egyptians before Christ, I believe it was not so much a testimony of the gift of the stars as it was a prophecy of the power <note>original: "uirtutis." This refers to the inherent spiritual or medicinal efficacy.</note> it was to receive from Christ. Moreover, the astrologers who lived immediately after Christ, seeing the wonders performed by Christians through the cross, but being either ignorant or unwilling to credit such things to Jesus, attributed this power to the heavens. Nevertheless, they ought to have considered that miracles are by no means performed through the cross itself without the name of Jesus. That the cross is suited to [astrological] images because it reflects the strength of all the planets and stars is, perhaps, probable. Yet it does not possess such immense power for this reason alone. It may, however, have some effect when joined with other necessary things, perhaps toward the prosperous health of the body. 

But let us return, as we began, to recounting the opinions of others. ¶ To achieve a long life, the ancients used to make an image of Saturn on a "Feyrizech" stone—that is, a sapphire <note>original: "lapide de Feyrizech, id est saphyro." "Feyrizech" is a Latinization of the Persian *firuzah*, meaning turquoise. Ficino, following certain medieval lapidaries, equates it here with sapphire.</note>—at the hour of Saturn, while Saturn was ascending and in a favorable position. The form was as follows: 

<note>A description of a Saturnian talisman: An old man sitting on a high throne or a dragon; his head covered with a dark linen cloth; his hands raised above his head; holding a scythe or a fish in his hand; and dressed in a dark garment.</note>

For a long and happy life, they made an image of Jupiter on a clear or white stone. The form was a man sitting upon an eagle or a dragon, wearing a crown, made during the hour of Jupiter when he was in his exaltation <note>term: "exaltation" (A point in the zodiac where a planet’s influence is most powerfully and harmoniously expressed).</note>.

<note>A red vertical line in the manuscript marks a transition in the text.</note> 

It would be tedious to relate which <term>faces<note>original: "facies." Ten-degree divisions of the zodiac signs, also called decans.</note></term> through each of the signs the ancients observed, and which <term>mansions of the Moon<note>original: "Lunae stationes." The 28 divisions of the sky the Moon passes through each month, essential in medieval and Renaissance elective astrology.</note></term> they deemed necessary in the crafting of images. For in the mansion of the Moon from the seventeenth degree of Virgo to its end, they made images against diseases and hatreds, and for a successful journey. In the mansion from the beginning of Capricorn to the twelfth degree: against diseases, discords, and captivity. In the mansion from the twelfth degree of Capricorn to the twenty-fifth degree: against weakness and imprisonment. In the mansion from the fourth degree of Pisces to the seventeenth degree of the same: for curing diseases, for profit, for companionship, and for increasing the harvests. 

And in other signs similarly, they contrived other things, often through a vain curiosity. I have reviewed only those which savor not so much of the mage as of the physician. For I suspect that medicine of this kind <note>meaning purely magical images</note> will be for the most part vain. However, for other more legitimate preparations of medicines, I think such mansions of the Moon should be chosen. And also specifically the sixth degree in Aries.

...again the nineteenth [degree], 26 minutes. Likewise, in Gemini, the tenth degree, 51 minutes. In Cancer, the nineteenth degree, 26 minutes. In Libra, the sixth degree, 34 minutes. In Capricorn, the nineteenth degree, 26 minutes. In Aquarius, the second degree, 17 minutes. In the same, the fifteenth degree, 8 minutes. 

Furthermore, the opinion of <term>Haly<note>Haly Abenragel (965–1050), a famous Arab astrologer whose "Complete Book on the Judgment of the Stars" was a primary source for Renaissance occultists.</note></term> must be kept in mind: that any sign becomes "alive" as long as the Sun is in it; it rules over the others, and its effect occurs above all the rest. Thus, you should direct the Moon there to receive its proper gift for <term>medicines<note>original: "medicinis." In Ficino's medical astrology, 'medicines' refers to both physical remedies and the celestial influences (spiritus) that heal the body.</note></term>. Direct it there, I say—that is, to the sign, the <term>face<note>original: "faciem." A subdivision of a zodiac sign spanning ten degrees.</note></term>, and especially the degree—so that if you desire the benefits of <term>Jupiter<note>original: "Iouis." Jupiter was considered the "Great Benefic" planet, associated with health, growth, and joy.</note></term>, you should raise the Moon directed toward these points, or joined with them, as long as the Sun illuminates such a place; for therein Jupiterian power flourishes. And it is the same for the other planets.

Indeed, it would be curious to tell—and perhaps even harmful—how they used to fashion <term>images<note>original: "imagines." These are astrological talismans, often engraved on stones or metals, intended to capture the power of a specific star or planet.</note></term>, and how they would bind souls together or separate them, to bring about happiness or inflict calamity upon an individual, a household, or a city. For my part, I do not affirm that such things can be done. 

But astrologers believe they are possible and teach how they are done—things which I do not dare to recount. <term>Porphyry<note>A Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) and student of Plotinus who wrote extensively on the soul and theurgy.</note></term>, where he describes the life of his master <term>Plotinus<note>The founder of Neoplatonism; Ficino translated his entire works into Latin.</note></term>, confirms that such things can happen. He tells of <term>Olympius the Magician<note>original: "Olympium magum." An Alexandrian philosopher who allegedly tried to use "star-striking" magic to harm Plotinus, only to have the magic recoil upon himself because of Plotinus's superior spiritual purity.</note></term>

...Plotinus. But these attempts were turned back against their own author because of the exalted nature of Plotinus's soul. <note>original: "ob excelsam Plotini animam." This refers to an anecdote from Porphyry’s "Life of Plotinus" where a rival's magical curses backfired because Plotinus's spiritual state was so elevated that the harmful influences could not find a foothold in him.</note>

Albertus Magnus, who was a professor of astrology as well as theology, also states in his *Mirror* <note>The *Speculum Astronomiae* (Mirror of Astronomy), a 13th-century text often attributed to Albertus Magnus that sought to distinguish "natural" magic and astrology from "necromancy" or demonic magic.</note>—where he says he distinguishes between what is licit and what is forbidden—that <term>images<note>original: "imagines." Ficino distinguishes between the natural, impersonal forces of the stars and the conscious, often malevolent spirits addressed in "black" magic.</note></term> when making these images. Nevertheless, he does not reject the figures, letters, and words impressed upon images for the sole purpose of receiving a specific gift <note>original: "donum aliquod accipiant."
