<meta>Ficino continues his argument from the previous page, where he advised the reader to prioritize medicinal preparations over astrological images (talismans). Here, he provides a physical and philosophical explanation for why the process of making an object—rather than its final shape—might be what actually activates its hidden powers.</meta>

...medicines rather than in figures <note>In this context, "figures" refers to the specific shapes or symbols engraved onto astrological talismans, such as the image of a lion or a planetary seal.</note> can have efficacy. For it is probable that if images have any power, they do not acquire this so much through the **figure** recently engraved, as they possess it naturally through the **material** being so disposed. And if anything is acquired anew while it is being engraved, it is obtained not so much through the figure as through the **heating** arising from a certain **pounding**.

Which indeed, this pounding and heating, done under a **celestial harmony** <note>original: "harmonia caelesti." Ficino refers to a specific alignment of the planets and stars that matches the "inner music" or resonance of the material being worked.</note> similar to the harmony which once infused power into the material, excites and corroborates that very power—just as a breath stirs a flame—and makes manifest what was previously hidden. 

Just as the heat of fire brings into sight letters written with **onion juice** <note>Ficino uses the "invisible ink" of the Renaissance—onion juice—as a metaphor for "occult" or hidden qualities that require an external trigger to become visible or active.</note> that were previously hiding; or as letters inscribed with **goat fat** upon a stone, entirely hidden, come forth and stand out as if engraved in relief if the stone is submerged in **vinegar**. Nay rather, just as the touch of a twig or a shrub stirs up a slumbering rage: so perhaps a certain pounding and heating alone brings forth the power latent in the material, provided it is done at the **opportune time**. 

Indeed, it is expedient to use this celestial opportunity in preparing medicines; or if someone should perhaps wish to work metals and stones, it is better only to strike and heat them than to shape them into figures. For besides the fact that I suspect figures are **empty** <note>original: "manes." This term can mean "vain," "empty," or even "ghosts," suggesting that the shapes themselves have no inherent soul or power.</note>, we ought not rashly admit even the **shadow of idolatry**. Likewise, we should not rashly use the stars, even the healthful ones, to expel diseases very similar to them. For they often increase these. Just as noxious stars sometimes diminish diseases difficult to themselves, which indeed **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose work formed the basis of Renaissance astrology.</note>...

<summary>Ficino argues that the power of talismans comes from their material and the heat of their manufacture rather than their symbolic shapes, using this "natural" explanation to avoid charges of idolatry.</summary>

**B**ut why indeed do we pass over the universal image itself—that is <note>original: ".i." (id est)</note>, the image of the universe itself? When the Sun reaches the first minute of Aries <note>The first minute of Aries refers to the Vernal Equinox, the start of the astrological year and a point of supreme vitality in Ficino’s system.</note>. For from this, astrologers, as if from its own birthday revolution <note>Ficino is referring to a "Solar Return," a chart cast for the exact moment the sun returns to its natal position to predict the year ahead.</note>, forecast the fortune of the world at least for that imminent year. But do you see how beautifully the argument of the world having once been born comes to our aid while discussing this? Since it is reborn every year: Do not astrologers, in the very birth of a man, measure first in what sign, what degree, and what minute the Sun was located? And there they lay the foundation of the whole figure <note>In this context, the "figure" is the astrological birth chart or horoscope.</note>. And in every subsequent year, as soon as the Sun enters the same minute, they consider it as if the man were reborn, and from this they foretell the fortune of the year. Just as, therefore, they would not be able to do this in a man unless he were, as it were, reborn. And he could not be reborn, as it were, unless he had at some point been born: So also it is permitted to conjecture that the world was once born, namely with the Sun then positioned under the first minute of Aries: since, through the same position every year, a certain fate of the world as if being reborn revolves...

...these two stones <note>original: "duo lapides," referring to Lapis Lazuli and the Armenian stone mentioned below.</note> have a special privilege; for Lapis Lazuli is born with gold and is distinguished by golden marks, and is thus a companion of gold just as Jupiter is a companion of the Sun. A follower of these celestial things will either wear such a formula upon himself or will gaze upon it when it is placed before him.

Consequently, one should construct in the innermost parts of his house a bedchamber arched into a vault, marked with figures and colors of this kind. There, he should primarily wake and sleep. When he leaves the house, he will look not so much at the spectacles of individual things with great attention, but rather he will perceive the form and colors of the universe as a whole. 

But let those makers of images see to these things. You, however, shall fashion a more excellent image within yourself. For since you have learned that nothing is more orderly than heaven, nor can anything more temperate be imagined than Jupiter, you will finally hope to obtain the benefits of heaven and Jupiter if you show yourself to be most orderly and most temperate in your thoughts, affections, actions, and way of life. But since we have mentioned the temperance of heaven...

# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...ok upon celestial things, <note>original: "celestibus incidimus," completing the word and thought from the previous page.</note> it might perhaps be opportune to remember that no excess of any elemental quality exists in the heavens. To speak in a Peripatetic <note>Refers to the Aristotelian tradition of philosophy; "Peripatetic" derives from the Greek word for walking, as Aristotle famously taught while walking with his students.</note> manner: if the heaven were composed of such conflicting elements, it would have been dissolved ages ago; or if it were simply a single element, it would have lost its other properties through its own vast magnitude, power, and motion.

But indeed, as something most moderate, the heaven moderates all things and blends diverse elements into one. Furthermore, both through this very temperance <note>original: "temperantia," referring here to the "temperamentum" or the perfect harmonic balance of the four qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note> of its own and the excellence of its form, it has divinely earned the gift of life. 

For we observe that composite things finally attain life only when a perfect mixture of qualities is seen to have broken their prior opposition—as seen in plants. Next, a more perfect life is found in animals, insofar as their "complexion" <note>The specific physiological balance of humors and qualities in a living body.</note> is further removed from such internal opposition than that of plants. In humans, for the same reason, life is more perfect still, and in a certain way already celestial. Since the human complexion has already approached celestial temperance, especially

<summary>Ficino argues that the heavens are a living, perfectly balanced body that moderates all earthly things. He describes a hierarchy of life—from plants to animals to humans—where "perfection" is defined by how closely a creature's internal balance mimics the harmony of the stars.</summary>

# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...powers, or rather effective tempered qualities; or if there are qualities there somewhat similar to ours, they possess a sort of airy temperament. And when we call certain things there cold and dry, we understand this according to Platonic reasoning <note>Ficino often uses "Platonic reasoning" to describe a system where physical qualities are treated as metaphysical causes rather than just tactile sensations.</note>—that is, so that it may be called "cold" because it is the cause of the least heat. Indeed, it is called "dry" because it offers us the least moisture.

Thus the astrologer Abraham <note>Refers to Abraham Ibn Ezra (c. 1089–1167), a celebrated Jewish scholar whose astrological works were central to Renaissance thought.</note> says that Saturn, in a certain way, leaves our body cold and dry, because it brings less heat and moisture than our own bodies possess. By the same reasoning, the flesh of the ox and the hare are indeed hot and moist in themselves, yet to us they are cold and dry <note>In Galenic medicine, foods were classified by their effect on the human "humors" rather than their physical temperature.</note>.

Now, from this induction, accept these two corollaries:

First, that if bodies that are successively more <term>tempered</term><note>In this context, "tempered" (temperata) refers to the perfect harmonic balance of the four primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note> live more, then the heaven—being the most tempered—lives most of all. Nay, rather, on the other hand, it can be conjectured from the fact that the heaven is most exactly tempered and possesses in itself the most absolute life: that insofar as other things approach its temperament and life, to that extent they obtain a more excellent life.

Second, that life is a form perfect in itself, perfecting the body, and providing the principle of motion. I mean an internal principle, of a motion acted upon from within, and also directed outward through every part. If, therefore, this itself is life, consider him out of his mind who does not recognize that such a form exists in the heaven—that most excellent body, always circling with perfect motion, giving life to all things, and more so to those things gradually which either to...

<summary>Ficino argues that the heavens possess a "tempered" balance that is the source of all life, explaining that astrological influences like Saturn's "coldness" are relative to human biology rather than absolute qualities.</summary>

-># ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS<-

...[to] perish and waste away. But for my part, I do not sufficiently understand how **images** <note>Ficino uses the term *imagines* to refer to astrological talismans—objects engraved with specific symbols at precise astrological moments to capture celestial power.</note> can have any power over a **distant object**. However, I suspect they have **some** [power] over the **one wearing them**; yet I do not think it is of the sort that many people imagine. I believe this power exists by **reason of the material** rather than the **shape** of the image; and (as I have said) I prefer **medicines** to images by a long interval.

Although the **Arabs and Egyptians** <note>Ficino refers here to the Hermetic and Arabic traditions of astral magic, such as the *Picatrix*, which were highly influential but controversial in the Renaissance.</note> attribute so much to **statues and images** fashioned by **astronomical art** and **magic**, that they think the **spirits of the stars** are enclosed within them. Now, regarding these "spirits of the stars": some understand them as the **wonderful powers** of the celestial bodies; others truly understand them as **Demons** who are the attendants of this or that star. 

Therefore, they believe that the **spirits of the stars**, of whatever kind they may be, are **inserted into statues and images**, no differently than **Demons** are accustomed sometimes to **occupy human bodies**: and through them to speak, to move, to be moved, and to perform wonders. They believe that the spirits of the stars perform certain similar things through images. 

They think that **Demons**, inhabitants of the **worldly fire**, insinuate themselves into our bodies through **fiery humors** <note>The four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) were thought to govern health. "Fiery humors" likely refers to choler or overheated blood which makes the body a receptive "antenna" for certain spirits.</note> or ignited substances, and similarly through **ignited spirits** and **affects** <note>original: *affectus*, referring to emotional or mental states.</note> of this kind. Similarly, [they believe] the **spirits of the stars** can be **inserted**—through rays received at the **opportune time**, through **fumigations** <note>The ritual burning of incense or herbs corresponding to a specific planet.</note>, lights, and **powerful sounds**—into the appropriate **materials of the images**: and can effect wonders upon the one **wearing** them or even **standing near** them. Which things we indeed think can happen through **demons**, not so much by **certain material**...

...ship, and who are restrained and rejoice in worship. But these things are discussed more diligently elsewhere.

¶ The **Arabs** hand down the tradition that when we rightly fashion **images** <note>original: "imagines"; specifically astrological talismans engraved with symbols to capture celestial influence.</note>, if through imagination and emotion our spirit is most attentive to the work and to the stars, it is joined with the **Spirit of the World** <note>The *spiritus mundi*, a subtle, vaporous substance that Ficino believes bridges the gap between the physical world and the cosmic soul.</note> itself and with the rays of the stars, through which the World Spirit acts. Thus joined, the human spirit is itself a cause whereby a certain spirit of a star—that is, a **particular power** <note>original: "individua virtus"; likely misread in OCR as "inuidia." This refers to the specific qualities or "gifts" of a particular planet.</note>—is poured from the World Spirit through the rays into the image. This power is then most immediately present to the spirit of the person performing the work. They also say that work of this kind is aided by **fumigations** <note>The ritual burning of incense or aromatic herbs corresponding to specific planets to "tune" the environment.</note> adapted to the stars, insofar as such fumigations affect the air, the rays, the spirit of the maker, and the material of the image in a thorough way.

¶ I, however, believe that odors—being quite similar in nature to our own spirit and the air—are particularly receptive to the rays of the stars when they are set alight. If they are **Solar** or **Jovial** <note>original: "Iouii"; relating to the planet Jupiter, associated in the Renaissance with life-force, growth, and benevolence.</note>, they powerfully affect the air and our spirit toward the gifts of the Sun or Jupiter then ruling, which must be captured opportunely under their rays. Once the spirit is so affected and gifted, it can, through a more intense emotion, act not only upon its own body but also upon a nearby body—especially one that is naturally similar but weaker—and affect it with a similar quality. However, I think that the harder **material of the image** can hardly receive even the smallest influence from these odors or the worker's imagination. Nevertheless, the spirit itself [is affected] by the odor...

<summary>Ficino explains the Arabic theory of how human intention and ritual incense (fumigation) connect the practitioner's spirit to the "Spirit of the World" to charge talismans with stellar power.</summary>
<keywords>astrological images, talismans, Spiritus Mundi, Marsilio Ficino, fumigation, Solar influence, Jovial influence, planetary rays</keywords>

-># ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS<-

...to be so affected that one single thing is fashioned from both. This is indeed evident from the fact that an odor no longer acts upon the sense of smell once it has acted sufficiently. For the sense of smell—and indeed anything else—suffers nothing further from itself or from something very similar to itself. But of these matters we shall speak elsewhere.

¶ Furthermore, I believe that the **intensity of the imagination** <note>original: "imaginationis intentionem"; the focused willpower or mental "stretching" toward an object.</note> possesses power not so much in the literal crafting of **images** <note>original: "imagines"; astrological talismans engraved with planetary symbols.</note> or medicines, but in their application and consumption. For example, if someone wearing an image (as they say) that has been properly fashioned, or certainly using a medicine in a similar way, vehemently desires help from it and believes and hopes most firmly and without doubt, then surely a vast heap of assistance will be added from this source. 

¶ For when either the power of the image (if there is any), having been warmed by contact, penetrates the flesh—or at least the **natural power** <note>The inherent physical properties of the material, such as a stone or herb, as opposed to its ritual or symbolic power.</note> residing in its specially chosen material—or certainly the vigor of a medicine taken internally glides into the veins and the marrow, it carries with it a **Jovial** <note>Relating to the planet Jupiter, which Ficino considers the most "temperate" and life-giving planet, associated with health and vital spirit.</note> property. By this means, the human spirit is transferred into a spirit of that same Jovial kind through affection—that is, through **love**. For the very power of love is to transfer and unite. 

¶ Moreover, unwavering **faith and hope** place the human spirit, which has already been thus stirred, deep within the Jovial spirit and strengthen it there. If, as **Hippocrates** and **Galen** <note>The primary authorities of Greek medicine; Galen's work formed the basis of medieval and Renaissance medical practice.</note> teach, the love and faith of a sick person toward even an inferior, external physician contribute greatly to their health—and indeed **Avicenna** <note>original: "Auicenna"; the 11th-century Persian physician whose *Canon of Medicine* was a standard text in European universities.</note> says that this confidence effects more than the medicine itself—how much more must we think that affection and faith toward the celestial influence (which is already implanted in us and acting within) leads us toward heavenly aid?
                                                             p.i.

<summary>Ficino explains that the effectiveness of talismans and medicines is primarily driven by the user's intense imagination, faith, and love, which allow the human spirit to harmonize with benevolent planetary influences like those of Jupiter. He supports this by citing medical authorities like Galen and Avicenna on the psychological power of a patient's trust in their doctor.</summary>

...penetrating the vitals. Now indeed, love itself and faith toward a celestial gift are often the cause of celestial aid; and in turn, love and faith here perhaps sometimes arise from the fact that the mercy of heaven already favors us in this very matter.

->### ON THE POWER OF WORDS AND SONG FOR CAPTURING CELESTIAL BENEFIT, AND ON THE SEVEN STEPS LEADING TO CELESTIAL THINGS.<-
->#### CHAPTER XXI<-

**W**ords, furthermore, when pronounced with a certain sharper emotion, are thought to possess great power regarding **images** <note>original: "imagines"; specifically astrological talismans or icons engraved under specific planetary alignments.</note> to direct their effect precisely toward the goal at which the emotions and words are aimed. Therefore, to reconcile two people in a most burning love, they used to fashion an image under a waning Moon with Venus in Pisces or Taurus; meanwhile, many details concerning the stars and words were observed quite curiously—which I do not intend to report here. For we do not teach **love potions** <note>original: "philtra"; magic charms used to excite lust or love, which Ficino distinguishes from his "medicines."</note>, but medicines.

¶ It is more probable, however, that effects of this kind are accomplished either through **Venereal Daemons** <note>original: "Venereos Dęmonas"; in the Neoplatonic tradition, daemons are intermediary spirits that inhabit the air and mediate between the divine and the human. "Venereal" refers to those associated with the planet Venus.</note> who delight in these works and words, or simply through seductive daemons. For they say that **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A famous first-century Greek philosopher and miracle-worker often compared to Pythagoras or Jesus in antiquity.</note> often detected and exposed **Lamias** <note>mythological female spirits or monsters who lured and preyed on young men</note>—that is, certain deceitful and Venereal daemons who imitate beautiful girls and entice handsome young men. Just as a serpent is said to suck an elephant through its mouth, so do these spirits suck and drain men through the mouth and womb alike until they are exhausted. But let Apollonius himself see to those matters. In certain words, however, there is a certain power...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the power of words from Chapter XXI. It moves from mentioning Apollonius of Tyana to citing other authorities such as Origen, Zoroaster, and the Pythagoreans, before introducing a systematic hierarchy of celestial attraction.</meta>

-># ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE<-

...and Origen asserts its great power in his work *Against Celsus*, as do Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher and bishop who wrote on dreams and magic.</note> and Al-Kindi <note>The 9th-century Arab philosopher Al-Kindi, whose work *De Radiis* influenced Western theories of magic and celestial influence.</note> when debating on magic. Likewise Zoroaster, forbidding that "barbarous words" <note>original: "barbara verba"; ancient names or incantations in their original languages, which were thought to lose their inherent power if translated into more "civilized" languages like Greek or Latin.</note> be changed; Iamblichus says the same. Similarly, the Pythagoreans were accustomed to performing certain wonders through words, songs, and sounds, in the manner of Phoebus [Apollo] and Orpheus. The ancient doctors of the Hebrews observed this above all others, and all poets sing of how wonders are accomplished through verses. Even that most serious man, Cato, in his book *On Agriculture*, sometimes uses "barbarous" incantations <note>original: "cantionibus"; Cato the Elder’s agricultural manual famously includes rhythmic charms for healing sprains and illness.</note> for curing the diseases of animals. 

But it is better to set aside incantations. As for that harmony by which the young David redeemed Saul from madness, unless the mystery commands it be attributed to divinity, someone might perhaps attribute it to nature. Since indeed, according to the number of the seven planets, there are also seven steps through which an attraction is made from higher things to lower ones, voices hold the middle step and are dedicated to Apollo. 

The hardest materials—stones and metals—hold the lowest step, and they seem to relate to the Moon. The second place in the ascent is held by those things composed of herbs, the fruits of trees, gums, and the parts of animals; these correspond to Mercury, if we follow the Chaldean order <note>The "Chaldean order" refers to the sequence of planets based on their perceived distance from Earth: Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn.</note> of the heavens. The third step consists of very fine powders and their vapors chosen from the aforementioned things, as well as the scents of simple herbs, flowers, and ointments pertaining to Venus. The fourth step consists of words, songs, and sounds, all of which are rightly dedicated to Apollo, the author of the precepts of music. The fifth, vehement...

p. ii.

<meta>This page continues the list of the seven steps of celestial attraction. ...of the imagination, concepts of forms, motions, and affects, referring to the power of Mars. The sixth [step] consists of the discourses and considered deliberations of human reason pertaining to Jupiter. The seventh [step] includes the more secret and simple intelligences, as if already separated from motion and joined to the divine; these are destined for Saturn. Whom the Hebrews, by the name of their nation, rightly call "Shabbat" <note>original: "Sabath." The Hebrew name for Saturn is *Shabbatai*, which shares a linguistic root with *Shabbat* (Sabbath), the seventh day. Astrologically, Saturn was often identified as the protector of the Jewish people.</note>. Why mention these things? So that you may understand how, from a certain composition of herbs and vapors crafted through art—both medical and astronomical—there results a certain common form like a harmony, endowed with the gifts of the stars; so too, from tones first selected according to the rule of the stars, and then composed among themselves for their congruence, a common form is made, as it were; and in this form, a certain celestial power arises.

It is indeed most difficult to judge which tones specifically suit which stars, and likewise which compositions of tones agree primarily with which celestial bodies and their aspects. <note>In astrology, an "aspect" is the angular relationship between planets, believed to govern their influence.</note> But partly through our diligence and partly through a certain divine chance, we can achieve this in no other way than how Andromachus <note>Andromachus the Elder (1st century AD), physician to Emperor Nero, who spent years developing "Theriac," a complex universal antidote containing dozens of ingredients including viper flesh.</note>, having toiled for a very long time in compounding theriac, finally, after his diligence, obtained the power of theriac through divine luck. 

Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential Greek physician of antiquity, whose works were the foundation of medical knowledge for over a millennium.</note> and Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose *Canon of Medicine* integrated Greek and Arabic medical traditions.</note> confirm that this indeed happened by divine intervention. Indeed, Iamblichus and Apollonius of Tyana <note>original: "Apolonius theaneus"; a famous 1st-century Neopythagorean philosopher and traveling sage reputed to have performed miracles.</note> bear witness that all medicine had its beginning in prophecies. For this reason, [they call] Phoebus [Apollo] the prophet of medicine...

# ON OBTAINING LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS <note>original: "DE VITA CAELITUS." This is the title of the third book of Marsilio Ficino's *Three Books on Life* (1489), focusing on astral magic and health.</note>

Three main rules are proposed for this purpose; we shall bring them forward, provided we first warn you not to think that we are speaking at present of worshipping the stars, but rather of imitating them and capturing them by imitation. Nor should you believe we are dealing with gifts that the stars are going to give by choice <note>Ficino is careful to distinguish his "natural magic" from religious worship or demonic invocation. He argues that stars do not have "will" or "choice" like humans, but act through natural laws.</note>, but rather by a natural <term>influx</term> <note>Influx (*influxus*) refers to the flowing down of celestial energy or "spirit" from the stars into the material world.</note>. Indeed, we strive to accommodate ourselves to this manifold and hidden influx by exquisite means, just as we daily accommodate ourselves to the manifest light and heat of the Sun to receive them healthfully. To adapt oneself to the hidden and miraculous gifts of the heavens is the duty of the wise man alone.

But now we shall come to the rules for accommodating songs to the stars.

The first [rule] is to investigate what powers or what effects each star, constellation, and <term>aspect</term> <note>An "aspect" is the specific geometric angle between planets (e.g., square, trine), which astrologers believe modifies their influence.</note> possesses within itself or produces from itself: what they take away, and what they bring. One should then insert these things into the meanings of our words, detesting those things which they take away and approving those which they bring.

The second [rule]: to consider which star rules most over which place or person. Then, observe what tones and songs these regions and persons commonly use, so that you yourself may apply similar tones, together with the meanings just mentioned, to the words which you desire to set before these same stars.

The third [rule]: to observe the daily positions and aspects of the stars, and to explore under these what kinds of speech, songs, motions, dances, behaviors, and acts people are most incited to...
                                         p.iii. <note>Printer's signature mark for the third page of the "p" gathering.</note>

<summary>Ficino outlines the first three rules for composing music that can capture celestial influence, emphasizing natural imitation over religious worship. He explains how to align lyrics and melodies with the specific powers and cultural associations of the planets.</summary>
<keywords>astral magic, music theory, influx, celestial imitation, Marsilio Ficino, astrology, planetary influence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific rules for "capturing" celestial power through music, moving from the content of the lyrics (Rule 1) to the alignment of the stars with the specific audience and the spiritual state of the performer (Rules 2 and 3).</meta>

...investigate what they take away from or bestow upon these or those things contained under them. Then, gather these elements and include them in the lyrics of your songs, so that you may seek those specific powers of the stars. In this way, you create a harmony that is either consonant with or dissonant to the celestial bodies. <note>Ficino is describing a "musical pharmacy" where lyrics and melodies act as the active ingredients that resonate with planetary spirits.</note>

The **second rule** is to observe which stars primarily dominate which places or people under their influence. You should then choose the stars that especially favor the person or the location where the music is to be performed. <note>This suggests that a song effective for one person in one city might be ineffective for another person elsewhere, depending on their astrological "signatures."</note>

The **third rule**, finally, is to observe what individual people experience daily, whether in common circumstances or in personal <term>junctures</term> <note>original: *articulis*. In this context, it refers to specific critical moments or turning points in time when the celestial influence is most potent.</note>. When, through the **affection** <note>The "affection" (*affectus*) of the imagination refers to a state where the mind is deeply moved or focused, making it a better conduit for spiritual energy.</note> of the imagination, a person’s spirit corresponds to this or that star, the song transmits no less power into the singer than any other astrological composition. From the singer, this power is then projected into the nearby listener, so long as the song preserves its vigor and the <term>spirit</term> <note>original: *spiritum*. For Ficino, the "spirit" is a subtle, vaporous substance that connects the physical body to the soul; it is the medium through which music affects the listener's health.</note> of the one singing remains active. This is especially effective if the singer himself is by nature **Phoebic** <note>original: *phębeus*. A person whose nature is governed by Apollo (the Sun), characterized by solar clarity, vitality, and musical talent.</note> and possesses a powerful vital spirit.

<summary>Ficino details three rules for using music to channel planetary influence: aligning song content with planetary traits, matching the music to the dominant stars of the listener or location, and utilizing the singer's focused imagination and natural vitality to transmit celestial power.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ficino’s explanation of how the human spirit—specifically the "vital" and "animal" spirits—acts as a conduit for celestial power. <note>A decorative initial "D" in blue ink marks the beginning of this section.</note> ->ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.<-

...the spirit of the heart, and moreover the **animal spirit**. <note>Ficino follows the medical tradition of three spirits: the "natural" (centered in the liver, governing nutrition), the "vital" (centered in the heart, governing life force), and the "animal" (centered in the brain, governing sensation and movement).</note> For just as the **natural power** and spirit, when it is most potent, immediately softens and dissolves the hardest food, and soon turns bitter things into sweet ones—and even produces offspring outside of itself through the creation of seminal spirit—so too do the **vital** and **animal** powers act. When these are at their most effective, they act powerfully upon one's own body through a certain intense application of their spirit via song, through both internal conception and agitation. Then, by a pouring out of that spirit, it moves those nearby. It affects both its own body and that of others with a certain **sidereal property** <note>original: *siderea quadam proprietate*. This refers to qualities or "signatures" derived from the stars.</note> which it has conceived both from its own essential form and from the chosen opportune moment of time.

For this reason, many Eastern and Southern peoples—especially the **Indians**—are said to possess a marvelous power in their words. This is because they are largely **Solar** in nature; I do not mean their physical force, but rather that their vital and animal powers are nearly the most potent of all. This is also true of anyone in other regions who is particularly **Phœbean** <note>original: *phæbei*. People influenced by Phoebus Apollo, the sun god, and thus possessing qualities of the Sun: vitality, clarity, and musicality.</note>. | <note>A red vertical line in the margin marks the following definition of song.</note> **Song**, when conceived through this power, opportunity, and intention, is almost nothing other than a **second spirit** <note>Ficino views a properly composed song as a living entity, a temporary "spirit" formed by the singer that carries the specific medicine or influence of a planet.</note> recently formed within you alongside your own spirit. It becomes Solar, and acts both upon you and your neighbor through that Solar power. For if a certain vapor or spirit, emitted through the rays of the eyes or by other means, can sometimes **bewitch** <note>original: *fascinare*. This refers to "fascination" in the Renaissance sense: the ability to cast a spell or influence someone through the gaze.</note>, infect, or otherwise affect a person nearby, how much more powerful is that spirit which proceeds from the **imagination** and the heart...

                                                                    p.iiii.

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion from "On Life," specifically Book III, which deals with drawing down life from the heavens. Having established that the human spirit acts as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul, Ficino now explains how music—as a form of "audible spirit"—can physically and spiritually heal or influence a person based on planetary characteristics.</meta>

...much more abundant, flowing, and fervent: and more powerful in its motion. So that it is not at all surprising that certain diseases of the soul and body can sometimes be taken away in this manner, or even brought on. Especially since a **musical spirit** <note>Ficino views music not just as sound, but as "spirit" (air refined by the soul) that physically vibrates the listener's own internal spirit.</note> of this kind properly touches and acts upon the **spirit**: that medium between the body and the soul, which first affects both by its own disposition. 

You will truly grant that there is a marvelous power in the spirit when it is agitated and singing, if you grant to the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed the universe was structured according to mathematical and musical ratios.</note> and **Platonists** that the heavens are a spirit, arranging all things through its motions and tones. Remember, however, that all music proceeds from **Apollo**. <note>Ficino identifies Apollo with the Sun, the leader of the celestial choir.</note> And that **Jupiter** is musical only insofar as he is in harmony with Apollo. Moreover, **Venus** and **Mercury** carry back music because of their proximity to Apollo. 

Likewise, musical harmonies <note>original: *concentus*. Refers to the blending of different sounds or the "concert" of the planets.</note> belong only to these four planets. The other three have voices indeed, but not songs. Now then, we attribute slow, deep, hoarse, and complaining voices to **Saturn**. To **Mars**, on the other hand, we attribute the opposite: swift, sharp, harsh, and threatening voices. To the **Moon**, we attribute middle voices. 

As for harmonies, however, we assign to **Jupiter** those that are deep, earnest, sweet, and joyful with a certain stability. To **Venus**, by contrast, we ascribe voluptuous songs with playfulness and softness. Between these, we attribute middle harmonies to the **Sun** and **Mercury**. If they are venerable, simple, and earnest, combined with grace and sweetness, they are judged to be **Apollonian**. If they are somewhat more relaxed along with their pleasantness, yet vigorous and multifaceted, they turn out to be **Mercurial**...

...stand. You, therefore, will win over each of these four <note>Referring to the four "musical" planets mentioned on the previous page: the Sun, Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury.</note> to yourself by their own songs; especially if you apply sounds suitable to the songs, so that when you have cried out in their manner by singing and sounding at the right time, they seem to respond immediately: either like an **Echo**, or like a certain string on a **lute** <note>original: *cithara*. While often translated as "lyre," Ficino's audience would associate this with the lute or similar Renaissance stringed instruments.</note> vibrating whenever another string, tuned similarly, is struck. 

And as **Plotinus** and **Iamblichus** <note>Neoplatonist philosophers (3rd-4th century CE) who argued that the universe is a living organism where parts resonate with one another.</note> wish, this will happen to you naturally from the heavens, just as a resonance or vibration comes from a lute, or as an Echo is produced by an opposite wall. Indeed, whenever through frequent practice—using **Jovial**, **Mercurial**, or **Venereal** <note>Harmonies corresponding to Jupiter, Mercury, or Venus.</note> harmonies made while these planets are ruling—your own spirit, singing most attentively to this very thing and shaped by the harmony, becomes **Jovial**, **Mercurial**, or **Venereal**. 

Meanwhile, it also becomes **Solar** <note>original: *Phoebeus*, referring to Phoebus Apollo, the Sun.</note>; since indeed the power of **Apollo** himself, the leader of music, flourishes in every consonance. And conversely, by becoming "Solar" through Solar song and sound, you claim for yourself at the same time the power of **Jupiter**, and of **Venus**, and of **Mercury**. And again, from the spirit thus affected within, you similarly affect the soul and the body. 

Remember truly that a **prayer** <note>original: *orationem*. This can mean a speech, an oration, or a liturgical prayer; here it refers to a structured invocation intended to produce a result.</note> aptly and opportunely composed, full of emotion and meaning, and delivered with vehemence, possesses a power similar to songs. How much power **Damis** and **Philostratus** <note>Philostratus was the author of the *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*, a famous biography of a 1st-century wonder-worker; Damis was Apollonius’s companion and chronicler.</note> relate certain Indian priests to have in praying, it is not necessary to recount; nor even with what words they say **Apollonius** evoked the spirits of the dead...

...of Achilles affirm. For we are not speaking now of deities to be worshipped, but of a certain natural power of speech, song, and words. ### How we can accommodate ourselves to the heavens in seven ways; and for whom Saturn is malevolent, and for whom propitious; whom Jupiter defends from Saturn; and how the heavens act upon the spirit, body, and soul. Chapter XXII.

**Since**, indeed, the heaven is composed according to harmonic proportion and moves harmonically, and effects all things through harmonic motions and sounds, it is rightly through harmony alone that not only humans, but all these lower things, are prepared according to their capacities to receive celestial influences. We distribute this harmony, which is capable of receiving higher things, through **seven grades** of things among those above:

<meta>This page continues Chapter XXII of Ficino’s "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Having previously discussed the power of music and the physical preparation of the body, Ficino now shifts focus to the internal psychological and spiritual preparation necessary to attract specific planetary influences, moving from the physical spirit to the faculties of the imagination and reason.</meta>

¶ **ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE.**

Through concepts of the imagination and harmonious motions; through the appropriate discourses of reason; and through the tranquil contemplations of the mind. ¶ Just as we daily expose the body through its own harmony—that is, through its position, habit, and form—to the light and heat of the sun at the opportune time; so also must we prepare the **spirit** <term>In Neoplatonic thought, the *spiritus* is a subtle, airy substance that acts as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</term> for the hidden powers of the stars. This is achieved through a certain similar harmony of its own, prepared by means of images (as some believe) and certainly by medicines and scents composed according to harmonic principles. And finally, through the spirit thus prepared for the things above—as we have already often stated—we expose both the soul and the body to those same things. I mean the soul, insofar as it is inclined by affection toward the spirit, and the body likewise.

Within the soul, we now identify the imagination, the reason, and the mind. Our imagination can certainly be so disposed, composed, and conformed to **Mars** or the **Sun** <note>original: *Phębei*, referring to Phoebus Apollo, the sun god.</note>—whether because of the quality and motion of the spirit, or through our own choice, or even both—that it becomes immediately a proper vessel for the influx of the Sun or of Mars. Likewise, reason—either through the imagination and spirit together, or through deliberation, or both—can prepare itself for **Jupiter** through a certain imitation. Thus, on account of its own dignity and proximity, reason itself may capture Jupiter and the gifts of Jupiter far more effectively than the imagination or the spirit can; just as, by the same logic, the imagination and spirit capture celestial things much more than any lower things or material substances do.

<summary>Ficino explains how the human spirit, imagination, and reason can be tuned like a musical instrument to receive specific planetary influences, such as those from the Sun, Mars, or Jupiter.</summary>
<keywords>celestial influence, spiritus, imagination, reason, Mars, Sun, Jupiter, astrological medicine, harmony</keywords>

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter XXII of Ficino’s "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Ficino is expanding on how different parts of the soul—the imagination, the reason, and now the mind—can be tuned to specific planets. Having previously discussed Jupiter and Mars, he now addresses the highest and most difficult planetary influence: Saturn.</meta>

Finally, the **contemplative mind** <term>The *mens contemplatrix* is the highest faculty of the soul, which in Neoplatonism transcends both sensory experience and logical reasoning to achieve direct intellectual intuition of divine truths.</term> exposes itself to **Saturn** <note>In Renaissance astrology, Saturn was the planet of deep thought, solitude, and the "melancholy" of scholars. While often seen as a malevolent influence on the body, it was considered the highest planet for spiritual and intellectual achievement.</note> insofar as it withdraws itself not only from those things we perceive through the senses, but even from those things we commonly imagine or argue through human habits. By calling itself back—through its affection, intention, and way of life—toward things that are separate and immaterial, it aligns itself with the Saturnine.

Saturn is favorable to this type of mind alone. For just as the **Sun** <note>original: *Sol*</note> is indeed an enemy to nocturnal animals but a friend to those of the day, so Saturn is hostile to men who either live an openly common life or even to those who flee the customs of the crowd but refuse to abandon their common emotional attachments. For Saturn has granted the common, social life to **Jupiter**, but has claimed the separate and divine life for himself. 

To human minds that are truly separated from earthly things according to their capacity, Saturn acts as a friend, recognizing them as kindred spirits in a certain way. For even to those spirits inhabiting the high air, Saturn himself—to speak in a **Platonic manner** <note>Ficino refers to the Neoplatonic hierarchy where Saturn represents the "Divine Intellect" (Kronos), standing above Jupiter, who represents the "World Soul" or the ruler of the manifest world.</note>—stands in the place of Jupiter, just as Jupiter stands for common men...

<summary>Ficino explains that the contemplative mind can align itself with Saturn by withdrawing from sensory and common human concerns. He distinguishes Saturn's influence as being favorable only to those who lead a "separate and divine" life, while Jupiter governs the social and common life of humanity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter XXII of Ficino’s "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Ficino is expanding on how the contemplative mind, by separating itself from mundane concerns, can align with the influence of Saturn. This page discusses the "Saturnine" people who achieve a near-immortal state and the astrological reasons why Saturn is considered the most powerful of the planets.</meta>

[blue paragraph mark] ## ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE

...those living in the clouds, living very soberly, content with the fruits of the earth, and dedicated to the study of secret wisdom and religion, taste the happiness of Saturn. They lead a life so prosperous and so long-lived that they are considered not so much mortal men as immortal **daemons** <term>In the Platonic sense, *daemones* are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not "demons" in the modern sense of evil spirits.</term>. Many call these "heroes," the **Golden Race** <note>In classical mythology, the Golden Age was a period of primordial peace and harmony ruled by Saturn.</note> rejoicing in a certain Saturnine age and kingdom. I think perhaps the **Arabian astrologers** <note>Ficino frequently cites Arabic sources like Albumasar and Al-Kindi, who were the primary authorities for medieval and Renaissance astrology.</note> meant this when they said that beyond the equinoctial line toward the south there are certain very subtle inhabitants, daemons who seem neither to be born nor to die. They say that Saturn and the **Dragon’s Tail** <term>In astrology, the *Cauda Draconis* or descending node of the Moon is often associated with Saturnine qualities of depletion, endings, or deep spiritual withdrawal.</term> hold power there. This certainly seems to be confirmed by **Albumasar** <note>original: *Albumasar*; Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a highly influential Persian astrologer whose works were translated into Latin.</note> in the book *Sadar*, saying that certain regions of India are subject to Saturn, and that the men there are exceedingly long-lived, passing away only in the most extreme old age. He assigns a reason for this: that Saturn does not harm his own household, but only outsiders. 

[red paragraph mark] Indeed, do not neglect the power of Saturn. For the Arabs say he is the most powerful of all. Certainly, the planets take on the powers of those they approach. But they all approach him rather than the other way around. And the planets joined to him act according to his nature. For he is the head among the planets of the widest sphere. Indeed, any planet is the head, the heart, and the eye of its own sphere. Likewise, Saturn is closest to the **innumerable stars** <note>This refers to the *Firmamentum* or the sphere of the fixed stars, which in the traditional geocentric model sits immediately above the sphere of Saturn.</note>; he is most similar to the **First Mover** <term>The *Primum Mobile*, the outermost sphere of the universe that imparts motion to all the inner celestial spheres.</term>; he performs a long circuit. He is the highest of the planets. Whence...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Saturn’s influence from Chapter XXII. Having previously described the "Golden Race" of people who live a contemplative, near-immortal life under Saturn, Ficino now addresses how this powerful planet can be either a source of supreme happiness or a dangerous poison, depending on the individual's physical makeup and the mitigating influence of Jupiter.</meta>

...they call him happy, upon whom he [Saturn] has breathed favorably. And although men mostly fear him greatly as one alienated from the common life of people, they nevertheless believe he can be reconciled even to common life, if at any time he holds the greatest power and dignity in the **Ascendant** <term>The zodiacal sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth, considered the most important point in an astrological chart.</term>, or if his own Jupiter has looked upon him favorably, or has received him excellently in his own **bounds** <term>Specific degrees within a zodiac sign where a particular planet has increased influence or "dignity."</term>. 

Otherwise, his influence, when unsuitably received—especially in thick or coarse matter <note>Ficino refers to the "spirit" or the physical body which might be too dense or "heavy" to process Saturn's high-vibration intellectual influence.</note>—becomes like a poison, just as an egg becomes poisonous through putrefaction or overcooking. From this are born or emerge certain filthy, lazy, sad, and envious people, exposed to **unclean spirits** <note>original: *Demonibus immundis*; these are the lower, malevolent spirits that prey on those suffering from "black bile" or extreme melancholy.</note>. Flee far from their company! 

For the poison of Saturn lies dormant in other places, like sulfur removed from a flame. But in living bodies it often flares up; and like ignited sulfur, it not only burns, but also fills everything around it with a noxious vapor and infects those nearby. 

Against his influence—which is generally foreign and in some way discordant to human life—Jupiter arms us: both by his own natural quality, and certainly by his foods and medicines, and (as they believe) even by **astrological images** <note>Ficino is famous for his theory of "natural magic," which involved using talismans or images engraved during specific planetary alignments to capture beneficial celestial rays.</note>, as well as by morals, occupations, studies, and things properly pertaining to himself. 

They truly escape the harmful influence of Saturn and come under his favor—not only those who flee to Jupiter, but also those who [flee] to divine [contemplation]...

<summary>Ficino explains that Saturn's influence is double-edged: it can grant supreme wisdom or cause a poisonous, melancholic decay. He notes that the "harmful" side of Saturn can be mitigated by the benevolent qualities of the planet Jupiter or by rising to a state of divine contemplation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary influences on human life. Having established that Saturn can be a "poison" to the unprepared, Ficino now explains how the wise—following the traditions of the Chaldeans, Egyptians, and Platonists—can escape the "malignancy of fate" by elevating their minds toward Saturn's higher, contemplative nature. This transition moves from the physical dangers of astrology to the spiritual remedies found in philosophy and religious observance.</meta>

# ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS <note>original: *DE VITA CAELITUS*; this is the title of the third book of Ficino's "Three Books on Life," focusing on how to draw celestial benefits down to earth.</note>

...contemplation signified by **Saturn** himself, they devote themselves with their whole mind. For the **Chaldeans**, **Egyptians**, and **Platonists** believe that in this way the malignancy of fate can be avoided. For since they do not wish the celestial bodies to be empty shells, but rather divinely animated, and moreover governed by divine minds, they certainly hold that from there proceed to men not only very many things pertaining to the body and the spirit, but also many goods overflowing in a certain way into the soul—not coming from the physical bodies of the planets into the human soul, but rather from the souls of the planets. But even more, these and many such things flow forth from minds that are superior to the heavens.

Among these things, if you wish to assign all the reasons by which **Moses** was led to command the rest of the **Sabbath** <term>The Jewish day of rest, which falls on Saturday, the day associated with the planet Saturn.</term> to the Hebrews, perhaps beyond a more sublime and secret allegory, you will find that the day of Saturn is unsuitable for civil and military actions, but fit for contemplations; and on that day, divine protection against dangers must be implored. **Abraham**, **Samuel**, and many Hebrew astrologers acknowledge that this protection can indeed be obtained against the threats of **Mars** and **Saturn** through the elevation of the mind to God, and through vows and sacrifices, confirming that **Chaldean precept** <note>Ficino often cites the "Chaldean Oracles," a set of mystical texts he believed contained ancient wisdom compatible with Christianity.</note>, namely: *If you raise your mind burning with a work of piety, you will also save your frail body.* <note>original: *si mentem ad pietatis opus ardentem erexeris: corpus quoque caducum servabis*</note>

It is worth considering that saying of **Iamblichus** <term>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who emphasized the importance of ritual and theurgy in connecting with the divine.</term>: that the celestial and worldly deities have certain superior powers in themselves, and some inferior ones.

<summary>Ficino explains that by focusing the mind on spiritual contemplation, one can transcend the negative physical influences of the planets. He links the Hebrew Sabbath to Saturn’s day, arguing that Moses designated it for rest and prayer because Saturn's energy is ill-suited for worldly business but perfect for connecting with the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Moses, Sabbath, Chaldeans, Iamblichus, planetary souls, contemplation, astrology, fate, piety</keywords>

--- Page 244 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s exploration of how humans can transcend astrological fate. On the previous page, he discussed how the Hebrew Sabbath and the contemplations of the Chaldeans and Egyptians allow the soul to connect with the divine minds behind the planets, specifically Saturn, to avoid "the malignancy of fate." He now transitions into the mechanisms of this spiritual release and the role of human will.</meta>

Indeed, through these influences we are bound by the chains of fate; but through those other means, in turn, we are released from fate. It is as if they possess the "keys," as **Orpheus** <note>original: *ut inquit Orpheus*; Ficino refers to the legendary Greek poet Orpheus, whom he believed possessed ancient, divine wisdom regarding the "keys" of the universe.</note> says, for both opening and closing. Therefore, the divinity that is superior to the physical world redeems us from fatal necessity to a much greater degree. It is also a matter most worthy of investigation that the **Hebrew** practice—concerning the slaughter of animals and the dispersal of our possessions in sacrifice—suggests that evils threatening from the heavens can be deflected away from us and toward our external things. But we leave these matters to be explored by our friend **Pico** <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), Ficino’s brilliant colleague who was an expert in Hebrew and Kabbalistic traditions.</note>. [small blue ink mark]

Finally, whenever we say that the "gifts" of the celestial beings descend to us, you must understand it in three ways: first, that the qualities of the celestial bodies enter our own bodies through our **spirit** <term>In Ficino’s philosophy, the *spiritus* is a subtle, airy substance that acts as a bridge, connecting the physical body to the immaterial soul.</term> when it is properly prepared; second, that these same gifts first flow through their rays into the spirit naturally, or in whatever way the spirit is exposed to them; and third, that the goods of the **celestial souls** <note>Ficino followed the Platonic view that planets were not just rocks, but living beings with souls and governing "Minds."</note> partly leap into that same spirit through their rays and overflow from there into our souls, and partly proceed from their souls (or from **angels**) directly into the souls of men who are "exposed" to them.

I say "exposed" not so much by some natural contract or physical arrangement, but rather by the choice of **free will** or by our inner affection. In short, consider that whoever imitates the beneficence, action, and order of the celestial beings through their vows, study, life, and habits, will receive more abundant gifts from above, as they have made themselves more similar to those higher things. However, those men who are artfully dissimilar and discordant with the celestial disposition are secretly wretched, and ultimately, they turn out to be openly unhappy.

<summary>Ficino explains that while physical bodies are bound by fate, the human soul can escape "astrological necessity" through ritual, free will, and by imitating the orderly and beneficent nature of the heavens. He emphasizes that the "spirit" acts as a conduit for these celestial gifts, provided a person aligns their life with the divine order.</summary>
<keywords>fate vs. free will, Orpheus, Pico della Mirandola, sacrifice, spiritus, celestial souls, astrological influence, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s Book III of "Three Books on Life." Having previously established that the human spirit acts as a bridge for celestial gifts, Ficino now moves from the general theory of influence to the practical necessity of aligning one's life choices with their specific astrological "character" and guardian spirit to achieve success and happiness.</meta>

# ON OBTAINING LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.

¶ To live and act prosperously: first of all, know your natural character, your star, your guardian spirit, and the place suitable for them; live in that place. Follow your natural vocation.

### Chapter XXIII.

**Q**uiever is born of sound mind and in control of themselves is naturally ordained by the heavens toward some honorable work and way of life. Therefore, whoever desires to have the heavens favorable should, above all, undertake this work and this way of life, and pursue it diligently. For the heavens favor their own beginnings. 

Indeed, you are made by nature for this very thing above all others: that which you first do, speak, imagine, desire, dream of, and imitate from your tender years; that which you attempt most frequently and accomplish most easily; that in which you make the greatest progress and in which you delight above all else; and that which you leave behind only with reluctance. This is truly the thing for which the heavens and the ruler of the heavens brought you forth. To that extent, therefore, it will favor your undertakings and breathe life into your days, insofar as you follow the guidance of your parent <note>original: *genitoris*; Ficino refers to the heavens as the "parent" or progenitor of the individual’s earthly path.</note>. 

This is especially true if that **Platonic** doctrine is correct—with which all antiquity agrees—that for every person being born, there is a certain **daimon** <term>original: *Dæmon*; in the Platonic tradition, a *daimon* is not a malevolent "demon," but a protective guardian spirit or guiding intelligence assigned to an individual at birth based on their astrological profile.</term> who is the guardian of their life, destined for them by their own star. This guardian assists in the very duty to which the celestial powers assigned the person at birth. Therefore, whoever has scrutinized their own character <note>original: *ingenium*; refers to one's innate disposition or natural talent.</note> through the signs we have just mentioned will find their natural work, and in doing so, will simultaneously find their star and their guardian spirit. By following their guidance, he will act prosperously and live happily. Otherwise... 

<summary>Ficino explains that human happiness depends on identifying one's natural vocation and "guardian spirit" (daimon) by observing childhood inclinations and natural talents, which reveal the influence of one's specific star.</summary>
<keywords>vocation, daimon, guardian spirit, astrology, character, Marsilio Ficino, Platonism, natural talent</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion in Chapter XXIII of "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Indeed, they will experience adverse fortune and will feel the heavens to be hostile. 

¶ Therefore, there are two kinds of unfortunate people above all others: the first are those who, having professed nothing, do nothing. The second are those who undertake a profession foreign to their natural talent <note>original: *ingenio*</note> and contrary to their guardian spirit <note>original: *genio*</note>. The former certainly grow numb with laziness, while the ever-active heavens inwardly incite them to action. The latter, while they do things foreign to their celestial patrons, labor in vain, destitute of their heavenly protectors. 

The first point is confirmed by an ancient proverb: "The gods help those who act; but they are hostile to the lazy." The second by a similar proverb: "Do nothing against the will of Minerva" <note>original: *Nihil agas invita Minerva*; an idiom meaning one should not attempt anything that goes against their own natural ability or inclination. Minerva was the goddess of wisdom and weaving, representing the "thread" of one's natural talent.</note>. For this reason, I believe Jupiter is beseeched in the **Pythagorean verses** <note>Ficino refers to the "Golden Verses of Pythagoras," a collection of moral exhortations popular in the Renaissance.</note>: that either he himself might relieve the human race from so many evils, or at least show us what guardian spirit we should use as our guide. 

Furthermore, it would be worthwhile to investigate to which region especially your star and your guardian spirit have designated you from the beginning to inhabit and to cultivate. For there they breathe upon you more favorably. That region is indeed the one where, upon first arriving, your spirit <term>original: *spiritus*; in Ficinian medicine, this is a subtle vapor that connects the soul to the physical body</term> is in some way refreshed; where your senses remain more vigorous; where the constitution of your body is stronger; where most people favor you; and where your prayers succeed. 

Experience these things, then. Choose this region. Cultivate this one happily. From here, clearly, you will depart unhappy, unless you are both going to return and proceed to similar places. But meanwhile, exercise yourself in the customs frequent in this region, and drive yourself in certain cycles like the celestial bodies. For by circular motion...

[Blue pilcrow] ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE.

...that being born of such a kind, you will be preserved by similar things. Furthermore, as far as it pertains to where you dwell, you will find it useful to remember: just as the countryside supplies the city with the necessary food for life, but the city consumes it, so too is life itself greatly increased by frequent stays in the country <note>original: *rusticatione*; Ficino, like many Renaissance humanists, championed "villa life" as essential for health and intellectual clarity.</note>—provided that boredom has not yet begun to take hold of you. However, life is worn away by both urban leisure and urban business. 

As for what concerns both your dwelling and your profession simultaneously, one should by no means despise that saying of the Eastern astrologers: namely, that by a change of name, profession, clothing, diet, or location, the celestial influx <note>The "flow" of planetary energy into the human body and soul.</note> acting upon us can be changed for either the better or the worse. The Platonists <note>Followers of Plato, whose philosophy Ficino revived in Florence.</note> also judge that even our guiding spirits <note>original: *daemones*; in this context, these are not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits that link a person to the heavens.</note> are either changed, or that we relate to these same spirits differently in one place than we do in another. 

Indeed, astrologers—together with the Platonists—agree that the guardian spirit of each person can be twofold: one proper to their birth <note>original: *geniturae*; the spirit assigned by the positions of the stars at the moment of birth.</note>, and another proper to their profession. Whenever one’s profession agrees with one’s nature, the same spirit—or at least one very similar—is present for both duties. Consequently, our life will be more concordant with itself and more peaceful. But if the profession is at odds with one’s natural talent <note>original: *ingenio*</note>, the spirit acquired by art or training will be discordant with the natural guardian spirit. The resulting life is then laborious and full of anxiety.

[Red pilcrow] For those wishing to discover what kind of Spirit presides over each person from the moment of birth, Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the "daimon" or guardian spirit.</note> investigates a rule based on the planet that is the lord of the birth. Julius Firmicus <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century Roman lawyer and astrologer.</note> says that the lord of the birth is that planet which has the most [dignities] there...

q.ii.

<summary>Ficino explains how choosing a lifestyle and profession in harmony with one's astrological birth-chart ensures a peaceful life guided by a singular, powerful guardian spirit. He warns that living contrary to one's nature creates a conflict between one's natural and professional spirits, leading to exhaustion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s technical explanation from Chapter XXIII of "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." ...blesses these dignities; or rather, according to a firmer opinion, it is the planet whose domicile the Moon is about to seek immediately after the sign which it already holds at the moment of a person's birth. But he <note>Ficino is likely referring to Porphyry, a 3rd-century Neoplatonist who wrote extensively on the "Master of the Geniture."</note> thinks that the Guardian Spirit <note>original: *Daemon*; in Renaissance thought, this is a protective guiding spirit, not an evil entity.</note> should not be investigated by this same rule. Instead, according to the opinion of the Chaldeans <note>Ancient Babylonian astronomers and astrologers.</note>, it should be sought from either the Sun or the Moon. Specifically, one calculates from the Sun to the Moon in a daytime birth, and conversely from the Moon to the Sun in a nighttime birth. By calculating the interval between these two, you apply an equal distance descending from the degree of the Ascendant <note>The point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</note>; and you should take note of which <term>term|A "term" is a specific subdivision of a zodiac sign (usually 5-7 degrees) ruled by a particular planet.</term> you end upon. For they believe that the Guardian Spirit belongs to the same planet that rules that term. They are accustomed to weigh the course of life and fortune collectively from both the lord of the birth <note>The primary ruling planet of the horoscope.</note> and the Guardian Spirit. I have added "fortune" here because some calculate the Part of Fortune <note>A mathematical point in the birth chart representing prosperity, health, and success.</note> by almost the same method.

[Red pilcrow symbol] The ancients hoped that their Guardian Spirit had descended to them from one of the <term>cardinal points|The four most powerful angles of an astrological chart: the East (Ascendant), West (Descendant), Midheaven, and Lower Midheaven.</term> of the sky: namely from the East, or the West, or from either Midheaven <note>The highest and lowest points of the sky relative to the birthplace.</note>. Or at least that it came from the eleventh or the fifth region <note>The "houses" or divisions of the astrological chart.</note>. The eleventh house follows the Midheaven above our heads; it is nicknamed the "Good Spirit" <note>original: *Bonus Daemon*</note>, and it regards the Ascending degree in the East through a <term>sextile|A harmonious astrological aspect where two points are 60 degrees apart.</term> aspect. The fifth house, however, follows the Midheaven of the antipodes <note>The *Imum Coeli*, or the bottom of the sky directly beneath the earth.</note>; it is called "Good Fortune" <note>original: *Bona Fortuna*</note>, and it gazes upon the Ascendant degree through a <term>trine|The most beneficial astrological aspect, occurring when points are 120 degrees apart.</term> aspect. In the third place, if the Guardian Spirit should come from a <term>cadent|A house that "falls away" from a cardinal angle, generally considered weaker in influence.</term> region, they hoped it would at least come from the ninth or the third house. For the ninth is called "God," while the third...

<summary>Ficino explains the Chaldean method for calculating the personal Guardian Spirit and the Part of Fortune using the relationship between the Sun, Moon, and Ascendant. He also lists the most auspicious astrological houses for these spirits to reside in, such as the 11th (Good Spirit) and the 5th (Good Fortune).</summary>
<keywords>Guardian Spirit, Daemon, Part of Fortune, Chaldeans, Ascendant, Midheaven, Bonus Daemon, Bona Fortuna, astrological aspects, sextile, trine</keywords>

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s technical discussion from Book III of "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Having just explained how to calculate the "Part of Fortune" and identify the "Guardian Spirit" (Daemon), Ficino now details the specific astrological houses where these points should ideally be located to be effective.</meta>

...which they desired, to be observed by making the planets and stars suitable for accomplishing the work: so that they are either in the **angles** <note>The "angles" are the four most powerful points in a horoscope: the 1st house (Ascendant), 10th (Midheaven), 7th (Descendant), and 4th (Imum Coeli).</note>, or in the two **succedent** houses <note>The "succedent" houses follow the angles and are considered moderately strong: specifically the 11th and 2nd houses in this context.</note> which we spoke of: or at least in only the two **cadent** houses <note>The "cadent" houses "fall away" from the angles and are generally weaker; Ficino is likely referring to the 9th and 3rd houses here.</note> which we named before. For it is not without reason <note>original: *ab re*; literally "away from the matter."</note> that they say the Sun **rejoices** <term>rejoices|In traditional astrology, a planet "rejoices" when it is located in a specific house that matches its natural character, enhancing its influence.</term> in the ninth house, the Moon in the third, Jupiter in the eleventh, and Venus in the fifth. For these houses look upon the degree of the Ascendant. But let us return to the subject.

Whether, therefore, we first investigate the instinct of nature and of the Guardian Spirit <note>original: *Daemon*; in Ficino’s Neoplatonic philosophy, this is a personal guiding spirit assigned at birth, not a "demon" in the modern sense.</note> through that experience and diligence which I was describing above, or through this art which I have just reviewed: we shall judge that man to be **unfortunate** who professes no honorable duty <note>original: *officium honestum*; a noble career or moral vocation aligned with one's nature.</note>. For he who does not undertake an honorable work truly has no leader for his profession; and he has scarcely any natural leader, because the leadership of the stars and of the Guardian Spirits, or angelic leaders...

...more closely following the angels and the governance of the state, and more worthy duties. Indeed, a way of life and a skill can be adopted that is not contrary to one's **Guardian Spirit** <note>original: *genio*; the personal guiding spirit assigned at birth according to the stars.</note> and natural **disposition** <term>disposition|original: *ingenio*; the innate talent or character a person is born with.</term>, nor far removed from it, even if you should progress toward even more excellent things. Remember also to enter into the friendship of those upon whom the celestial graces breathe.

This you shall judge by the goods of the soul, the body, and of fortune. For just as a scent exhales from **musk** <note>A highly prized aromatic in the Renaissance, believed to strengthen the human spirit through its powerful scent.</note>, so from a good person, something of their goodness exhales onto their neighbor, and once infused, it often persists. Moreover, a gathering of three fortunate people, or even two who wonderfully overflow into one another, would be marvelous.

Remember, finally, to flee far from the unrestrained, the impudent, the malignant, and the unfortunate. For these are full of evil spirits <note>original: *daemonum*; here Ficino uses the term in its negative, harmful sense, unlike the protective "Guardian Spirit" mentioned earlier.</note> or of harmful rays; they are evildoers, and like those suffering from leprosy or the plague, they cause harm not only by touch, but even by their proximity and their very **gaze**. <note>Ficino's theory of "contagion" suggests that the eyes emit spiritual vapors or rays that can physically transmit a person's inner state—whether good or evil—to others.</note> Truly, proxim...

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion on the "contagion" of spirits. Having ended the previous page with a warning about how a person's gaze can transmit spiritual harm, he begins here by illustrating how a person’s mere presence can be either a plague or a blessing before transitioning into a guide for scholars to identify their governing planets.</meta>

¶ ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE.

They say that Apollonius of Tyana <note>original: *Ioniū theaneū*; Apollonius was a famous first-century Greek philosopher and reputed wonder-worker often compared to Pythagoras.</note> discovered an old man in Ephesus under whose form a **demon** was hiding; this figure, by his presence alone, was infecting the entire city with the plague. On the other hand, Xenophon and Plato testify to how much Socrates profited many people by his presence alone.

¶ By what means scholars may recognize their own **natural disposition**, and follow a way of life agreeable to their spirit. [Red underline] Chapter XXIV.

**S**ince I am speaking to students of letters, I want each one who is captured by the love of letters to remember that he is first and foremost **Mercurial** <note>Influenced by the planet Mercury, traditionally the patron of speech, writing, and commerce.</note>. Furthermore, he is **Solar**, insofar as Mercury himself is Solar <note>Ficino views Mercury as a companion to the Sun, reflecting the Sun's intellectual light.</note>. And this condition is common to all of them. 

Specifically, however, beyond the Mercurial nature, whoever excels in the grace of eloquence, in wit, dignity, and beauty, should recognize **Apollo** and **Venus** within himself. He who is more inclined toward laws or toward natural and common philosophy should not be unaware that he has **Jupiter** as his patron. But he who is more curiously driven to investigate all the most secret things to their depths should know that he is not only Mercurial, but **Saturnian** <note>Saturn was associated with deep, often melancholy contemplation and the study of occult or hidden mysteries.</note>. Under whose lordship also are all those who are diligent in any study until the very end—especially those who are negligent in all other matters. 

Finally, if it is true what some—both physicians and astronomers—hand down, that the soul endowed with **intellect** <term>intellect|original: *intellectu*; here referring to the highest part of the soul, the capacity for divine reasoning.</term> descends into the human conception in the month of the Sun (that is, the fourth month), then those who live mostly by the intellect are especially and daily "Solar" from the beginning. Of these planets, therefore...
                                                                    q.iiii.

<summary>Ficino explains how the presence of individuals can transmit spiritual influence and provides a guide for scholars to identify which planets (Mercury, Sun, Apollo, Venus, Jupiter, or Saturn) govern their specific intellectual talents.</summary>
