the favor of these [planets] must be sought for these men. Under their breath <note>original: *spiraculo*; referring to the subtle influence or "breathing" of the stars upon the world.</note> medicines are to be forged. In their regions one must dwell.

¶ But to **Apollo**, leader of the Muses, I especially call you, O learned cultivators of the Muses. Therefore, whoever among you, most beloved brothers in the love of the Muses, are much more and longer strong in intellect than in body, let them certainly know that in their birth Phoebe <note>The Moon, traditionally associated with the formation of the physical body and "matter."</note> indeed supplied very little matter. But she <note>Or rather, the celestial influence at birth.</note> infused a Phoebean <note>original: *Phoebeum*; referring to Phoebus Apollo, the Sun, representing the light of the mind and spirit.</note> spirit most abundantly. Indeed, they daily resolve their humors and nourishment within the body into **spirit** for the greatest part. Each of you, therefore, is almost entirely spirit—a spiritual man, so to speak, sounding through this earthly little body <note>original: *personatus*; this suggests the body is a "persona" or mask through which the spirit "sounds through" (*per-sonat*), like an actor in a play.</note>. You wear out the spirit before others with a certain perpetual labor: so that, for you more than for others, the spirit must be assiduously recreated; and in old age...

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s "On the Celestial Life" (De Vita Coelitus Comparanda), specifically Chapter XXIV. # [red flourish] ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE.

heat <note>original: *calorem*; completing the word from the previous page</note>: from ambrosia into nectar: from the intuition of truth into a burning love of truth. Indeed, **Phoebus** and **Bacchus** are inseparable brothers and companions. The former, indeed, brings two things to you above all: namely, **light** and the **lyre**. The latter likewise brings two: **wine** and the **scent of wine**, in order to recreate the **spirit**. Through the daily use of these, the spirit itself finally emerges as Phoebean and <term>free</term> <note>original: *liber*; Ficino is employing a wordplay here: *liber* means "free," but *Liber* is also the Roman name for Bacchus, the god of wine. To be "free" is to be "Bacchic."</note>. Wherefore, prepare yourselves daily to receive the **light of the Sun** in such a way that—as far as <term>distillation</term> <note>original: *destillatione*; a medical term for the "dropping" or "dripping" of excess phlegm/humors, often caused by cold or dampness.</note> and <term>desiccation</term> <note>original: *exsiccatione*; the "drying out" of the body's natural moisture, a common ailment for scholars studied by Ficino.</note> can be avoided—you may live as frequently as possible under the light. At the very least, live within sight of the light, whether from a distance or close at hand, whether sheltered or in the open, everywhere tempering the vital power of the **Sun** for your use. And in the night, recall the Sun by means of fire, not forgetting meanwhile the songs of the lyre. Breathe, indeed, always—both while waking and sleeping—the air...

<summary>Ficino explains how the "spiritual" nature of the scholar is nourished by the solar influence of Apollo (light and music) and the jovial influence of Bacchus (wine), which together "recreate" the spirit. He advises scholars to seek sunlight and firelight while avoiding the physical extremes of dampness and dehydration.</summary>

# -> [Blue pilcrow] Astronomical diligence in procreating children; in preparing feasts; in buildings and habitation; and in garments; and to what extent it is permitted to care for such things. <-
# -> [Blue pilcrow] Chapter XXV. <-

[Large red initial S] **B**ut it also helps to speak for a little while with the strict priest of religion. Tell me, come now, what exactly do you condemn in the use of the stars, Priest? You will say: "Whatever takes away from our **free will**; whatever detracts from the worship of the **one God**." I not only condemn those same things along with you, but I also greatly detest them. Furthermore, I know you curse—and I, moreover, shudder at—those certain individuals who, when they wish to entreat God, flee (as wretched as they are foolish) to **Jupiter** in mid-heaven as he enters that vast <term>Head of the Dragon</term> <note>original: *draconis caput*; in astrology, this refers to the North Node of the moon, but Ficino here conflates it with the biblical dragon (Lucifer) to illustrate the spiritual danger of "superstitious" astrology.</note>; destined, clearly, to be devoured by that very dragon which once fell from heaven, dragging a third part of the stars with it.

But will you grant that one may choose opportune hours for making contracts, marriages, conferences, journeys, and similar works? I see that you will not easily assent to these things, fearing for I know not what regarding our **free will**. Therefore, even if that great theologian **Albert** <note>Ficino refers to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a prominent Scholastic philosopher and theologian who argued that the stars influence the body and physical world, but not the human soul or will.</note> grants those things—and even if a certain reason might perhaps dictate that through the very process of <term>election</term> <note>original: *electionem*; "Electional astrology" is the practice of choosing the most auspicious moment to begin an activity based on the positions of the planets.</note>, which pertains to our judgment, celestial things are subjected to the use of our **prudence** just as herbs are supplied to a physician—nevertheless, for the present, I will believe you instead. And those things which you now permit with such difficulty, I myself shall just as easily dismiss. Indeed, observations...

<summary>Ficino addresses the theological concerns of practicing astrology, specifically "electional astrology," arguing that while he respects the priest's concerns for free will, some theologians like Albertus Magnus allowed for the practical use of celestial timing.</summary>

### -> ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE <-

...of the **Moon** and therefore of the other stars for the purpose of curing diseases: and for that reason, you have for a long time (as I believe) permitted the preparation of remedies. It is also conceded by you, and moreover approved, that while the **Moon** is waxing—provided its light is increasing and it is not otherwise afflicted by unfortunate aspects—one may scatter seeds in the fields and plant vines and olives.

[Blue pilcrow] Why then should we not use the benefit of the **Moon**, **Jupiter**, and **Phoebus** <note>original: *Phębi*; the god Phoebus Apollo, representing the Sun.</note> for "planting" (to speak bluntly) a human being? For indeed, we always employ the service of **Venus** for these duties—though I should have said more correctly just now, *they* always employ it; for to me, **Venus** herself is as **Diana** <note>Ficino, a priest, is making a joke about his own celibacy. While others use Venus for procreation, for him "Venus" (beauty) is "Diana" (chastity).</note>.

Will it not be permitted, and will it not be beneficial under a favorable star, to produce wine in the autumn, and to prepare our daily bread and feasts? And if we are unable to wait for the specific aspects of the stars <note>In astrology, "aspects" are the geometric angles between planets that determine their influence.</note> while preparing these things, it will at least be expedient...

...and they may indulge in various honors. Let us therefore be in agreement.

¶ But will they build houses at random, or live in ill-omened ones? Where a certain contagious disaster of a building infects the dweller almost in the same way as a poisonous vapor of pestilence—one even preserved in a wall for two years. Just as a plague lurking in a garment infects and destroys an unwary user long afterward. 

But since we have happened upon the mention of clothes here, will you, pious father, forbid us to diligently seek out a "breath of Venus" <note>Ficino refers to choosing an astrologically auspicious time when the planet Venus—associated with beauty and comfort—is well-positioned.</note> in the making of clothing, or when it is first put on? Do they not approve of lamb skins? <note>Medical theory of the time believed the "nature" of the animal remained in the fur and affected the wearer's temperament.</note> Perhaps a star affects clothes as they are "born" <note>i.e., manufactured</note> day by day no differently than it affects those born once at the beginning of their lives. Indeed, Thomas Aquinas <note>The most authoritative theologian of the Catholic Church; Ficino cites him to prove his ideas are not heretical.</note> confirms in his book *On Fate* that clothes and other works of art receive a certain quality from the stars.

¶ You will therefore affirm this. Indeed, scabies and leprosy are witnesses that infected clothes infect the user. Finally, if you care for the life of the people (as I believe you do), you will permit these things. And by your permission, I shall advise on what must be observed. 

But if you do not exactly disapprove of the care for this mortal life, but rather advise that it should be neglected, I indeed shall neglect it—relying on the confidence of a better life <note>referring to the eternal afterlife</note>—and I advise others to do the same. Farewell.

¶ How through lower things exposed to higher ones [celestial influences are] drawn down... <note>This is a "catchword," indicating the beginning of the next section on how physical objects can attract spiritual/celestial power.</note>

->**ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS**<-

...the higher things are attracted; and through worldly materials, worldly gifts especially are received. 

### Chapter XXVI.

**B**ut lest we digress too far from that which we established at the beginning when interpreting Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 CE) was the founder of Neoplatonism, whom Ficino translated and synthesized with Christian thought.</note>, briefly conclude thus. The world was created by the Good itself <note>In Platonic philosophy, "The Good" is the supreme source of all being, often identified with God.</note>, as Plato, together with Timaeus the Pythagorean <note>Referring to the *Timaeus*, Plato's dialogue on the creation of the universe.</note>, teaches, in the best way it could be made. It is, therefore, not only corporeal, but also a participant in life and intelligence. For which reason, besides this body of the world which is familiarly manifest to the senses, there lies hidden within it a certain spirit-body <note>original: *spiritus corpus*. Ficino refers to the "spiritus mundi" or World Spirit, a subtle substance that acts as a bridge between the physical and the spiritual.</note> that exceeds the capacity of our frail senses. 

In the spirit, the soul flourishes; in the soul, intelligence shines. And just as under the Moon <note>In the "sublunary" or earthly realm.</note> air is not mixed with earth except through water, nor fire with water except through air: so in the universe there is a certain "bait," or kindling <note>original: *esca* or *fomes*. These terms describe how physical objects are "primed" to catch higher spiritual influences, like tinder catching a spark.</note>, to couple the soul to the body; it is that very thing which we call the spirit. The soul is also a kind of kindling within the spirit and body of the world, for attaining intelligence from the divine. 

¶ Just as a certain extreme dryness in wood is prepared for oil to penetrate; and the oil soaked into it is fuel for the fire—I mean for the heat, most directly. Heat itself is the vehicle of light; and if this wood were of such a kind that it might shine in the presence of fire without being consumed—the likes of which we have sometimes seen—we shall now see by this example whether either a human or some other thing under the Moon, by certain preparations—partly natural and partly sought through art, might...

<summary>Ficino explains the Neoplatonic structure of the universe, arguing that the "World Spirit" acts as a vital intermediary bridge connecting the physical body of the world to its soul and divine intelligence.</summary>

<keywords>World Soul, World Spirit, Neoplatonism, Plotinus, Timaeus, celestial influence, spiritus mundi, elemental theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s argument in Chapter XXVI of "On Life" (De Vita). He is explaining how physical matter is prepared to receive spiritual and intellectual gifts through "baits" or "kindlings," a process he describes as the foundation of natural magic. He uses the development of a fetus as a biological analogy for how the universe works.</meta>

...such things: and also perhaps certain intellectual goods can be received from above in a timely manner.

But what pertains here to religion we will discuss elsewhere, where Plotinus brings these matters into the midst. Concerning natural influences, of whatever kind they may be coming from above: know that they can only be prepared in us and in our materials through art <term>art: "ars" or human skill, specifically the theurgical or medical technique of preparing matter to be receptive to celestial influence</term> when nature has supplied us and our things with kindlings <note>original: *fomenta*</note> for them, and the heavens have conspired more opportunely toward them.

Is it not true that in the fetus itself, nature—the artisan of that very fetus—when it has affected and shaped the little body in a certain way, immediately draws down the spirit <term>spirit: *spiritus*, the subtle, airy substance that acts as a bridge between the immaterial soul and the physical body</term> from the universe by this very preparation, as if by a kind of bait <note>original: *esca*</note>?

And through this, as if through kindling <note>original: *fomitem*</note>, it drinks in life and soul; and finally, through a certain form and disposition of the soul, the body thus living is at last worthy of the presence of the mind, granted from the divine.

Everywhere, therefore, nature is a magician <note>original: *maga*</note>, as Plotinus and Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 CE), a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on the "sympathy" or hidden connections between different parts of the universe.</note> say; namely, enticing certain things everywhere with certain baits: no differently than heavy things are drawn to the center of the earth, light things to the curve of the Moon, leaves by heat, roots by moisture, and other things similarly. 

By this attraction, the wise men of India <note>A reference to the "Gymnosophists" or ancient Indian philosophers whom the Renaissance Neoplatonists believed shared a common "ancient theology" (prisca theologia) with the Greeks.</note> testify that the world is bound to itself, saying that the world is an animal that is everywhere both male and female; and by the mutual love of its members, it couples with itself everywhere, and thus remains whole.

<summary>Ficino explains that nature acts as a "magician" by using physical matter as "bait" to attract celestial spirit and life, comparing the process to the development of a fetus and the self-sustaining unity of the world as a living animal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s argument from Chapter XXVI of "On Life" (De Vita), specifically "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Having established that the universe is a living organism, he now describes the internal bonds of this "Great Animal" and how its parts—gendered metaphorically as male and female—interact through a kind of cosmic marriage or sympathy.</meta>

->[ornamental mark] ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE<-

...the bond of the members, moreover, exists through the mind <note>original: *mens*. Ficino refers to the World Soul or Cosmic Mind that gives structure to the universe.</note> implanted within it; which, infused throughout the limbs, moves the entire mass and mingles itself with the great body. <note>original: *mens agitat molem, et magno se corpore miscet*. This is a famous quotation from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (VI.726-727), describing how a divine spirit sustains the universe from within.</note> Hence Orpheus <note>The mythical poet Orpheus was considered by Ficino to be one of the "Ancient Theologians" (*Prisca Theologia*) who understood the hidden structure of the world.</note> calls nature itself, and the worldly Jupiter, both male and female. To such an extent is the world everywhere greedy for the mutual marriage of its parts. 

That the masculine sex is everywhere mixed with the feminine is declared, on the one hand, by the order of the signs <note>This refers to the Zodiac signs, which alternate between masculine (fire/air) and feminine (earth/water) qualities.</note>. Where that which precedes is perpetually in succession masculine, and that which follows is feminine. From here indeed [we see] trees and herbs: which, just like animals, possess both sexes. I pass over the fact that fire stands in the place of the male to the air, and water to the earth, as a male to a female. 

So it is no wonder that the worldly members and all its joints desire mutual marriage among themselves. This [marriage] the planets also reconcile—some being male, others female. Especially Mercury, who is both male and female, the father of Hermaphroditus. <note>Mercury (Hermes) was often depicted as androgynous or "common" in astrology because he takes on the nature of the planet he is paired with. In alchemy and magic, this represents a bridge between opposing forces.</note>

[ornamental mark] Truly observing this, agriculture prepares the field—which is like a female—for celestial gifts. By certain graftings <term>grafting: the horticultural practice of joining two plants so they grow as one; Ficino uses this as a metaphor for how magic "grafts" celestial power onto earthly matter</term>, it propagates the life of the plant and leads it toward another and better species. The physician, the natural philosopher <note>original: *physicus*</note>, and the surgeon effect similar things in our bodies, both to nourish us and to more fruitfully match us to the nature of the universe. 

So too the philosopher skilled in natural things and the stars—whom we are accustomed to call a "magician" <note>original: *magum*. Ficino is careful to define "magician" here as a natural scientist or "wise man" who understands the sympathies of the world, rather than a sorcerer.</note> in the proper sense—[does this] with certain...

<summary>Ficino explains that the universe is bound together by a cosmic "marriage" between masculine and feminine forces found in the Zodiac, elements, and plants. He argues that a true "magician" is like a farmer or physician who uses these natural sympathies to align human life with celestial influences.</summary>

<keywords>Cosmic Sympathy, Orpheus, Virgil, Masculine and Feminine, Zodiac, Mercury, Natural Magic, Agriculture, Celestial Gifts, World Soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion from "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." Having previously discussed the gendered nature of the cosmos, he now explains how the human "mage" or "wise man" acts as an intermediary, using natural attractions—or "allurements"—to join the heavens to the earth.</meta>

...[with certain] allurements, celestial things to earthly things quite opportunely; and not otherwise than an eager farmer grafts a new shoot onto an old branch. Which [method] Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer of antiquity. Ficino refers here to the idea from Ptolemy’s *Centiloquium* that a wise man can mitigate the effects of the stars.</note> strongly approves of, affirming that a wise man of this kind can assist the work of the stars in the same way a farmer assists the natural power of the earth. 

The mage subjects earthly things to celestial ones—or rather, everywhere subjects lower things to the higher—as if everywhere offering proper females to be fertilized by their own males: as iron is to be drawn by a magnet; as camphor is to be driven off by boiling air; as crystal is to be illuminated by the Sun; as sulfur and "sublime liquor" <note>original: *sublimem liquorem*. In the alchemical tradition, this usually refers to "spirit of wine" or highly distilled alcohol, prized for its ability to catch fire instantly.</note> are to be ignited by a flame; as an empty eggshell filled with dew is to be raised up by the Sun; or even as the egg itself is to be warmed by the hen. 

[red mark] Furthermore, just as some people, by warming eggs even without the presence of animals, procure life for them from the universe; and often, by preparing certain materials at the right time without eggs or visible seeds, procreate animals: as from basil sc[orpions]... <note>original: *ex ocimo sc[orpiones]*. A common belief in Renaissance natural magic was "spontaneous generation," such as the idea that scorpions would grow from crushed basil placed between two stones and left in the sun.</note>

<summary>Ficino explains how a "mage" uses natural sympathies and "allurements" to graft celestial power onto earthly objects, comparing the process to agriculture and spontaneous generation.</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, Ptolemy, magnetism, alchemy, grafting, spontaneous generation, celestial influence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s exploration of how earthly materials can be prepared to "capture" or "reflect" celestial influences. ### ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.

...[just as if you were to offe]r a glass <note>original: *vitrum*. Here referring to a mirror.</note> to your face, or a wall placed opposite your voice; it suddenly receives an influence from above—namely from a most powerful agent, a power and a wonderful life present everywhere—and through this reception, it carries back that power. In no other way does a mirror represent an image from a face, or a wall represent an Echo from a voice. 

Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD), the founding philosopher of Neoplatonism, whom Ficino translated into Latin.</note> himself uses these very examples where, imitating Hermes <term>Trismegistus</term> <note>The legendary "Thrice-Great" Hermes, the mythical Egyptian author of the Hermetic Corpus, which combined philosophy, magic, and alchemy.</note>, he says: the ancient priests, or mages, used to capture something divine and wonderful in statues and through sensible sacrifices. He intends, however, along with Trismegistus, that through these material things they do not receive divinities that are entirely separated from matter, but only "worldly" ones, as I said from the beginning and as Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a philosopher-bishop who wrote on how the soul can be influenced by the stars.</note> approves. 

By "worldly," I mean a certain life, or something vital, drawn from the Soul of the World <term>Anima Mundi</term> <note>The "Soul of the World"; in Ficino’s system, this is a mediating spirit that connects the physical universe to the divine mind.</note> and from the souls of the spheres and stars, or even a certain motion and a vital presence, as it were, from the <term>daemons</term> <note>In the Platonic tradition, *daemons* are not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits of the air and stars that act as messengers between gods and humans.</note>. Indeed, Hermes himself—whom Plotinus follows—says that sometimes these very daemons are present in such materials; he means aerial daemons, not celestial ones, much less those even more sublime. 

Hermes himself composes statues out of herbs, trees, stones, and aromatics that possess in themselves (as he says) a natural power of divinity. He adds songs similar to the celestial [harmonies], by which he says the daemons are delighted, and thus they remain in the statues for a longer time, and either benefit or harm men. He adds that the ancient Egyptian sages, who were also priests, since they could not persuade the people by reason that there were "Gods"—that is, certain spirits...

<summary>Ficino explains the Neoplatonic and Hermetic theory of "ensouling" statues, arguing that physical materials can be tuned like a mirror or an echo to catch the vital, spiritual influences of the World Soul and intermediary spirits.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Hermes Trismegistus, Anima Mundi, daemons, statues, natural magic, Neoplatonism, Egyptian priests, celestial harmony</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion on the "Soul of the World" and how ancient practitioners believed they could capture celestial or "daemonic" spirits within physical objects. It transitions from the general theory of Plotinus to specific historical practices of the Egyptians and Chaldeans.</meta>

...that [they] devised this magic lure <note>original: *magicum hoc illicium*. A "lure" or "bait" implies the material object acts as a trap for spiritual influences.</note> above common men, by which, attracting **Demons** <note>original: *Daemones*. In the Neoplatonic sense, these are not necessarily "evil" spirits but intermediate beings between gods and humans.</note>, they declared them to be divinities within statues. 

But **Iamblichus** <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD), a Syrian Neoplatonist philosopher who defended the use of "theurgy" or divine ritual.</note> condemns the Egyptians because they did not merely accept **Demons** as certain steps toward investigating the higher gods, but worshipped them most greatly. He prefers the **Chaldeans** <note>The Chaldeans were often associated with pure astrology and "higher" celestial magic, whereas the Egyptians were sometimes accused of lower, more material idolatry.</note> to the Egyptians, as the Chaldeans were not occupied by [lower] Demons; he calls the Chaldeans the "high priests of religion." 

For we suspect that the astrologers of both the Chaldeans and the Egyptians attempted, in some way, to draw **Demons** into clay statues through celestial harmony. This seems to be indicated by the Hebrew astrologer **Samuel**, relying on the authority of **David Bil** the astrologer. Namely, that the ancient makers of images made statues that pronounced the future. 

He reports that the **harmony of the heavens** was adapted to these: specifically, to cast metal into the form of a beautiful man on the day of **Mercury**, at the third hour—namely, the hour of **Saturn**. This occurs when Mercury enters Saturn in **Aquarius**, in the ninth region of the sky, which designates prophecy. And the constellation of **Gemini** rises, signifying prophets (as they say); **Mars** is "combust" <term>combustus</term> <note>In astrology, a planet is "combust" when it is so close to the Sun that its influence is supposedly hidden or burned away.</note> by the **Sun**, and does not look upon Mercury. The Sun, however, gazes upon the place of that conjunction. Meanwhile, **Venus** occupies a certain angle; she is western and powerful. The **Moon** from a trine <term>trigono</term> <note>A "trine" is a harmonious astrological aspect where planets are 120 degrees apart.</note> gazes upon the ascending degree, and Saturn does the same. 

These are their views. I, however, think first of all according to the opinion of **Blessed Thomas** <note>St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the preeminent Catholic theologian. Ficino often cites him to prove his "natural magic" is compatible with Christian doctrine.</note>, if only statues [were] speaking...

<summary>Ficino discusses the use of astrological timing and materials by ancient Egyptians and Chaldeans to create prophetic statues, while contrasting their methods and referencing St. Thomas Aquinas.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Chaldeans, Egyptians, prophetic statues, astrological timing, Mercury, Saturn, Mars combust, trine aspect, Thomas Aquinas</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s exploration of the "Soul of the World" and the animation of statues. It picks up from a discussion on how ancient practitioners believed Mercury’s influence could be captured in physical forms to make them speak.</meta>

[¶] ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.

...they <note>Referring back to the ancient image-makers or "priests" mentioned on the previous page.</note> brought it about that words were not formed there simply by the influx of the stars themselves, but by **demons**. Furthermore, if it should happen that they enter into such statues, I do not believe that they were bound there by celestial influx, but rather that they voluntarily obeyed their worshippers; and ultimately, that they were there to deceive. For a superior nature is indeed sometimes enticed <note>original: *conciliatur*. Suggests a "reconciliation" or "winning over" through natural affinity.</note> by an inferior one, but it cannot be constrained. <note>original: *cohiberi nequit*. A central point for Ficino: the spiritual world can be attracted by material "bait," but the human will or ritual cannot truly imprison a higher spirit.</note> And that disposition of the stars described a little earlier perhaps cannot concur with the ritual. 

Although **demons** are not included in statues by astronomical reasoning, yet when they are present through the worship offered to them, **Porphyry** <note>Porphyry (c. 234–305 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Plotinus who wrote on the nature of spiritual beings.</note> says they gave oracles according to astronomical rules; and therefore, they were frequently ambiguous. And rightly so, since **Iamblichus** <note>A Syrian Neoplatonist who emphasized the importance of ritual (theurgy) in reaching the divine.</note> proves that true and certain prophecy neither suits evil demons, nor results from human arts or nature, but comes from divine inspiration to purified minds.

But let us now return to **Hermes** <note>original: *Mercurium*. Marsilio Ficino refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage believed to be the author of the Hermetic texts.</note>—or rather, to **Plotinus**. Hermes says that the priests received a power suited to the nature of the world, and mixed it. Plotinus, following him, thinks that this whole thing can be accomplished through the mediation of the **Soul of the World** <note>original: *anima mundi*. In Neoplatonic thought, this is the intermediary soul that connects the eternal "Ideas" of the divine mind to the physical, changing world.</note>. Insofar as that Soul generates and moves the forms of natural things through certain **seminal reasons** <term>seminal reasons: *rationes seminales*, the underlying blueprints or formative powers within the Soul of the World that allow it to produce physical objects according to divine patterns.</term> divinely implanted within it. These "reasons" he even calls **Gods**, since they are never abandoned by the Ideas of the Supreme Mind. Therefore, through such reasons, the Soul of the World easily applies itself to materials which it formed from the beginning through...

<summary>Ficino argues that while statues do not "trap" spirits by astrology alone, they can attract them through natural affinity, though true prophecy only comes from divine inspiration. He explains how the Soul of the World uses "seminal reasons" to bridge the gap between divine ideas and physical matter.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Book III of Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life" (De vita libri tres), titled "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." The text finishes the discussion on how material objects can be "attuned" to celestial influences through natural sympathies and transitions into an "Apology" where Ficino defends his work against charges of magic.</meta>

...the same things: when a magus or a priest, at the appropriate times, applies the forms of things correctly collected which correspond closely to this or that **seminal reason** <note>original: *rationem*. In Neoplatonic thought, "seminal reasons" are the formative, spiritual blueprints within the Soul of the World that shape physical matter.</note>; just as the magnet attracts iron, rhubarb attracts bile, saffron the heart, agrimony and burnt ivory the liver, and spikenard and musk the brain.

Indeed, it can sometimes happen that when these reasons are applied to physical forms in this way, even more sublime gifts may descend, insofar as the reasons in the **Soul of the World** <note>original: *anima mundi*. The vital spirit that connects the physical universe to the divine realm.</note> are joined to the intellectual forms of that same soul, and through them to the **Ideas** of the Divine Mind. **Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who argued that ritual and material objects could be used to reach the divine.</note> also approves of this where he treats sacrifices. We shall discuss this matter more opportunely elsewhere. There it will also be made clear how impure the superstition of the pagan people was, and by contrast, how pure the piety of the Gospel is; a task we have already performed for the most part in our book *On the Christian Religion*.

<meta>This page begins the "Apology" or formal defense of the *Three Books on Life*. Ficino addresses this section to three of his close friends, all of whom shared the name Piero (Peter): Piero del Nero, Piero Guicciardini, and Piero Soderini. He uses this rhetorical letter to justify his interest in "natural magic" and medicine while asserting his identity as a Christian priest.</meta>

# APOLOGY

## -> MARSILIO FICINO the Florentine to his most beloved Peters: Nero, Guicciardini, and Soderini; greetings three and four times over. <-

Perhaps I should have more correctly said **Triple-Peter** rather than **three Peters**. For just as where there is one palm, the many fingers upon it do not make many hands; so your three bodies, my friends, seem to offer no obstacle to a single will making of you one **Peter**. 

Christ, that Craftsman of the heavenly homeland, created so great a **Rock** <note>original: *petram*. Ficino is playing on the Latin name *Petrus* (Peter) and the word *petra* (rock/stone), referencing the biblical foundation of the Church on "Peter the Rock."</note> that for the immense edifice of the Church...

<column-break/>

...Someone will therefore ask: is Marsilio not a priest? He is indeed. What then <note>original: *Quid*. Ficino is beginning a rhetorical defense of why a priest is writing about natural medicine and celestial influences.</note> 

-> r.iii. <- <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of a book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Ficino begins his "Apology" by addressing three friends named Piero, using a clever play on words to link their unity of will to the "Rock" of the Church. He begins to address the potential criticism that his medical and philosophical writings are inappropriate for a priest.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Apology, Piero del Nero, Piero Guicciardini, Piero Soderini, Church, Peter the Rock, priesthood, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s defense of his work, specifically addressing the three "Peters" mentioned on the previous page. He is responding to potential critics who argue that his interests in medicine and astrology are inappropriate for a Christian priest.</meta>

...has to do with priests and medicine? What business do they have with astrology? Another likewise asks: "What does a Christian have to do with magic or images?" Yet another, truly unworthy of life himself, will begrudge life to the heavens <note>Ficino implies that these critics are so narrow-minded they refuse to see the life-giving influence of the celestial bodies created by God.</note>. Finally, all those so affected will be far too ungrateful for the benefit we offer them. And they will not be ashamed to be cruel toward our charity—that charity through which we provide for the life and public prosperity to the best of our ability. 

This labor will therefore be shared among you. But so that it may be somewhat lighter: since you are three, you shall undergo a struggle distributed against three enemies. Nor will you refute invective with invective (for I know your character); rather, you will overcome the bitterness of another’s gall with the wonderful sweetness of your own honey.

¶ To begin, most brilliant Nero <note>Piero del Nero, one of the three "Peters" addressed in the prologue.</note>, respond to the first critics that the most ancient priests were once physicians and likewise astronomers. This is certainly testified by the histories of the Chaldeans <note>Ancient Mesopotamian people renowned in the Renaissance for their supposed foundational knowledge of astrology and magic.</note>, Persians, and Egyptians. Furthermore, the duties of singular charity pertain to no one more than to a pious priest; and these duties shine most brightly in the greatest benefit of all. For the most excellent duty is beyond doubt that which is both most necessary and most desired by everyone: namely, to bring it about that men have "a sound mind in a sound body" <note>original: *mens sana in corpore sano*. A famous phrase from the Roman poet Juvenal, emphasizing the importance of physical health to mental and spiritual well-being.</note>. This, however, we can only provide if we join medicine to the priesthood.

<summary>Ficino defends the priest's role in medicine and astrology, arguing that ancient civilizations saw no conflict between these roles and that the priest's duty of charity includes caring for both mind and body.</summary>
<keywords>Piero del Nero, medicine, astrology, priesthood, charity, Chaldeans, sound mind in a sound body, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s defense (the "Apologia") of his work *On Life* (*De Vita*). Having established that ancient priests were also physicians and astronomers, he now argues that celestial influence is necessary for medicine to be effective, and therefore a priest—driven by charity—must understand astrology to truly help the sick.</meta>

# DEFENSE

But since medicine without celestial favor—as both Hippocrates and Galen <note>Hippocrates (c. 460–370 BCE) and Galen (129–c. 216 CE) were the two most authoritative figures in ancient Greek medicine; Ficino cites them to show that even the "fathers" of medicine recognized the influence of the stars.</note> admit, and as we ourselves have experienced—is often useless and often even harmful, astrology certainly belongs to that same priestly charity to which we said medicine belongs. Sacred Scripture commands that a physician of this kind be honored, since the Most High created him because of necessity <note>A reference to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 38:1: "Honor the physician for the need thou hast of him: for the Most High hath created him."</note>. And Christ himself, the giver of life, who commanded his disciples to cure the sick throughout the whole world, will also command priests to heal: if they cannot do so with words <note>original: "uerbis"</note> as the disciples once did, they should at least heal with herbs and stones. 

If these things are not sufficient by themselves, he will command that they be fused with the timely breath of heaven <note>original: "afflatu cęli." Ficino refers to the *spiritus mundi* or celestial influence that can be "captured" in medicine when prepared at the right astrological moment.</note> and applied to the sick. For God himself, through that same celestial breath, stirs animals everywhere toward their own remedies, so abundantly does he provide for the life of all. Thus, driven by divine celestial instinct, serpents treat their eyes with fennel <note>original: "maratro." In classical lore, serpents were believed to eat fennel to shed their skins and sharpen their sight.</note>, and swallows treat theirs with celandine. Eagles, troubled by labor, divinely discovered the eagle-stone <note>original: "aetitem lapidẽ." Also known as an eaglestone, this was a hollow geode believed to assist in childbirth if bound to the thigh.</note>, which allows them to bring forth their eggs successfully and swiftly. 

Therefore, God himself, who through the heavens urges all animals toward medicines, surely permits priests—not for pay, I say, but out of charity—to drive out diseases with medicines strengthened by the heavens. To these points, you shall thereafter add more, if it is useful, with the sharpness of your own wit.

->r.iiii.<- <note>This is a quire signature, a printer's mark used to keep the pages of a book in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>Ficino argues that since God provides animals with natural instincts and celestial remedies, it is the duty of a charitable priest to use astrology and medicine to heal the sick.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, medical astrology, priesthood, charity, herbal medicine, eagle-stone, Hippocrates, Galen, natural magic</keywords>

--- Page 268 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s defense (the "Apologia") of his work *On Life* (*De Vita*). In the preceding text, Ficino argued that priests should be physicians and use astrology to heal. Here, he specifically addresses his critics—personified by his friend Piero Guicciardini—to distinguish his "natural magic" from forbidden sorcery.</meta>

Arise now after these things, you too, vehement Guicciardini <note>Piero Guicciardini (1454–1513) was a Florentine diplomat and a supporter of Ficino; Ficino addresses him here as a friendly intermediary to speak against his critics.</note>, and answer those inquisitive minds: tell them that magic or the use of images are not indeed "approved" by Marsilio, but rather recounted, as he interprets Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 CE) was the founder of Neoplatonism. Ficino’s work *On Life* was heavily influenced by Plotinus’s theories on how the soul relates to the body and the cosmos.</note> himself. This his writings plainly declare, if they are read with an open mind. 

Not a single word is asserted here concerning profane magic—which relies on the worship of demons—but mention is made instead of natural magic, which captures celestial benefits through natural things to ensure the healthy state of our bodies. This faculty should surely be as granted to those who use it legitimately as medicine and agriculture are rightly granted. Indeed, it is all the more worthy as it is a more perfect labor, coupling heavenly things with earthly things. 

It was from this workshop <note>original: "officina"; here used metaphorically to mean a school of knowledge or a tradition of practice.</note> that the Magi, the first of all, adored Christ immediately upon his birth. Why then, O fearful man, do you dread the name of Magus? It is a name favored by the Gospel. It does not imply an evildoer or a poisoner, but rather sounds of a wise man and a priest. 

What indeed does that Magus, the first worshiper of Christ, profess? If you wish to hear: he is like a farmer; he is certainly a "cultivator of the world" <note>original: "mundicola"; a play on the word *agricola* (field-cultivator). Ficino suggests that just as a farmer tends the earth, the Magus tends the relationship between the earth and the stars.</note>. Nor does he worship the world because of this, just as a farmer does not worship the earth. But just as a farmer, for the sake of human nourishment, tempers his field to the air, so that wise man—that priest—for the sake of human health, tempers the lower parts of the world to the higher parts; and just as a hen does with her eggs, he opportunely subjects earthly things to be nurtured by the heavens.

<summary>Ficino defends his work against charges of sorcery by distinguishing "natural magic" (which uses the stars for healing) from "profane magic" (which involves demons). He argues that the Magus is a "cultivator of the world" much like a farmer is a cultivator of the earth.</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, Plotinus, Magi, medicine, agriculture, Piero Guicciardini, astrology, celestial influence</keywords>

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s defense (the "Apologia") of his work *On Life*. In the previous section, Ficino argued that the term "Magus" is an honorable one, sanctioned by the Gospel, and signifies a wise priest rather than a sorcerer. He now moves to define the specific types of magic to clarify which one he advocates.</meta>

# DEFENSE.

God himself always effects this: and by doing so, he teaches and persuades us to do the same, so that the lowest things may be generated, moved, and ruled by the higher things. Finally, there are two kinds of magic. One indeed is of those who, through a certain worship, win over demons <note>original: "Dęmonas"; in this context, Ficino refers to the forbidden practice of summoning spirits or fallen angels.</note> to themselves; relying on their help, they often perform wonders. This kind, however, was utterly cast out when the "prince of this world" <note>A reference to John 12:31; Ficino argues that demonic magic was rendered powerless and illicit after the coming of Christ.</note> was driven out. The other kind is of those who opportunely subject natural materials to natural causes, to be formed in a certain marvelous way.

Of this craft, too, there are two types. One is indeed curious; the other is necessary. The former surely invents useless prodigies for the sake of show: as when the Persian magi, from sage rotted under manure while the Sun and Moon occupied the second "face" of Leo <note>A "face" (or decan) is an astrological subdivision of a zodiac sign consisting of ten degrees. Here, Ficino describes a specific, albeit bizarre, ritual timing.</note> and held the same degree there, used to generate a bird similar to a blackbird with a serpent's tail; and having reduced it to ash, they would pour it into a lamp. Whereupon the house would immediately appear full of serpents. This is to be avoided from afar, as something vain and harmful to one's salvation. Nevertheless, the necessary type must be maintained, which couples medicine with astrology. 

But if anyone stubbornly persists further [in criticizing us], deal with him in this way, Guicciardini: let him not read these writings of ours, nor understand them, nor remember them; nor let that man—if he is a man—use them, for he is utterly unworthy of so great a benefit. There are many other things besides which you, by your own wit, can bring forward against ungrateful ignorance. 

What then are you doing and...

<summary>Ficino distinguishes between forbidden demonic magic and legitimate natural magic, further dividing the latter into "curious" (vain tricks) and "necessary" (the medical application of astrology).</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, demonic magic, astrology, medicine, Leo, Persian magi, Piero Guicciardini, decans, occult philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s *Apologia*, following his distinction between demonic magic (which is forbidden) and natural magic (which is a necessary art). He is now transitioning into a defense of the "World Soul," arguing that if the smallest plants have life, the entire universe must also be alive.</meta>

... [must be maintained,] and will you, our vigorous Soderini <note>Francesco Soderini (1453–1524), a Florentine diplomat and friend to whom Ficino addressed this defense.</note>, tolerate those certain superstitious and blind men of the future? They see life clearly manifest in animals—even the most abject—and in the lowliest herbs, yet they do not see it in the heavens or in the world. 

Now indeed, if these little men <note>original: "homunciones"; a diminutive, mocking term for his critics.</note> grant life to the smallest particles of the world, what madness is this at last? What envy is it to neither know nor wish for the whole in which "we live, and move, and have our being" <note>A quotation from Acts 17:28, where St. Paul addresses the Athenians.</note> to be alive? Indeed, Aratus <note>A Greek didactic poet (c. 315–240 BCE). Paul quoted his poem *Phaenomena* in the Book of Acts.</note> sings of this, clearly signifying "Jove" as the common life of the mundane body. 

I seem to have stumbled upon these words of Aratus now quite opportunely. I remember Luke the Evangelist and I remember Paul the Apostle freely using these words. In these words, those wise men do not shudder at the "life of the world" <note>Ficino is arguing that if the Bible uses language suggesting the universe is alive, then the philosophical concept of the World Soul (*Anima Mundi*) should not be considered heretical.</note>. 

But indeed, some superstitious person will object to this, saying it is not easily proven from words of this kind that Paul agrees the world has a soul, but only that it is subject to God, and that we live in this God himself. Let it be so, then. Let us not name a "soul" in the world if it does not please them; let the name "soul" be considered profane. But will it be permitted at least to speak of some kind of "life" which God himself, the craftsman of the world, might mercifully inspire into this work of His, so happily completed? 

Since indeed He is not stingy toward even the lowliest living things, and daily through the heavens He most generously provides life to the many things that are in it. Tell me, I pray—do you not see that oxen and donkeys (O you ox! O you donkey! <note>Ficino humorously insults his critics by addressing them as the very animals he is discussing.</note>) who generate living things from themselves in a certain silent way, are alive? If therefore these things, moreover, from themselves...

<summary>Ficino defends the concept of a living universe (World Soul) by citing St. Paul's use of the poet Aratus, arguing that if low animals have life, the heavens must also be alive.</summary>
<keywords>Anima Mundi, World Soul, Aratus, St. Paul, Francesco Soderini, natural philosophy, theology vs. philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s defense of the World Soul. Following the previous page’s argument that the universe must be alive because it is the source of all life, Ficino now uses the metaphor of the Heavens as a husband to the Earth and calls upon his famous friends—Landino and Poliziano—to help defend his work against "superstitious" critics.</meta>

[¶ in red ink] APOLOGIA.

...if certain things were to generate even by their very gaze, would you not judge these things to be living in a far more wondrous way? That is, if you possess any judgment or life yourself. Heaven, the husband of the earth (as is the common opinion), does not touch the earth. He does not lie with his wife in a physical sense; instead, with the rays of the sun and stars—as if with eyes—he illuminates his wife from every side. By this illumination, he makes her fertile and procreates living beings. Will you then grant that he bestows life simply by looking, yet has no life of his own? And that which gave life and a life-giving gaze to the ostrich <note>original: "aui strutho"; referring to a common medieval and Renaissance belief that the ostrich hatches its eggs simply by staring at them intensely.</note>: is this heaven then far inferior to that bird? Now that these arguments have finally been brought into the open, unless you can persuade that superstitious man, let him go as one who is half-dead—or rather, not living at all. 

[¶ in red ink] Therefore, so that we may plead our cause with even more defenders, my Pietro Nero <note>Pietro Nero was a Florentine student and close associate of Ficino.</note>, add our "Amphion" <note>In Greek mythology, Amphion was a musician who moved stones with the sweetness of his lyre to build the walls of Thebes; Ficino uses him as a metaphor for persuasive eloquence.</note>, Cristoforo Landino <note>A famous humanist scholar and commentator on Dante and Virgil.</note>, the orator and poet alike. Our Amphion, with his wonderful sweetness, will quickly soften the stony hearts of our enemies. 

[¶ in red ink] But you, Guicciardini <note>Piero Guicciardini (1454–1513), a prominent Florentine and father of the historian Francesco Guicciardini.</note>, my dearest godfather, go now, go swiftly, and summon the "Polizian Hercules" <note>Angelo Poliziano (1454–1494), the preeminent poet and scholar of the Medici circle, known for his formidable intellectual defenses.</note>. Hercules once, when a more dangerous struggle was at hand, would call upon Iolaus <note>Hercules' nephew and companion who helped him defeat the Lernaean Hydra.</note>; you now, in similar fashion, must call upon Hercules. You surely know how our Polizian Hercules attacked, tore apart, and slew the barbarian monsters <note>Ficino refers to unlearned or overly rigid critics who he felt were "devastating" the refined Latin culture of Italy.</note> already devastating Latium <note>Italy.</note>, and how fiercely he fights them everywhere. How safely he defends! For here, even a hundred heads of the Hydra threaten our children <note>original: "liberis"; a pun on "books" (libri) and "children" (liberi). Ficino viewed his published works as his spiritual offspring.</note>.

<summary>Ficino argues that if the heavens fertilize the earth through celestial light, the universe must be alive. He calls upon his intellectual allies, Cristoforo Landino and Angelo Poliziano, to defend his "children" (his books) against hostile, superstitious critics.</summary>

<keywords>Anima Mundi, World Soul, celestial influence, ostrich egg myth, Cristoforo Landino, Angelo Poliziano, Hydra, Pietro Nero, Piero Guicciardini, Apologia</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Ficino’s "Apologia" (Defense) of his work *On Life*, specifically addressing his critics through the metaphor of a hunt and mythological battle. It then transitions into a new letter addressed to his fellow "hunters of truth" regarding the necessity of a tranquil mind.</meta>

...with a club, and he will burn them with flames.

¶ Come, my sweetest Soderini <note>Francesco Soderini (1453–1524), a Florentine diplomat and bishop, and a close friend of Ficino.</note>; rise, go now, and greet our Phoebean Pico <note>Referring to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), whom Ficino compares to Phoebus Apollo, the god of light and wisdom.</note>. I often call him my Phoebus. He, in turn, calls me Dionysus and Bacchus <note>Dionysus and Bacchus are names for the god of wine and divine ecstasy; Ficino and Pico viewed their friendship as a union of Apollonian clarity and Dionysian inspiration.</note>. Therefore, we are brothers. Announce to my Phoebus that a poisonous python <note>original: "pythonem"; in mythology, Apollo slew the monstrous serpent Python at Delphi. Here, Ficino uses it to represent his venomous critics.</note> is even now emerging from the marsh against us. Beseech him, I pray, to bend his bow. Let him hurl his arrows at once. He will stretch it immediately—I know what I say—and he will kill all that venom at once with a single death-blow.

¶ Farewell now in happiness, my most beloved brothers; you are worthy not only of happy health, but of happiness itself. Take care of the health and happiness of my children <note>Ficino frequently referred to his books and philosophical writings as his "children" (*liberorum*).</note> now coming forth into the light.

->¶ September 15, 1489. At the estate in Careggi.<-

## ¶ That security and tranquility of the soul are necessary for life.

<note>A large, decorative blue initial letter M contains a smaller, simplified letter 'm' inside it to guide the rubricator.</note> **MARSILIO Ficino** to his most beloved brothers in the hunt for truth, Bernardo Canigiani, Giovanni Canaccio, and Amerigo Corsini, sends greetings. Since we established a certain hunt in these opening words, we perhaps rightly and immediately employed hounds and runners. Indeed, we aptly called those practicing philosophy "hunters," as they are always laboring in the breathless pursuit <note>original: "idagine"; a technical term for encircling an area with nets during a hunt.</note> of truth. But are they also aptly called hounds? "Most aptly," says Socrates in the *Republic*. For those who philosophize are either legitimate or spurious. Both are hounds. Those who...

<summary>Ficino concludes his defense by calling on Pico della Mirandola to silence his critics and begins a new discourse on mental tranquility, using a hunting metaphor to describe the philosophical search for truth.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Pico della Mirandola, Soderini, Apollo and Dionysus, Careggi, hunting metaphor, Socrates' Republic, tranquility of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page provides the conclusion to Ficino’s "Apologia" (Defense) of his work *On Life*. He moves from defending his books against "wolves" (critics) to addressing his inner circle of friends in the Florentine Academy using the metaphor of hunting dogs. He then transitions into a discussion on the importance of mental tranquility.</meta>

# APOLOGIA.

...[who] sagaciously investigate the truth itself, and once found, they hold onto it with their teeth. These others, however, bark, bite, and tear on behalf of mere opinion. Indeed, dogs claim such a place among philosophers that they have not only inserted themselves into a certain sect, but they even once consecrated their own sect with the name "Cynic" <note>original: *Cynicā*; the Cynic school of philosophy, founded by Antisthenes, took its name from the Greek word for "dog-like" (*kynikos*) to reflect their rejection of social conventions and their "watchdog" role over morality.</note>.

Moreover, the Academy has its own dogs. Therefore, come here, you sagacious dogs of the Academy; come here, you swiftest runners, I call upon you. For you are three. I pray you three now defend my children <note>Ficino frequently referred to his books and philosophical writings as his "children" (*liberos*).</note>, who are still quite tender and, I fear, about to be lost at the track among wolves <note>Ficino compares his critics to wolves who devour the "children" of his mind.</note>. Run, I say, with alacrity: for I entrust to you tasks that are desired, not cares. 

You know my George Benignus Salviati <note>Juraj Dragišić (c. 1445–1520), a Franciscan scholar and theologian from Croatia who lived in Florence and was a member of Ficino’s circle.</note>: He has long since sagaciously attained that truth whose tracks you are now hunting everywhere. Like the Sun, he, being greater, illuminates his smaller brothers. Therefore, if you hear any howling of wolves, announce it to him. That most brave George will easily put all the wolves to flight, for he once pierced a vast dragon <note>A playful reference to the legend of Saint George and the Dragon, punning on Benigno’s first name and his ability to defeat "monstrous" intellectual opponents.</note>. He, therefore, will relieve me of anxiety and you of care at the same time.

¶ For one among you is accustomed to say—and indeed quite often now—that nothing more healthy can be experienced in life than to "swallow the seasons" <note>original: *tempora deglutire*; an idiomatic expression suggesting the consumption of time with ease, or perhaps living so fully in the moment that one "devours" time rather than being devoured by it.</note> with great security. The others, indeed, immediately smile at the one saying this. But come, tell us, Canaces <note>Likely a nickname for one of Ficino's friends, taken from the name of one of Actaeon's hounds in Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*, keeping with the "Academy dogs" metaphor.</note>, what is this saying of yours that you so often...

<summary>Ficino concludes his defense by calling upon his friends in the Platonic Academy to protect his writings from critics, comparing his allies to loyal hunting dogs and his enemies to wolves. He specifically praises the scholar George Benignus Salviati as a defender of truth.</summary>

<keywords>Platonic Academy, George Benignus Salviati, Cynicism, allegory of the hunt, defense of philosophy, tranquility of soul, Juraj Dragišić</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s concluding remarks in his "Apologia" (Defense). Having addressed his critics and his circle of friends, he now turns to a philosophical reflection on the nature of time and the human soul. He argues that trying to "grasp" or "consume" time and spiritual truths too tightly is counterproductive, using the physics of fluids and the elements as a metaphor for the mind's need for expansive freedom.</meta>

...to flight. <note>original: *repetitum?*—this appears to be the end of the sentence from the previous page, perhaps "all the wolves he will put to flight having been sought out again?"</note> To devour time? What, finally, do you want for yourself? You will say, "Not to eat, but rather to drink." Not to chew or grind, but to gulp down with full throats. For indeed, time itself is a certain fluid nature (so to speak) and fleeting. 

This is the condition of fluid things: that if you confine them within a narrow space, you suddenly lose them. For when they are forced together, they flow away and quickly escape. If you happen to squeeze water that has been soaked into a sponge, you will press it out and immediately scatter it. But if you hold it more broadly, you will retain it. This is much more the case with air, fire, and the ether <note>The fifth element in ancient and Renaissance science, thought to compose the celestial spheres.</note>. 

Hence, among the poets, those who strive to grasp the vast images or hands of the gods with their arms struggle in vain. The broadest things must be received in a very broad manner. Things that are fluid and most vast must be possessed in a most vast way. Then, certainly, <term>distress</term> <note>original: *angustia*; Ficino is playing on the double meaning of this Latin word, which refers both to a narrow, confined space and to a state of mental anxiety or suffering.</note> weighs heavily upon us when we reduce the soul itself and its motion—which is naturally broad—into a narrow space. Whoever weighs their studies and business with a balance...

<summary>Ficino uses metaphors of fluids and the elements to argue that the human soul and time itself cannot be "grasped" or "squeezed" through narrow-mindedness without being lost. He suggests that spiritual and intellectual pursuits require an expansive, broad state of mind to avoid the distress of anxiety.</summary>
<keywords>time, fluidity, soul, distress, ether, metaphorical physics, Neoplatonism, George Benignus Salviati</keywords>

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>This page concludes Marsilio Ficino’s "Apologia" (Defense). Having argued in the previous pages that the soul needs "breadth" rather than "narrowness" to thrive, Ficino now offers his final advice to his friends on how to avoid the "constriction" of anxiety through a paradoxical embrace of relaxation.</meta>

# APOLOGIA.

...more safely. <note>original: *tutius*? This is the conclusion of the sentence from the previous page.</note> ¶ Live, therefore, expansive and far from distress, O my friends. Live in joy. Heaven created you with its own joy. This joy the heavens declare through a kind of smile—that is, through expansion, motion, and splendor—as if exulting. Your own joy will preserve you for heaven. Therefore, live joyfully every day, above all else. For anxiety over present things snatches the present away from you, and it steals the future in advance. Curiosity about the future quickly drags you into the past. Again, therefore, I pray, and pray again: live in joy.

For the Fates allow it, so long as you live free from care <note>original: *securi*; literally "without care."</note>. But so that you may truly live without care, do not take up even this one care: to worry anxiously about how you might most diligently escape cares. ¶ For this single care, alas, burns the hearts of wretched mortals with every kind of worry. Disregard "diligence," then; instead, love "negligence" <note>Ficino plays on the Latin words *diligere* (to love/choose) and *negligere* (to neglect/disregard). He is advising a state of "holy indifference" or relaxation rather than obsessive effort.</note>. Yet even love this negligence in a negligent way, so far as is permitted and fitting for you. 

¶ I command these things to you, my friends, not as a priest, but as a physician. For without this one thing—which is, as it were, the very life of all medicines—all other medicines applied to prolong life will simply perish.

->September 16, 1489. In the fields of Careggi.<- <note>The Medici villa at Careggi was the heart of the Platonic Academy in Florence, where Ficino lived and worked.</note>

<note>A circular library stamp is visible at the bottom of the page. It contains the text "BIBLIOTHEQUE DE..." surrounding a central figure, likely a classical head in profile, marking the volume as part of a formal collection.</note>

<summary>Ficino concludes his defense by urging his friends to live joyfully and avoid the mental "narrowness" of anxiety, framing this advice as medical necessity rather than just religious or moral instruction.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Joy (Letitia), Anxiety (Sollicitudo), Careggi, Renaissance Medicine, Platonic Academy, Carefree living (Securitas)</keywords>
